明 · 張應俞 · Bilingual Reader

騙經

A New Book for Foiling Swindlers, Based on Worldly Experience — Zhang Yingyu’s late-Ming collection of fraud stories, with a moral note after each tale.

第一類 脫剝騙

Category 1 · Making Off with Goods

假馬脫緞

Using a Horse as Bait to Make Off with Silk

江西有陳姓慶名者，常販馬，徃南京承恩寺前，三山街賣。時有一疋銀合好馬，價約值四十金。忽有一棍，擎好傘，穿色衣，翩然而來，佇立瞻顧，不忍舍去。遂問曰：「此馬價賣幾許？」慶曰：「四十兩。」棍曰：「我買，但要歸家作契對銀。」慶問：「何住？」棍曰：「居洪武門。」棍遂騎銀合馬徃，慶亦騎馬随後。行至半途，棍見一緞鋪，即下馬放傘於酒坊邊，囑慶曰：「代看住，待我買緞幾疋，少頃與你同歸。」慶忖：「此人想是富翁，馬諒買得成矣。」棍入緞鋪，故意與之爭價。待緞客以不識價責之，遂佯曰：「我把與一相知者看，即來還價何如？」緞客曰：「有此好物，憑伊與人看，但不可遠去。」棍曰：「我有馬與夥在，更何慮乎？」将緞拿過手，出門便迯去。緞客見馬與夥尚在，心中安然。慶待至午，杳不見來，意必棍徒也。遂舍其傘，騎銀合，又牵一馬囬店。緞客忙奔前，扯住慶曰：「你夥拿吾緞去，你将焉徃？」慶曰：「何人是我夥？」緞客曰：「適間與你同騎馬來者。你何佯推？定要問你取。」慶曰：「那人不知何方鬼，祇是問我買馬，令我同到他家接銀，故與之同來矣。他說在你店買緞，少頃與我同去。我待乆不見來，故騎自馬囬店。你何得妄纏我乎？」緞客曰：「若不是你夥，何�纏我乎？」緞客曰：「若不是你夥，何呌你看傘與馬？我因見你與馬在，始以緞與他。你何通同粧套，脫我緞去？」二人爭辯不伏，扭在應天府理論。緞客以前情直告。慶訴曰：「慶籍江西，販馬為主，常在三山街，翁春店發賣，何嘗作棍？緣遇一人，問我買馬，必要到他家還銀，是以同行。彼中途下馬，在他店拿緞迯去，我亦不知，怎說我是棍之夥？」府尹曰：「不必言，拘店家來問，即見明白。」其店家曰：「慶常販馬，安歇吾家，乃老實本分人也。」緞客曰：「既是老實人，緣何代那棍看傘與馬？此我明白聽見，况他應諾。」慶曰：「呌我看傘，多因為他買馬故也，豈與之同夥？」府尹曰：「那人去，傘亦拿去否？」緞客曰：「未曾拿去。」府尹曰：「此真是棍了。欲脫你緞，故託買馬，以陳慶為質。以他人之馬，賺你之緞，是假道滅虢術也。此你自遭騙，何可罪慶？」各逐出免供。

In Jiangxi there was a man surnamed Chen, given name Qing, who regularly dealt in horses, taking them to Nanjing to sell in front of Cheng'en Temple on Sanshan Street. At the time he had a fine silver-gray horse worth about forty taels. Suddenly a con man appeared, holding a fine umbrella and wearing showy clothes, sauntering up with an easy air. He stood there gazing at the horse, reluctant to tear himself away. Then he asked, "How much are you asking for this horse?" Qing said, "Forty taels." The con man said, "I'll buy it, but I have to go back home to draw up the deed and weigh out the silver." Qing asked, "Where do you live?" The con man said, "By Hongwu Gate." So the con man rode off on the silver-gray horse, with Qing following behind on another horse. Halfway there, the con man saw a silk shop. He dismounted, set his umbrella down beside a tavern, and told Qing, "Keep an eye on these for me while I buy a few bolts of silk; in a moment we'll go on together." Qing thought, "This man must be rich—the horse sale is as good as done." The con man went into the silk shop and deliberately haggled over the price. When the silk dealer scolded him for not knowing what the goods were worth, he pretended to relent: "Let me take it to a friend who knows the goods, and I'll come right back with an offer. How about that?" The silk dealer said, "With goods this fine, feel free to show them around—just don't go far." The con man said, "I have a horse and a partner right here—what is there to worry about?" He took the silk in hand, walked out the door, and ran off. The silk dealer, seeing the horse and the partner still there, felt perfectly at ease. Qing waited until noon; the man never came back, and Qing concluded he must be a swindler. So he left the umbrella, mounted the silver-gray, and led his other horse back toward the inn. The silk dealer rushed up and grabbed him: "Your partner made off with my silk—where do you think you're going?" Qing said, "What partner of mine?" The silk dealer said, "The man who rode here with you just now. Why play dumb? I'm taking it out of you." Qing said, "I don't know what devil that man was. He only asked to buy my horse and told me to come to his house to collect the silver, so I came along. He said he was buying silk at your shop and would rejoin me in a moment. I waited a long time and he never came, so I rode my own horse back to the inn. How can you harass me over nothing?" The silk dealer said, "If he wasn't your partner, why did he have you watch his umbrella and horse? I only handed him the silk because I saw you and the horse standing here. How can you two work a setup together and cheat me out of my silk?" The two of them argued, neither giving in, and hauled each other to the Yingtian prefectural office to have the case judged. The silk dealer told the whole story plainly. Qing pleaded, "I am registered in Jiangxi and deal in horses for a living. I regularly sell at Sanshan Street and put up at Weng Chun's inn. When have I ever been a swindler? I happened to meet a man who wanted to buy my horse and insisted I come to his house for payment, so I went with him. Midway he dismounted, took silk from this man's shop, and ran off—I knew nothing about it. How can you say I was his partner?" The prefect said, "Say no more. Bring in the innkeeper and all will be clear." The innkeeper said, "Qing regularly deals in horses and lodges at my place. He is an honest, law-abiding man." The silk dealer said, "If he's so honest, why did he watch the umbrella and horse for that swindler? I heard it plainly, and he agreed to it." Qing said, "He asked me to watch the umbrella because he was buying my horse—how does that make me his partner?" The prefect asked, "When the man left, did he take the umbrella?" The silk dealer said, "He did not." The prefect said, "Then he truly was a swindler. He wanted to make off with your silk, so he pretended to buy a horse and used Chen Qing as a pledge. Using another man's horse to trick you out of your silk—that is the 'borrow a path to destroy Guo' trick, using one thing as a pretext to take another. You fell for the swindle yourself; how can you blame Qing?" He dismissed them both without further testimony.

吾觀作棍亦多術矣。言買馬，非買馬，實欲假馬作囮，為脫緞之術。故先以色服章身，令人信其為真豪富；既而佇立相馬，令人信其為真作家；迨入緞鋪，誑言有馬與夥，令人信其為真實言；至脫緞而走，以一傘貽慶，與緞客爭訟：此皆以巧術愚弄人也。若非府尹明察，断其為假道滅虢，則行人得牛，不幾邑人之災乎？雖然慶未至混跡於縲絏，緞客已被鬼迷於白晝矣。小人之計甚詭，君子之防宜密。庶棍術雖多，亦不能愚弄我也。

Note: Swindlers have many tricks. He spoke of buying a horse, but it was no horse purchase; what he really wanted was to use the horse as a decoy, a means of making off with silk. So first he dressed in showy clothes to make people believe he was truly rich; then he stood appraising the horse to make people believe he was a real connoisseur; then he entered the silk shop and lied about having a horse and a partner to make people believe his words were solid; and when he made off with the silk and ran, he left an umbrella with Qing so that Qing and the silk dealer would end up in court. All of it was clever trickery to make fools of people. Had the prefect not seen clearly and judged it a case of 'borrowing a path to destroy Guo,' then the passerby who picks up an ox would have become the townsman's calamity—the innocent bystander paying for another man's crime. Though Qing escaped being thrown into fetters, the silk dealer had already been bewitched in broad daylight. The petty man's schemes are crafty indeed; the gentleman's guard must be tight. Then, however many tricks swindlers have, they cannot make a fool of me.

先寄銀而後拐迯

First Depositing Silver, Then Absconding

通州有姓蘇名廣者，同一子販松江梭布，徃福建賣。布銀入手，囬至半途，遇一人姓紀名勝，自稱同府異縣，鄉語相同，亦在福建賣布而歸。勝乃雛家，途中認廣為親鄉里，見廣財本更多，乃以己銀貳拾餘兩寄藏於蘇廣箱内。一路小心代劳，渾如同伴。後至日乆，勝見利而生姦。一夜，佯稱瀉病，連起開門出去数次。不知廣乃老客也，見其開門徃返，疑彼有詐謀；且其來歷不明，「彼雖有銀貳拾餘兩寄我箱内，今夜似有歹意。」乗其出，即潛起來，将己銀與紀勝銀并實落衣物另藏别包袱，置在己身邊；仍以舊衣被包数片磚石放在原箱内，佯作熟睡。勝察廣父子都睡去，将廣銀箱夤夜挑走。廣在床聽勝動静，出門不歸，曰：「此果棍也，非我，險遭此脫迯矣。」次日，廣起，故驚訝勝竊他銀本，将店主扭打，說他通同将我銀偷去。其子弗知父之謀，尤怒歐不已。父密謂曰：「此事我已如此如此。」方止。早飯後，廣曰：「我徃縣告，若捕得那棍，你來作證；不然，定要問你取矣。」廣知勝反中己術，徑從小路潛歸。勝自幸竊得廣銀，茫茫然行至午，路将百里。開其箱，内乃磚石、舊衣也。頓足大恨，復囬原店。却被店主扭打一場，大罵曰：「這賊！你偷人銀，致我被累！」将绳繫頸，欲要送官。隻得吐出真情，叩頭懇免。時勝與廣已隔数日程途，追之不及，徒自悔恨而已。

In Tongzhou there was a man named Su Guang who, together with his son, dealt in Songjiang loom-woven cloth, taking it to Fujian to sell. With the silver from the cloth in hand, they were on their way back when they met a man named Ji Sheng, who said he was from the same prefecture but a different county—he spoke the same dialect—and was likewise returning from selling cloth in Fujian. Sheng was a green hand. On the road he claimed Guang as a fellow townsman, and seeing that Guang had the larger capital, he deposited twenty-odd taels of his own silver for safekeeping in Su Guang's chest. All along the way he was attentive and took on the chores, just like a traveling companion. But as the days wore on, Sheng saw the profit to be had and his mind turned crooked. One night he faked an upset stomach, getting up and opening the door to go out several times. He did not know that Guang was a seasoned traveler. Watching him open the door and go back and forth, Guang suspected a scheme; besides, the man's background was unclear. "True, he has twenty-odd taels deposited in my chest, but tonight he seems to be up to no good." Taking advantage of one of Sheng's trips out, Guang quietly got up, took his own silver together with Ji Sheng's silver and all the decent clothes and goods, hid them in another bundle, and kept it by his side. Then he wrapped several bricks and stones in the old clothes and bedding, put them back in the original chest, and pretended to be fast asleep. Sheng, seeing that Guang and his son were both asleep, shouldered Guang's silver chest and carried it off in the dead of night. In bed, Guang listened to Sheng's movements, and when Sheng went out and did not come back, he said, "So he really is a swindler. If it weren't for me, we'd nearly have been cleaned out." The next morning Guang got up and feigned shock that Sheng had stolen his capital; he seized the innkeeper and started beating him, accusing him of colluding to steal the silver. His son, not knowing his father's plan, was even more enraged and pummeled the man without letup. The father secretly told him, "I've already taken care of it, so-and-so." Only then did the son stop. After breakfast, Guang said, "I'm going to the county yamen to file a complaint. If they catch that swindler, you'll testify; otherwise I'll take it out of you." Guang knew that Sheng had fallen into his own trap, and he slipped straight home by a side road. Sheng, congratulating himself on stealing Guang's silver, trudged on in a daze until noon, nearly a hundred li down the road. He opened the chest: inside were bricks, stones, and old clothes. Stamping his feet in fury, he went back to the inn—where the innkeeper seized him and beat him, cursing, "You thief! You stole a man's silver and got me blamed for it!" He tied a rope around Sheng's neck and meant to haul him before the magistrate. Sheng had no choice but to spit out the whole truth and kowtow, begging to be spared. By then he and Guang were already several days' journey apart; there was no catching him, and Sheng could only stew in his own regret.

按：紀勝非雛客，乃雛棍也。先将己銀託寄於廣，令其不疑；後以詐瀉開門，候其熟睡，即連彼銀共竊而迯。彼之為計，亦甚巧矣。盖此乃「欲取姑與」，棍局中一甜術也。孰知廣乃老客，見出其上，察其動静，已照其肝胆。故因機乗機，将計就計，勝已入厥筭中而不自知矣。夫勝欲利人之有，反自喪其有，雛家光棍，又不如老年江湖也。待後囬店，被其扭打，捻頸哀告以求免，是自貽伊戚，又誰咎也？天理昭昭，此足為鑑。

Note: Ji Sheng was no novice merchant—he was a novice swindler. First he deposited his own silver with Guang so Guang would not suspect; then he faked sickness to open the door, waited until they were sound asleep, and stole their silver along with his own and fled. His scheme was clever enough: this is 'give a little to take a lot,' a sweet trick in the swindler's repertoire. Who would have known that Guang was an old hand who saw one move ahead? Watching Sheng's comings and goings, he had already read him to the core. So he seized the moment and turned the scheme back on itself; Sheng had walked into his reckoning without ever knowing it. Sheng wanted to profit from another man's goods and ended up losing his own. A green sharp is no match for an old hand on the road. When he later returned to the inn, was seized and beaten, and had a rope twisted around his neck while he begged to be spared—he brought that misery on himself; who else is to blame? Heaven's justice is clear; let this serve as a warning.

明騙販猪

Openly Swindling a Pig Peddler

福建建陽人鄧招寶者，常以挑販為生。一日，販小猪四隻，徃崇安、大安去賣。行至馬安嶺上，遇一棍問他買猪。寶意此山徑僻嶺，無人徃來，人家又遠，何此人在路上買猪？疑之，因問其何住。棍曰：「既前馬安坪也。」寶曰：「既要買，我同你家去。」棍曰：「我要徃縣。你拿出與我看，若合吾意，議定價方好囬家秤銀；不然，恐阻程途矣。」此棍言之近理，寶即然之，遂拿一猪與看。棍接過手，拿住猪尾放地上細看，乃故放手，致猪便走。佯作驚恐状曰：「差矣，差矣！」即忙趕捉——不知趕之正驱之也。寶見猪遠走，猛心奔前追捉，豈知已墮其術也。棍見寶趕猪，約離籠二三百步，即旋於籠内拿一猪在手，又踢倒二籠，猪俱迯出，大聲曰：「多謝你！慢慢尋！」寶欲趕棍，三猪出籠迯走，恐因此而失彼；况棍走遠難追，但呪罵一場。幸得三猪成聚，收拾入籠，抱恨而去。

Deng Zhaobao of Jianyang, Fujian, made his living as a porter-peddler. One day he was carrying four piglets to sell in Chong'an and Da'an. On Ma'an Ridge he met a con man who asked about buying pigs. Bao thought: this is a lonely mountain path, no one comes through, and the houses are far away—why would this man be buying pigs on the road? Suspicious, he asked where the man lived. The con man said, "Just ahead, at Ma'anping." Bao said, "If you mean to buy, I'll go home with you." The con man said, "I'm on my way to the county seat. Take one out for me to look at. If it suits me, we'll settle on a price and then I'll go home to weigh out the silver; otherwise I'm afraid it will delay my trip." The con man sounded reasonable, so Bao agreed and took out a pig to show him. The con man took it, held it by the tail, and set it on the ground to examine closely—then deliberately let go, and the pig bolted. Feigning alarm, he cried, "Oh no, oh no!" and rushed after it—though his chasing was really driving it farther away. Bao saw the pig running off and dashed forward with all his might to catch it, never realizing he had already fallen into the trap. When the con man saw Bao chasing the pig some two or three hundred paces from the cages, he turned back, grabbed a pig from a cage, kicked over the other two cages so the pigs all escaped, and shouted, "Much obliged! Take your time looking!" Bao wanted to chase the con man, but three pigs had bolted from the cages, and he feared losing them while chasing him; besides, the con man had gotten too far to catch. He could only curse him roundly. Luckily the three pigs bunched together; he gathered them back into the cages and went off nursing his grievance.

吾觀棍之脫猪也，一邂逅相逢之頃，賊念即生，乃以詭言相哄，致寶深信，所謂「君子可欺以其方」者也。乃始也放猪佯逐，以误其遠趕之於前；繼也擒猪踢籠，以制其不趕之於後：使人明墮其術而不自知。倉卒粧套，抑何譎也！商者鑑此！勿謂暗機隱械，宜為慎防；即明圈顯套，尤當加謹。

Note: Look how the swindler got away with the pig. In the space of a chance meeting, the thievish thought was born; he fooled Bao with lying words until Bao believed him completely—what is meant by 'a gentleman can be deceived by what seems plausible.' First he turned a pig loose and pretended to chase it, to draw Bao far off in pursuit; then he grabbed a pig and kicked over the cages, to keep Bao from coming after him: he made a man fall openly into the trap without ever knowing it. A setup thrown together on the spot—what cunning! Merchants, take warning from this. Do not think that only hidden snares and secret devices deserve careful guarding; even open traps and brazen setups demand still greater care.

遇里長反脫茶壺

Meeting the Village Head, He Walks Off with a Teapot Instead

趙通，延平府南平縣人也。家世積善，錢糧頗多，差當七啚一甲裡役。其甲首林錢一者，機智過人，不務生理，第飲賭宿娼，後來家業消條，無處栖身，隻得迯外。通亦不知其向徃。一日，通與僕徃杭貿易，經過浦城，憩息於亭，�僕徃杭貿易，經過浦城，憩息於亭，遆見錢一。通遂罵之曰：「這奴才！你迯外数年，户丁不納，糧差累賠，是何理也？今你見我，你何以說？」錢一被罵不甘，心生一計，向前賠笑曰：「我每欲囬，送條編與里長，奈我家中欠人財物甚多，難以抵償，故不敢囬矣。今幸遇里長，如天降下，敢再推辭？况這幾年賴里長福庇，開店西關码頭，家中稍裕。新娶邑人徐某之妾為妻，被人欺姦。我乃孤身一人，出外獨居，無奈伊何！今幸遇里長，則有主矣。里長徃杭州，亦經門處過，即到我家暫歇，自當筭還編銀；又煩代我作主。」通聽其言，私心喜曰：「今日得此，可作徃杭盤費，誠可謂出門招財也。」遂與同行。至一店所，錢曰：「里長今朝起早，又路行半日，肚又飢矣，上店沽酒濕口何如？」應曰：「可。」遂入店，呌店主煖酒、切豆腐，與通食。更問店主曰：「這裡有好紅酒、猪肉否？」店主曰：「市前游店肉酒俱有。」錢一曰：「可借壺、秤一用。」店主拿壺、秤出，錢接過手，直望游店轉轉抹角潛躲而去。通與僕吃酒，一壺将盡，乃對僕曰：「錢一去許乆不來，莫非與人爭闘？不然，此時當來矣。汝徃看之。」僕即徃酒肉店去問，說并無錢一。待欲尋他，又不知他去向，隻得秤銀還酒。店主收銀，索取壺、秤。通怒曰：「酒是我吃，我還你猶可；壺、秤是你自交錢一，何干我事？」店主曰：「人同你來，你在我店飲酒，故把壺、秤借他。不然，我曉得甚么錢一？」言來言去，兩下角口大鬧。眾人來勸，問其來歷，始知甲首騙里長入店，更脫店主壺、秤。眾大笑曰：「是他自錯，賠他也罷。」不得已代賠，嘔氣抱忿而去。

Zhao Tong was a man of Nanping County in Yanping Prefecture. His family had done good deeds for generations and held considerable tax-grain property, and by rotation he served as village head (lizhang) for the first tithing of the seventh ward. One of his tithing heads (jiashou), Lin Qianyi, was clever beyond most men but pursued no honest livelihood—he only drank, gambled, and visited prostitutes. In time his property dwindled away; with nowhere left to live, he had to flee the district, and Tong did not know where he had gone. One day Tong and his servant were traveling to Hangzhou on business. Passing through Pucheng, they stopped to rest at a pavilion, and there he caught sight of Qianyi. Tong lit into him: "You wretch! You've been on the run for years; the men of your household pay nothing, and I've had to cover the grain and service levies out of my own pocket. What do you call that? Now that you see me, what have you got to say for yourself?" Qianyi, stung by the scolding, hit on a plan. He stepped forward with an ingratiating smile: "I've always meant to come back and hand over the levy silver to you, village head, but my family owes people so much that I couldn't make it good, so I didn't dare return. Now meeting you here is like a gift from heaven—how could I put it off again? Besides, these past years, thanks to your blessing, I've opened a shop at the Xiguan wharf and my household is doing passably well. I recently married the concubine of a townsman named Xu, and some man has been forcing himself on her. I'm all alone out here, living by myself, and there's nothing I can do about it. Now that I've met you, village head, I have someone to stand up for me. On your way to Hangzhou you pass right by my door—stop and rest at my house a while, and of course I'll reckon up and repay the levy silver; and I'll trouble you to take my side." Hearing this, Tong rejoiced inwardly: "What I collect today can serve as travel money for Hangzhou. Truly, step out the door and wealth comes calling." So they went on together. At a tavern, Qianyi said, "Village head, you rose early this morning and have walked half the day; you must be hungry. How about we stop in and buy some wine to wet our mouths?" Tong answered, "Fine." They went in, and Qianyi called for the tavern keeper to warm wine and slice tofu for Tong to eat. Then he asked, "Do you have good red wine and pork here?" The tavern keeper said, "The You shop in front of the market has both meat and wine." Qianyi said, "May I borrow a pot and a scale?" The tavern keeper brought out a pot and scale; Qianyi took them, headed toward the You shop, turned corner after corner, and slipped away. Tong and his servant drank, and one pot was nearly gone when Tong said to the servant, "Qianyi's been gone a long time—could he have gotten into a fight? Otherwise he should be back by now. Go take a look." The servant went to the wine-and-meat shop to ask; they said there was no Qianyi there. He wanted to search but had no idea where the man had gone, so they could only weigh out silver to pay for the wine. The tavern keeper took the silver, then demanded the pot and scale. Tong said angrily, "I drank the wine, so paying you is fair enough; but you handed the pot and scale to Qianyi yourself—what has that to do with me?" The tavern keeper said, "The man came with you, and you were drinking in my shop—that's why I lent him the pot and scale. Otherwise, what do I know of any Qianyi?" Words flew back and forth, and the two fell into a shouting match. A crowd came to mediate and asked how it had started; only then did they learn that a tithing head had lured his village head into the shop and, into the bargain, walked off with the tavern keeper's pot and scale. Everyone laughed: "It was his own mistake—just pay the man and be done with it." Tong had no choice but to pay for them, and he left choking with resentment.

按：林錢一始說家頗充裕，妻被棍姦，欲投里長作主，致人不疑；繼也入店，借壺、秤沽酒肉，以叙間濶之情，使人不備：玩通於股掌之中，術亦巧矣。然錢一狡猾有素，通亦知之，乃一卒遇之，通遂信其言而入店飲酒；更欲沽紅買肉，皆非欵待之真情。在通當燭其偽而止之曰：「店中不便，有酒有肉，到家食之未晚也。」則錢一姦無所施。将道旁脫走不暇，何至賠壺、秤而受嘔氣也？故錢一狡也，而通亦欠檢點焉。嘻！

Note: Lin Qianyi began by saying his household was fairly comfortable, that his wife had been violated by some rogue, and that he wanted the village head to stand up for him—so that no one would suspect. Then he went into the shop and borrowed pot and scale to buy wine and meat, as if to renew an old friendship—so that no one was on guard. He played Tong like a toy in the palm of his hand; the trick was clever enough. Yet Qianyi's cunning was long-standing, and Tong knew it. Still, meeting him suddenly, Tong believed his words and went into the shop to drink; and the wish to buy red wine and meat was no true hospitality. Tong should have seen through the sham and stopped him: "The shop is inconvenient; if there's wine and meat, it won't be too late to eat it at your house." Then Qianyi's scheme would have had nowhere to go—he would have been too busy making his escape by the roadside. How would it ever have come to paying for a pot and scale and swallowing insults? So Qianyi was crafty—but Tong also failed to watch himself. Ah!

乗鬧明竊店中布

Openly Stealing a Shop's Cloth Amid the Bustle

吴勝理，徽州府休寧縣人，在蘇州府開鋪，收買各樣色布，揭行生意最大，四方買者極多，每日有幾十兩銀交易。外開鋪面，裡藏各貨。一日，有幾夥客人凑集買布，皆在内堂作帐對銀。一棍乗其叢雜，亦在鋪呌買布。勝理出於施礼，待茶畢，安頓外鋪少坐。勝理復入内，與前客對銀。其棍蓦其鋪無守者，故近門邊，詐拱揖相辭状，遂近鋪邊拿布一捆，拖在肩上，缓步行去。雖對鋪者，亦不覺其盗。後内堂诸商交易畢，勝理送客出外，忽不見鋪上布，問對門店人曰：「我鋪裡一捆布是何人拿去？」對門店人曰：「你�鋪裡一捆布是何人拿去？」對門店人曰：「你遆間後來那客人，與你拱手作辭，方拖布去，眾皆見之，你何佯失布？」勝理曰：「因内忙，故安他在外鋪坐，候前客事畢，然後與他作帐。何曾賣布與他？」鄰人訝曰：「狡哉，此棍！彼佯拱手相辭，令我辈不敢說他是賊，缓步而行，明白脫去矣！将奈何？」勝理隻得懊恨一場而罷。

Wu Shengli, a man of Xiuning County in Huizhou Prefecture, ran a shop in Suzhou Prefecture buying up colored cloth of every kind. His wholesale business was the biggest around, buyers came from all quarters, and every day tens of taels of silver changed hands. The shop front opened to the street, and the goods were stored inside. One day several parties of customers crowded in to buy cloth, all of them in the inner hall settling accounts and weighing out silver. A swindler, taking advantage of the crush, also called out in the shop to buy cloth. Shengli came out to greet him politely, served him tea, and settled him in the outer shop to sit a while. Then Shengli went back inside to weigh out silver with the earlier customers. The swindler, seeing the shop unwatched, moved toward the doorway and pretended to cup his hands in a gesture of farewell; then he went to the edge of the shop, picked up a bundle of cloth, slung it over his shoulder, and strolled slowly away. Even the shop across the street did not see it as theft. Later, when the merchants in the inner hall had finished their dealings, Shengli saw his customers out—and suddenly found the bundle of cloth gone from the shop. He asked the people in the shop opposite, "Who took a bundle of cloth from my shop?" The people opposite said, "That customer who came later just now cupped his hands to take leave of you, then hauled the cloth off. We all saw it—why pretend you've lost cloth?" Shengli said, "Because I was busy inside, I seated him in the outer shop to wait until the earlier customers were done, and then I would settle accounts with him. When did I ever sell him cloth?" The neighbors marveled, "What a cunning swindler! He pretended to cup his hands in farewell so none of us dared call him a thief, and he strolled off and walked clean away with it in broad daylight. What can be done?" Shengli could only nurse his chagrin and let it go.

按：棍之竊斯布也，初須乗其叢雜，入其店中，尚未定其騙局之所出也。至勝理待其茶而安之外鋪少坐，左顧右盼而姦謀遂决矣。故拱揖而辭，而明拖其布，如荆州之暗袭，不甚費力，真可謂高手矣。在勝理，店積貨物，宜不離看守，方可保無虞。關防不密，安知無棍徒混入行姦乎？待布既失而後扼腕，何益哉？大凡坐鋪者，當知此而謹慎之可也。

Note: When the swindler stole this cloth, he first had to take advantage of the crowd to get into the shop; he had not yet settled on how the swindle would play out. Only when Shengli served him tea and sat him down in the outer shop did he glance left and right—and then the crooked plan was fixed. So he cupped his hands in farewell and openly dragged off the cloth: like the sneak attack that took Jingzhou, it cost him hardly any effort. A real master of the game. As for Shengli, with goods piled up in the shop, he should never have left them unwatched; only so could he be sure of their safety. If the guard is not tight, how can you know no swindler will slip in and work his mischief? To wring your hands after the cloth is already lost—what good is that? All who keep shop should take this to heart and be careful.

詐稱偷鹅脫青布

Pretending to Steal a Goose to Make Off with Blue Cloth

有一大鋪，布匹極多，交易叢雜，隻自己一人看店。其店之對門人，養一圈鹅，鸣聲嘈雜。開鋪者惡其聒耳，尝曰：「此惡物何無盗之者？與我耳頭得沉静些。」忽棍聞之。一日，乗其店中閑寂，遂入店拱手，以手按柜頭一捆青布，輕輕言曰：「不敢相瞒，我實是一小偷，愛得對門店下一隻鹅吃，隻大街面難下手。我有一小術，祇要一个人贊成。」店主曰：「如何贊成？」小偷曰：「我在這邊問曰：『可拿去否？』汝在内高聲應曰：『可。』又再問曰：『我真拿去？』汝再應曰：『說定了，任從拿去。』我便去拿，方掩得路人耳目。託你贊成，後日你家不須閉門，亦無賊入矣。但你須在内去，莫得竊視，視則法不灵。你直聽鹅聲息，我事方畢，你可出來。」店主然之。小偷高聲問曰：「我拿去否？」内高聲應曰：「憑你拿去。」又再高聲問曰：「我真拿去？」内又高聲應曰：「說定了，任你拿去。」兩旁店人皆聞其問答之語，小偷遂負其柜上一捆青布而去——人以為借去也。其店主在内，聽得鹅聲，不敢出來。其盗布者匆匆行之乆矣。待之多時，鹅聲不绝。其店主恐店内乆無人守，隻得外出。看鹅尚在，自己柜頭反失一捆青布。顧問兩旁店曰：「遆才誰上我店，拿我一捆布去？」左右店皆答曰：「是那个問你買的，你再三應聲，呌他祇管拿去。今拿去已乆矣。」店主抚心自悔曰：「我明被此人騙了！祇是自己皆死，說不得也。」事乆，眾鄰覺之，始笑此人之痴，而深服此棍賊之高手矣。

There was a big shop with bolts of cloth in great number and a constant press of business, and only the owner himself to mind the store. The people across the street kept a pen of geese whose honking was a constant racket. The shopkeeper hated the din and once said, "Why has no one stolen those wretched creatures? Then my ears could have some peace." A swindler happened to hear this. One day, finding the shop idle and quiet, he went in, cupped his hands, laid a hand on a bundle of blue cloth on the counter, and said softly, "I won't hide it from you—I'm really a petty thief. I'd love to make a meal of one of the geese at the shop across the way, but it's a busy street and hard to make a move. I have a little trick; I just need one person to play along." The shopkeeper said, "Play along how?" The thief said, "I'll stand here and ask, 'May I take it?' You answer loudly from inside, 'Yes.' I'll ask again, 'I'm really taking it?' You answer again, 'It's settled—take it as you please.' Then I'll go take it, and that will cover the eyes and ears of people in the street. Do me this favor, and afterward your house won't need to shut its door—no thief will ever enter. But you must stay inside; don't peek—if you look, the method won't work. Listen until the goose sounds stop; when my business is done, you can come out." The shopkeeper agreed. The thief asked loudly, "May I take it?" From inside came the loud answer, "Take it as you please." He asked again, louder, "I'm really taking it?" From inside again, "It's settled—take it as you please." The shops on both sides all heard this exchange. The thief then shouldered the bundle of blue cloth from the counter and walked off—people assumed it was a loan. Inside, the shopkeeper heard the geese still honking and dared not come out. The cloth thief had long since hurried away. He waited a good while; the honking never stopped. Fearing the shop had been left unwatched too long, he had to come out. He looked: the geese were still there—and a bundle of blue cloth was gone from his own counter. He turned to the neighboring shops: "Who was it just now came into my shop and took a bundle of my cloth?" The shops on either side all answered, "It was the one who asked to buy from you. You answered him again and again, telling him to go ahead and take it. He's been gone a long time." The shopkeeper beat his breast in remorse: "That man swindled me in broad daylight! And it's all my own doing—I can't even tell anyone." In time the neighbors figured it out; they laughed at the man's folly and deeply admired the swindler-thief's masterly skill.

按：君子仁民愛物，而仁之先施者，莫如鄰；物之愛者，即鹅亦居其一。何對鄰人養鹅，惡在嘈雜之聲，必欲盗之者以殺之，愛物之謂何哉？利失對鄰之鹅，而贊成棍賊以盗之，仁心安在？是以致使棍聞其言，乗機而行竊，反贊成其偷，亦是鼠辈也。欲去人之鹅，而反自失其布，是自貽祸也，将誰怨哉？若能仁以處鄰，而量足以容物，何至有此失也！

Note: A gentleman is kind to people and cares for living things. Neighbors come first in kindness, and even geese count among the things to care for. What kind of care for living things is it, when a neighbor keeps geese, to hate their noise and wish a thief would make off with them and kill them? He counted the loss of his neighbor's geese a gain, and even helped the swindler-thief steal them—where was his humane heart? So when the swindler heard his words, seized the chance, and carried out his theft—and the man even abetted the stealing—he was a rat of the same nest. He wanted to be rid of another man's geese and lost his own cloth instead; he brought the misfortune on himself—whom can he blame? Had he treated his neighbor kindly and had the breadth of mind to tolerate things, he would never have suffered this loss.

借他人屋脫客布

Borrowing Another Man's House to Make Off with a Customer's Cloth

聂道應，别号西湖，邵武六都人。家原富厚，住屋宏深。後因訟耗家，以裁缝為業。忽一日徃人家裁衣。有一光棍見客人賣布，知應出外，故领到應家前栋坐定，竟入内堂，私問應妻云：「汝丈夫在家否？」其妻曰：「徃前村裁衣。」棍曰：「我要造数件衣服，今日歸否？」對曰：「要明日歸。」棍曰：「我有同伴在你前栋坐，口渴，求茶一杯吃。」應妻即討茶二杯，放於斫凳上。棍将茶捧與布客飲。飲罷，接杯入，方出拣布四匹，還銀壹兩，隻銀不成色。客曰：「此價要換好銀。」棍曰：「我儿子為人裁衣，待明日歸換與你。」言未畢，棍预套一人來問：「針工在家否？」棍應曰：「要明日歸。」其人即去。布客曰：「你收起布，明日換之與我。」客既出，少頃棍亦拖布迯去。次早，布客到應家問曰：「針工歸否？」應妻曰：「午後囬。」布客次早又問：「針工歸否？」應妻又曰：「今午囬。」布客午後又來問，應妻曰：「未歸。」布客怒曰：「你公公前日拿布四匹，說要針工歸來還銀，何再三推託？你公公何去？」應妻道：「這客人好胡說！我家哪有公公？誰人拿你布？」二人角口大鬧。鄰人辯曰：「他何曾有公公？况其丈夫又不在家，你布不知何人拿去，安可妄取？」布客無奈，状投署印同知钟爷。状准，即拘四鄰來审。眾云：「應不在家，况父已死。其布不知甚人脫去。」钟爷曰：「布在他家脫去，那日何人到他家下？着鄰約為之穷究，必有着落矣。」鄰約不能究，乃勸西湖曰：「令正不合被棍脫茶，致误客人以布付棍，當認一半；布客不合輕易以布付人，亦當自認一半。」二家諾然，依此囬報。钟爷以鄰約處得明白，俱各免供。

Nie Daoying, also known by the name Xihu, was a man of Liudu in Shaowu. His family had once been wealthy, with a large, deep-set house. Later, lawsuits ate up the family fortune, and he made his living as a tailor. One day he went out to a customer's house to cut clothes. A smooth operator saw a cloth merchant selling cloth and, knowing Ying was out, led the man to Ying's house and seated him in the front hall. Then he went straight into the inner rooms and privately asked Ying's wife, "Is your husband home?" His wife said, "He's gone to the next village to cut clothes." The crook said, "I need several garments made. Will he be back today?" She answered, "Not until tomorrow." The crook said, "I have a companion sitting in your front hall who's thirsty. May I beg a cup of tea?" Ying's wife fetched two cups of tea and set them on the cutting bench. The crook carried the tea out to the cloth merchant. When the man had drunk, the crook took the cups back in, then came out, picked four bolts of cloth, and paid one tael of silver—but the silver was not pure. The merchant said, "At this price I need pure silver." The crook said, "My son is out cutting clothes for people; when he comes back tomorrow, I'll exchange it for you." Before he had finished speaking, a man the crook had planted in advance came and asked, "Is the tailor home?" The crook answered, "Not until tomorrow." The man left. The cloth merchant said, "Put the cloth away, then, and exchange it for me tomorrow." Once the merchant had gone, the crook a moment later hauled off the cloth and fled. The next morning the cloth merchant came to Ying's house and asked, "Is the tailor back?" Ying's wife said, "After noon." The following morning he asked again, "Is the tailor back?" Ying's wife said again, "Today at noon." In the afternoon the merchant came once more; Ying's wife said, "Not back yet." The merchant flared up: "The day before yesterday your father-in-law took four bolts of cloth, saying he'd pay the silver when the tailor came home. Why do you keep putting me off? Where has your father-in-law gone?" Ying's wife said, "What nonsense! What father-in-law do we have in this house? Who took your cloth?" The two fell into a shouting match. The neighbors reasoned with him: "When did this family ever have a father-in-law? Besides, her husband isn't home. We don't know who took your cloth—how can you demand it from them?" With no other recourse, the merchant filed a complaint with the vice prefect, Master Zhong. The complaint was accepted, and the neighbors on all four sides were summoned for questioning. They all said, "Ying was not home, and his father is long dead. We don't know who made off with the cloth." Master Zhong said, "The cloth was taken at his house. Who came to the house that day? Have the neighborhood elders investigate it thoroughly; there must be some trace." The neighborhood elders could find nothing, so they urged Xihu: "Your good wife should not have let the swindler wangle tea out of her, which misled the merchant into handing his cloth to him—you should bear half the loss. The merchant should not have handed his cloth to a stranger so lightly—he should bear half himself." Both households agreed and reported back accordingly. Master Zhong, finding the elders' settlement fair and clear, dismissed both parties without further testimony.

按：布入人家賣，又飲人家之茶，則買主似有着落矣，誰不肯以布與之？詎料此棍借其屋，賺其茶，以為脫布之媒；又還其銀，止爭銀色而許換，誰知防之？今後交易，惟兩相交付。彼雖許換銀，布隻抱去，明日重來，則無受脫之事矣。

Note: When cloth is sold right into a man's house, and you drink the house's tea besides, the buyer seems to have a fixed address—who wouldn't hand over the cloth? Who would have thought this swindler borrowed the house and cadged the tea as a means to make off with the cloth—and even paid silver, quibbling only over its purity and promising to exchange it? Who would know to be on guard? From now on, in any deal, let both sides hand over on the spot. Even if he promises to exchange the silver, just carry the cloth back with you and return tomorrow—then you'll never be cheated out of your goods.

詐匠修換錢桌厨

Posing as a Carpenter to Make Off with a Money-Changer's Table

建寧府凡換錢者，皆以一椅一桌厨列於街上，置錢於桌，以待人換。午則歸家食飯，晚則收起錢，以桌厨寄附近人家。明日復然。有一人桌厨内約積有錢五六千，其桌破坏一角。旁有一棍，看此破桌厨内多錢，心生一計。待此人起身食午，即粧做一木匠，以手巾缚腰，插一利斧於旁，手拿六尺，将此桌厨横量直量一次，高聲自說自應曰：「這樣破东西，當做一新的來換，反呌我修补，怎么修得？真是吝啬的人！」自說了一場。一手拿六尺，将桌厨錢輕輕侧倾作一邊，将桌厨負在無人處，以斧砍開，取錢而迯。時旁人都道是換錢的呌木匠拿去修，那料大眾人群中，有棍敢脫此也。乃午後，換錢者到，問旁人曰：「我桌厨哪裡去？」眾合答曰：「你呌木匠拿去修，匠還說你吝啬，何不再做新的，乃修此破物？彼已負去修矣。」換錢者曰：「我并未呌匠來，此是光棍脫去。」急沿途而访問，見空僻處桌厨剖破，錢無一文，怅恨而歸。

In Jianning Prefecture, the money-changers all set out a chair and a table-cabinet in the street, placed their coins on the table, and waited for people to come exchange. At noon they went home to eat; in the evening they gathered up the coins and left the table-cabinet with a household nearby. The next day, the same again. One changer had five or six thousand cash stored up in his table-cabinet, and the table had a broken corner. A swindler nearby, seeing how much money sat in that broken table-cabinet, hit on a plan. When the man got up to go eat lunch, the swindler dressed himself up as a carpenter—a towel tied around his waist, a sharp axe stuck at his side, a six-foot rule in his hand. He measured the table-cabinet lengthwise and crosswise, then said loudly, answering himself, "A thing this broken—they ought to have a new one made, and instead they tell me to patch it. How can this be patched? What a stingy man!" On and on he talked to himself. Then, rule in one hand, he gently tipped the coins in the cabinet over to one side, hoisted the table-cabinet onto his back, carried it to a deserted spot, chopped it open with the axe, took the coins, and ran. The bystanders all assumed the money-changer had called a carpenter to take it away for repair; who would have guessed that in the middle of a crowd a swindler would dare walk off with it? That afternoon the money-changer returned and asked the bystanders, "Where has my table-cabinet gone?" They all answered, "You had a carpenter take it for repair. The carpenter even said you were stingy—why not have a new one made instead of fixing that broken thing? He's already carried it off to fix." The money-changer said, "I never called any carpenter. A sharp has walked off with it." He hurried along the road asking after it, and found the table-cabinet split open in a deserted spot, not a single cash left. He went home sick with regret.

按：此棍粧匠而來，大舉大動，大志大言，人哪知他是脫？祇匠人修舊物，須在作場内，何須帶斧帶六尺而來？粧為匠便非匠矣。但他人物件，他人為修，何人替他盤詰？此棍所以得行其詐也。然因此以推其餘，凡來歷不明而粧情甚肖者，倍宜加察也。

Note: This swindler came dressed as a carpenter, all big gestures and big talk—who would have known he was there to walk off with the goods? But a carpenter mending an old piece does the work in his own shop; why would he show up with an axe and a six-foot rule? Dressing up as a carpenter was itself the proof he was no carpenter. Still, it was another man's property and another man's repair job—who was going to cross-examine on his behalf? That is how the swindler got away with his fraud. And from this we can draw the general lesson: whenever a person's background is unclear and the act is all too convincing, watch him twice as carefully.

第二類 丟包騙

Category 2 · The Dropped-Parcel Swindle

丟包於路行脫換

Dropping a Parcel on the Road to Pull a Swap

江賢，江西臨川縣人。錢本稀少，每年至七月割早穀之後，往福建崇安地方以绱鞋為生。積至年冬，約有銀一拾餘兩，收拾回家。

Jiang Xian was from Linchuan County in Jiangxi. With little capital to his name, every year after the early rice was cut in the seventh month he would go to Chong'an in Fujian and make a living stitching shoes. By winter he had saved up ten-odd taels of silver, so he packed up and headed home.

中途偶見一包，賢撿入手，約有銀二三兩，不勝喜悅。從前一人曰：「見者有分，不許獨得。可藏在你箱中，待僻靜處拿出來分。你撿者得二分，我見者得一分。」賢意亦肯，況銀納置彼箱，心中坦然無疑。行未數十步，忽一人忙趕到來，啼哭哀告曰：「我失銀三兩作一包，是措借納官的。你客官若拾得者，願體天心還我，陰功萬代。」前見者故作憐憫之容，曰：「是此绱鞋財主拾得，要與我均分。既是你貧苦人的，我情願不分，你可出些收贖與他，叫他把還你。」賢被此人證出，只得開箱，叫失銀者將原銀包自己取去。但得其一錢收贖，亦自以為幸；不知自銀已被棍將偽包換去矣。至晚到烏石地方，取出收贖銀還酒，將剩者欲併入大包。打開只見銅鐵，其銀一毫也無，只得大哭而罷。

Partway home he happened to spot a parcel on the road. Xian picked it up: inside was some two or three taels of silver. He was overjoyed. A man up ahead turned and said, "Whoever sees a find shares in it — no keeping it all to yourself. Hide it in your chest, and when we reach a quiet spot we'll take it out and split it. You picked it up, so you get two shares; I saw it, so I get one." Xian was willing; besides, the silver would be sitting in his own chest, so he felt perfectly at ease and suspected nothing. They had gone barely a few dozen paces when a man came hurrying after them, weeping and pleading: "I lost a parcel with three taels of silver — money I scraped together and borrowed to pay the authorities. If you, good traveler, have found it, please honor Heaven's compassion and give it back to me; the merit will bless you for ten thousand generations." The first man put on a look of pity and said, "It was this well-to-do cobbler here who picked it up, and he meant to split it with me. But since it belongs to a poor man, I am willing to give up my share. Offer him a little money to redeem it, and have him hand it back to you." Xian had been pointed out by the man, so he had no choice but to open the chest and let the one who had lost the silver take out the original parcel himself. He came away with a mere one qian for his trouble and still counted himself lucky — never guessing that the crook had already swapped his own silver for a fake parcel. That evening he reached Wushi. He took out the reward silver to pay for wine and meant to add what was left to the big parcel. When he opened it, there was nothing but copper and iron inside — not a speck of silver. All he could do was weep bitterly and leave it at that.

按：賢所賺銀，必早被棍覷見，故先偽設銀包套合。一棍在賢之先於荒僻處，俟賢來，投銀包於地。彼必撿之，乃出而欲與之分，令藏彼箱，則與彼銀共一處矣。其後棍妝情哀取，賢自應開箱還之，何自開箱使棍手親取其原包？則棍得以偽包換賢之銀，賢豈知防其脫換哉？故撿銀之時，即以其撿者與前棍均分，勿入箱中，則彼窮於計矣。然二棍亦必於僻處再搶之矣。故客路不在虛得人之有，而在密藏己之有也，斯無所失矣。

Note: The silver Xian had earned must have been spotted by the swindlers early on, so they rigged up a fake parcel in advance to match it. One of them went ahead of Xian to a deserted spot, waited for him to come, and tossed the parcel on the ground. Xian was sure to pick it up; the man then stepped out and pressed to share it, and had him hide it in his chest — and just like that, it was sitting in the same place as Xian's own silver. When the second swindler played the grieving loser and begged it back, Xian of course had to open the chest and return it — but why did he let the swindler's own hand reach in and take the original parcel? That is what let the crook trade a fake parcel for Xian's silver; how was Xian to know he should guard against the switch? The right move, the moment he picked up the silver, was to split the find with the first swindler on the spot and never let it into his chest — then they would have been out of tricks. Though even then, the two of them would likely have robbed him outright in some lonely place. So for a man traveling the roads, what matters is not picking up windfalls from other people's losses, but keeping what you have well hidden. Do that, and you will lose nothing.

第三類 換銀騙

Category 3 · Silver-Exchange Swindles

成錠假銀換真銀

Swapping a Fake Ingot for Real Silver

泉州府客人孫滔，為人誠實，有長者風。帶銀百餘兩，往南京買布。在沿山搭船，陡遇一棍名汪蘭，詐稱興化府人，鄉語略同，因與孫同船數日，甚歡。習知滔樸實人，的可騙也。因言他故，往蕪湖起岸買貨。舟中說他尚未傾銀，有銀一錠，細絲，十二兩重，若有便銀，打換為妙，意在就孫換之。孫因請看，汪欣然取出真銀。孫接過手，曰：「果是金花細絲。」汪欲顯真銀，因轉在孫手接出，遍與舟中客人看，問：「好否？」都道：「是細絲。」遂因舟上有筆硯在，汪微微冷笑，將此銀寫「十二兩足在風窠底」。孫心中道：「此人輕薄，有銀何必如此翻弄？」因潛對汪曰：「出來人，謹慎些。」汪曰：「無妨。」孫因問：「要換折多少？」汪曰：「弟只零買雜貨，憑兄銀色估折便是。」孫因取出小八九錢重的，只九一二成色。汪看喜曰：「此銀九四五傾來麼？俱一樣如此即好矣。」蓋汪重估孫銀水，使孫樂換。孫取天平兩對，估折明白。汪即箱中取出白綿紙，與孫麵包作兩包。汪因佯起，轉身一回，故意誤收原銀入袖，曰：「此包是我的了。」孫曰：「不是，這包是你的。」汪即替出那假，亦綿紙包，與真銀一樣，交與孫收。孫接過手，亦微開包緊，見銀字無異，概不深省，即鎖封笥篋中。汪須臾起岸分別。孫一向到南京，取出前銀，乃是錫，懊恨無及，始知被他替包騙去矣。

A merchant from Quanzhou Prefecture named Sun Tao was an honest man with the bearing of an elder. Carrying more than a hundred taels of silver, he was traveling to Nanjing to buy cloth. At Yanshan he boarded a boat, where he chanced to meet a swindler named Wang Lan. Wang falsely claimed to be from Xinghua Prefecture, and since their local speech was much alike, he shared the boat with Sun for several days, and they got along well. He came to see that Sun was a simple, honest man who could certainly be fooled. Making some excuse, he said he would go ashore at Wuhu to buy goods. On the boat he mentioned that he had not yet had his silver melted down: he had one ingot of fine-thread silver, twelve taels in weight, and if anyone had loose silver at hand, a swap would suit him best. His real aim was to swap with Sun. Sun asked to see it, and Wang readily took out a real silver ingot. Sun took it in hand and said, "It really is golden-flower fine-thread." Wanting to show off that the silver was genuine, Wang took it back from Sun's hand and passed it around to everyone on the boat, asking, "Is it good?" They all said, "It's fine-thread." Since there happened to be a brush and inkstone on board, Wang gave a faint cold smile and wrote on the ingot: "Twelve taels, full weight, at the bottom of the wind-nest." Sun thought to himself, "What a flighty man—if you have silver, why fiddle with it like that?" So he quietly said to Wang, "A man out traveling should be more careful." Wang said, "No harm done." Sun then asked, "What discount do you want on the swap?" Wang said, "I'm only buying sundry goods in small lots. Whatever fineness you judge it, brother, set the rate as you see fit." Sun took out small pieces of eight or nine qian each, only about ninety-one or ninety-two percent pure. Wang looked at them and said happily, "Was this silver cast at ninety-four or ninety-five? If it's all like this, that will do nicely." Wang was deliberately overestimating the fineness of Sun's silver to make him eager to swap. Sun fetched a balance, they weighed the two lots against each other, and the discount was settled clearly. Wang then took white cotton paper from his trunk, and together with Sun wrapped the silver into two parcels. Pretending to get up, he turned away for a moment and deliberately "mistook" the parcels, slipping the original silver into his sleeve and saying, "This parcel is mine." Sun said, "No—this parcel is yours." Wang then switched in the fake, also wrapped in cotton paper and looking just like the real silver parcel, and handed it to Sun to keep. Sun took it, and though he slightly opened the tight wrapping and saw the writing on the silver looked the same, he did not examine it closely and locked it away in his trunk. Soon afterward Wang went ashore and they parted. Sun traveled on to Nanjing, and when he took out the silver from before, it was tin. He was sick with regret, but too late—only then did he realize the man had switched parcels and cheated him.

按：孫滔，樸實人也。其看銀時，但稱彼輕薄，不知此人輕薄處，正要如此，人方不疑，後方好用假。不然，待打換之後，或有人從旁取視，豈不敗機？故坐舟冷笑，為書銀模樣，無非為眩視計耳，向後誰復細認哉？說者曰：「假令包銀時，孫即取真銀入手，後令汪取銀，則汪不窮乎？」曰：「雖然，彼棍者變計百端，即令真銀入手，彼又別有脫法。」但各守本分，各用己財，勿貪小可便宜，則不落圈套矣。

Note: Sun Tao was a simple, honest man. When he examined the silver, he only thought the other man flighty; he did not see that the flightiness was exactly the point. It kept people from suspecting anything, so the fake could be used later. Otherwise, once the swap was made, if someone nearby had picked up the silver for a look, wouldn't the scheme have collapsed? So the cold smile on the boat and the show of writing on the ingot were nothing but a trick to dazzle the eyes—after that, who would examine it closely again? Someone might ask: "Suppose that when the silver was being wrapped, Sun had taken the real silver into his own hand first and only then let Wang take his—wouldn't Wang have been stuck?" The answer: even so, a swindler like him has a hundred tricks; even with the real silver in Sun's hand, he would have found some other way to make off with it. Just keep to your own station, spend your own money, and never covet a small advantage—then you will not fall into the snare.

道士船中換轉金

A Daoist Swaps the Gold on Board

賁監生在南監，期滿將歸，欲換好金數十兩，歸遺妻妾，以將遠敬。同鄉鄧監生阻之曰：「京城換金者，屢被棍以銅脫去。金非急用，何必在此換為？」賁曰：「京城方有好金。若有棍能脫我者，亦服他好手段。」數日內換金十餘兩，皆照金色交易，都是好金。

Ben, a student of the Imperial Academy, was at the Southern Academy, and with his term finished he was about to head home. He wanted to exchange for several dozen taels of good gold to bring back to his wife and concubines, a token of regard after his long time away. His fellow townsman Deng, also an Academy student, tried to stop him: "People exchanging gold in the capital are forever being cheated by swindlers passing off copper. You don't need the gold urgently—why exchange it here?" Ben said, "Only the capital has good gold. If any swindler can get the better of me, I'll grant he has real skill." Within a few days he had exchanged for more than ten taels of gold, each deal checked against the gold's fineness, and all of it good gold.

後有一後生，以金錠十二兩來換。賁生取看，幾有足色金。問其換數，後生曰：「某鄉官命換的，要作五換。」賁遞與鄧看，曰：「此金可有六換，若五換，價公道矣。」鄧看曰：「果好。可將此金對明收起，勿過他手，然後對銀六十兩還之。」賁依言，先收入此金，然後還其銀。後生不得展轉，只得領銀歸，見其父，云：「兩監生如此關妨，不能再脫出。」父頓足曰：「一家生意在此，把本子送去了，何以為生？速去訪此監生何時歸？」回報：「已討定船，某日刻期登舟矣。」體探已的。

Later a young man came with a twelve-tael gold ingot to exchange. Ben took it and looked: it was nearly pure gold. He asked the rate, and the young man said, "A country gentleman ordered it exchanged; it has to go at five to one." Ben passed it to Deng: "This gold could fetch six to one; at five, the price is fair." Deng looked and said, "It really is good. Check this gold and put it away first—don't let it pass through his hands again—then weigh out sixty taels of silver to pay him." Ben followed the advice: he took in the gold first, then paid over the silver. The young man had no chance to work a switch and had to take the silver home. He told his father, "The two Academy students were so on their guard that I couldn't pull it off." The father stamped his foot: "Our whole family's living was in that ingot, and you've handed them our capital! How are we supposed to live? Quick—find out when this Academy student is heading home." The report came back: "They've already booked a boat; they board on a set day at a fixed hour." The information checked out.

至期，兩監生到船坐定。老棍裝為一道士，衣冠淨潔，亦來搭船，舵工收之在船中。共談處，道士言詞雍容，或談及京中官民事體，一一練熟。兩監生及同船諸人，亦樂與談。兩日後，將近晚間，道士故提及辨珠玉寶貝之法，諸人閒談一番。又說到辨金上去，道他更辨得真。賁監生因自誇彼在京換一錠足色金，換數又便宜。諸人中有求看估色數者，賁生誇耀，取出與諸人遞觀，皆誇羨好金。遍觀已訖，時天色漸晚，复付還賁生。將收入箱際，道士亦曰：「願藉觀。」接過一看，曰：「果好真金！」隨手即付還訖，又道及別新話上去。賁監生收入金。晚飯已熟，各散而餐。飲日，道士以船錢還舵工，與諸人別而登岸去。

On the appointed day the two Academy students boarded the boat and settled in. The old con man had disguised himself as a Daoist priest, robe and cap neat and clean, and came to take the same boat; the helmsman let him aboard. In conversation the Daoist spoke with an easy grace, and when talk turned to the doings of officials and commoners in the capital, he knew it all inside out. The two Academy students and the other passengers enjoyed talking with him. Two days later, toward evening, the Daoist deliberately brought up methods for judging pearls, jade, and treasures, and everyone chatted idly for a while. Then the talk came around to judging gold, and he said he was an even better judge of that. Ben then boasted that in the capital he had exchanged for an ingot of pure gold, and at a bargain rate besides. Some of the passengers asked to see it and estimate its fineness. Eager to show off, Ben took it out and passed it around, and everyone admired the fine gold. When all had looked their fill, the sky was growing dark and it was handed back to Ben. Just as he was about to put it into his trunk, the Daoist said, "May I have a look too?" He took it, glanced at it, and said, "It really is fine, true gold!" Then he handed it straight back and moved on to some fresh topic. Ben put the gold away. Dinner was ready, and they all scattered to eat. The next day the Daoist paid his fare to the helmsman, took leave of the company, and went ashore.

及賁監生歸，以金分贈妻妾。數日後，叫匠人來打釧鈿。先以小錠金打，匠皆稱金好。賁誇曰：「更換有一錠十二兩的尤好。」匠曰：「大錠金京中光棍多以銅脫人。」賁曰：「取與你看，有何棍能脫我乎？」匠接過手，笑曰：「正是銅也。」賁怪之，急取回看曰：「果銅也。我與鄧相公看定是上好金，又同船諸人看皆是好金，何都被瞞過？」忽猛省曰：「噯，是也！最後是一道士看，付還時天色近晚，我未及再檢視，即收藏箱中。是此時換去也！此道士何得一銅如此相似，又早已在手，如此換得容易？想京中換金後生，即老棍之子，彼換時未能脫，故來搭船脫歸也。」

When Ben got home, he divided the gold as gifts among his wife and concubines. A few days later he called in a goldsmith to make bracelets and hair ornaments. First he had the small ingots worked, and the goldsmith praised the gold. Ben boasted, "I also exchanged for a twelve-tael ingot that is even better." The goldsmith said, "With big ingots in the capital, smooth operators often cheat people with copper." Ben said, "I'll fetch it for you to see—what swindler could cheat me?" The goldsmith took it in hand and laughed: "This is copper, all right." Ben was stunned; he snatched it back and looked: "It really is copper. Deng and I examined it and judged it first-rate gold, and everyone on the boat who saw it called it good gold—how were we all deceived?" Then it hit him: "Ah, that's it! The last one to look at it was that Daoist. When he handed it back, the sky was nearly dark; I never checked it again before locking it in the trunk. That is when it was switched! How did that Daoist come to have a piece of copper so alike, already in his hand, to make the switch so easily? The young man who exchanged the gold in the capital must have been the old crook's son. When the son couldn't pull the switch at the exchange, the father came aboard to make the switch on the way home."

按：老棍之脫賁生金也，人謂其棍真高手矣。吾曰不然。設若賁生韜藏不露，則老棍雖有諸葛神機，莊周妙智，安能得其金而窺之？何以脫為？故責在賁生矜誇炫耀，是自招其脫也。噫！

Note: As for the old con man making off with Ben's gold, people call this swindler a real master. I say otherwise. If Ben had kept it hidden and never shown it, then even with the divine cunning of a Zhuge Liang and the subtle wit of a Zhuangzi, how could the old crook have gotten the gold into his hands to look at it? What switch could he have pulled? So the blame lies with Ben's own boasting and showing off—he invited the swindle himself. Ah.

第四類 詐哄騙

Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles

诈学道书报好梦

Forging Letters from the Education Commissioner to Report Lucky Dreams

庚子年，福建乡科，上府所中诸士，多系沈宗师取在首列者。人皆服沈宗师为得人。

In the gengzi year, at the Fujian provincial examination, most of the successful candidates from the upper prefectures were men whom Education Commissioner Shen had placed at the top of his list. Everyone praised Commissioner Shen for knowing talent when he saw it.

十二月初间，诸举人都上京矣。省城一棍，与本府一善书秀才谋，各诈为沈道一书，用小印图书，护封完密，分递于新春元家。每到一家，则云：“沈爷有书，专差小人来，口嘱付说，你家相公明年必有大捷。他得异梦，特令先来报知，但须谨密勿泄。更某某相公家，与尊府相近，恐他知有专使来，谓老爷厚此薄彼，故亦附有问安书在；特搭带耳，非专为彼来也。”及到他家，所言亦复如是，谓专为此来，餘者都搭带也。及开书看，则字画精楷，书词玄妙，皆称彼得祥梦，其兆应在某，当得大魁。或借其名，或因其地取义，各做一梦语为由，以报他先兆之意。曾见写与举人熊绍祖之书云：“闽省多才，甲于天下，虽京、浙不多让也。特阅麟经诸卷，无如贤最者。以深沉浑厚之养，发以雄俊爽锐之锋，来春大捷南宫，不卜而决矣。子月念二日夜将半，梦一飞熊，手擎红春花，行红日之中，上有金字‘大魁’二字，看甚分明。醒而忆之，日者，建阳也；熊者，君姓也；春花者，君治《春秋》经也。红亦彩色之象，‘大魁’金字则明有吉兆矣。以君之才，叶我之梦，则际明时魁天下，确有明证。若得大魁出于吾门，喜不能寐，人驰报，幸谨之勿泄。”熊举人之家阅之大喜，赏使银三两。请益，复与二两，曰：“明年有大捷，再赏你十两。”及他所奉之书，大抵都述吉梦，都是此意。人赏之者，皆三五金以上。

Early in the twelfth month, when the new juren had all gone up to the capital, a con man in the provincial city plotted with a xiucai of this prefecture who wrote a fine hand. For each new graduate they forged a letter in Commissioner Shen's name, stamped it with a small seal, wrapped and sealed it tight, and delivered the letters one by one to the graduates' homes. At each house the messenger would say: "Master Shen has a letter and has sent me specially. He charged me by word of mouth: your young master is certain of a great triumph next year. The master had a wondrous dream and ordered me to bring you word of it first—but you must guard it closely and let nothing leak. There is also young master so-and-so, whose family lives near yours; if he learned that a special messenger had come, he would say the master favors one and slights another, so a letter of greeting for him is enclosed as well. I merely brought it along—I was not sent for him." And when they came to that other house, they said exactly the same: that they had come specially for him, and all the rest were just brought along. When the families opened the letters, the calligraphy was in a fine, exact hand and the wording subtle and marvelous. Each said the commissioner had received an auspicious dream whose omen fell on that particular man, who was destined to be the great champion. Drawing now on a man's name, now on the meaning of his native place, they contrived a dream for each as the excuse, to foretell his coming glory. I once saw the letter they wrote to the juren Xiong Shaozu. It read: "Fujian abounds in talent, the first in the empire; even the capital and Zhejiang can hardly outdo it. And among all the essays on the Spring and Autumn that I read, none stood above yours. A deep and ample cultivation, issuing in a bold and shining edge—your triumph next spring in the Southern Palace, the metropolitan examination, is certain without divination. On the twenty-second night of the eleventh month, near midnight, I dreamed of a flying bear, a red spring flower raised in its paw, walking through the heart of a red sun, with two gold characters above it, 'Great Champion,' all plain to see. Waking, I pondered it: the sun is Jianyang, your native place; the bear is your surname; the spring flower is the Spring and Autumn, the classic you have made your own. Red is the hue of celebration, and the gold words 'Great Champion' are a manifest omen of luck. Your talent answering my dream is sure proof that in this bright reign you will stand first in the empire. If the great champion is to come from my own school, I shall be too joyful to sleep. I send this man at a gallop to tell you; keep it close and let nothing leak." The Xiong household read it with huge delight and rewarded the messenger with three taels of silver. When he begged for more, they gave him two taels more, saying, "If the great triumph comes next year, we will give you another ten." The letters delivered to the other houses all told lucky dreams to the same effect, and everyone who rewarded him gave three to five taels or more.

到次年，都银南翮而归，诸春元会时，各述沈道之书叙梦之事，各抚掌大笑曰：“真是好一场春梦也！”此棍真出奇绝巧矣。以此骗人，人谁不乐与之？算其所得，不止百金以上。聊述之以助一笑。

The following year every one of them came winging back south with clipped feathers. When the new juren met and each told of Commissioner Shen's letter and the dream it recounted, they clapped their hands and roared: "A fine spring dream that was, truly!" The trickster was astonishingly, surpassingly clever: cheat people like this, and who would not gladly pay? Reckon his takings, and they came to well over a hundred taels. I record the affair briefly, to give my reader a laugh.

按：此棍骗新举人，骗亦不痛，虽赏他几两银，亦博得举家人肚中欢喜四个月。惜此棍不再来，若再为之，人亦乐赏之矣。此骗局中最妙者。

Note: This crook cheated new juren, and the cheating barely stung. For a few taels of silver they bought four months of joy in their hearts, the whole household of them. The pity is that he never came back; had he run the same game again, people would gladly have paid again. Of all swindles, this is the most delightful.

诈无常烧牒捕人

Posing as Impermanence and Burning a Warrant to Arrest People

长源地方，人烟过千，亦一大市镇也。有一日者，推命人也。至其间推算甚精，断人死生寿夭，最是灵验，以故乡里之老幼男女，多以命与算。凡三年内有该病者、该死者，各问其姓名，暗登记之，以为后验。昼往于市卜命，夜则归宿于僧寺。

Changyuan was a place of over a thousand households, a sizable market town. There was a fortune-teller there, of the kind called a day-master. His reckonings were very fine, and his verdicts on who would die and who would live, who would last long and who would die young, were uncannily accurate; so the old and young, the men and women of the countryside, mostly gave him their fates to reckon. For anyone fated to sicken or die within three years, he asked the name and secretly noted it down, to check against later events. By day he went to the market to tell fortunes; at night he returned to sleep in a Buddhist temple.

有一游方道士至寺，形容半槁，黄瘦黧黑，敬谒日者曰：“闻先生推命极验，敢求此地老幼，有本年命运该死者、当有疾病者，悉以其姓名八字授我，我愿以游方经验药方几种奉换。”日者曰：“你不知命，要此何干？”道士曰：“我自有别用。”日者悉以推过之命，本年有该病者、该死者，尽录付之。道士后乞食诸家，每逢痴愚样人，辄自称是生无常，奉阴司差，同鬼使捕拿此方某人某人等，限此一季到。痴人代之播传，人多未言。又私将黄纸写一牌文，末写“阴司”二大字，中间计开依日者所授之老幼命该死者，写于上半行。又向本僧寺问本地富家男女，及人家钟爱之子姓名，写于后上层。夜间故在社司前，将黄纸牌从下截无人名处焚化，其上半有人名处打灭存之。次日，人来社司祈，见香炉上有黄纸字半截未焚者，取视之，都是乡人姓名，后有“阴司”字，大怪异之，持以传闻于乡。不一月间，此姓名内果死两人，遂相传谓“前瘦道士是生无常，此阴司黄纸牌彼必知之。”凡牌中有名者皆来问，无名者恐下截已焚处有，亦往问之。道士半吞半吐，认是己同鬼使焚的。由是畏死者问：“阴司牌可计免否？”道士曰：“阴司与阳间衙门则同，有银用者，计较免到；或必要再拿者，亦可挨延二三年。奈何不可用银也。”由是，富家男女多以银贿道士，兼以冥财金银，托其计较免到，亦赚得数十金去。其后牌中有名者多不死，反以为得道士计免之力也，岂不惑哉！

A wandering Daoist priest came to the temple, his face half-withered, sallow, gaunt, and dark. He paid a respectful call on the day-master and said: "I hear, sir, that your reckonings are infallible. Might I ask you for the names and eight characters of all the old and young of this place who are fated to die this year or to fall ill? In exchange I offer several proven remedies gathered in my wanderings." The day-master said, "You cannot read fates—what use are they to you?" The Daoist said, "I have a use of my own." So the day-master copied out every fate he had reckoned—all who were due for sickness or death that year—and handed the list over. Afterward the Daoist went begging from door to door, and whenever he met a simple-looking soul he would announce that he was a Living Impermanence, one of the underworld's soul-fetchers, commissioned by the court below to join its ghost bailiffs in arresting such-and-such persons of the district, due within the season. The fools spread the word for him, though most people had yet to hear of it. He also secretly wrote out a warrant on yellow paper, ending with the two large characters "Underworld Court." In the middle he itemized, from the day-master's list, the old and young fated to die, written in the upper rows; and he asked at the temple for the names of rich families' sons and daughters and of well-beloved children, and wrote them in the rows just behind. At night he went deliberately to the shrine of the earth god and set the yellow warrant alight from the lower end, where there were no names, then stamped out the flames and kept the upper half with the names. Next day, when people came to pray at the shrine, they saw on the incense burner a half-burned slip of yellow paper with writing on it. Picking it up, they found it full of villagers' names, with "Underworld Court" at the end. Much astonished, they carried the story all through the district. Within the month, two of those named actually died, and word went around: "That gaunt Daoist is a Living Impermanence—he must know all about this yellow warrant from the underworld." Everyone whose name was on the warrant came to ask about it; and those not named, afraid their names had been in the burned part, went to ask too. The Daoist, mumbling and half-hesitant, admitted that he and the ghost bailiffs had burned it. Then those who feared death asked, "Can the underworld warrant be bought off?" The Daoist said, "The underworld is just like a yamen of the mortal world: spend silver and your summons can be arranged away; if they absolutely must arrest you, it can still be put off two or three years. There is nothing silver cannot do." So the sons and daughters of rich houses bribed the Daoist with silver, adding gold and silver spirit money for the dead, and begged him to arrange their exemption; he made off with several dozen taels. Afterward most of those named on the warrant did not die—and people actually credited the Daoist's negotiations. What delusion!

按：阴司拘人，何须纸牌？即有牌票，亦何必焚？即焚矣，何为故留残纸餘字以扬于众？此必无之理也。观瘦无常一节，则惑世诬民昭昭矣。人之信鬼幻者，鉴此可以提醒。

Note: When the underworld arrests a man, what need has it of a paper warrant? If it did issue warrants, why burn one? And if it were burned, why deliberately leave a charred scrap with writing on it for all to see? None of this could possibly be. Look at the episode of the gaunt Impermanence: his duping of the world and swindling of the people is plain to see. Let those who believe in ghostly marvels take this story and wake up.

诈以帚柄耍轿夫

Making Fools of Chair-Bearers with Broom Handles

城西驿上至建溪陆路一百二十里，常轿价只一钱六分；或路少行客，则减下一钱四分或一钱二分，亦抬。但先邀轿价入手，便五里一放。略有小坡，又放下不抬。大抵坐轿两分，步走一分。凡往来客旅，无不被其笼络者。或当考期，应试士子归家，轿价便增至二钱四分，至少者二钱。不先秤银不抬。若银揽到手，不抬上二十里，便转雇上路夫去，把好价减，只以一分一铺转雇他人抬之。其下手抬者，仍旧五里一放，动曰：“我未得时价。”士子不得已，又重加之。但士人往来简少，都无与较。

From the post station west of the city up to Jianxi is a hundred and twenty li by land, and the regular sedan-chair fare is only one qian six fen. When travelers are scarce on the road, the bearers will knock it down to one qian four or one qian two and still carry you. But once they have coaxed the fare into their hands first, they set the chair down every five li; at the least little slope they put it down again and refuse to go on. All told, you ride two parts of the road and walk one, and every traveler passing through falls into their trap. At examination time, when the candidates head home from the exams, the fare jumps to two qian four, two qian at the very least, and they will not lift the poles until the silver is weighed out in advance. Once the silver is clutched in hand, before they have carried you twenty li they subcontract you to bearers bound up the road, shaving your good price down and hiring others at a mere one fen a stage to do the carrying. The men who take over still set you down every five li, forever protesting, "I never got the going rate." The scholars have no choice but to pay more. But scholars seldom travel, and none of them bothers to quarrel over it.

有一提控，不时往来于路，屡被轿夫刁蹬。一日，复要上县，先把两条纸，题四句嘲诗，以方纸包之，再用敝帚柄两个截齐，以绵纸封之，如两匹缎样。次日，自负上路，轿夫争来抬之。提控曰：“吾为一紧急事回家，身无现银。有能送我直到家者，议轿价二钱，又赏汝今晚明早酒饭。若要现银及转雇，则不能也。”内有二轿夫愿抬。遂以两封缎缚于轿，叮咛曰：“善安顿之，勿损坏！”才升轿，又曰：“我到回窑街，要寄一急信与人。你等到那里，慎勿忘也！”未半午后，已到回窑。提控曰：“你在此暂等，我去寄信便来。”其实抽身从小路归家，一饭久不来。两轿夫曰：“他坐话不觉久。有此两匹绸缎在此，我与你奔回，何须等他！”二人疾行，近晚归家。一曰：“各执一匹去。”一曰：“倘有好歹，须相添贴。”两人扯开绵纸，只是两截敝帚柄，重重封裹。又各有一方包，疑是书信。开之，见有纸题大字云：“轿夫常骗人，今也被我骗。若非两帚柄，险失两匹缎。”二人在家大骂曰：“光棍精光棍！”邻家轿夫闻之，入问何故各骂光棍，二轿夫叙其缘由如此。邻轿夫大笑而出，将两帚柄半封半露挂于排栅边，以两纸诗贴于旁。见者诵其诗，又看其帚柄，无不大笑曰：“此提控甚善骗。只你二轿夫亦不合起歹心，早是敝帚柄，故敢扬言骂人；若果是绸缎，你尚恐人知，那相公能寻汝取乎？此是你不是，何骂相公为？”

There was a yamen clerk (a tikong) who often traveled this road and had been given the runaround by the chair-bearers time and again. One day he had to go up to the county town again. First he wrote a four-line mocking verse on two slips of paper and wrapped them in square paper; then he took two worn broom handles, cut them to matching lengths, and sealed them up in cotton paper so they looked exactly like two bolts of silk. The next day he set out on the road carrying them himself, and the chair-bearers fought for the job. The clerk said, "I am heading home on urgent business and carry no ready silver. Whoever will take me straight home, we will agree on a fare of two qian, and I will stand you wine and supper tonight and breakfast tomorrow. If you want cash down or to subcontract me, that I cannot do." Two of the bearers agreed to carry him. He tied the two sealed "bolts" to the chair and charged them, "Stow them carefully—mind you don't damage them!" The moment he was seated he added, "At Huiyao Street I must send off an urgent letter to someone. When you get there, whatever you do, don't forget!" Before the afternoon was half spent they reached Huiyao. "Wait here a moment," said the clerk; "I will post the letter and be right back." In fact he slipped away by a side lane and headed home. A meal's time passed and he did not come. The two bearers said, "He is sitting there talking and has lost track of the time. Here are two bolts of silk in our hands—let us just run off home with them. Why wait for him?" The two hurried off and reached home toward evening. One said, "Each of us takes a bolt." The other said, "If one proves better than the other, we even it up between us." They tore open the cotton paper: nothing but two lengths of worn broom handle, wrapped round and round. Each bundle also held a square packet they took for a letter. Opening it, they found a slip inscribed in large characters: "Chair-bearers cheat people every day; today the cheat was mine. But for two broom handles, I would have lost two bolts of silk." At home the two cursed loudly: "The sharp! The very prince of sharps!" Chair-bearers from next door heard the noise and came in to ask why they were cursing sharps, and the two told them the whole story. The neighbors roared with laughter, then went out and hung the two broom handles, still half-wrapped and half-showing, on the palisade fence, pasting the two verse slips beside them. Everyone who passed read the verse aloud, looked at the broom handles, and burst out laughing: "This clerk is a master at the game. But you two bearers had no business nursing thieving thoughts. Luckily they were only broom handles, so you can shout your curses in public; had they really been silk, you would be terrified of anyone knowing, and how could the gentleman have come after you for them? The fault is yours—why curse the gentleman?"

后三日，提控回，见此诗尚贴在排栅，故问居旁人曰：“前日人寄我两匹缎，被两轿夫抬走，你们亦闻得乎？”人知是此提控弄轿，曰：“你也勿寻缎，那轿夫亦不敢出索轿钱矣。”提控亦大笑而去。

Three days later the clerk came back, saw the verse still pasted on the fence, and deliberately asked the people living nearby: "The other day someone entrusted me with two bolts of silk, and two chair-bearers carried them off. Did you happen to hear about it?" People knew the clerk was making game of the bearers and said, "You need not hunt for the silk—those bearers will not dare show their faces to claim the fare." The clerk laughed heartily and went on his way.

按：提控骗轿坐者非棍也，此两轿夫则棍耳。不然，何提控再回询问，而轿夫不敢出也？此谓借棍术，还驭棍徒，亦巧矣。然凡远出，若雇轿夫、挑夫，须从店主同雇。彼知役夫根脚，斯无拐逃失落之虞矣。

Note: The clerk who swindled a free chair ride was no swindler; the two chair-bearers were the swindlers. If not, why, when the clerk came back asking questions, did the bearers not dare show themselves? This is called borrowing the crooks' own tricks to drive the crooks—neatly done. Still, whenever you travel far and need chair-bearers or porters, hire them through your innkeeper. He knows the laborers' origins, and you need not fear them running off with your goods.

巷门口诈买脱布

A Sham Purchase at the Alley Mouth to Make Off with the Cloth

建城大街中，旁有一巷，路透后街。巷口为亭，旁列两凳，与人坐息，似人家门下一样。亭旁两边，俱土城，似入人家之门路，稍转，则见前大路矣。

In the main street of Jiancheng there was a side alley that ran through to the back street. At the alley mouth stood a pavilion, with two benches set beside it where people sat and rested, so that it looked just like the entrance to someone's house. Earthen walls flanked both sides of the pavilion, like the gateway path into a residence; only after a turn did the main road ahead come back into view.

忽日，有一棍在亭坐，见客负布而来，认非本城之人，心知其可哄，即叫曰：“买布！客人入亭来。”棍取其布，反复拣择，拿六匹在手，曰：“要买三匹，我拿六匹入内去拣。”即转入巷路，从后大街逃矣。布客在巷凳坐许久，时有一二行路者过此，心疑之，因随其后而入。转一曲墙路，见两旁并无人家，直前则出大路，心方知是被棍脱出。只问街两旁人曰：“方才有一人拿布六匹而来，兄曾见否？”旁人曰：“此巷往来极多，哪知甚人拿布？”布客道其哄买之由，旁人曰：“此是棍明骗去矣。”布客只得大骂，懊恨而去。

One day a crook was sitting in the pavilion when he saw a trader coming with cloth on his back. Recognizing him as no man of this city, he knew the fellow could be gulled. "Buying cloth!" he called. "Bring it into the pavilion, friend." The crook took the cloth and turned it over, picking and choosing, then gathered six bolts in his arms. "I mean to buy three," he said; "let me take six inside to choose among them." With that he turned into the alley and fled out the back street. The cloth seller sat on the bench in the alley for a long while. When one or two passersby came through, suspicion finally stirred, and he followed them in. Rounding the bend of the walled lane, he saw there were no houses on either side; straight ahead, the lane came out on the main road. Only then did he understand that the crook had walked off with his cloth. He could only ask the people along the street, "A man came this way just now carrying six bolts of cloth—did any of you see him?" The bystanders said, "This alley sees crowds all day—how would we know who was carrying cloth?" The cloth seller told them how the man had gulled him with a sham purchase. "A swindler has cheated you in broad daylight," the bystanders said. The cloth seller could only curse at the top of his voice and go off sick with regret.

按：卖物者虽入人门下，亦必跟至其家，见其人居止实落，方可以物付与。不然，虽公共之门，里面人烟丛杂，亦未可轻易信也。商者可以鉴此。

Note: A man selling goods, even when invited in at a gate, must follow the buyer all the way to his house and see that his dwelling is real and settled before he hands anything over. Otherwise, even at a public gateway where households press in thick behind it, you must not be quick to trust. Let every merchant take this to heart.

第五類 偽交騙

Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles

哄饮嫖害其身名

Luring Him into Drink and Whoring to Ruin His Life and Name

石涓，湖广麻城人。富而多诈，负气好胜，与族兄石涧尝争买田宅致隙。涧男石孝，读书进学，人品俊秀，性敏能文，人多拟其可中。石涓尝怀妒忌，思“吾生平发财，被涧兄所压。今其子又居士列，是虎而辅翼也。”因思计暗伤涧、孝父子。

Shi Juan was a native of Macheng in Huguang. Rich and full of deceit, he was touchy and hated to lose. He had once clashed with his elder cousin Shi Jian over rival bids for fields and houses, and bad blood remained between them. Jian’s son Shi Xiao studied, won a place in the county school, and was handsome, quick-witted, and a gifted writer; many predicted he would pass the examinations. Shi Juan nursed his jealousy: “All my life I have made money, yet brother Jian has always kept me in his shadow. Now his son has joined the ranks of the scholars—a tiger grown wings.” So he cast about for a scheme to wound Jian and Xiao, father and son, in secret.

不数年，涧故。石孝居忧，无人检束。涓思孝年少不羁，或可诱以酒色。因伪相结纳，孝趋亦趋，孝诺亦诺，终日游戏相征逐，数以曲蘖为欢。或时有美妓，涓邀孝饮其邸；或有好戏妇，涓每搬戏邀孝饮，又令戏妇曲意奉承，务挑其淫荡之心。孝堕其术中而不觉，玩日月，荒废诗书。及服阕补考，竟列劣等。孝因发奋，往寺读书，涓辄拉友挟妓，载酒至寺欢饮。孝见妓，不觉有喜心，故态复萌。涓又劝孝娶美妾二人，朝夕纵淫。内荒于色，外湎于酒，手沾战疯，不能楷书。道考被黜，家业凋零。石涓抚掌大笑曰：“吾生平之恨泄矣，计亦遂矣！”乃呼其子而训之曰：“涧兄在日，家富于我。因生孝不肖，酷好饮酒宿娼，不事诗书，致令丧却前程，身如丧家之狗。尔辈宜以为鉴，慎勿踏其覆辙。”未几，其子亦被人引诱赌嫖，所费不訾。涓因年老，无如之何，唯付之长叹而已。

A few years later Jian died. Shi Xiao was in mourning, with no one to keep him in line. Juan thought: Xiao is young and unbridled; perhaps he can be baited with wine and women. So he feigned friendship and attached himself to the boy: where Xiao went, Juan went; whatever Xiao approved, Juan approved. They spent whole days chasing amusements together and made merry over wine again and again. Whenever a pretty courtesan appeared, Juan invited Xiao to drink at her house; whenever a good actress came to town, Juan would stage a play and invite Xiao to drink, telling the actress to fawn on him with every art, bent on stirring his lust. Xiao fell into the trap without knowing it. He idled away the months and let his books go to waste. When the mourning ended and he sat the make-up examination, he was placed in the bottom grade. Xiao then roused himself and went to study at a temple, but Juan would drag along friends and courtesans, carting wine to the temple for drinking bouts. At the sight of the courtesans Xiao’s heart lifted despite himself, and his old habits sprouted again. Juan next urged Xiao to take two beautiful concubines, and he wallowed in lust morning and night. Wasted by sex within and soaked in wine without, his hands were seized with a tremor and he could no longer write a steady hand. At the circuit examination he was struck off the rolls, and the family estate withered. Shi Juan clapped and roared with laughter: “The grudge of a lifetime is vented, and my scheme has succeeded!” Then he called his sons and lectured them: “While brother Jian lived, his house was richer than mine. But he sired that worthless Xiao, whose one love was drinking and whoring and who would not touch his books, so he threw away his future and ended up a homeless dog. Take him as a warning, and never follow in his ruts.” Not long after, his own sons were led by others into gambling and whoring, and the money they burned through was beyond counting. Juan was old by then and could do nothing; he could only heave long sighs.

按：石涓奸巧百端，匿怨友人，使孝淫溺酒色，名利俱丧。彼虽自谓得计，足以快其宿忿，殊不思杀人之父，人亦杀其父；杀人之兄，人亦杀其兄。天网恢恢，报应不爽，安能保他人不袭彼故智，而子孙不蹈其覆辙乎？垂戒二子所繇，殆与义方之训异矣，又何怪其子之复然耶！然孝亦自愚也。使孝稍有心智，宜忖父在之时，与彼有怨，今父已即世，得彼不念足矣，顾安望深交乃尔！此其中情，叵测可知。由是以怠惰荒淫为戒，勤励不息自强，则石涓虽诈，安能中自立之士哉！

Note: Shi Juan had a hundred crafty tricks. He hid an old grudge and made friends with Xiao, and drowned him in wine and lust until his name and his fortune were both lost. Juan thought he had won and could vent his long-held spite; it never crossed his mind that if you kill a man’s father, others will kill yours, and if you kill a man’s brother, others will kill yours. Heaven’s net is vast, and its reckoning never errs. How could he be sure no one else would borrow his old trick, or that his own sons and grandsons would not follow the same ruts? The warning he gave his two sons was a far cry from a righteous father’s teaching—so why be surprised that his sons went the same way? And yet Xiao was a fool too. Had he possessed an ounce of sense, he should have reckoned: while my father lived, this man was his enemy; now that my father is gone, the most I can ask is that he let the grudge go—how could I expect friendship that deep? That something unfathomable lay behind it should have been plain. If Xiao had taken sloth and debauchery as his warning and driven himself on without rest, then however crafty Shi Juan was, how could he have struck a man who stood on his own feet?

哄友犯奸谋其田

Luring a Friend into Adultery to Take His Land

毕和，山西人。心术狡险，阴悍暗毒，乡人无不被其害者。族弟毕松，有田一段，价值五十餘金，与和田毗连。和屡谋不遂，因诈与交好，屡席相款，旦夕游戏，即同胞不啻焉。

Bi He was a native of Shanxi, sly and ruthless at heart, vicious and poisonous in secret; there was no one in the village he had not harmed. His younger cousin Bi Song owned a stretch of land worth more than fifty taels, right next to He’s fields. He had schemed for it time and again without success, so he feigned friendship instead: he feasted Song again and again and kept him company from morning to night, as close as any blood brother.

同乡有林远者，性刚而暴。其妻罗氏，貌美好淫，与夫反睦〔目〕。和乘机挑之，遂通往来，情甚密。假意不令松知，实欲使之知之。故遮头露尾，为松觑破。松乃怪和曰：“枉自与你相知。有此美妇人，何不引我一宿，岂便夺你爱乎？”和逊谢曰：“此妇极有情，若引你去，必深相怜爱。恐你往来无节，事机不密，其夫若知，有误身家不便矣。”松只疑其专宠，乃私往挑之，罗氏遂允。后来情更绸缪，每候其夫出外，非和往则松往，甚且三人同床，情如一体。

In the same village lived a man named Lin Yuan, hard and violent by nature. His wife, born Luo, was beautiful and loose, and she and her husband were at odds. He seized the opening and seduced her; they began an affair and grew very close. He pretended to keep it from Song while in fact wanting Song to find out—half hiding it, half letting it show—until Song saw through him. Song reproached him: “So much for our friendship. With a beauty like that, why not take me along for a night? Do you think I would steal her from you?” He answered with a show of deference: “The woman is full of feeling. If I brought you, she would surely dote on you. But I fear you would come and go without restraint, word would leak out, and if her husband found out, it could cost you your family and your neck.” Song only suspected He of keeping her all to himself, so he went on his own to seduce her, and Luo gave in. After that the affair grew still more tangled: whenever the husband was out, if it was not He who went, it was Song; at times all three even shared one bed, close as a single body.

将及月餘，和密报其夫曰：“松弟与我至知，今闻与令正有情，我屡谏不听。闻你欲捕之，若捕得，可轻打些。彼必叫我解交，我谕他多送你些银，以绝他后日妄为。慎勿害他性命。”林远闻言，怒气填胸。次日即托言外出，须三日后方归。松专瞰远去向，闻其出外，即往其家，搂罗氏入房调耍。林远从密处突出，打入房中，二人已解衣在床。远揪松于床下凶打，罗氏拼命拿住夫手，远不能多打。松求放曰：“愿以银赎免。”远曰：“要何人来保认？”松曰：“叫我和兄来。”远正合意，即遣人呼和至。和曰：“不行正路，以至于此。须召见你亲兄来。”松曰：“勿召我兄，只你代我出银与之，后日即还。”和曰：“我代议事，怎好出银？但今事急矣，我若不出银，此事无由解释。然必有实物相当方可。”松因写前毗连之田契卖之。和曰：“只可少作价，多则亦为林远所得。”止作价四十两。和归，取银三十两相付。远曰：“须六十两。”和曰：“奸情被获，合输妇价一半。纵令正美貌，可值六十金，此已一半矣。”远再三不肯。和曰：“彼田价四十两，我手中无现银，不如约一月后，再在我手接十两。”远要约批。和曰：“若他人议事，须加二抽头，我已该八两矣。今为你息事，何逼我约批乎？”遂无约批，放松同归。

When nearly a month had passed, He secretly tipped off the husband: “Song is my dearest friend, but now I hear he is carrying on with your wife. I have warned him again and again and he will not listen. I hear you mean to catch them. If you do, give him only a light beating. He is sure to call me in to mediate, and I will make him pay you a good sum of silver to put an end to his mischief. Whatever you do, do not take his life.” Lin Yuan heard this and rage filled his chest. The next day he announced he was going out and would not be back for three days. Song had been watching for Yuan’s departure; the moment he heard the man was away, he went to the house, pulled Luo into the bedroom, and began to fool around. Lin Yuan burst out of hiding and charged into the room, where the two had already undressed on the bed. Yuan hauled Song off the bed and beat him savagely; Luo threw herself on her husband’s arms with all her strength, so Yuan could not land many blows. Song begged for release: “Let me buy my way out with silver.” Yuan said: “Who will stand surety for you?” Song said: “Fetch my cousin He.” That was exactly what Yuan wanted, and he sent for He at once. He said: “You strayed from the straight path, and it has come to this. We must call your own elder brother.” Song said: “Don’t call my brother. Just put up the silver for me and I will pay you back in a few days.” He said: “I came to mediate—how can I be the one paying? But the matter is urgent. If I do not put up the silver, there is no way out of this. Still, there must be real property to match it.” So Song wrote out a deed selling He the adjoining field. He said: “You can only price it low; any more would just end up in Lin Yuan’s pocket.” They set the price at a mere forty taels. He went home, brought back thirty taels, and handed them over. Yuan said: “I want sixty.” He said: “For adultery caught in the act, the forfeit is half the wife’s value. Even granting your wife is a beauty worth sixty taels, this is already half.” Yuan refused again and again. He said: “His field is priced at forty taels, and I have no more ready silver on hand. Better to agree that a month from now he collects another ten taels from me.” Yuan demanded a written pledge. He said: “If anyone else had mediated, he would take a twenty percent cut—I would already be owed eight taels. Here I am settling your affair for nothing, and you press me for a note?” So no note was written, and He took Song home with him.

数日后，松备本息四十四两赎前田，和不肯退。一月后，林远向和取约银。和曰：“指示你撰银三十两，二两谢我，岂为多乎？”远后对人说出和教捉奸之由，松方知为和所卖。然已堕其诡计，悔无及矣。

A few days later Song brought principal and interest—forty-four taels—to redeem the field, but He refused to give it back. A month later Lin Yuan came to He for the promised silver. He said: “I showed you how to pocket thirty taels; is two taels in thanks too much to ask?” Yuan later told others how He had put him up to catching the adulterers, and only then did Song learn that He had sold him out. But he had already fallen into the trap, and regret came too late.

按：和欲谋松田，先引之奸；欲诱其奸，先与之友。且其奸也，非彼明引，而令其自入。其要之田也，俟其有急而为之解纷，以徐收之，计亦巧矣。向非赖后约银，则林远必不言其所由，彼和之深情厚毒，畴能测之？故人而毒行不端者，彼虽与我交密，亦须提防者也。

Note: He wanted Song’s field, so first he led him into adultery; and to lead him into adultery, he first made himself Song’s friend. Nor did he openly push Song into the affair—he let Song walk into it on his own. And in claiming the field, he waited for Song’s hour of desperation, then ‘settled the quarrel’ for him and gathered it in at his leisure. A clever scheme indeed.
