Had it not been for the silver promised afterward, Lin Yuan would never have revealed how it began, and who could have fathomed the depth of He’s devotion—or the depth of his poison? So when a man’s conduct is venomous and crooked, then however close he is to you, you must keep your guard up.

累算友财倾其家

Piling Up a Friend’s Debts to Ruin His House

金从宇、洪起予，俱是应天府人。相隔一十餘店，皆开大京铺，各有资本千餘金。但从宇狡猾奸险，起予温良朴实。时常贩买京货，累相会席，各有酒量，欢相劝酬。

Jin Congyu and Hong Qiyu were both men of Yingtian Prefecture. Their shops stood a dozen or so doors apart, and both ran large stores dealing in goods from the capital, each with capital of more than a thousand taels. But Congyu was sly and ruthless, while Qiyu was gentle, honest, and simple. Both often went to buy capital goods and met again and again at feasts; each could hold his wine, and they toasted one another merrily.

从宇思曰：“人言慈不掌兵，义不掌财。我观起予慈善好义，诚直无智。何彼铺买卖与我相并也？当以智术笼络之。”以故伪相交密，时节以物相馈送，有庆贺礼，皆相请召。起予只以金为好意，皆薄来厚往以答之。从宇曰：“此人好酒，须以酒误之。”乃时时饮月福、打平和、邀庆纲，招饮殆无虚日。有芳辰佳景，邀与同游；夜月清凉，私谈竟夕。起予果中其奸，日在醉乡，不事买卖。从宇虽日伴起予游饮，彼有弟济宇在店，凡事皆能代理。起予一向闲游，店中虚无人守。有客来店者，寻之不在，多往济宇铺买。由是金铺日盛，洪铺日替，起予渐穷于用。从宇随取随与之，每一半九成，一半七八成；银又等头轻少，不索其借批，但云“须明白记帐也”。不四五年间，陆续借上六百餘两。乃使济宇往取之，起予别借二百两以还。后算过帐，尚欠四百餘两。逼其写田宅为当，方思还债取田。起予一皆从言。再过两年，本息合四百五十餘两矣。济宇力逼全收。起予求从宇稍宽。从宇曰：“吾银本与舍弟相共，彼在家尝怨我不合把银借你。今我不理，任你两下何如？”此时金宅有新立当契在手，起予推延不过，只得将产业尽数写契填还之。他债主知其落寞，都来逼取。千餘金家，不两三栽，一旦罄空。皆金从宇倾陷累算之故也。

Congyu thought: “As the saying goes, the kind cannot command troops and the upright cannot hold on to money. I see that Qiyu is charitable and fond of doing good, honest and without guile. Why should his shop’s trade rival mine? I must snare him with craft.” So he feigned close friendship: he sent gifts at every season, and whenever there was a celebration he invited Qiyu. Qiyu took Jin’s attentions as goodwill and always returned small gifts with generous ones. Congyu said: “The man loves wine; wine will be his ruin.” So he kept up a round of monthly blessing feasts, split-the-bill dinners, and guild celebrations—there was hardly a day without a drinking invitation. On fine days with lovely views he took Qiyu roaming; on cool moonlit nights they talked in private until dawn. Qiyu duly fell for the trap: he spent his days in a drunken haze and gave no thought to business. Congyu might spend his days drinking and roaming with Qiyu, but he had a younger brother, Jiyu, in the shop who could manage everything. Qiyu idled away his time while his own shop stood empty and unwatched. Customers who came looking for him and found him gone mostly went to buy at Jiyu’s shop instead. So Jin’s shop flourished by the day while Hong’s declined by the day, and Qiyu gradually ran short of funds. Congyu gave him money whenever he asked—but each time half of it was ninety-percent silver and half only seventy or eighty, and the weight came up light on the scale. He never asked for a loan note, only saying, “Just keep a clear account.” In less than four or five years the loans had mounted to more than six hundred taels. Then he sent Jiyu to collect. Qiyu borrowed two hundred taels elsewhere to repay, and when the accounts were settled he still owed more than four hundred. They pressed him to sign over his fields and houses as a mortgage—Qiyu meaning to repay the debt later and redeem the land. Qiyu went along with everything they said. Two more years passed, and principal and interest together came to more than four hundred fifty taels. Jiyu pressed hard to collect in full. Qiyu begged Congyu for a little leniency. Congyu said: “My silver is held in common with my younger brother. At home he has often complained that I should never have lent it to you. I will stay out of it now—settle it between the two of you.” By then the Jin household held the freshly written mortgage deed. Qiyu could stall no longer and had to sign over every property he owned to cover the debt. His other creditors, seeing him ruined, all came pressing for payment. A household worth more than a thousand taels was emptied in a stroke within two or three years. It was all the work of Jin Congyu’s entrapment and compounded scheming.

洪已破家之后，从宇全不瞅睐，虽求分文相借，一毫不与矣。从宇又用此术，再交杨店之子。有识者笑杨子曰：“汝是洪起予替身，何不取鉴前车乎？”杨乃渐疏绝之。

Once Hong’s house was broken, Congyu would not spare him a glance; even if he begged to borrow a single cash, not a hair was given. Congyu then worked the same trick on the son of the Yang shop. Those who saw through it laughed at young Yang: “You are Hong Qiyu’s replacement—why not learn from the cart that overturned ahead of you?” Yang gradually drew away and cut him off.

按：以银借人，收其子利，未为累算。特洪本富贾，从宇诱其游饮，不事生理，致资本消折，而以银借之。期间以八当十，加三算息，亏短田价，稍蚕食之。从宇之奸贪极矣！为富不仁，从宇其何说之辞？

Note: Lending silver at interest is not, in itself, a compounded swindle. The point is that Hong was a rich merchant to begin with, and Congyu lured him into roaming and drinking until he abandoned his trade and his capital melted away—and only then lent him the silver. Along the way he counted eight as ten, charged thirty percent interest, shorted him on the land price, and nibbled him down like a silkworm on a leaf. Congyu’s treachery and greed knew no limit. Wealth without humanity—what defense can a man like Congyu possibly offer?

激友讼奸以败家

Goading a Friend into an Adultery Suit to Ruin His House

马自鸣，浙江绍兴人。狷巧小人，柔媚多奸。族弟马应，轻浮愚昧，家更富于自鸣。其父素与鸣父不睦，两相图而未发。自鸣见应愚呆，性又嗜酒，故时时与之会饮；亦连引诸人，共打平和。唯此两人深相结纳，人多厌之，不与共饮。二人乃对斟对酌，此唱彼和，自号为莫逆交。应有事，多取决于自鸣；鸣亦时献小计，以效忠款。应素与亲兄不睦，数扬其短，欲状告之。自鸣假意劝阻，实于当机处反言以激之，益深其怒。应遂先往告兄，经官断明，拟应欧兄之罪；又投分上解释。此为破家之始。又屡屡唆其与人争讼，家日破败。

Ma Ziming was a native of Shaoxing in Zhejiang—a small-minded schemer, smooth and fawning and full of guile. His younger cousin Ma Ying was flighty and dull, and his household was even richer than Ziming’s. Ying’s father had long been at odds with Ziming’s father; each side was plotting against the other, but neither had struck. Ziming saw that Ying was a fool and a drunk besides, so he drank with him constantly, sometimes rounding up others to split the bill. But only these two formed a real bond; everyone else grew sick of them and would not drink with them. So the two poured for each other, sang each other’s praises, and called themselves bosom friends. Whenever Ying had business, he mostly let Ziming decide, and Ziming would offer little schemes from time to time to prove his devotion. Ying had long been on bad terms with his own elder brother; he often aired the man’s faults and wanted to file a complaint against him. Ziming made a show of talking him out of it, but at the decisive moment he would say just the opposite to provoke him, and Ying’s anger deepened. Ying went ahead and accused his brother first. The officials saw the case clearly and found Ying guilty of assaulting an elder brother; he got off only by calling in favors. This was the beginning of the family’s ruin. After that Ziming egged him into lawsuit after lawsuit, and the household crumbled by the day.

后自鸣往小户人家取债，见其妇幼美，归家向应前夸曰：“我今往某家取债，其媳妇生甚美貌，女流中西施也。我以目挑之，俯首而过。其屋只一楹，数往来于前。我神魂飘荡，不能自禁。又以笑语挑之，此妇亦笑脸回答，似亦可图。只怕其夫姑有碍，未敢施为。至今挂恋在心，寤寐思服。”应曰：“此家是我甲首，又系佃户，图亦何难？我必先取之。”自鸣激之曰：“汝若能得，我输你一大东道。依我说，勿去惹此愚夫，若捉住，彼粗拳，真打死也。”应曰：“未闻佃客敢殴主人者。”次日，即往其家收条编。一见其妇，即挑之，遣其婆出外曰：“可外出觅菜来作午。”婆方出，即强抱其妇入房。妇在从否之间，见隔壁一妇窥见躲开。妇指之曰：“某姆在隔壁窥见，你勿为此。”哪肯休，只以为推托也。相缠已久，婆在外归。妇只得叫妈妈曰：“主人如此野意。”婆作色叱。怒，先往县呈其拖欠条编，反允殴里长。其佃人以强奸诉官拘审，邻妇窥见，亲姑捉获。其妇又貌美倾城，满堂聚观，啧啧叹赏。因审作强奸，应拟死罪。后投分上，改作戏奸未就。而家业尽倾，田宅皆卖与自鸣。（自鸣）反责曰：“我当初叫你勿为，你不听吾言，以至于此。”应曰：“你口虽叫我勿为，先已造桥，送我在桥中去矣，难回步也。今欲怪你，又怪不得。孟子谓‘非之无可举，刺之无可刺’，正你这样人也。”

Later Ziming went to a humble household to collect a debt and saw that the wife was young and beautiful. He came home and boasted to Ying: “Today I went to such-and-such a house to collect a debt. The daughter-in-law is a rare beauty, a veritable Xi Shi among women. I made eyes at her and she passed with her head bowed. The house is only one bay wide, so I strolled back and forth in front of it several times. My soul went adrift and I could not help myself. Then I teased her with laughing words, and she answered with a smile—she seemed there for the taking. My only fear was that her husband and his mother would be in the way, so I dared not act. I have been pining for her ever since, waking and sleeping.” Ying said: “That household is one of my tithing heads (jiashou), and they are my tenants besides. What is hard about having her? I will take her first.” Ziming provoked him: “If you can have her, I will stand you a grand feast. But take my advice: do not provoke that dolt of a husband. If he catches you, those coarse fists of his will beat you to death for real.” Ying said: “I have never heard of a tenant daring to lay a hand on his landlord.” The next day he went to the house to collect the consolidated tax. The moment he saw the wife he began teasing her, and sent her mother-in-law out: “Go find some vegetables for lunch.” As soon as the old woman left, he seized the wife and dragged her into the bedroom. She wavered between yielding and refusing; then she saw a woman next door peek in and duck away. She pointed: “Auntie next door saw us—you mustn’t.” He would not stop, taking it for a mere excuse. They had been grappling for a good while when the old woman came back. The wife could only cry out, “Mother! The master is forcing himself on me!” The old woman flared up and scolded him. Enraged, Ying went straight to the county and filed a complaint that the household had defaulted on the consolidated tax, and even accused them of assaulting the village head (lizhang). The tenant in turn accused Ying of rape, and the officials arrested the parties for trial. The neighbor woman had seen it, and the wife’s own mother-in-law had caught them. The wife was, moreover, a city-toppling beauty; the whole courtroom crowded in to stare, clucking in admiration. The case was tried as rape, and Ying was sentenced to death. Later he called in favors and had the charge reduced to seduction in sport, not consummated. But the family estate was ruined; the fields and houses were all sold to Ziming. And Ziming had the gall to scold him: “I told you at the start not to do it. You would not listen, and it has come to this.” Ying said: “Your mouth told me not to, but you had already built the bridge and walked me out onto it—there was no turning back. I want to blame you, yet I cannot quite pin it on you. Mencius said, ‘Condemn him and there is nothing to cite; censure him and there is nothing to seize.’ That is exactly a man like you.”

田卖尽，自鸣绝不与往来。朝夕相借，惟干谒亲兄，方知亲者终是亲；彼酒肉朋友，真伪情也。

Once the land was all sold, Ziming cut off all dealings with him. Ying had to beg loans morning and night, and in the end only his own elder brother would take him in. Only then did he learn that blood is blood after all, and that his wine-and-meat friend had been false from the start.

按：应被自鸣笼络，家破，产业尽被鸣收之，反与之莫逆之交，何其愚也！苟有心智人之处世，内而兄弟叔侄，外而朋友亲戚，皆不能无。与兄结讼而求昵于友，是其所厚者薄，而薄者反厚也。何不观孙荣之间革孙华而亦昵于友，使非杨氏贤德，后始有悔悟。而能以是而自新之，彼虽有百般巧计，安能中自新之士哉！

Note: Ying was snared by Ziming; his house was broken and every bit of his property gathered into Ziming’s hands, yet he had taken the man for a bosom friend. What folly! A man of any sense, living in the world, cannot do without brothers, uncles, and nephews within the family, nor without friends and kin beyond it. To sue his own brother while courting the intimacy of a friend is to slight those he should hold dear and hold dear the one he should slight. Consider Sun Hua of old, who cast out his brother Sun Rong and clung to his friends instead: had his wife Lady Yang not been a woman of such virtue, he would have been slow indeed to repent. If a man can take warning and make himself new, then however many clever tricks they have, how could they ever strike a man who has made himself new?

第六類 牙行騙

Category 6 · Broker Swindles

狡牙脱纸以女偿

A Cunning Broker Diverts the Paper and Repays with His Daughter

施守训，福建大安人。家资殷富，常造纸卖客。一日，自装千餘篓，价值八百餘两，往苏州卖，寓牙人翁滨二店。滨乃宿牙，叠积前客债甚多，见施雏商，将其纸尽还前客，误施坐候半年。家中又发纸五百餘篓到苏州，滨代现卖。付银讫，托言系取旧帐者，复候半年。知受其笼络，施乃怒骂殴之。滨无言可应，当凭乡亲刘光前议，谕滨立过借批银八百两，劝施暂回。

Shi Shouxun was a native of Da'an in Fujian. His family was wealthy, and he regularly made paper to sell to traveling merchants. One day he loaded more than a thousand baskets himself, worth over eight hundred taels, and went to Suzhou to sell them, lodging at the shop of the broker Weng Bin'er. Weng was an old hand among brokers and had piled up heavy debts to earlier clients. Seeing that Shi was a novice merchant, he used the entire shipment of paper to pay off those earlier clients and left Shi cooling his heels for half a year. Shi's family then sent another five hundred-odd baskets of paper to Suzhou, and Weng sold them for him for cash. When the silver came in, he claimed it had gone to settle old accounts, and Shi waited another half a year. Realizing he had been strung along, Shi cursed him and beat him. Weng had no answer. With their fellow townsman Liu Guangqian mediating, Weng was made to write out an IOU for eight hundred taels, and Shi was advised to go back home for the time being.

次年，复载纸到苏州，滨代现卖。只前帐难还，施又坐待半年。见其女云英有貌，未曾许配，思此银难取，乃浼刘光前为媒，求其女为妾，抵还前帐。滨悦许之。其女年方十五，执不肯从。滨与妻入劝曰：“古有缇萦愿没官为婢，以赎父罪。今父欠客人银八百两，以汝填还；况福建客家多巨富，若后日生子，分其家财，居此致富，享福非小。”女始允诺。时施已六十餘矣。成婚近四载，施后回家身故。未及周期服，滨将女重嫁南京溧水县梁恩赐为妾，重收聘礼一百两。

The next year Shi again shipped paper to Suzhou, and Weng sold it for cash. But the old debt was still hard to recover, and Shi sat waiting another half a year. He saw that Weng's daughter Yunying was good-looking and not yet betrothed. Thinking the silver would be hard to collect, he asked Liu Guangqian to act as matchmaker and sought the daughter as a concubine, to offset the old debt. Weng happily agreed. The girl had just turned fifteen and flatly refused. Weng and his wife went in to press her: "In ancient times the girl Tiying offered to become a state slave to ransom her father from punishment. Now your father owes a client eight hundred taels of silver, and we are making it good with you. Besides, Fujian merchant families are often immensely rich; if you later bear a son and get a share of the family property, you will be set up in wealth here and enjoy no small fortune." Only then did the girl consent. Shi was already over sixty. They were married nearly four years; later Shi went back home and died. Before the year of mourning was out, Weng married the girl off again as a concubine to Liang Enci of Lishui County, Nanjing, and collected another hundred taels in betrothal gifts.

守训男施钦知之为，本年亦装纸到苏州，往拜翁家，呼翁为“外祖”。翁不瞅睬他。请庶母出见，亦拒不出。众客伙皆怒而嗾曰：“你父以八百两聘礼，止成亲四载，未期服又重嫁他人。今一出见何害？情甚可恶！汝何不鸣官？”钦乃告于巡街蔡御史。时翁滨二得施为婿，复振家风，又发资金千餘，见告，毫无惧意。两下各投分上，讦讼几二年。各司道皆纳分上，附会而判。后钦状告刑部，始获公断曰：“翁滨二以女抵偿八百两，几与绿珠同价矣。但守训自肯，其财礼勿论。今夫服未满重嫁梁客，兜重财物，是以女为货，不顾律法。合责三十板，断身资银一百两，并守训为云英置衣资首饰银五十两，共与施钦领之。”因此积讼连年，滨二之家财尽倾，仍流落于贫矣。

When Shouxun's son Shi Qin learned of this, that same year he too shipped paper to Suzhou. He went to pay his respects at the Weng house and addressed Weng as "maternal grandfather." Weng ignored him. He asked for his stepmother to come out and see him; she too refused to come out. The other merchants there were angry and egged him on: "Your father paid eight hundred taels in betrothal gifts, they were man and wife only four years, and before the mourning was over she was married off to another man. What harm would it do her to come out and see you once? The whole thing is outrageous! Why don't you report it to the authorities?" Qin then filed a complaint with the street-patrol censor Cai. By then Weng Bin'er, having gained Shi as a son-in-law, had restored the family fortunes and put out another thousand-odd in capital, so when the complaint came he felt not the slightest fear. Both sides enlisted backers, and the litigation dragged on nearly two years. Every office that handled the case took the inducements and stretched the facts to decide it. Only when Qin filed with the Ministry of Justice did he get a fair judgment: "Weng Bin'er paid off eight hundred taels with his daughter — nearly the price of Lüzhu, the most costly concubine of old. But since Shouxun consented of his own accord, we will not go into the betrothal money. Now, before her husband's mourning was complete, she was married off again to the merchant Liang, scooping up a second payment — this is treating a daughter as merchandise, with no regard for the law. He shall be beaten thirty strokes, and we award the body-price of one hundred taels, plus the fifty taels Shouxun spent on clothes and jewelry for Yunying, all to be paid over to Shi Qin." Because the lawsuits piled up year after year, Bin'er's family wealth was entirely drained, and he sank back into poverty.

按：脱骗之害，首侠棍，次狡侩。侠棍设局暗脱，窃盗也。狡侩骗货明卖，强盗也。二者当与盗同科。凡牙侩之弊，客货入店，彼皆作纲抵偿，又多窃取供家，每以后客货盖前客帐，此穷牙常态也。施守训在不早审牙家，致落此坑堑。只可小心逼取，或继以告，不当图其女为妾。夫以六旬上人，岁月几何，纳妾异地，能无后患乎？贻子后讼，所费不资。虽最终取胜，得不偿失矣。独恨翁滨二负心歹汉，以一女而还银八百两，得已过分，又得婿扶以成家；后女虽再嫁，当以身资还施之男，永可无患矣。乃贪心不满，再致倾家，真可为欺心负义之鉴。

Note: Of the harm done by swindles, the worst offenders are the swaggering sharps, and next the cunning brokers. The swaggering sharps set up schemes and swindle you in the dark — that is theft. The cunning brokers cheat you out of your goods and sell them in broad daylight — that is robbery. Both should be sentenced the same as bandits. As for the usual abuses of brokers: once a client's goods come into the shop, they lump them in to cover their debts, and they often skim off more for their own households; they habitually use a later client's goods to cover an earlier client's account. This is the normal state of a poor broker. Shi Shouxun's mistake was not sizing up the broker early on, so he fell into this pit. He should have pressed carefully for payment, or followed up with a lawsuit; he should not have schemed to take the daughter as a concubine. A man past sixty — how many years could he have left? Taking a concubine in a strange place — how could there not be trouble afterward? He left his son a lawsuit, and the costs were beyond counting. Though he won in the end, the gain did not cover the loss. What is hateful is Weng Bin'er, a heartless scoundrel: with one daughter he repaid eight hundred taels, already more than enough, and on top of that got a son-in-law's help to rebuild his household. Later, even when the girl remarried, he should have returned the body-price to Shi's son, and there would have been no trouble ever after. But his greedy heart was not satisfied, and he brought ruin on his house a second time. Truly this can serve as a warning mirror for those who cheat their conscience and betray what is right.

贫牙脱蜡还旧债

A Poor Broker Diverts the Wax to Pay Old Debts

张霸，四川人。为人机关精密，身长力勇。一日，买蜡百餘担，往福建建宁府丘店发卖。此牙家盆彻骨，外张富态，欠前客货银极多。霸蜡到，即以光棍顶作鬼名来借蜡，约后还银。数日后，霸往街游玩，其蜡遍在诸铺。及问其姓名，皆与帐名不同。霸心疑必有弊，故回店讯问牙人曰：“你脱我蜡去还前帐，可一一实报帐来。若不实言，你乘我几拳不得。”丘牙哑口无应。霸轮拳擒打，如鹰擒雀，如踢戏球。丘牙连忙求饶，曰：“公神人也。此蜡真还前客旧帐，并家用去矣，何能问各店重取？”张霸曰：“你将还人的及各店买去的，都登上帐，只说他揭借去，俱未还银。我将帐去告，取你硬作证，怕他各店不再还我？”丘牙依言，一一写成发货帐。

Zhang Ba was a native of Sichuan, a shrewd and meticulous man, tall and strong. One day he bought more than a hundred loads of wax and went to sell it at the Qiu shop in Jianning Prefecture, Fujian. This broker's house was poor to the bone but put on a wealthy look outside, and it owed a great deal of silver for earlier clients' goods. When Ba's wax arrived, the broker had smooth operators come under false names to "borrow" the wax, promising to pay the silver later. A few days later Ba went strolling in the streets and saw his wax spread through all the shops. When he asked for the buyers' names, none matched the names on the account. Suspecting a trick, he went back to the shop and questioned the broker: "You diverted my wax to pay off old accounts. Give me a truthful report of every account. If you don't tell the truth, you won't be able to take a few of my punches." Broker Qiu was struck dumb and had no answer. Ba swung his fists and pummeled him, like a hawk seizing a sparrow, like kicking a ball around. Qiu hastily begged for mercy: "You must be superhuman, sir. The wax truly went to pay earlier clients' old accounts, and some went for household use. How could I ask the shops to give it back?" Zhang Ba said: "Write down what you repaid to people and what the shops bought, and just record that they took it on credit and have not yet paid the silver. I will take the account and file a complaint, with you standing firm as my witness. Why should I fear the shops won't pay me a second time?" Qiu did as he was told and wrote out the delivery ledger item by item.

张霸即具状告府。署印梅爷看状，掷地不准。霸心伤失本，两眼自然垂。再三哀告，梅爷乃准其状。先差皂隶往查各店蜡。霸以银贿公差，回报曰：“各店果有张霸印号蜡。”梅爷曰：“哪有揭借客蜡都不还银者！”即出牌拘审。

Zhang Ba then drew up a complaint and filed it with the prefecture. The acting prefect, Master Mei, read the complaint, threw it on the ground, and refused to accept it. Ba was heartsick at losing his capital, and tears fell from his eyes of their own accord. After he pleaded again and again, Master Mei accepted the complaint. He first sent yamen runners to check the wax in the shops. Ba bribed the runners with silver, and they reported back: "The shops do indeed have wax bearing Zhang Ba's stamp." Master Mei said: "Who ever heard of taking a client's wax on credit and never paying the silver!" He at once issued a warrant to bring them in for trial.

各店在外商量曰：“我店买张客蜡，俱已还银，牙家收讫。又牙人自用蜡还我者，是他所合抽得牙钱，何得今更重告？吾与汝等敛银共用，投一分上，先去讲明，然后对审。”敛银已毕，即将银一百两投梅爷乡亲。

The shops conferred among themselves outside: "Our shops bought merchant Zhang's wax and have already paid the silver; the broker received it. And the wax the broker used to repay us on his own account was the brokerage cut he was entitled to take. How can he now sue us over it a second time? Let us pool silver for the expenses, place it with a well-connected backer, explain our side first, and then face the trial." When the silver had been collected, they sent one hundred taels to a fellow townsman of Master Mei.

梅爷刚正之官，弗听，即拘来审。内有江店客人，乃惯讼者，先对理曰：“蜡乃丘牙明卖与我，公平交易，张霸安得重取？即未全交付，亦牙家刻落，与我辈何干？”丘牙曰：“蜡非卖他。是小人先欠诸店旧帐，张霸蜡到，他等诈言措借，数日后即还银。及得蜡到手，即坐以抵前帐。非小人敢兜客银也。”梅爷曰：“丘牙欠债，须问彼自取，安得坐客人货以还彼债？你众等可将价还张霸，免你等罪。”江店恃有分上，再三辩论，说是明白交易，并无对债之事。梅爷触怒，将江店责十板；江店又辩论不已，又被责二十板。后诸人惊惧，皆称愿赔求饶。以江店监禁，诸人讨保，断蜡银，限三日，不完再重责。三日果追完。霸领银讫，深感梅爷恩泽，顶戴香炉，到于堂下，叩拜而去。

Master Mei was an upright official and would not listen to it; he had the men brought in for trial. Among them was the Jiang shop's man, a habitual litigant, who argued first: "The wax was sold to me openly by broker Qiu, a fair transaction. How can Zhang Ba collect for it a second time? And even if it was not paid for in full, that is the broker's skimming — what has it to do with us?" Broker Qiu said: "The wax was not sold to them. I already owed the shops old debts, and when Zhang Ba's wax arrived, they falsely said they would borrow it and pay the silver in a few days. Once the wax was in their hands, they held on to it to offset the old debts. It is not that I dared to pocket the client's silver." Master Mei said: "If broker Qiu owes debts, they must collect from him. How can they hold a client's goods to repay his debts? All of you will pay the price to Zhang Ba, and I will spare you punishment." The Jiang shop man, relying on his backer, argued over and over that it had been a straightforward transaction with no offsetting of debts. Master Mei flared up and had him beaten ten strokes; the man still would not stop arguing and was beaten twenty more. Then the others took fright and all said they were willing to pay, begging for mercy. The Jiang man was jailed, and the rest found guarantors. The wax money was assessed with a three-day limit; if it was not paid in full, they would be beaten again. In three days it was indeed collected in full. Ba received the silver and, deeply grateful for Master Mei's kindness, came before the hall wearing an incense burner on his head, kowtowed, and took his leave.

按：出外为商，以缥缈之身，涉寡亲之境，全仗经纪以为耳目。若遇经纪公正，则货物有主；一投狡侩，而抑货亏价必矣。是择经纪，乃经商一大关系也，可不慎哉！如其人言谈直率，此是公正之人。若初会晤间，上下估看，方露微言，则其心中狡猾可知。若价即言而不远，应对迟慢，心必怀欺。若屋宇精致，分外巧样，多是奢华务外之人，内必不能积聚。倘衣补垢腻，人鄙形猥，肩耸目光，巾帽不称寒暑，此皆贫穷之辈。若巧异妆扮，服色变常，必非创置之人，其内必无财钞。若衣冠不华，惟服布衣，此乃老实本分，不可以斯之曰贫。商而知此，何至如张霸，被牙所脱也？况非刚正之梅爷，肯听分上，几乎素手归矣。故录之，以示为商者。当货物发脱之初，细审经纪对手，发落方可保无虞矣。

Note: When you go abroad to trade, a lone drifter in a country with few kin, you rely entirely on the broker as your eyes and ears. If you meet an honest broker, your goods have a master; once you fall in with a cunning one, your goods will be held back and your price cheated — that is certain. So choosing a broker is one of the great matters of trade; how can you not be careful? If a man's talk is straight and frank, he is an honest man. If at the first meeting he sizes you up and down and only then lets slip a few words, you can tell his mind is sly. If he names a price readily and it is not far off the mark, yet his replies are slow and halting, his heart must harbor deceit. If his house is finely built and fancier than it needs to be, he is usually a showy, extravagant man who cannot have saved much inside. If his clothes are patched and greasy, his look mean and his frame shabby, his shoulders hunched and his gaze shifty, his cap and kerchief out of season — these are all poor men. If he dresses in clever, outlandish ways and his colors are out of the ordinary, he is surely not a man who has built anything up, and he has no cash inside. If his cap and gown are plain and he wears only cloth, that is an honest, steady man; you cannot call him poor on that account. If a merchant knows this, how would he end up like Zhang Ba, swindled by a broker? And if the judge had not been the upright Master Mei but one willing to listen to backers, he would very nearly have gone home empty-handed. So I record this to show merchants: at the very start, when your goods are about to be released for sale, look carefully at the broker across from you; only then can you let the goods go and be sure of no mishap.

第七類 引賭騙

Category 7 · Lured into Gambling

危言激人引再赌

Alarming Talk Goads a Man Back to Gambling

张士升，莒溪人，膏粱子弟也。父致万金，均分于士升兄弟，田园膏腴，坐享成业。一旦父卒，时初行万历钱，被棍徒引其赌博。彼富豪雏子，惟见场中饮酒豪放，可轻狂快意，哪知财帛当惜？不数月间，输去银数百两，尚欣欣喜赌，未肯休也。

Zhang Shisheng of Juxi was a pampered son of wealth. His father had amassed ten thousand taels and divided them equally among Shisheng and his brothers; the fields and gardens were fertile, and the sons lived at ease on a ready-made estate. When the father died, just as the Wanli coinage was first coming into circulation, swindlers lured Shisheng into gambling. A greenhorn from a rich house, he saw only the bold drinking and swagger of the gambling rooms, a wild and carefree life that suited him. How would he know that money is to be treasured? Within a few months he had lost several hundred taels of silver, yet he still went on gambling happily, with no thought of stopping.

乡有陈荣一者，乃士升父在日所用做中保供呼唤者。人虽微贱，却有忠义之心，不忍士升之被棍诱引也。乃备一盛筵，单请士升一人，酒筵中慢慢缓谈，将其父在日，始终生财缘由，爱惜钱米实事，一一从头细讲；且赞羡其能，慨叹其苦，后又谈及民情世故，及钱米难得之状，穷民无钱之苦，因劝之曰：“令先尊发此巨富非易，你须念先人勤劳，保守基业，切不可去赌。前者虽赌去数百金，已往勿咎；但从今改过，依旧坐享福泽矣。”士升见荣一词情恳切，一时良心发动，曰：“吾依你言，从今誓不赌也。”次日棍徒引之，果不去赌。众方怪异，后知出于荣一所劝，无可奈何，商议曰：“谁能引其再赌者，众敛十金与之。”有柴昆者曰：“我能引之。”众将银十两封存。

In the village lived a man named Chen Rongyi, whom Shisheng's late father had employed as a middleman and errand runner. Humble as he was, he had a loyal and upright heart, and he could not bear to watch Shisheng being led on by swindlers. So he prepared a fine feast and invited Shisheng alone. Over the wine he talked slowly and at length: from beginning to end he told how the father had built his fortune, and exactly how he had watched over every coin and every grain of rice. He praised the father's ability and sighed over his hardships; then he spoke of the ways of the world, of how hard cash and rice are to come by, and of the misery of the poor who have none. At last he urged him: “It was not easy for your late father to amass such wealth. You must remember your forebears' toil and hold on to the family foundation. Whatever you do, stop gambling. True, you have already lost several hundred taels, but let bygones be bygones. Mend your ways from today, and you can still live at ease on the family fortune.” Shisheng saw that Rongyi's words were earnest, and for a moment his conscience stirred. “I will do as you say,” he said. “From today I swear off gambling.” The next day the swindlers came for him, and sure enough he would not go. The gang was baffled; when they later learned it was Rongyi's doing, there was nothing they could do. They took counsel: “Whoever can get him gambling again, we will pool ten taels for him.” A man named Chai Kun said, “I can do it.” The gang sealed up ten taels of silver as the prize.

昆见士升在路亭闲坐，挨近其身，先闲谈他事，后问曰：“闻汝今收手不赌乎？”士升曰：“然。”昆曰：“赌非好事，今能自知回头，真是豪杰。盛族富家子弟，果有知识高人，我真羡服！只外人都传，是荣一老劝你而止。果是他劝否？”士升曰：“的是得他劝。”柴昆嗟叹曰：“荣一小辈奔走下贱之流，岂是你父兄，岂是你叔伯，何禁止得人？你名门子弟，聪明男子，何待贱人训诲？使路人传你听下贱人主使，皆暗中非笑，谓你无能为。依我所见，还当暂出小赌，过了半月一月，自己收手，人便说你是自不爱赌，非关听下辈命令也。如此方是大丈夫所为，不羞了故家门风。”士升是无识雏子，闻此佞言，心自猜曰：“果是我今若便止，人道是荣一之功。须再去赌一月，然后自止，岂不挺豪杰哉！”随即入场复赌。柴昆暗领众银而去。

Kun found Shisheng sitting idly in a roadside pavilion. He sidled up, chatted of this and that, and then asked, “Is it true you have quit gambling?” Shisheng said it was. Kun said, “Gambling is a bad business. To see that and turn back on your own—that is the mark of a true hero. For the son of a great and wealthy house to show such sense, I honestly admire you. Only—everyone is saying it was old Rongyi who talked you into stopping. Is that really so?” Shisheng said, “It is. It was his advice.” Chai Kun sighed. “Rongyi is a menial, a runner of errands. Is he your father or brother? Is he your uncle? What right has he to forbid you anything? You are the son of a famous family, a clever man—why take instruction from a menial? If word gets around that you take orders from a low fellow, people will laugh at you behind your back and say you have no spine. In my view, you should go back for a little light gambling. After half a month or a month, quit on your own. Then people will say you stopped because you no longer cared for gambling, not because you obeyed a servant's orders. That is how a true man acts, and it brings no shame on your family's name.” Shisheng was a witless greenhorn. Hearing this smooth, false talk, he thought to himself: “True enough—if I stop now, people will call it Rongyi's achievement. Better to gamble another month and then quit on my own. Won't I stand out as a hero then?” He went straight back to the gambling rooms. Chai Kun quietly collected the gang's silver and left.

士升赌了一月，野心复逞。后荣一虽言，亦不见纳，终至于尽赌倾家。皆柴昆一激之也，其祸烈也。

Shisheng gambled for a month, and his reckless appetite came roaring back. After that, though Rongyi spoke to him again, the words no longer got through, and in the end he gambled away the whole family fortune. All of it sprang from Chai Kun's single provocation. Fierce was the ruin it wrought.

按：士升惑柴昆之瞽说，拒荣一之忠言，徒以其人卑微，谓受其谏为耻。不知尧请问下民，舜下询荛，周公走迎乎下士，韩信乞策于左车。彼帝王将相，犹俯听微言若是，岂以人之贱而可废其言之善乎！惜士升黄口之子，目不知古今，故中谗言而不察也。噫！

Note: Shisheng was taken in by Chai Kun's specious nonsense and rejected Rongyi's loyal counsel, all because the man was humble and he thought it shameful to be advised by him. He did not know that Yao put questions to the common people, that Shun sought counsel from woodcutters, that the Duke of Zhou hurried out to welcome men of low rank, that Han Xin begged a plan from Li Zuoche. Emperors, kings, generals, and ministers still bent down to hear humble words like these—how can you throw away good advice because the man who gives it is low? Pity that Shisheng was a green boy who knew nothing of past or present, and so swallowed the slander without ever seeing it for what it was. Alas.

装公子套妓脱赌

A Fake Young Lord and a Courtesan Fleece a Gambler

王荻溪，万金之子。好赌无厌，多被赌朋合谋，尽倾其家。后收拾餘资，只得三百两，乃带一仆，复往县中赌。众棍后合本，迭来与赌。时荻溪家已尽破，而赌已学得甚高，虽未能胜众棍，亦不至为棍所胜。相持半月余，无好子弟到，无雏家可网，乃投府去。更无大赌场可快意者，遂往嫖李细卿家。

Wang Dixi was the son of a family worth ten thousand taels. He loved gambling insatiably, and his gambling friends conspired against him so often that his whole fortune was lost. Later he scraped together what capital remained—barely three hundred taels—took a single servant, and went back to the county town to gamble. The sharps there pooled their funds and took turns at the table with him. By then Dixi's household was ruined, but his skill at gambling had grown very high: he could not beat the pack of sharps, yet they could not beat him either. For over half a month they held each other off, but no new young gentlemen came to town and there were no greenhorns to net. So Dixi went on to the prefectural city; and since no gambling den there was big enough to please him either, he took up with the courtesan Li Xiqing.

有二三赌伙寻至府，闻荻溪已入妓家，众即划计曰：“如此如此笼络之，可尽夺其金矣。”次日，候荻溪出外寻赌伙，即入对细卿曰：“荻溪只好赌，不好嫖，彼无厚物与你。今依我如此如此行，先送你二十两人事。后赌得的，每一百两复许加二抽。”细卿许诺，午设盛馔，方与荻溪入席饮数杯。忽二家人来送礼物，辉煌熳烂，皆上好物件，约值二十餘金。曰：“公子命送此薄仪，少倾便到。”细卿逐一看过，尽数收起，以茶待二家人于外，复来席陪荻溪，且喜且作懊恼之意。荻溪曰：“是何人送你厚礼，你反似犹闷，何故？”细卿曰：“不问正难开口。此是黄公子送的。旧年在此赌钱，输去银千餘两，我亦得他厚惠。今日将到，望相公赦我，妾须出去迎他。容后日多陪相公几日，以补罪。”荻溪曰：“既是公子，我便出外让他。”细卿喜曰：“相公如此宽容，是妾有二天也。”荻溪将拂衣起，细卿挽住曰：“少坐不妨！更有一件，此人极活泼无崖岸。少间乘机提起，若请相见，或在此同话，为我陪客。得借重高贤，亦为我增声价也。”荻溪本欲避席，只闻公子旧在此赌，心中早已喜十分。使一仆伏侍，在内独酌，叫细卿出外迎客。

Two or three of his gambling partners tracked him to the prefectural city. Hearing that Dixi had moved in with a courtesan, they laid their plan: “We will rope him in thus-and-so, and we can take every coin he has.” The next day, waiting until Dixi had gone out to look for a game, they went in and said to Xiqing: “Dixi only cares for gambling, not for women; he has no rich gifts for you. Do as we tell you, thus-and-so. We will send you twenty taels as a gift up front, and afterward, of whatever we win at the table, you shall have an extra twenty in every hundred.” Xiqing agreed. At noon she laid out a rich meal, and she and Dixi had drunk only a few cups when two menservants suddenly arrived bearing gifts—dazzling, splendid things, all first-rate, worth more than twenty taels. “Our young lord sends this trifling token,” they said. “He will be here shortly.” Xiqing looked the gifts over one by one and had them all put away, served tea to the two servants outside, then returned to the table to keep Dixi company—half delighted, half putting on a show of vexation. Dixi said, “Someone sends you rich gifts, and you look put out instead. Why?” Xiqing said, “If you had not asked, I could hardly have brought it up. These are from Young Lord Huang. Last year he gambled here and lost more than a thousand taels, and he was generous to me besides. He is due to arrive today. I beg your pardon, sir—I must go out to welcome him. Let me make it up to you with extra days of company afterward.” Dixi said, “If it is a young lord, I will step out and leave the field to him.” Xiqing said brightly, “With such forbearance, sir, you are a second heaven to me.” Dixi made to rise, but she held him back. “Sit a while longer, it does no harm. One thing more: this man is thoroughly easygoing, with no standoffish airs. In a while, if the chance arises and he asks to meet you, or wants to talk here together, keep him company for me. If you lend me your worthy presence, it raises my standing too.” Dixi had meant to leave the table, but the moment he heard that the young lord used to gamble here, his heart was already ten parts pleased. He kept his one servant to wait on him, drank alone in the inner room, and sent Xiqing out to receive the guest.

须臾，公子到，细卿从容奉茶。叙寒温讫，公子径起，欲入内游玩。细卿慌忙请止，曰：“适有一外亲远来，在内留一水饭，恐无处可避也。”公子笑曰：“孤老便是孤老，何须托外亲也？既是你情人，我生平不吃醋，便请相见何妨？”即遣二仆入请。尚未出，又促细卿曰：“汝去请之。”细卿入内邀出。公子张看荻溪一表非俗，呵呵笑曰：“细卿妙人，果会择好才子。”即降前叙礼，院内备筵已到，公子坐上，荻溪前，细卿左陪。席间谈笑，并不及赌中去。到晚，索骰子行令，公子耍曰：“只恐卑人未晓好色。”细卿曰：“公子有一掷百万之豪，荻卿亦有呼庐赐绯之兴，愧小婢未足当好色耳。”公子曰：“荻溪亦作家乎？明早略赌一东道何如？”荻溪曰：“东道当小弟奉，何劳赌也？”公子曰：“空食未佳，须赢为奇。”先取掷之，无色。荻溪一掷即胜。公子须再加一台戏，又输；热性一起，曰：“荻溪有此妙手乎！与汝再决输赢。”荻溪曰：“不敢扳高耳，亦愿陪。”两下赌起，互有胜负。

Presently the young lord arrived, and Xiqing served tea with an easy grace. When the usual civilities were done, the young lord got straight up, meaning to stroll into the inner rooms. Xiqing hurriedly begged him to stop. “A kinsman from elsewhere has just come a long way,” she said, “and is inside having a simple meal. I am afraid there is nowhere for him to withdraw.” The young lord laughed. “A lover is a lover—why dress him up as an outside kinsman? If he is your man, I have never been the jealous sort. What harm is there in asking him out to meet me?” He sent two servants in to invite him. When they did not come out at once, he pressed Xiqing: “You go and ask him.” Xiqing went in and brought Dixi out. The young lord looked him over—a fine figure, no common man—and laughed, “Xiqing is a woman of taste; she really does know how to pick a fine gentleman.” He came forward and they exchanged courtesies. The banquet ordered for the courtyard had arrived; the young lord took the seat of honor, Dixi sat facing him, and Xiqing sat at the left in attendance. All through the meal they talked and laughed, never once touching on gambling. At nightfall they called for dice to play drinking games. The young lord joked, “I only fear I don't yet know a good ‘color’ when I see one.” Xiqing said, “The young lord has the dash to stake a million on a single cast, and Dixi has the zest to shout for the winning throw and carry off the scarlet. This poor girl is only ashamed she is no great ‘color’ to be fond of.” The young lord said, “So Dixi is a real player too? Tomorrow morning, what say we gamble for a small treat?” Dixi said, “The treat is mine to offer—no need to gamble for it.” The young lord said, “A meal that costs nothing has no savor; it must be won to be worth anything.” He took the dice and threw first—nothing. Dixi won with a single cast. The young lord raised the stake by another play and lost again. His blood was up. “Dixi, what a hand you have! Once more, you and I, to settle win and loss.” Dixi said, “I would not presume to reach so high, but I am game to keep you company.” The two fell to gambling, and the wins went back and forth.

至一更，公子输上百金。细卿亦抽头十餘两矣，即将骰子收起，曰：“今日乘轿劳顿，夜已深矣，须去睡。明日看戏时酒席中再翻，稍抬举我抽头。”公子以输多，发怒要赌。荻溪亦发大言曰：“若再来，须百金一堆，不然且罢！”公子先取定银在，以一百为堆。细卿故执骰不与。公子大怒曰：“只凭一掷，随有无便罢！”细卿付还骰。公子一掷即胜，得百金，曰：“更照前一堆。”又胜；曰：“我生平好大不好细，须二百为堆。”方发性间，忽闻门外火把轿来。慌入报曰：“老爷跟寻至急，可速回去！”公子曰：“我色方来，奈何阻我兴！”其后一掷，又赢二百为堆。家人催如星火，公子曰：“我明日昼间不来，夜定来矣。”荻溪留之，不能得。细卿亦惊作痴呆样，慌忙送别。归怨荻溪曰：“人无全胜。你先赢许多，须当知止。奈何公子欲翻，你更出大堆，是不晓避色也。空作惯家，不及我妇人见矣。”荻溪曰：“吾万金赌尽，何数他三百两！有甚大事，空怨恨为！”在细卿家留宿数日；再留之，坚辞而去。

By the first watch the young lord had lost more than a hundred taels, and Xiqing had taken over ten taels as her cut. She gathered up the dice and said, “You have had a tiring day in the sedan chair, and the night is deep. You should get some sleep. Tomorrow, over the wine at the play, you can win it back, and perhaps show me a little favor with the cut.” The young lord, having lost heavily, flew into a temper and demanded to play on. Dixi too made a big speech: “If we go on, it is a hundred taels a pile, or we stop where we are!” The young lord pulled out ready silver and stacked it a hundred to the pile. Xiqing deliberately held on to the dice and would not hand them over. The young lord flared up: “One throw, that is all—win or lose, we are done!” Xiqing gave the dice back. The young lord won at a single cast and took the hundred taels. “Another pile, same as before,” he said. He won again. “All my life I have liked things large, never small. Make it two hundred a pile.” Just as he was working himself into a fever, torches and a sedan chair were suddenly heard at the gate. A servant rushed in: “His Lordship is out searching for you, most urgently. You must go back at once!” The young lord said, “My luck has just turned—why spoil my run?” His next throw won another two-hundred pile. The servants pressed him as if the house were on fire. “Tomorrow I cannot come by day,” the young lord said, “but at night I will surely be here.” Dixi tried to keep him, without success. Xiqing put on a stunned, witless look of fright and hurried to see him off. Then she came back and rounded on Dixi: “No one wins forever. You had won a great deal at first; you should have known when to stop. When the young lord wanted to win it back, why did you put out even bigger piles? That is not knowing how to duck a hot hand. A fine old hand you are—your judgment does not match a mere woman's.” Dixi said, “I have gambled away ten thousand taels in my time. What is a mere three hundred to me? What is the great matter, that you should fret and fume over it?” He stayed on at Xiqing's house for several days; when she pressed him to stay longer, he firmly declined and left.

按：公子是装来的，先以厚礼送妓，令荻溪信为真公子。后来圈套，皆是装成。其药骰已先藏在细卿手，故令其抢起真骰，然后以药骰付还之，使其不疑。三执皆胜，套定催归，其谁防之？然荻溪虽作家，安能测其弊哉！吁！凡赌博者，弊外生弊，鉴此而知机，收手勿赌，真良策也，莫如彼之一旦尽囊而空矣！

Note: The young lord was an impostor. The rich gifts sent to the courtesan came first, to make Dixi believe he was a real young lord; everything after was staged. The loaded dice had already been hidden in Xiqing's hand: that is why she snatched up the honest dice and then handed back the loaded ones, so he would suspect nothing. Three throws, three wins; then the staged summons that hurried him home—who would guard against that? Yet even Dixi, practiced hand that he was, could not have fathomed such a cheat. In gambling, trick lurks beyond trick. To see the snare in this story, know the moment, and take your hand off the table for good—that is the truly sound plan. Do not end like him, his whole purse emptied in a single day.

好赌反落人术中

A Lover of Gambling Falls into Another's Trap

闽人徐华胜，号含秀。为人矜夸骄亢，酷好赌钱。一日，买纸往京卖。有张鄂号叔真者，先富，因赌倾折，后有餘囊数百两，亦买纸往京卖。二人同县异乡，托处共店。鄂心怀一术，每在店中与华胜着棋，或赌东道，或赌时果。鄂棋本能让华胜一车，鄂乃孤客，徐姓众多，鄂每诈输东道，暗结华之乡亲，使钳其口，无得而议论之。故输五而赢二，而华胜不知鄂怀锋而之。

Xu Huasheng of Fujian, styled Hanxiu, was boastful and overbearing by nature, and he loved gambling fiercely. One day he bought a stock of paper to sell in the capital. There was also a man named Zhang E, styled Shuzhen, who had once been rich, had been ruined by gambling, and still had a few hundred taels left in his purse; he too had bought paper to sell in the capital. The two were from the same county but different townships, and they lodged at the same inn. E had a scheme in his heart. He often played chess with Huasheng at the inn, sometimes betting a treat, sometimes betting seasonal fruit. In truth E's chess was strong enough to give Huasheng a chariot, but E was a lone traveler and the men of the Xu surname were many. So E often lost the treat on purpose, while secretly cultivating Huasheng's fellow townsmen, making them hold their tongues and say nothing about it. He would lose five games and win two, and Huasheng never guessed that E had come with a hidden blade.

一日，鄂已睡，华胜邀起而赌东道。鄂心喜曰：“此夜机会可矣。”故推不起。又强之，鄂曰：“我不赌东道，要赌银。”胜心暗忖：“我棋高他，何惧之有？”连声应曰：“更好。”不知已堕其术矣。且素性亢傲，乡亲咸憎之，大家赞成其赌。鄂曰：“先以银对定，输即收去，无得抵赖。要一两一局，每两与众抽头二钱，作东道并做戏。”胜曰：“虽二两无妨。”未几，输数局。心中热起，说要十两一局。客中老成者曰：“不可，且休矣！”其后生辈反以言激之。胜心益热，再对银十两而赌。不数局，共输数十餘两。时天色已曙，鄂将胜银卷入囊中而起。胜邀再赌，鄂坚执不许，二人相扭甚热。鄂曰：“是你邀我赌，非我邀你赌。我若输你，肯饶否？我家财数千，因赌而倾。你输此些小，何得鼻血？好不为男子也！任你经官不还。”客中老成者曰：“是我辈无见识，不阻你，以致覆败。但张兄说得是，倘他输你，必不放他。不如我辈抽头者尽还你罢。俗云‘豪杰对豪杰，齿打落，吞肚里’。”鄂曰：“众既以抽头者退出，我亦退银三两做戏，更出一两作东道。内抽五钱，换金一线，打一戒指与徐兄，作好赌之戒。”众曰：“张兄之言大有理，乃豪杰惜豪杰，真慷慨丈夫也！”华胜默然。真个是：“安分不贪难诱引，贪心萌内必遭殃。”

One evening E had already gone to bed when Huasheng roused him to gamble for a treat. E was delighted at heart: “Tonight the chance has come.” But he made excuses and would not get up. Huasheng pressed him. E said, “I will not gamble for a treat. If we gamble, it is for silver.” Sheng thought to himself, “My chess is better than his—what have I to fear?” and answered at once, “Even better.” He did not know he had already fallen into the snare. What is more, he was arrogant by nature, and his fellow townsmen all detested him, so the whole company egged the wager on. E said, “First we weigh out the silver face to face; the loser hands it over on the spot, no backing out. One tael a game, and from each tael two qian go to the company as a cut, to pay for a treat and a play.” Sheng said, “Make it two taels for all I care.” Before long he had lost several games. His blood heated, and he called for ten taels a game. The older, steadier guests said, “No—you should stop now.” The younger ones only provoked him with words. Sheng's blood grew hotter; he weighed out another ten taels and played on. Within a few more games he had lost several dozen taels in all. By then the sky was growing light. E swept the winnings into his bag and got up. Sheng begged him to play on; E flatly refused, and the two grappled hotly. E said, “You invited me to gamble, not I you. If I had lost to you, would you have let me off? My family had several thousand taels, and gambling emptied it. You have lost this trifle—why the nosebleed? Some man you are. Take me to the magistrate if you like; I will not pay it back.” The older guests said, “It was we who lacked the sense to stop you, and so you came to ruin. But Brother Zhang is right: if he had lost to you, you would never have let him off. Better that those of us who took the cut give it all back to you. As the saying goes: when hero meets hero, a knocked-out tooth is swallowed and done with.” E said, “Since the company is returning the cut, I too will give back three taels for the play and put out one tael more for a treat. From it, take five qian, exchange it for a thread of gold, and have a ring made for Brother Xu as a caution against gambling—for ‘ring’ and ‘caution’ are one and the same word.” The company all said, “Brother Zhang's words are most reasonable. This is a hero cherishing a hero, a truly generous man.” Huasheng was silent. Truly it is said: keep to your lot and covet nothing, and no one can lure you; let greed sprout within, and ruin is sure to follow.

按：好赌者落人圈套，何止若是。鄂犹是有本富商，故不尽取，而又善处于终，致戒其后。若赌场中光棍，何怕你万贯家财，尽落于伊手乎！睹徐华胜之输而返悔者，后人鉴之，可勿蹈矣。

Note: That gamblers fall into other men's snares is nothing new, and many come off far worse than this. E, after all, was a merchant who had once had capital; that is why he did not take everything, and why he managed the ending well, leaving a warning behind him. Had it been one of the smooth operators who haunt the gambling dens, what would he care that your family fortune ran to ten thousand? Every last coin would have fallen into his hands. Let those who come after look at Xu Huasheng, who lost and repented too late—take warning, and do not tread the same path.

第八類 露財騙

Category 8 · Displaying Wealth

诈称公子盗商银

Posing as a Young Lord to Steal a Merchant’s Silver

陈栋，山东人也，屡年入福建建阳地名长埂贩买机布。万历三十二年季春，同二仆带银壹千馀两，复往长埂买布。途逢一棍，窥其银多，欲谋之。见栋乃老练惯客，每迟行早宿，关防严密，难以动手。诈称福建分巡建南道公子，甚有规模态度，乃带四仆，一路与栋同店。棍不与栋交语，而栋亦不之顾也。

Chen Dong was a Shandong man. For years he had gone to Changgeng, a place in Jianyang, Fujian, to trade in woven cloth. In late spring of the thirty-second year of the Wanli reign, taking two servants and more than a thousand taels of silver, he set out once more for Changgeng to buy cloth. On the road a con man spotted his heavy purse and plotted to take it. But he saw that Dong was a seasoned traveler who started late and stopped early each day and kept strict watch, so there was no opening to strike. The man passed himself off as a son of the Fujian circuit intendant for Jiannan, with all the airs and bearing of the part, and, attended by four servants, stayed at the same inns as Dong the whole way. The con man never spoke to Dong, and Dong paid him no mind.

直至江西铅山县，其县丞姓蔡名渊者，乃广东人也，与巡道同府异县，素不相识。棍往拜之。县丞闻是巡道公子，待之甚厚，即来回拜，送下程。栋见县丞回拜，信其为真公子。是夜，棍以下程请栋。栋欢领之，而心中犹谨防他盗，不敢痛饮；棍犹动手不得。次日，经乌石宿。其地非大口岸，栋欲办酒回礼，以无物可买而止。

They traveled on until they reached Yanshan county in Jiangxi. The assistant magistrate there, Cai Yuan by name, was a Cantonese—from the same prefecture as the circuit intendant but a different county—and the two had never met. The con man went to call on him. Hearing that this was the circuit intendant’s son, the assistant magistrate treated him handsomely, then came in person to return the call and sent provisions for the road. When Dong saw the assistant magistrate return the visit, he believed the man was a genuine young lord. That night the con man invited Dong to share the gift. Dong accepted gladly, but inwardly he still kept careful guard against theft and dared not drink deep, so the con man still found no chance to strike. The next day they stopped for the night at Wushi. It was no major port; Dong wanted to lay on a return feast, but with nothing there to buy, he let the idea drop.

又次日，到崇安县宿。栋心谓：此到长埂旧主不远，犹其外之故家也。且来日与公子别矣，不答敬殊非礼也，遂买肴馔请之。棍谓栋曰：“同舟过江，前缘非偶。与君一路同来，岂非偶乎？明日与君分路，燕鸿南北，未知何日再会，各开怀畅饮。”延至三更，其仆皆困顿熟睡。栋醉甚，亦伏桌睡。棍遂将栋之财物悉偷去。

The day after that they lodged in Chong’an county. Dong thought to himself: From here it is not far to my old host at Changgeng—he is like family away from home. And tomorrow I part from the young lord; not to return his hospitality would be poor manners. So he bought meat and wine and invited the man to dinner. The con man said to Dong: “To cross a river in the same boat is a bond from a former life, not mere chance. You and I have come the whole way together—how is that not fate? Tomorrow we take separate roads, like the swallow and the wild goose, one flying south, one north, and who knows when we shall meet again. Let us each open his heart and drink deep.” They spun the drinking out to the third watch. The servants were all worn out and fast asleep. Dong, very drunk, also fell asleep slumped over the table. The con man then made off with every bit of Dong’s money and goods.

待栋醒觉，不知棍何处去矣。即在崇安县告店家通同作弊。随即往江西广信府，告其县丞勾引光棍，而以原店家作证。县丞诉曰：“福建巡道实与我同府异县，其人姓氏，我素知之；但公子并未会面，他称其姓氏来拜我，我乃县丞小官，安得不回他拜，不送他赆？今至崇安已经数日，盗你银去，与我何干？”栋曰：“那棍一路同来，我防之甚切。他来谒你，而你回拜，我方信是真公子，故堕其术。今其人系你相识，安得不告你？”本府不能判断。栋又在史大巡处告。史爷判是县丞不合错拜公子，轻易便送下程，致误客商，不无公错。谅断银壹百两，与栋作盘缠之资而归。

When Dong awoke, the con man was nowhere to be found. He promptly filed a complaint in Chong’an county accusing the innkeeper of colluding in the swindle, then went on to Guangxin Prefecture in Jiangxi and charged the assistant magistrate with being in league with the sharp, naming the innkeeper as his witness. The assistant magistrate protested: “The Fujian circuit intendant really is from my prefecture, though a different county, and I have long known his name. But I had never met his son. He came to call on me under that name, and I am a mere assistant magistrate—how could I not return his call or send him a parting gift? By the time you reached Chong’an, days had passed. He stole your silver—what has that to do with me?” Dong said: “That crook traveled with me the whole way, and I guarded against him closely. Only when he called on you and you returned the call did I believe he was a true young lord, and so I fell into his trap. Since the man is an acquaintance of yours, how can I not bring the charge against you?” The prefecture could not decide the case. Dong then brought it before Inspector Shi. Master Shi ruled that the assistant magistrate had been wrong to pay respects to a false “young lord” and to send provisions so lightly, thereby misleading a traveling merchant—there was official fault in it. He saw fit to award Dong one hundred taels of silver as travel money for the journey home.

噫：棍之设机巧矣！一路妆作公子，商人犹知防之。至拜县丞，而县丞回拜送赆，孰不以为真公子也？又先设机以请商人，则商人备礼以答敬，亦理所必然也。乃故缠饮，困其主仆，则乘夜行窃易矣。故曰其设机最巧也。使栋更能慎防一夜，则棍奸无所施。故慎始不如慎终，日乾更继以夕惕，斯可万无一失。不然，抱瓮汲井，几至井口而败其瓮，与不慎何异？吾愿为商者，处终如谨始可也。

Note: Ah, how cleverly this swindler laid his trap! All along the road he played the young lord, and the merchant still knew to be on guard. But once he called on the assistant magistrate, and the magistrate returned the call and sent a gift, who would not have taken him for a genuine young lord? And since the trap had been laid by playing host first, the merchant’s return of the courtesy was only natural. Then he deliberately dragged out the drinking to wear down master and servants, and stealing in the night was easy. That is why I call his trap the cleverest kind. Had Dong kept his guard up just one more night, the swindler’s scheme would have had nowhere to bite. So vigilance at the start counts for less than vigilance at the end: diligent by day and watchful by night—only then is nothing left to chance. Otherwise it is like hauling a jar of water up from a well and smashing it just as it reaches the well’s mouth—how is that different from never being careful at all? My wish for merchants is this: be as careful at the end as you were at the beginning.

炫耀衣妆启盗心

Flaunting Fine Clothes Awakens a Thief’s Heart

游天生，徽州府人。丰采俊雅，好装饰。尝同一仆徐丁携本银五百馀两，往建宁府买铁。始到崇安县，搭一青流船，艄公名李雅，水手名翁迓。雅先以嫖赌破家，后无赖而撑船。其时船至建阳县，天生起岸，往拜乡亲。将衣箱打开，取出衣服鲜丽，所带用物俱美。雅一见生心。

You Tiansheng was a man of Huizhou Prefecture, handsome and elegant in bearing, and he loved to dress well. Once, with a single servant, Xu Ding, and more than five hundred taels of capital, he set out for Jianning Prefecture to buy iron. Reaching Chong’an county, he took passage on a Qingliu boat. The boatman was named Li Ya, the deckhand Weng Ya. Ya had ruined his family with whoring and gambling and, left with nothing, took to poling a boat. When the boat reached Jianyang county, Tiansheng went ashore to call on fellow townsmen. He opened his clothes chest and took out bright, handsome garments; everything he carried was fine. The moment Ya saw it, covetousness stirred in his heart.

至晚，天生叫艄公买些酒馔。雅暗将陀陀花入酒中———陀陀花者，乃三年茄花也，人服此则昏迷不能语。是夜，天生主仆中了此毒，醉不能醒。三鼓时候，雅邀水手行谋。水手曰：“钱财有命，不可逆理妄求。倘若事泄，罪将安逃？吾不敢为也。”雅狠心一起，不听水手之阻，将其主仆推入深潭。天生淹死，徐丁幸饮酒少，入水复苏，颇识水性，水上岸。次日，搭后船往建宁府，即抱牌告于王大爷。当差捕兵六名，同徐丁到临江门去缉拿。临江门，乃建宁往来诸船凑集之口岸也。是时李雅谋财在手，正买酒上船，思量作乐。徐丁认得，即引捕兵擒锁，搜其赃物，尚在船中。遂并入赃，俱拿到府。

That evening Tiansheng told the boatman to buy some wine and food. Ya secretly slipped tuotuo blossoms into the wine—the tuotuo is the flower of a three-year eggplant, and whoever takes it falls senseless and cannot speak. That night Tiansheng and his servant took the poison and sank into a stupor from which they could not wake. At the third drum, Ya invited the deckhand to join the plot. The deckhand said: “Wealth is allotted by fate; you cannot chase it against all right. If this gets out, where will you flee from the crime? I will have no part in it.” But a ruthless resolve rose in Ya; he ignored the deckhand’s protest and pushed master and servant into a deep pool. Tiansheng drowned. Xu Ding, luckily, had drunk little; he came to in the water and, being a fair swimmer, made it up the bank. The next day he caught a later boat to Jianning Prefecture and lodged a formal complaint before Master Wang. Six constables were assigned to go with Xu Ding to the Linjiang Gate wharf to make the arrest—Linjiang Gate being the port where all boats to and from Jianning gathered. Just then Li Ya, the stolen money in hand, was buying wine to take aboard, planning to make merry. Xu Ding recognized him and led the constables to seize and chain him; a search turned up the loot, still on the boat. They took him, loot and all, to the prefecture.

王爷审问。雅见事露，难以推托，一概供招，攀及水手同谋。徐丁曰：“我当中毒时，酩酊不能言，如梦中，闻得水手劝阻，不与同谋，已先逃去。今若枉及此人，令后人不肯向善也。”王爷即将李雅责四十板收监，依律拟斩。其行李并原银，差防夫二名，同徐丁直解至天生家去。李雅次年冬季处决。后水手翁迓弃船归农，颇致丰足。雅以谋人而促死，迓以阻谏而全家，谚曰：“善有善报，恶有恶报”，信不虚也。

Master Wang held the hearing. Seeing the affair was out and denial useless, Ya confessed to everything—and dragged in the deckhand as an accomplice. Xu Ding said: “When I was poisoned I was dead drunk and could not speak, as if in a dream; but I heard the deckhand argue against it and refuse to join, and he had already fled. If we wrong this man now, it will make others unwilling to choose the good.” Master Wang had Li Ya beaten forty strokes and thrown in jail, and sentenced him to beheading under the law. The baggage and the original silver were sent under two guards, together with Xu Ding, straight to Tiansheng’s family. Li Ya was executed in the winter of the following year. The deckhand Weng Ya later gave up the boat and went back to farming, and came to live in fair comfort. Li Ya plotted against others and hastened his own death; Weng Ya spoke against it and kept his family whole. As the proverb says, “Good is repaid with good, evil with evil”—truly, it is no empty saying.

按：游天生之召祸，良由衣服华丽，致使贼艄垂涎。大凡孤客搭船，切须提防贼艄谋害。昼宜略睡，夜方易醒。煮菜暖酒，尤防放毒。服宜朴素，勿太炫耀。故老子曰：“良贾深藏若虚。”孔子曰：“以约失之者鲜。”此诚养德之言，抑亦远祸之道也。

Note: You Tiansheng invited his own disaster: it was his gorgeous clothing that made the thieving boatman drool. Any lone traveler taking a boat must be on his guard against a murderous boatman. Nap a little by day, so you can wake easily at night. When your food is cooked and your wine warmed, be doubly watchful for poison. Dress plainly; never show off. So Laozi said: “A good merchant keeps his goods hidden, as if he had nothing.” Confucius said: “Few go wrong through restraint.” These are truly words for cultivating virtue—and a way to keep disaster at bay.

第九類 謀財騙

Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth

商伙财反丧财

A Fellow Merchant Turns Thief and Loses His Own Fortune

张沛，徽州休宁人，大贾也。财本数千两，在瓜州买棉花三百餘担。歙县刘兴，乃孤苦茕民，一向出外肩挑买卖，十餘载未归家，苦积财本七十餘两，亦到此店买棉花。二人同府异县，沛一相见，乡语相同，认为梓里，意气相投，有如兄弟焉。棉花各买毕，同在福建省城陈四店卖，房舍与沛内外。

Zhang Pei of Xiuning in Huizhou was a great merchant, with several thousand taels of capital. At Guazhou he bought more than three hundred piculs of cotton. Liu Xing of Shexian was a poor, lonely man. He had long gone out peddling with a shoulder pole and for more than ten years had not been home. By hard saving he had gathered a little over seventy taels of capital, and he too came to this shop to buy cotton. The two were of the same prefecture but different counties. The moment Pei met him, their home dialect matched; he took him for a man of his own native place. They found each other congenial and became as close as brothers. When each had finished buying his cotton, they sold it together at Chen Si’s shop in the Fujian provincial capital, lodging in adjoining rooms, one inner and one outer.

数日后，兴棉花卖讫。沛者止卖小半，收得银五百餘两。兴见其银，遂起不良念，与本店隔邻孤身一人赵同商议：“我店一客，有银若干。你在南台讨荡船等候，待我拿出来即上船去，随路寻一山庵去躲，与你均分。”赵同许诺。兴佯谓沛曰：“我要同一乡亲到海澄买些南货，今尚未来，要待几日。”一日，有客伙请沛午席，兴将水城挖开，将沛衣箱内银五百餘两，悉偷装在自己行李担内，倩雇一人，说是乡里来催，欲去之速。兴佯曰：“行李收拾已定。奈张兄有人请吃酒，未能辞别。”沛家人曰：“相公一时未归，我代你拜上。”兴即辞主人陈四。陈四亦老练牙人，四顾兴房；兴所挖水城，已将物蔽矣。雇夫佯担海口去，旋即卖踪转南台，来荡船上水口。

A few days later Xing sold all his cotton. Pei had sold only a small part and had taken in more than five hundred taels. When Xing saw that silver, a crooked thought rose in him. He talked it over with Zhao Tong, a man living alone next door to the shop: “A guest in my shop has a good deal of silver. You hire a river boat at Nantai and wait. When I bring it out we board at once, then along the way find a mountain hermitage to hide in, and split it evenly.” Zhao Tong agreed. Xing made an excuse to Pei: “I want to go with a fellow townsman to Haicheng to buy some southern goods. He has not come yet; I will have to wait a few days.” One day a fellow merchant invited Pei to a midday feast. Xing dug through the partition wall, stole all the five hundred-odd taels from Pei’s clothes chest, and packed them into his own carrying loads. He hired a porter, saying a townsman had come to hurry him, so he had to leave at once. Xing pretended: “My luggage is already packed. But Brother Zhang has been invited out to drink, so I could not say goodbye.” Pei’s servant said: “The master is not back yet; I will give him your respects.” Xing then took leave of the host, Chen Si. Chen Si was a seasoned broker; he looked all around Xing’s room, but the hole Xing had dug was already covered with things. The hired porter pretended to head for the seaport, then quickly covered their tracks, turned toward Nantai, and boarded the river boat going up to Shuikou.

及张沛回，陈四曰：“贵乡里已去矣，托我拜上相公。”沛开房门，看衣箱挖一刀痕，遂曰：“遭瘟！”待开看，银悉偷去。四顾，又无踪迹。陈四入兴房细看，见水城挖开，曰：“了事不得，今无奈了。但相公主仆二人，可雇四名夫，直到海澄。我同一大官，更邀七八人，讨一荡船到水口。”于是陈四往上寻船。至半午后，有船下水来者，问曰：“你一路下来，见一荡船载二人，有行李三箱，上去赶得着否？”艄子曰：“有三人，行李三担，在水口上岸去矣。”荡船赶至将晚方到水口，并未见一人来往。少顷间，见二牧童看牛而归，问曰：“前有三人，行李三担，小官见否？”牧童曰：“其三人入上源垅去矣。”问曰：“那山源有甚乡村？”曰：“无。止有一寺，叫做上源寺。”陈四将银五分，雇一牧童引路，径至其寺，时将三鼓矣。陈四曰：“我等叫他开门，他必逃走。我数人分作两半，一半守前门，一半守后门。天明僧必开门，我等一齐拥入，彼不知逃，方可捉得。”众曰：“说得是。”

When Zhang Pei came back, Chen Si said: “Your fellow townsman has already gone. He asked me to give you his respects.” Pei opened the room door, saw a knife-cut on the clothes chest, and said, “Plague take him!” When he opened it, the silver was all gone. He looked all around; there was no trace. Chen Si went into Xing’s room and looked closely, saw the wall dug open, and said: “We cannot let this rest. There is nothing for it now. You two, master and servant, hire four porters and go straight to Haicheng. I will take an officer and round up seven or eight men, hire a river boat, and go to Shuikou.” So Chen Si went upriver looking for a boat. After midday a boat came downstream. He asked: “All the way down, did you see a river boat carrying two men, with three chests of luggage, going up—can we still catch them?” The boatman said: “There were three men and three loads. They went ashore at Shuikou.” The river boat they hired did not reach Shuikou until nearly evening, and they saw no one coming or going. In a little while they saw two herd-boys bringing cattle home, and asked: “There were three men ahead with three loads—did you young fellows see them?” The boys said: “Those three went into Shangyuan Gully.” They asked: “What villages are in that mountain gully?” The boys said: “None. There is only a temple, called Shangyuan Temple.” Chen Si gave five fen of silver and hired one herd-boy as a guide, and they went straight to the temple. By then it was nearly the third watch. Chen Si said: “If we call them to open the door, they will surely run. We will split in two: half watch the front door, half the back. At dawn the monks will open; we rush in together, they will not know how to flee, and then we can catch them.” Everyone said: “That is right.”

及僧开门，众等拥入。和尚惊曰：“众客官哪里来的？”陈四乃道其故，即问：“那三人是甚时候到寺？”僧曰：“到时天色已晚，在那一楼房宿，说他被难，至此逃难。”僧引入，齐拥擒获。见其将沛之银装作一担，自银七十餘两以鼠尾袋装，另藏在身，悉皆搜出。三人跪下求饶：“是我不良，将他银拿来。他者奉还他，我者乞还我。”众等不听他说，将石头乱打半死，行李尽数搬来，三人同系至陈四店内。沛时往海澄，尚未归矣。

When the monks opened the door, the whole party rushed in. A monk, startled, said: “Where have you gentlemen come from?” Chen Si told him why they were there, then asked: “What time did those three reach the temple?” The monk said: “They arrived when it was already dark, and are staying in that loft. They said they had met with trouble and had fled here.” The monk led them in; they rushed together and seized them. They found Pei’s silver packed as one load; Xing’s own seventy-odd taels were in a rat-tail pouch, hidden on his person. All of it was searched out. The three knelt and begged to be spared: “It was our wrongdoing; we took his silver. Give him his back, and please give us ours.” The party would not listen. They beat them half dead with stones, carried all the luggage over, and bound the three back to Chen Si’s shop. Pei had gone toward Haicheng and had not yet returned.

是日，客伙与地方众等，岂止数千人看，兴之廉耻尽丧。后数日，沛归，谓兴曰：“为你这贼，苦我往返海澄一遭。今幸原银仍在，我也不计较你，今后当做好人。若如汝见，定要呈官究治。”兴曰：“须念乡里二字。”曰：“若说乡里，正被乡里误矣！我念前日久与之情，不计较你，你急前去。”兴曰：“我银乞还我。”但兴银却被众等拿去。沛因叫众等“拿还他，我自谢你。”众人曰：“这贼若告官论，命也难保。今不计较，反敢图赖！”众人又欲殴他，沛劝乃止。谓兴曰：“你心不良，反为若此，今反害己，不足恤也。但我自推心，将银五两与你作盘缠。”兴且感且泣，抱头鼠窜而去。

That day fellow merchants and local people—no fewer than several thousand—came to look; Xing’s shame was complete. A few days later Pei returned and said to Xing: “Because of you, you thief, I had a bitter round trip to Haicheng. Luckily the original silver is still here. I will not settle scores with you. From now on be a decent man. If I treated you as you treated me, I would certainly file a charge and have you punished.” Xing said: “You must remember the two words ‘fellow townsman.’” Pei said: “If we speak of fellow townsmen—I was ruined precisely by a fellow townsman! I remember our old days together, so I will not press it. Get out at once.” Xing said: “Please give me my silver back.” But Xing’s silver had been taken by the crowd. Pei then told them, “Give it back to him; I will thank you myself.” The people said: “If this thief were taken to court, even his life would be hard to keep. You do not even press charges, and he still dares to claim it!” They wanted to beat him again; Pei talked them into stopping. He said to Xing: “Your heart was bad, and it came to this; now you have harmed yourself, and you are not worth pity. But I will open my own heart and give you five taels as travel money.” Xing was grateful and in tears; he covered his head and scurried away.

噫！久旱甘雨，他乡故知。客于外者，一见乡里，朝夕与游，即成绸缪之交，有如兄弟者，人之情也。沛之与兴，以同郡乡人，又同兹贸易，与之共店托处，亦处旅者之势然也。何兴之包藏祸心，同室操戈，利其财而盗之？彼之暗渡荡船，自谓得计；岂知天理昭彰。奸盗不容，卒之擒获丛殴，噬脐无及，数十年苦积七十金，一旦失之。图未得之财，丧已获之利，何其愚也！予深有慨焉，故笔之，以为奸贪丧心者戒，而因告商者之宜慎，勿如乡里之为盗者误也。

Note: Ah! Sweet rain after a long drought; an old acquaintance in a strange land. A man sojourning away from home, once he meets a fellow townsman, goes about with him morning and evening and soon forms a close bond, like brothers—that is human feeling. Pei and Xing were men of the same prefecture, in the same trade, sharing lodgings at the same shop; that too is how travelers are placed. How could Xing hide a vicious heart, turn the blade on a man under the same roof, and steal for the sake of the money? He slipped onto a river boat in secret and thought himself clever; he did not know Heaven’s justice is plain. Treachery and theft are not tolerated. In the end he was caught and beaten by the crowd, too late for regret. Seventy taels painfully saved over several decades were lost in a single morning. He schemed for wealth he did not yet have, and lost the profit he already held—how foolish! I am deeply moved, so I write this down as a warning to the treacherous and greedy who have lost their conscience, and to tell merchants they should be careful, and not be taken in by a “fellow townsman” who is in fact a thief.

傲气致讼伤财命

Pride Brings a Lawsuit That Costs Fortune and Life

魏邦材，广东客人，富冠一省。为人骄傲非常，辄夸巨富。出外为商，无人可入其目。一日，在湖州买丝一百担，转往本省去卖，在杭州讨大船，共客商二十餘人同船。因风有阻，在富阳县五七日。其仆屡天早争先炊饭，船中往来，略不如意，辄与众斗口。众皆以伙计相聚日知，况材亢傲而相让之，其仆亦倚主势，日与众忤。在邦材，当抑仆而慰同侪可也；反党其仆，屡出言不逊曰：“你这一起下等下流，哪一个来与我和？”动以千金为言。又曰：“一船之货，我一人可买。”如此言者数次，众皆不堪，大恨之。

Wei Bangcai was a Guangdong merchant, the richest in the province. He was extraordinarily arrogant and always boasted of his great wealth. When he went out to trade, no one was worth a glance. One day he bought a hundred piculs of silk in Huzhou, to take back to his own province to sell. In Hangzhou he hired a large boat; more than twenty merchants shared it. The wind held them up, and they stayed in Fuyang County five to seven days. His servants always rushed to cook first at dawn; as they came and went on the boat, if anything was slightly not to their liking they wrangled with the others. The rest, as fellow merchants thrown together for days and knowing how things stood—and besides because Bangcai was so high and mighty—yielded to them. The servants also leaned on their master’s power and clashed with people daily. Bangcai ought to have checked his servants and soothed his companions; instead he took his servants’ side, and more than once spoke insolently: “You pack of low, inferior types—which of you will come and match me?” He always talked in thousands of taels. He also said: “I could buy this whole boat’s cargo by myself.” He said such things several times. Everyone found it unbearable and hated him deeply.

时有徽州汪逢七，乃巨族显宦世家也。不忿材以财势压人，曰：“世长势短，辄以千金为言。昔石崇之富，岂出公之下哉？而后竟何如也？”材怒其敌己，曰：“船中有长于下流者，有本大于下流者，竟无一言，你敢挺出与我作对？以丝一百担，价值数千金，统与你和。”逢七骂曰：“这下流好不知趣！屡屡无状，真不知死小辈也。我有数千金与你和，叫你无命归故士！”二人争口不休。众皆暗喜汪魏角胜，中心大快。有爱汪者，相劝各自入仓。

At the time there was Wang Fengqi of Huizhou, from a great clan of prominent officials. He would not stand Bangcai using money and power to crush people, and said: “Power in this world does not last, yet you always talk in thousands of taels. In former days, was Shi Chong’s wealth any less than yours? And what became of him later?” Bangcai was angry that someone would match him, and said: “On this boat there are men senior to you lowlifes, and men with more capital than you lowlifes, and they have not said a word. You dare step out and stand against me? I will stake the whole hundred piculs of silk, worth several thousand taels, against you.” Fengqi cursed him: “You low creature, you truly do not know your place! Again and again you behave outrageously—you really are a little wretch who does not know he can die. I have several thousand taels to match you, and I will see you have no life left to return to your native soil!” The two wrangled without stopping. Everyone secretly delighted that Wang and Wei were locking horns, and were greatly pleased at heart. Someone who sided with Wang urged them each to go into their own cabins.

次日，李汉卿背云：“幸得汪兄为对。”材听之，乃骂汉卿而及逢七，语甚不逊，大都材出言极伤众。众不甘而忿恨曰：“一船人却被一人欺！我等歃血为盟，与他定夺！”逢七曰：“众等帮我，等我与他作对，以泄众等恨也。他有丝一百担，众助我打他半死，他必去告状，我搬他丝另藏一处。留一半，方好与他对官，将其底帐灭之。他若告我，众不可星散，坚言证之，即将他丝卖来与他使。俗云‘穿他衫，拜他年’。斗殴之讼，岂比人命重情？”众曰：“说得是。我等皆欲报忿，戒勿漏泄。”布谋已定。逢七乃与材在船中相殴数次，材极受亏，奔告在县，状已准矣。

The next day Li Hanqing said behind Wei’s back: “Luckily we have Brother Wang to stand up to him.” Bangcai heard it, and cursed Hanqing and then Fengqi, in most insolent language; in the main, Bangcai’s words cut the whole company deeply. They would not swallow it, and said in anger: “A whole boat of people bullied by one man! We will smear blood and swear an alliance, and settle it with him!” Fengqi said: “You all help me. Let me stand against him, and vent everyone’s hatred. He has a hundred piculs of silk. Help me beat him half dead; he will certainly go and file a charge. We will move his silk and hide it somewhere else. Leave half, so we can face him in court, and destroy his account books. If he sues me, you must not scatter; hold firm and testify. Then we will sell his silk and use it against him. As the saying goes, wear his shirt to keep his New Year—use his own goods against him. A brawl in court is hardly a homicide case.” Everyone said: “That is right. We all want to pay back the insult. Swear not to leak it.” The plot was set. Fengqi then fought Bangcai several times on the boat; Bangcai got the worst of it, ran to the county to sue, and the plaint was accepted.

逢七将材丝挑去一半藏讫，以材买丝底帐、各处税票悉皆灭矣。自己货发落在牙人张春店内。材上船见丝搬去，乃大与逢七殴；即补状，复告抢丝五十担，以一船客伙、艄公作证。逢七以猪血涂头，令二人抬入衙内，告急救人命事抵。即将银一百两，投本县抽丰官客，系本县霍爷母舅。材将银一百五十两，投本县进士魏贤及春元九位。逢七又将银二百两，亦投此数人。进士魏贤等先见本县为魏，又后催书，言辞支离，两下都不合矣。及审一起干证，艄公齐说：“相殴是实，未见搬丝。”本县判断担丝情捏，只以争殴致讼，俱各不合。材不甘，又赴本道告，批与本府推官陈爷审问。二人俱有分上，依县原审回招。材又奔大巡军门各司道告，及南京刑部告。然文状不离原词，皆因（袭）原断。

Fengqi had half of Bangcai’s silk carried off and hidden, and destroyed Bangcai’s silk-purchase ledgers and the tax receipts from every station. His own goods he lodged at broker Zhang Chun’s shop. When Bangcai went on board and saw the silk gone, he fought Fengqi hard; he filed a supplementary plaint, further charging robbery of fifty piculs of silk, and named a boatful of fellow merchants and the boatman as witnesses. Fengqi smeared pig blood on his head, had two men carry him into the yamen, and filed a countercharge of a life-and-death emergency. He then sent a hundred taels to a hanger-on who lived off official connections—Magistrate Huo’s maternal uncle. Bangcai sent a hundred and fifty taels to the county’s jinshi, Wei Xian, and to nine juren. Fengqi then also sent two hundred taels to the same men. Wei Xian and the others first went to the magistrate on Wei’s behalf; later their urging letters came, the language became disjointed, and they no longer fitted either side. When the whole set of witnesses was examined, the boatman and they all said: “The fighting was real; we did not see silk being moved.” The county judged the claim about piculs of silk to be fabricated; it was only a quarrel that led to a suit; both sides were in the wrong. Bangcai would not accept it, and appealed to the circuit; the case was assigned to Judge Chen of the prefecture. Both men had connections; the report followed the county’s original finding. Bangcai then ran to the grand inspector, the military governor, every circuit office, and even the Nanjing Ministry of Justice. But the papers never left the original wording, so all followed the original judgment.

二人争讼一年许，材前餘丝皆已用尽。材叫一亲兄来帮讼，带银五百餘两，亦多用去。材又患病店中，家中叫一亲叔来看。其人乃忠厚长者，询其来历，始知侄为人亢傲，乃致此也。众客商说出此事，要作和气处息，各出银一百两，收拾官府，内抽五十两，与材作盘费之资而归。材归，自思为商之日，带出许多财物，今空手回家，不胜愤郁；且受合家讪詈，益增呕气。未几发疽，数月而死。

The two men sued each other for about a year. Bangcai’s remaining silk was all spent. He called an elder brother to come help with the suit; the brother brought more than five hundred taels, which were also mostly used up. Bangcai then fell ill at the inn. The family sent a paternal uncle to come see him. The man was an honest, kindly elder. He asked how it had started, and only then learned that the nephew’s arrogant ways had brought this on. The merchants told the whole affair and wanted a peaceful settlement: each side would put up a hundred taels to settle with the officials, and fifty taels would be taken from that for Bangcai as travel money so he could head home. Bangcai headed home, thought of how much goods and money he had taken out in his trading days, and now was returning empty-handed; he could not bear the anger and gloom. He was also mocked and scolded by the whole household, which only added to his sick rage. Before long a carbuncle broke out; in a few months he died.

噫！邦材以巨富自恃，想其待童仆与乡人也。酷虐暴戾，人皆让之，酿成桀骜之性，是亢极而不知返者也。一旦出外为商，井蛙痴子，眼孔不宏，呶呶贯钱，知有己而不知有人，口角无惩，致逢七等忿而布谋，搬丝诘讼。始自挟其财多，可投分上凌人，意谓逢七等皆在其掌股玩弄矣；殊知县、府、道、司、刑部遍告，财本俱空，皆不能胜。斯时也，羝羊角藩，抑郁成疾，悔无及矣。非伊叔见机收拾归家，几郁死于外，作他乡之鬼矣。“谦受益，满招损”，自古记之。故匹夫胜予，无以国骄人。圣人之训，三致意焉。即王公大人，矜骄贾灭，比比皆然，况夫公么之辈乎！即庭闱密迩，傲惰而辟，已为非宜，况处羁旅之地乎！为商者，寄寡亲之境，群异乡之人，刚柔得中，止而丽明，尚恐意外之变，而可以傲临人乎！故曰：和以处众，四海之内皆兄弟；满以自骄，舟中之人皆敌国。商者鉴此，可以自省矣。

Note: Ah! Bangcai relied on being hugely rich. Think how he treated servants and fellow villagers: cruel, violent, overbearing. Everyone yielded to him, and that bred a savage stubbornness; he had reached the extreme of arrogance and did not know how to turn back. Once he went out to trade, he was a frog in a well, a fool with a narrow eye; he rattled on about money, knew himself and not others, and never checked his tongue. That drove Fengqi and the rest to fury and to lay a plot, move the silk, and drag him into court. At first he leaned on having much money, thinking he could buy influence and crush people, and supposed Fengqi and the others were toys in the palm of his hand. He did not know that after appeals through county, prefecture, circuit, the offices, and the Ministry of Justice, his capital would all be gone and he still could not win. By then he was a ram with its horns caught in the hedge—stuck, with no way forward or back; gloom made him ill, and regret came too late. If his uncle had not seen the moment to wrap it up and send him home, he would nearly have died of misery abroad and become a ghost in a strange land. “Humility brings benefit; fullness invites loss”—this has been recorded since ancient times. So even if a commoner outdoes you, do not swagger over others on the strength of rank or state. The Sage’s teaching returns to this again and again. Even princes and great men, time after time, have pride purchase their ruin—how much more so ordinary private men! Even in the inner rooms among close kin, arrogance and slackness that go off the path are already wrong—how much more in a place of sojourn! A merchant lodges in a region with few kin, among people from other parts. Keep hardness and softness in balance, stay composed and hold to clarity, and you still fear an unexpected turn—can you then look down on people with pride? So it is said: treat the many with harmony, and all within the four seas are brothers; swell with pride, and even the people in your boat become an enemy state. Merchants should look at this and examine themselves.

轿抬童生入僻路

Sedan Bearers Lead a Young Candidate onto a Lonely Path

超世材，建阳人也。年方垂髫，往府应茂才之选，未取而归。以行李三担，雇挑费大，乃寄船中，命仆护之。己独于陆路轿行，只一日可归。在路雇轿时，打开银包，取二钱碎银与之。两轿夫从旁看窥，有银一大锭。不行上三十里，扛入山僻路去。超生曰：“我昨从船往府，此陆路虽今日初行，但官路须是往来通途，不当在此偏僻去处。”轿夫曰：“正是此去，望前便大官道矣。”又行，更入山径。超生心悟，即呼曰：“我知此不是大路。你们不过是要银，我身上只一锭银三两。我家富万金，只我一人，便把此三银子送你不妨，何必要起歹意？”二轿夫放下，曰：“如此便把来与我，免你一命。”超生笑解付之，曰：“此何大事，而作此举动？好小器！可送我还大路。”二轿夫不顾，得了银，径从山路奔去。

Chao Shicai was a man of Jianyang. He was still a boy with his hair in tufts. He went to the prefecture for the xiucai selection, was not chosen, and headed home. He had three loads of luggage; hiring porters would cost a great deal, so he left them on a boat and ordered a servant to watch them. He himself went by land in a sedan chair; it was only one day’s trip home. When he hired the chair on the road, he opened his silver packet and took out two qian of broken silver to pay them. The two chair men peeked from the side: there was one large ingot. Before they had gone thirty li, they carried him onto a lonely mountain path. Young Chao said: “Yesterday I went to the prefecture by boat. Though I am on this land road for the first time today, an official road should be a through-route with traffic; we should not be in this out-of-the-way place.” The chair men said: “This is the way; look ahead and you will hit the main official road.” They went on, still further into mountain tracks. Young Chao understood, and called out: “I know this is not the main road. You only want the silver. I have on me only one ingot of three taels. My family is worth ten thousand taels, and I am an only son, so giving you these three taels is no matter—why turn to harm?” The two chair men set him down and said: “Then hand it over, and we will spare your life.” Young Chao smiled, undid it and gave it to them, saying: “What a fuss over so little—how petty! Now take me back to the main road.” The two chair men paid no heed; once they had the silver they ran off through the hills.

超生自还寻大路，行至路边店舍，问此处有某县人开店否？人指示之，即入，对店主曰：“我系超某家，因雇轿夫，被其谋去盘缠银，又不能徒步走路。汝若识我家，托代雇两轿夫，送我到家，加还其工钱。”店主曰：“尊府大家，人皆闻名，我岂不知？”即奉上午饭，命两轿夫送回。归家言被谋之事，及某店归之情，家中大喜，曰：“得不遭毒手，幸矣！三两银何足惜！”因厚款二轿夫，仍专人往谢其店。

Young Chao found his own way back to the main road. He came to a roadside inn and asked whether anyone from his own county kept a shop here. People pointed it out. He went in and said to the innkeeper: “I am of the Chao family. I hired chair men and they plotted and took my travel silver, and I cannot walk the rest of the way. If you know my family, please hire two chair men to send me home; we will pay extra wages.” The innkeeper said: “Yours is a great house; everyone has heard the name. How would I not know?” He served him a midday meal at once and ordered two chair men to take him back. When he reached home he told of the plot and of how that shop had sent him. The family was overjoyed and said: “Lucky you did not fall into their murderous hands! What are three taels worth grieving over?” So they treated the two chair men generously, and still sent someone specially to thank that inn.

按：超生初未晓此路程，但见扛入山僻，即知非是大路。察两人谋害之情，便捐银与之，免遭毒手。不然，命且不保，安能存银？又知寻本乡店主，托雇轿送归，方保泰然无危。此其年虽幼稚，而才智过人远矣。诗曰：“书显官人才，书添君子智。”令超生非读书明理，几何不蹈于陷井？

Note: Young Chao did not know this route at first, but as soon as they carried him into the hills he knew it was not the main road. He saw that the two meant harm, so he gave up the silver and escaped with his life. Otherwise he might not have kept his life—how could the silver remain? He also knew to find an innkeeper from his own county and have chairs hired to send him home, and so came through in safety. Young in years, his resourcefulness far surpassed others. As the verse says: “Books make talented men into officials; books add wisdom to a gentleman.” Had young Chao not read and understood principle, how far would he have been from stepping into the pit?

高抬重价反失利

Holding Out for a High Price, He Loses the Profit

于定志，云南西河县人。为人心贪性执，冒昧于利。一日，买栀子往四川处卖，得银八十餘两。复买当归、川芎，往江西樟树卖，每担止着本脚银二两六钱。到时，归、芎虽缺，然比前价稍落些。牙人代发当归，十两一担；川芎六两一担。定志怒责牙人曰：“前日十二两价，如何减许多？”牙人辩曰：“若到二三担，则可依前价；今到二十餘担，若从前价，何以服行情？公欲重价，凭公发别店卖之，何必怒焉？”定志与牙角口，旁有一客伙张淳者，劝曰：“公货获利三倍，当要见机。倘价若落，未免有失渡无船之悔矣。”定志坚执不听。

Yu Dingzhi was a man of Xihe County in Yunnan. He was greedy at heart and stubborn by nature, rash in the pursuit of profit. One day he bought gardenia, took it to Sichuan to sell, and got more than eighty taels. He then bought danggui and chuanxiong, to sell at Zhangshu in Jiangxi; each picul had cost him only two taels six qian, freight included.
