When he arrived, danggui and chuanxiong were still scarce, but the price had fallen a little from before. The broker sold danggui for him at ten taels a picul, and chuanxiong at six taels a picul. Dingzhi angrily blamed the broker: “The other day the price was twelve taels—why cut so much?” The broker answered: “If two or three piculs had arrived, we could follow the old price. Now more than twenty piculs have come; if we used the old price, how would that match the market? If you want a high price, take it to another shop yourself and sell—why get angry?” Dingzhi wrangled with the broker. A fellow merchant standing by, Zhang Chun, advised: “Your goods already triple the money; you should watch the moment. If the price falls, you will regret missing the ferry when no boat is left.” Dingzhi held firm and would not listen.

数日后，到有当归三四担，牙人发价十两卖讫。淳又劝之曰：“此客已卖十两价耳，公何不卖也？”彼亦不听。后又二客人，有十五担到，牙人发价七两，亦卖讫。过数日，又有十餘担来，止卖四两。定志暗悔无及，众客又背地代他扼腕。定志又坐一月餘，价落货贱，与牙人不合，遂转发到福建建宁府，止卖三两七钱一担，比樟树价又减，更废船脚又多。定志自恨命薄，不当赚钱。人谓其非命薄也，乃心高也；非挫时也，乃过贪也。故笔之，以为嗜利不饱者鉴。

A few days later three or four piculs of danggui arrived; the broker sold them at ten taels and they were gone. Chun advised him again: “That merchant already sold at ten taels—why do you not sell?” He still would not listen. Later two more merchants arrived with fifteen piculs; the broker sold them at seven taels, and they too were gone. After a few more days, another ten-odd piculs came, and sold at only four taels. Dingzhi secretly regretted too late; the other merchants also wrung their wrists for him behind his back. Dingzhi sat another month and more; prices fell and the goods grew cheap. He fell out with the broker, so he shipped the lot on to Jianning Prefecture in Fujian, and sold at only three taels seven qian a picul—lower still than the Zhangshu price, and more boat freight wasted besides. Dingzhi cursed his thin luck, that he was not meant to make money. People said it was not thin luck but a proud heart; not bad timing but too much greed. So I write this down as a mirror for those whose thirst for profit is never filled.

按：商为利而奔驰南北，谁不欲广收多获？特遇时而倍得其利，便可见机发脱。何乃贪黩无厌，至失机会而后扼腕，何益哉？甚矣，贪之为害也！不知凡物贱极征贵，贵极征贱，必无极而不返之理。此阴阳消长之数、造化否泰之机，往往皆然。岂可违时而遂贪心乎？是以从古君子，以不贪为宝。

Note: Merchants race north and south for profit; who does not want a wide take and a big haul? But when the times let you double what you put in, you should see the moment and sell. Why be endlessly greedy, and only wring your wrists after the chance is gone—what good is that? How harmful greed is! They do not know that when a thing is extremely cheap it trends toward dear, and when extremely dear it trends toward cheap; there is no extreme that does not turn back. This is the waxing and waning of yin and yang, the rise and fall in creation’s working—so it always is. How can you go against the time to feed a greedy heart? That is why gentlemen from of old have taken not being greedy as their treasure.

第十類 盜劫騙

Category 10 · Theft and Robbery Swindles

公子租屋劫寡妇

A Young Master Rents a House and Loots a Widow

会城中，每逢科试之年，各府举子到者极多。不论大小房屋，举子俱出重租，暂僦以居。东街王寡妇，其先得丹穴擅利，数世积镪巨万，名闻于人。止生二子，一弱冠，一垂髫。内止一丫头，外用一仆代管家，一小厮供役使，不过五六人家口。其厅堂高敞，房舍深广。其外厢每科租与举子居，常收厚利。

In the provincial capital, every examination year, candidates from the prefectures arrived in great numbers. They paid heavy rents for houses large and small, just to lodge for a short stay. Widow Wang of East Street came of a family that had grown rich from a cinnabar mine; several generations had piled up a fortune of tens of thousands, and her name was widely known. She had only two sons, one just come of age, one still a small boy. Inside there was only one maid; outside, one servant served as steward and a boy ran errands—no more than five or six mouths in the house. The halls were high and spacious, the rooms deep and wide. Each examination year she rented the outer wings to candidates, and often took in a handsome profit.

辛卯七月初，举子纷至。忽有二家仆，冠服齐楚，来择屋居。王管家引其看左右厅房，皆清幽洁净。二家仆曰：“此屋光明宽大，可中公子意。我全租之，不可再租他人。敢问租金多少？”王管家曰：“往年众人共租，租金常二十两。今你一家租，人少，不乱杂，只十五两亦可。”二家仆还十二两，即以现银付讫。一仆出引公子，乘四轿，带四仆并一小厮来。行李五六担，皆精好物件。到即以土仪送家主，又值银二三两。王寡妇曰：“往年举子送人事皆淡薄，今这公子真方家手面。”次日，命管家排大筵席，敬请公子，二子出陪。公子放怀欢饮，二更方散。

Early in the seventh month of a xinmao year, candidates poured in. Suddenly two family servants, neatly and smartly dressed, came to choose rooms. Steward Wang showed them the halls and rooms to left and right; all were quiet, secluded, and clean. The two servants said: “These rooms are bright and spacious; they will suit our young master. We will rent the whole place; you must not rent to anyone else. May we ask how much the rent is?” Steward Wang said: “In past years, when many men shared the rent, it was often twenty taels. Now your one household is renting—fewer people, not a mixed crowd—fifteen taels will do.” The two servants bargained it down to twelve taels and paid the silver on the spot. One servant went out and brought the young master, who came in a four-bearer sedan with four servants and a boy. There were five or six loads of luggage, all fine things. On arrival he sent local gifts to the householder, worth another two or three taels. Widow Wang said: “In past years the candidates’ gifts were always meager; this young master truly has a great house’s style.” The next day she ordered the steward to lay on a large feast, invited the young master with due respect, and had her two sons come out to keep him company. The young master drank freely; they did not break up until the second watch.

又次日，公子遣家仆叫厨子来做酒回席。一席请二幼主，一席送入内堂，与主母饮，叫其丫头边陪。命一小厮入，洒酒侍奉。一小席待两管家者，四仆陪之。各饮至二更，公子曰：“带来的酒开来饮。”少顷暖至，其酒味香甜，又不甚严，极是好饮。公子斟两大杯，奉二子曰：“此酒略爽口，各奉三杯。”二子各领饮。小厮在内，亦斟与主母饮。四仆亦劝两管家饮。二更已尽，赍发厨子去，收拾闭门讫。其后所奉酒内放陀陀花，其药性到，将一家人皆昏倒。假公子并六家仆，将寡妇等绑住，夤夜搜其财物，尽数收拾作五六担。晨钟一鸣，开大门公然挑去，并无人知。

The day after that, the young master sent a servant to fetch a cook and lay on a return feast. One table was for the two young masters of the house; one table was sent into the inner hall for the mistress, with her maid sitting by. He ordered a boy to go in, pour wine, and wait on them. A small table treated the two stewards, with the four servants sitting with them. Each party drank until the second watch. The young master said: “Open the wine we brought and drink that.” In a little while it was warmed and brought; it tasted fragrant and sweet, and was not too strong—excellent drinking. The young master poured two large cups and offered them to the two sons: “This wine is a little easier on the palate; take three cups each.” The two sons each drank. Inside, the boy also poured for the mistress. The four servants also pressed the two stewards to drink. When the second watch was over, they paid off the cook, tidied up, and shut the doors. In the wine served later they had put datura blossoms. When the drug took hold, the whole household collapsed in a stupor. The fake young master and his six servants bound the widow and the rest, searched their goods through the night, and packed everything into five or six loads. At the first stroke of the morning bell they opened the great gate and carried it all out in full view; no one knew.

次日，至午左右，邻居见其门大开，无一人来往，相邀入看，一家人皆被捆倒，如醉未醒。曰：“此必中毒被劫。”急代请医，解去其毒方醒，乃言被假公子租屋，投毒夜动。及寻究之，茫无跳影矣。

The next day, around noon, the neighbors saw the gate wide open and not a soul coming or going. They called to one another and went in to look: the whole household was bound and lying there, as if drunk and not yet awake. They said: “They must have been poisoned and robbed.” They hurriedly called a doctor; only after the poison was drawn off did the family wake, and then they told how a fake young master had rented the rooms, given them a drug, and struck in the night. When people tried to track the robbers, there was not a trace to be found.

按：科举租屋，历科皆然，谁知有大棍行此术？其欲独租，不令租他人，犹是常情；惟初至时送厚人事，主必设席相待，理固然也；旋即回席，又且甚丰，一家婢仆皆有酒，即有意投毒矣。善察者，于送人事时犹是难察；惟一家大小皆有酒席相待，此处宜参透之。彼以客回主席，何必并及内外贵贱人哉？然孀妇女流之辈，二子黄口娃儿；若两管家者，彼能以是而预防之，则棍何得而行劫乎！

Note: Renting rooms for the examinations has been so in every session; who knew a veteran crook would work this trick? Wanting the place to themselves, and not letting it to others, is ordinary enough. Sending generous gifts on first arrival, so that the host must lay on a feast, is only to be expected. Then at once a return feast, and a lavish one, with wine for every maid and servant in the house—that was already the intent to drug them. Even a sharp observer would still find the gifts hard to see through; but when everyone in the household, high and low, is given a feast, that is the place to see through it. He is a guest returning the host’s courtesy—why should that reach everyone inside and out, the mistress and the servants alike? Still, a widow is only a woman, and the two sons are little children; as for the two stewards, had they guarded against this, how could the crooks have carried out the robbery?

诈脱货物劫当铺

Using Sham Cargo to Raid a Pawnshop

县衙边有一大典当铺，贮积货物巨万。人以物件当者，不拘多少，皆能收之。一日，有客人容貌雄伟，径入堂内相拜，屏人语曰：“不敢相瞒，吾是异府人，常做君子生意，屡年积得器物甚多。前月拦得赃官七个柜，多有宝贝器玩。今幸藏到贵县，一时难以变卖。尊府若能收当，愿面估其值，以十分之一先交与我，待你卖后均分其价，每千两各得五百，明年对月来支。”店主曰：“愿借货物一看。”贼曰：“货物极多，共九大柜，外面难以开看。今夜须吩咐守城者勿闭门，待人定后，你雇十八人在船边来抬入宝店，当面看定，估计价值，两相交付。先求些现，餘者明年找完。”店主曰：“可。”夜间吩咐守城者留门，雇十八人往江边扛货。果抬九柜入店。

Beside the county yamen there was a large pawnshop, with goods stored by the tens of thousands. When people brought things to pawn, no matter how much, it could take them all in. One day a man of imposing looks went straight into the hall, made a bow, sent the others out, and said: “I will not hide it from you. I am from another prefecture. I make my living as a highwayman, and over the years I have piled up a great many objects. Last month I intercepted seven chests belonging to a corrupt official; there are many treasures and curios among them. Now luckily I have hidden them in your county, but I cannot sell them all at once. If your house can take them in pawn, I am willing to estimate the value face to face; give me one tenth first. After you sell, we split the price evenly—five hundred of every thousand taels each—and next year in the same month I will come to collect.” The shopkeeper said: “I would like a look at the goods.” The thief said: “There are a great many goods, nine large chests in all; they cannot be opened out in the open. Tonight you must tell the city-gate guards not to shut the gate. After the streets have grown quiet, hire eighteen men to come to the boat and carry them into your shop. We will look them over on the spot, estimate the value, and settle the exchange. I ask some cash first; the rest we will settle next year.” The shopkeeper said: “Agreed.” That night he told the gate guards to leave the gate open, and hired eighteen men to go to the river and carry the goods. Sure enough they carried nine chests into the shop.

赍发扛夫去讫，闭上外门。贼将锁匙将九柜锁都开讫，喝一声曰：“速出来！”每柜二人，各执短刀突出，将店主绑住，曰：“略做声便杀！”十九人争入内，把其男女都绑缚，然后将其铺内货物，尽数收入九柜内。十九人分抬出城，再嘱守城者曰：“可锁门矣。”夤夜扛上船去。半夜后，有渐解开绑者，因出解家人之缚，赶至城门，门已闭矣。问曰：“汝见扛柜者否？”守城人应曰：“扛柜者出城多时矣。”五鼓门开，寻至江边，贼夤夜开船，杳不知去向矣。

They paid off the porters and shut the outer door. The thief took a key and unlocked all nine chests, then shouted: “Come out at once!” Two men from each chest, each with a short blade, burst out, bound the shopkeeper, and said: “Make a sound and we kill you!” Nineteen men rushed inside, bound all the men and women, then packed the shop’s goods into the nine chests. The nineteen split the loads and carried them out of the city, then told the gate guards: “You may lock the gate now.” Through the night they carried them onto a boat. After midnight someone gradually worked his bonds loose, then went and freed the rest of the household. They ran to the city gate; the gate was already shut. They asked: “Did you see the men carrying chests?” The gate guard answered: “The chest-carriers left the city a good while ago.” At the fifth watch the gate opened. They searched as far as the river; the thieves had cast off in the night, and no one knew where they had gone.

按：一人来店，其柜皆系自雇人抬入，谁知防之？但彼既称九柜，何不日间躬到其船，面察其柜内货物，则贼计无所施矣。顾听其夜来，又嘱守城者留门以延之入，致堕贼计，是开门而揖盗也。谅哉，利令智昏矣！

Note: One man came to the shop; the chests were carried in by men he himself hired—who would think to guard against that? But since he claimed nine chests, why not go to the boat yourself in daylight and look at the goods inside? Then the thieves’ scheme would have had nowhere to work. Instead he let them come at night, and even told the gate guards to leave the gate open to let them in, and so fell into the plot: that is opening the door and bowing the robbers in. Truly, profit addles the wits.

京城店中响马贼

A Mounted Highwayman in a Shop in the Capital

董荣，山东人也。往南京廊下邓铺中买丝绸三匹，价银四两四钱。以天平对定，只差银色，讲议未成。忽一人骑白马，戴笼巾，穿青绢双摆，亦来铺买绸。邓店以绸与看。其人将董荣的绸来看：曰：“吾为你二家折衷。”叫董荣再添银二钱，荣意亦肯添。其人接银过手看，一跳上马，加鞭而行，马走如飞。荣忙赶上，过一巷，转一弯，其人与马俱不见踪。无奈，再至邓铺，谓其与棍相套，互争扭打。

Dong Rong was a Shandong man. He went to Deng’s shop in the Langxia corridors of Nanjing to buy three bolts of silk, at a price of four taels four qian. They had already settled the weighing on the balance; they only differed on the purity of the silver, and the deal was not yet made. Suddenly a man riding a white horse, wearing a cage-hat and a dark silk robe, also came to the shop to buy silk. Deng’s shop showed him silk. The man took Dong Rong’s silk to look at and said: “I will split the difference for you two.” He told Dong Rong to add two qian more; Rong was willing to add it. The man took the silver in hand to look, then sprang onto the horse, laid on the whip, and the horse flew. Rong hurried after him, went down an alley, turned a corner—man and horse were both gone. He had no choice but to go back to Deng’s shop, saying the shop was in cahoots with the crook; they wrangled and came to blows.

忽巡街刘御史到，二人皆拦街口告。御史带回衙，拘其左邻右舍来审。邻舍曰：“先是荣入铺买绸，只争银色未成。一棍忽骑马至，亦称买绸，自言为彼二家折衷，叫荣添银。棍把其银入手，一跳上马而去，荣忙赶未见。以故二人争打，告在天台。谅此棍正是响马贼，必非通同店家作弊者。”刘爷曰：“邻佑所证是实，此非店家通同者。但在伊店而遭失脱，合令邓店补还银二两二钱，董荣亦自认二两二钱。”发出依处，彼此无罪。

Suddenly Censor Liu, on street patrol, arrived; both men blocked his way in the street to complain. The censor took them back to his yamen and summoned the neighbors on either side to hear the case. The neighbors said: “First Rong came into the shop to buy silk; they only differed on the silver’s purity and had not closed. A crook suddenly rode up, also claiming to buy silk, said he would split the difference for the two of them, and told Rong to add silver. The crook got the silver in his hand, sprang onto the horse and left; Rong hurried after and saw nothing. That is why the two fought and complained before Your Honor. This crook was surely a mounted highwayman; he cannot have been in league with the shop.” Master Liu said: “The neighbors’ testimony is true; this was not a shop collusion. But the loss happened in his shop, so Deng’s shop should make good two taels two qian, and Dong Rong should also bear two taels two qian himself.” They were sent out under this settlement; neither was found guilty.

按：响马贼尝在林路僻处动夺行旅，飞马而去。今在京城中行此，亦大奇也。且彼衣冠既美，有马在旁，其谁防之？今后上店买物，或有异色人在旁，须当严防，勿使银入人手，是亦老实照管之一策也。

Note: Mounted highwaymen usually strike travelers on lonely forest roads and gallop off. To do this inside the capital is a rare wonder. And he was well dressed, with a horse beside him—who would guard against him? From now on, when you go into a shop to buy, if a strange-looking person is standing by, you must keep a close watch, and not let silver pass into another man’s hand. That too is one plain way of looking after yourself.

第十一類 搶劫騙

Category 11 · Robbery Swindles

私打印记占铺陈

A Secretly Stamped Mark Wins the Bedding

乡有尤刁民者，侮法健讼，渔猎下民。人闻其刁风，莫不畏而远之。一日，往府搭船，已先入船坐。后搭船者群至，萍水相逢，彼此各不相识，船中对坐漫谈。忽讲及按院拿刁民事。内有姓丘后生，不知尤刁民之在船也，与众曰：“闻此时本县唯尤五最刁，凡与人暂处，无不被其骗害者。若得按院除了此人，民亦安生。”尤五心中冷笑，谓“吾与尔何干，既扬我刁，又愿按院除我？此人若不白骗他一场，枉得此刁名也。”见丘生所带铺陈甚好，即取一木印，挨近其毡条白处，私打一印号于中。

In the country there was a man known as You the Shyster, who flouted the law, was a relentless litigant, and preyed on ordinary folk. Word of his shifty ways spread, and everyone feared him and kept their distance. One day he went to the prefectural city by boat and was the first aboard, already seated. The later passengers came crowding in — strangers thrown together by chance, none knowing any of the others — and they sat facing one another in the boat, chatting idly. The talk happened to turn to the touring censor's arrests of litigious troublemakers. Among them was a young man surnamed Qiu, who did not know You the Shyster was on the boat. He said to the group: “I hear that in our county these days You Wu is the worst shyster of all. No one who spends even a short time around him escapes being cheated and hurt. If the touring censor would only get rid of the man, the people could live in peace.” You Wu laughed coldly to himself: “What is it to you, that you trumpet my shiftiness and even wish the censor would be rid of me? If I don't swindle this fellow out of something for nothing, my name as a shyster is wasted on me.” Noticing that young Qiu's bedding roll was a fine one, he took out a wooden seal, edged up to a light patch on the felt wrapping, and secretly stamped a mark on it.

船晚至岸，各收拾自己行李而去。尤刁民尾丘生之后，行至府前，在仆担头把铺陈抢下，曰：“多劳你挑，我自拖去。”丘生来抢，曰：“是我铺陈，你拖何去？”二人互争不开，打入府堂上去。尤曰：“是我物，他强争。”丘亦曰：“是他争我物。”太爷曰：“你两人互争，各有甚记号？”丘曰：“我自买来的，未作记号。”尤曰：“我毡条内打有印记。”当堂开视，尤取衣带中木印对之，果相同。太府曰：“此是尤某之物，丘何得冒争！”将丘打十板，令尤领铺陈去，各赶出府外。丘骂曰：“你这贼是何人，敢如此骗我？后必报之！”尤五曰：“适船间你说尤刁民者，即是我。我与你何干，而终日道我刁？故教训你，刁人是这等做耳！”丘心中方悔：“是我妄称人恶，故致此失也。”

In the evening the boat reached the shore, and each man gathered his luggage and left. You the Shyster trailed along behind young Qiu. When they reached the front of the prefectural offices, he snatched the bedding from the servant's carrying pole, saying: “Much obliged for the carrying — I'll take it from here.” Young Qiu lunged to grab it back: “That's my bedding — where do you think you're taking it?” The two of them grappled, neither giving way, until the quarrel carried them right into the prefectural court. You said: “It's mine — he's trying to force a claim.” Qiu said: “He's seizing my property.” The prefect said: “You both claim it. What identifying mark does each of you have?” Qiu said: “I bought it myself and made no mark.” You said: “I stamped a seal inside the felt.” They opened it up right there in court; You took the wooden seal from his sash and matched it to the mark, and sure enough they were the same. The prefect said: “This is You's property. How dare Qiu falsely claim it!” He had Qiu given ten strokes of the bamboo, ordered You to take the bedding away, and drove them both out of the yamen. Qiu cursed him: “Who are you, you thief, that you dare cheat me like this? I will pay you back for this!” You Wu said: “That ‘You the Shyster’ you were talking about on the boat — that's me. What was it to you, going on all day about how shifty I am? So I taught you a lesson: this is how a shyster operates.” Only then did Qiu feel regret: “I spoke ill of a man recklessly, and so brought this loss on myself.”

按：刁恶者，人谁不憎？但未识其人，勿轻扬其过。彼或从旁听之，必致恨于心。待你有失处，乘其隙而毒之，使人不自知矣。故古人三缄其口而慎其言。庞公遗安之计，但称曰好。彼尤五虽恶，何丘后生背地谈之，而自取尤五白占铺陈？与庞公遗安之计异矣。故孔子恶称人之恶，孟氏惕言人之不善者，皆圣贤教人远怨之道，言不可不慎也。

Note: Who does not hate a vicious shyster? But if you do not know the man, do not lightly broadcast his faults. He may be listening at your elbow, and he will nurse the hatred in his heart. Then, the moment you show a weakness, he will seize the opening and poison you — and you will not even know where it came from. That is why the ancients thrice sealed their mouths and were careful with their words. Lord Pang's way of leaving his descendants peace was simply to speak well of everyone. You Wu was wicked, no doubt; but why did young Qiu gossip about him behind his back and bring on You Wu's brazen seizure of his bedding? That is a far cry from Lord Pang's way of leaving peace behind him. So Confucius hated those who proclaim other people's faults, and Mencius warned against speaking of others' failings — both are the sages' way of teaching us to keep clear of resentment. One cannot be too careful with words.

膏药贴眼抢元宝

A Plaster over the Eyes, an Ingot Snatched

县城有一银匠，家颇殷实，解户领秋粮银，常托其倾煎。一日倾煎元宝，心内尚有系未透处，夜间又煮洗之。其铺门有一大缝，外可窥见其内。一棍买一大膏药，夜间潜往窥之。见其把两元宝洗讫，放于炉边，棍在外作叫痛声，呼曰：“开门！”银匠问曰：“是谁？”棍外答曰：“被赃坯打得重，求你炉边灼一膏药贴之。”银匠开门与入。棍作瘸行状，且手战呼痛，蓬头俯视，以一大膏药在炉边灼开，把两手望银匠当面一贴，即抢一元宝以逃。银匠不胜热痛，急扯下膏药，元宝已被其窃一去矣。急叫：“有贼！”且出门追赶，不知从哪路去。彷徨追过数十步，只得怅恨而归。

In the county town there was a silversmith whose family was fairly well off. The tax forwarders who collected the autumn grain-tax silver often entrusted him with melting it down. One day he was melting silver ingots, and, feeling there were still spots inside that had not fused through, he boiled and washed them again that night. His shop door had a wide crack in it, and from outside one could see in. A crook bought a large medicinal plaster and crept up in the night to peek. He watched the man finish washing two ingots and set them by the furnace. Then the crook, outside, began to groan in pain and cried: “Open the door!” The silversmith asked: “Who is it?” The crook answered from outside: “I've been badly beaten by a thieving brute. I beg you, let me warm a plaster at your furnace so I can put it on.” The silversmith opened the door and let him in. The crook limped along, hands trembling, groaning in pain, his hair a tangled mess, head bowed. He warmed the large plaster at the furnace until it softened, then slapped it with both hands straight onto the silversmith's face, snatched one ingot, and ran. The burning pain was more than the silversmith could stand; he tore the plaster off in a frenzy, but the ingot was already gone with the thief. He shouted “Thief!” and rushed out in pursuit, but had no idea which way the man had gone. After a few dozen paces of chasing at a loss, he could only go back, bitter and heartsick.

按：此棍装痛呼门及炉边灼膏药，情果难察。但元宝重物，须先收藏，然后开门则可无失矣。后人观此，凡有银在身者，皆不可轻容异色人得近旁也。

Note: This crook's act — groaning in pain, begging to be let in, warming a plaster at the furnace — was truly hard to see through. But an ingot is a weighty thing; had he put it away first and only then opened the door, there would have been no loss. Let those who come after take note: anyone carrying silver must never lightly let a suspicious stranger get close.

石灰撒眼以抢银

Lime in the Eyes to Snatch the Silver

孙滔，河南人也。常买绵布，在福建建宁府卖。一夜，在银匠王六店煎银，倾煎已讫，时对二包在桌。二人复在对银，有一盗径入其铺，将石灰撒其目。二人救目不暇，盗即将桌上所包之银拿走。滔拼命赶去，将及，盗乃丢一包于地。滔拾包归，到银铺开视之，则皆铁矣。后竟无迹可捕也。

Sun Tao was a man of Henan. He regularly bought cotton cloth and sold it in Jianning Prefecture in Fujian. One night he was melting silver at the shop of Wang Liu the silversmith. The melting was done, and two packets of silver sat on the table. The two men were still checking the silver when a thief walked straight into the shop and flung lime into their eyes. The two had their hands full saving their eyes, and the thief simply took the packets of silver from the table and walked off. Tao chased after him for dear life. He had nearly caught him when the thief dropped one packet on the ground. Tao picked up the packet and went back; when he opened it at the silversmith's shop, it was all iron. And in the end there was no trail to catch him by.

按：语云：“贼是小人，智过君子。”诚哉是言也！其始入铺，撒灰腌人之目，致人无暇顾其财；追将近身、丢包于地，乃杜赶以脱其身也。此岂贼窥伺之机熟，而慢藏诲盗？然滔之不谨于其素，有以致之矣。鉴此惩噎，是为得之。

Note: As the saying goes, “A thief is a petty man, but his wits outrun a gentleman's.” How true that is. When he first came into the shop, he flung lime to burn their eyes, leaving them no time to look to their money; when the chase had nearly caught up with him, he dropped a packet on the ground — that stopped the pursuit and got him clear. Was this just a case of the thief biding his time to perfection, and of careless keeping inviting theft? Still, Tao's own habitual carelessness had brought it about. Be warned by this, as a man once choked grows careful with his food — that is the lesson to take away.

大解被棍白日抢

Robbed by Crooks in Broad Daylight While Relieving Himself

王亨，南京扬州府人，是本府典吏。二考已满，该上京办事。家贫无措，措借亲朋银十馀两，独往北京，为办事使用。始到京中，在教军场边草坪中大解。方脱下裤，陡被二棍拿住，且骂且剥曰：“你这贼偷我衣物来。”即把其衣服并银一时抢去逃走。待他起来，缚裤赶之，二棍逃已远矣。亨行路日久，力已疲倦，拼死赶他不上，懊恨冲天，只得在会同馆乞借盘缠回家，另作区处。

Wang Heng was a man of Yangzhou Prefecture in the Nanjing region, a clerk in the prefectural office. His two terms of service were complete, and he was due to go up to the capital to be processed for a post. His family was poor and he had no funds, so he scraped together a loan of ten-odd taels of silver from relatives and friends and set out alone for Beijing, to cover the costs of the appointment. He had just arrived in the capital and was relieving himself on the grass by the drill ground. No sooner had he pulled his trousers down than two crooks seized him, cursing him as they pulled at his clothes: “You thief — you stole our clothes!” Then and there they grabbed his clothes together with his silver and ran off. By the time he got up, tied his trousers, and gave chase, the two crooks were already far away. Heng had been on the road for many days and his strength was spent; though he chased for dear life, he could not catch them. Choked with regret and rage, he had no choice but to beg a loan of travel money at the Huitong government hostel and head home, to make other arrangements later.

按：孤客出外，非唯僻处可防劫夺，即大路解手之际，必当以裤脱之，挟在腋下。倘遇光棍若行歹意，则起而逃之亦可，或与之交战亦可。若王亨者，不知提防，而被棍将衣银尽剥一空。斯时也，盘缠无觅，顾何前程？苟非会同馆中同道辈乞借盘缠而归，几为乞丐矣。

Note: A lone traveler away from home must guard against robbery not only in out-of-the-way places. Even when relieving himself on a main road, he should take his trousers right off and keep them tucked under his arm. Then, if some smooth operator means mischief, he can get up and run, or stand and fight. Wang Heng knew no such caution, and the crooks took his clothes and his silver, every last bit of them. At that point, with no travel money to be found, what became of his prospects? Had his fellow clerks at the Huitong Hostel not lent him the money for the journey back, he would have ended up little better than a beggar.

第十二類 在船騙

Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles

船载家人行李逃

Boatmen Abscond with a Servant and the Luggage

倪典史以吏员出身，家实巨富。初受官，将赴新任，在京置买器用什物、珍玩缎匹，色色美丽，装作行李六担。打点俱备，先遣三个家人押往江边搭船，以一家人在船中守护，其二人复归。次日，同倪典史大伙人俱到江边寻船，并不见前船；其守船家人不知载在何去，知被贼艄所拐矣。

Assistant Magistrate Ni had risen from the clerical ranks; his family was in fact extremely wealthy. Newly appointed and about to leave for his post, he bought household goods, curios, and bolts of silk in the capital—every piece fine and beautiful—and packed them as six loads of luggage. When all was ready, he first sent three servants to the riverbank to hire a boat. One stayed on board to watch the luggage; the other two came back. The next day Assistant Magistrate Ni and the rest of the party went to the river to find the boat, and the earlier boat was nowhere to be seen. No one knew where the watching servant had been carried off to; they knew a rogue boatman had made off with them.

倪典史不得已，复入京城，向乡知借觅盘缠，欲往在京衙门告捕船贼。同选乡友阻之曰：“凡讨船须在捕头写定，其舵公有姓名可查，方保稳当。若自向江头讨船，彼此不相识，来历无可查，安得不致失误？且江边常有贼船，舵公伪装商贾，打听某船有好货，多致江中劫掠者，皆是在头查访去。若不识者，误上他船，虽主人亦同被害，何况载走一仆乎！今你赴任有限期，岂能在此久待？船贼又无名姓踪影，虽告何从追捕？不如罢休。”倪典史依劝，复在京中再置切要之物，急往赴任也。此不识写船而致误者，故述为舟行之戒。

Assistant Magistrate Ni had no choice but to go back into the capital and borrow travel money from fellow provincials, intending to file a charge at a capital yamen and have the boat thieves caught. Fellow appointees from his home region stopped him, saying: “Whenever you hire a boat you should book it at the registered wharf, so the helmsman has a name that can be looked up; only then is it safe. If you hire a boat yourself at the river’s edge, neither side knows the other, and there is no background to check—how can you not come to grief? Besides, there are often pirate boats along the river. Their helmsmen disguise themselves as merchants, find out which boat has good cargo, and many then rob it midstream. All of this can be found out by checking at the office first. If you do not know this and board their boat by mistake, even the master himself may be harmed—how much more when they carry off a single servant! You have a deadline to take up your post; how can you wait here long? The boat thieves have no names and leave no trail; even if you file a charge, where would you hunt them? Better drop it.” Assistant Magistrate Ni took the advice, bought the most needful things again in the capital, and hurried off to his post. This is a case of not knowing how to book a boat, and so coming to grief; it is recorded as a warning for those who travel by water.

娶妾在船夜被拐

A Concubine Spirited Off the Boat by Night

扬州有一危棍，以骗局为生。生一女危氏，美貌聪明，年方二八，尚未字人。同帮计棍，青年伶俐，家无父母，危棍因以女招赘为婿。夫妻欢爱，岳婿同心。

In Yangzhou there was a crook named Wei, who lived by running swindles. He had a daughter, Miss Wei, pretty and clever, just sixteen, not yet betrothed. A partner in the same racket, a young sharp named Ji, had no parents at home; Wei therefore took him as a live-in son-in-law. Husband and wife loved each other, and father-in-law and son-in-law were of one mind.

后半年内，无甚生意。适有贾知县新受官赴任，经过扬州，欲娶一妾。危与计私议，欲以女脱嫁之。计许诺，自为媒，往与贾爷议。来看称意，即行聘礼，受银八十两，择日成婚。危与计同对女曰：“今半年无生意，家用穷迫，故以你假嫁与贾知县。其实你夫少年人，何忍舍你？我为父母，止生你一人，何忍舍你去？只不得已，把你为货也。况贾爷年老，他眼下未带长妻来，自然爱惜你。但恐到任后，接长妻到，必然酷虐你，骂詈鞭挞，自是不免。自古道：‘宁作贫人妻，莫作贵人妾’。今暂送你去，不日即登船矣。你夫暗以船随行，其船夜挂一白裤为号。你夜间若可逃，即逃过白裤船来，夫即在接你矣。切莫贪睡，误你夫终身；且你自受苦楚。”计故挽妻衣，涕泣面恳曰：“你肯许归，任你去；若不能逃，吾宁与你同死，决不忍相舍。”危氏亦泣曰：“父母有命，怎的不归？只你要随船候接，不可耽误。”三人商议已定。

For half a year after that there was little business. It happened that Magistrate Jia, newly appointed and on his way to his post, passed through Yangzhou and wanted to take a concubine. Wei and Ji talked it over in private, and decided to palm the daughter off on him in a sham marriage. Ji agreed, acted as matchmaker himself, and went to discuss it with Magistrate Jia. Jia came to look, was pleased, sent betrothal gifts at once, paid eighty taels, and chose a day for the wedding. Wei and Ji together said to the daughter: “We have had no business for half a year; the household is hard pressed, so we are marrying you to Magistrate Jia in name only. In truth your husband is a young man—how could he bear to give you up? We are your parents, and we have only you—how could we bear to let you go? We simply have no choice; we are using you as goods. Besides, Magistrate Jia is old; he has not brought his principal wife with him for now, so of course he will cherish you. But I fear that after he takes up his post and sends for the principal wife, she will surely treat you cruelly; cursing and whipping will be hard to avoid. The old saying goes: ‘Better a poor man’s wife than a rich man’s concubine.’ For now we will send you; in a few days he will board a boat. Your husband will secretly follow in a boat, with a white pair of trousers hung up as a signal. If you can get away at night, cross over to the white-trousers boat; your husband will be there to take you. Do not oversleep and ruin your husband’s whole life; you yourself would also suffer.” Ji deliberately clutched his wife’s clothes, weeping, and pleaded to her face: “If you will promise to come back, I will let you go; if you cannot get away, I would rather die with you—I cannot bear to part.” Miss Wei also wept and said: “When father and mother have given the order, how would I not come back? Only you must follow in a boat and wait to take me; do not miss the time.” The three of them had the plan set.

次日，贾知县遣人迎婚，计为媒送去，贾与危氏在店成亲。又次日，危亦备席待婿，兼为起程。第四日，贾同妻收拾上船，危、计二人送别殷勤。船行一日无恙，次日泊于洲渚。计暗以船随，挨附其旁，挂一白裤于上。危氏同贾夫出船头观玩，见白裤船在旁，知计夫在候矣。夜与贾宿，着意绸缪，尽云雨之欢。贾以暮年新娶，夜夜不虚；况此夜船中又尽兴一次，帖然鼾睡矣。危氏遂密起，爬过有白裤船。计夫早已在候，相见欢甚，正似花再重开月再圆也。夤夜撑船逃回。

The next day Magistrate Jia sent men to receive the bride. Ji as matchmaker saw her off; Jia and Miss Wei consummated the marriage at an inn. The day after that, Wei also laid a feast for his son-in-law, and for the start of the journey as well. On the fourth day Jia and his wife packed and boarded; Wei and Ji saw them off with great care. The boat went one day without incident. The next day it moored at a sandbar. Ji secretly followed in a boat, drew up alongside, and hung a white pair of trousers on it. Miss Wei went out to the bow with husband Jia to look about, saw the white-trousers boat beside them, and knew husband Ji was waiting. That night she slept with Jia, made a point of being tender, and they made love to the full. Jia was old and newly married, and had not missed a night; besides, this night on the boat he spent himself once more, and then slept snoring, dead to the world. Miss Wei then rose in secret and crawled across to the boat with the white trousers. Husband Ji had long been waiting; they were overjoyed to see each other, as if a flower had bloomed twice and the moon were full once more. In the night they poled away and fled home.

次早，贾知县醒来，不见危氏，心甚疑怪。再差一家人，往危老家报。危家惊异，疑是船中乖争，致逼投水，即赴府具状，告苛逼溺命事。家人数日回报，贾知县欲赴任期，不能久待，亦不往诉辨，自径投任去。三年后，入京朝觐，差家人送些少仪物与危老，见其家有一少妇，抱一幼子，宛似危氏，驰归报主。及贾知县打轿往，并不见踪。问昨妇何人，危云：“妻姨之女。”其妻反出来涕泣诘骂，扭问取人，又被骗银十两，方得脱身。此误娶棍女而人财两空，又受尽多少闲气也。

The next morning Magistrate Jia woke and did not see Miss Wei; he was much puzzled and suspicious. He then sent a servant to report it at Old Wei’s house. The Wei family acted startled, and suspected a quarrel on the boat had driven her to throw herself in the water. They went at once to the prefecture and filed a charge of cruelty driving a person to drown. After several days the servant came back to report. Magistrate Jia had a deadline to take up his post and could not wait long; he did not go to plead his side, but went straight to his post. Three years later he went to the capital for the imperial audience, and sent a servant with a few ceremonial gifts to Old Wei. The servant saw that the house had a young woman holding a small child, the very image of Miss Wei, and galloped back to tell his master. When Magistrate Jia went in a sedan, there was no trace of her. He asked who yesterday’s woman was; Wei said: “My wife’s sister’s daughter.” Wei’s wife then came out weeping, questioning and cursing, grabbing him and demanding he produce the person; he was cheated of another ten taels before he could get away. This is a case of wrongly taking a crook’s daughter: he lost both the woman and the money, and swallowed no small amount of idle abuse besides.

按：娶妾于妻岳之家，既在店成亲，又送别登舟，可谓极稳矣。谁知在船后，夜复能逃？故在外娶妾，不唯审择外家，兼亦宜审媒人居止，及靠店家一同核实，方可无失。然大抵不及娶本地人女，为更稳也。

Note: He took a concubine from her father’s house; they consummated the marriage at an inn, and were even seen off onto the boat—one would call it perfectly safe. Who knew that later, on the boat, she could still slip away at night? So when you take a concubine away from home, you should not only look into the girl’s family; you should also look into where the matchmaker lives, and have the innkeeper join in checking the facts—only then will there be no loss. But on the whole it is still safer to take a local girl.

买铜物被艄谋死

Boatmen Murder a Merchant Whose Copper Looked Like Treasure

罗四维，南京凤阳府临淮县人。同仆程三郎，带银一百餘两往松江买梭布，往福建建宁府卖，复往崇安买笋。其年笋少价贵，即将银在此处买走乌铜物，并三夹杯盘诸项铜器，用竹箱盛贮，并行李装作三担。崇安发夫，直到水口陈四店写船。陡遇表亲林子达，亦在此店中。达问：“买甚货物？”维曰：“只买些铜器去，更带杯盘等，欲留家用。”达同牙人陈四，代讨一箭船。舵公赖富二、水手李彩、翁暨得搬其行李上船，甚重，舵公疑是金银，乃起不良心，一上船后，再不搭人。维曰：“我要速去，何如不搭人？”舵公曰：“今将晚矣。明日随搭数人，便开船。”维叫三郎买些酒菜，今晚饮用。舵工与水手三人商议：“今晚错过机会，明日不好动手。”维与仆饮醉熟睡。半夜后，舵公将船移于闲处，三人将他主仆以刀砍死，丢尸于江。打开打看，乃是铜物，止现银壹十五两。富二曰：“我说都是银子，三人一场富贵。原来是这东西！”彩曰：“有这等好货物，也多值银。”富二曰：“发在何处去卖？”彩曰：“何愁无卖处？可安船在一处，沿途发卖，岂无人买？”

Luo Siwei was a man of Linhuai County, Fengyang Prefecture, in the Nanjing region. With his servant Cheng Sanlang he took a little more than a hundred taels, went to Songjiang to buy cotton cloth, sold it in Jianning Prefecture in Fujian, then went on to Chong’an to buy bamboo shoots. That year shoots were scarce and dear, so he used the silver there to buy black copperware to take away, along with laminated cups and plates and other copper pieces. He packed them in bamboo chests, and with the luggage made three loads. From Chong’an he hired porters all the way to Chen Si’s shop at Shuikou to book a boat. There he suddenly met a cousin, Lin Zida, also in this shop. Zida asked: “What goods did you buy?” Siwei said: “Only some copperware to take, and also cups and plates I want to keep for the house.” Zida and the broker Chen Si hired a slim riverboat for him. The helmsman Lai Fuer and the sailors Li Cai and Weng Jide moved his luggage on board; it was very heavy. The helmsman suspected it was gold and silver, and a bad thought rose in him. Once they were on board he would take no more passengers. Siwei said: “I want to leave quickly—why take no one else?” The helmsman said: “It is almost evening now. Tomorrow we will take a few more and then cast off.” Siwei told Sanlang to buy some wine and food for tonight. The helmsman and the two sailors talked it over: “If we miss the chance tonight, tomorrow we cannot act.” Siwei and his servant drank themselves drunk and slept soundly. After midnight the helmsman moved the boat to a quiet spot; the three men cut master and servant to death with knives and threw the bodies in the river. They broke open the loads and looked: it was copperware, and only fifteen taels of ready silver. Fuer said: “I said it was all silver—a fortune for the three of us. And it turns out to be this stuff!” Cai said: “Goods this fine are still worth a good deal of silver.” Fuer said: “Where can we send them to sell?” Cai said: “Why worry about a place to sell? Moor the boat somewhere and sell along the way—will no one buy?”

林子达与四维分袂之后，已三个月矣，始到家中往拜四维。维父曰：“小儿出门，尚未归。”达曰：“差矣！”三月前，我在江西水口，同他在牙人陈四店相会。我与牙人同他去讨船，说他在福建买铜货，以竹箱装作三担，竟归来本处发脱。莫非舵公行歹意乎？”言未毕，父母妻子举家大哭。达曰：“且勿哭，倘在途中发卖也未可知。或舵公行歹意，必以铜物卖各处。试往各店踪迹铜物，问其来历，便见明白。纵铜物无踪，再到水口牙人陈四家，寻舵公问之，必得下落。”维父然之，叫次子罗逵随达去访。

It was already three months after Lin Zida had parted from Siwei when Zida reached home and went to call on Siwei. Siwei’s father said: “My son went out and has not yet come back.” Zida said: “That cannot be right! Three months ago I met him at broker Chen Si’s shop at Shuikou in Jiangxi. I went with the broker to hire a boat for him. He said he had bought copper goods in Fujian, packed in bamboo chests as three loads, and was coming straight home to this place to sell them. Can it be the helmsman meant harm?” Before he had finished, father, mother, wife, and the whole house burst into tears. Zida said: “Do not cry yet. He may still be selling along the way, for all we know. Or if the helmsman meant harm, he would have to sell the copper in various places. Try tracing the copper in the shops, ask where it came from, and the matter will be clear. Even if the copper leaves no trace, go on to broker Chen Si’s at Shuikou, find the helmsman and question him, and you will surely get to the bottom of it.” Siwei’s father agreed, and told his second son Luo Kui to go with Zida to inquire.

访至芜湖县，铺中见其铜物，即问：“此铜物是公自买的，抑或他客贩来发行的？”铺主曰：“三月前，有三个客人来卖者。”达曰：“何处人？”曰：“江西人。”达惊惶曰：“差矣，失手是实。”即同逵径至水口，问陈四曰：“前装表亲货物的舵公，是何处人？”陈四曰：“沿山县人。”达道其故，即同陈四到沿山捕捉。

They inquired as far as Wuhu County. In a shop they saw the copperware, and asked: “Did you buy this copper yourself, or did some other merchant bring it to sell?” The shopkeeper said: “Three months ago three travelers came and sold it.” Zida said: “Men of what place?” He said: “Jiangxi men.” Zida was alarmed: “That is not right—he has truly been lost.” He went with Kui straight to Shuikou and asked Chen Si: “The helmsman who loaded my cousin’s goods—what place was he from?” Chen Si said: “A man of Yanshan County.” Zida told him why, and went with Chen Si to Yanshan to seize them.

斯时，李彩、翁暨得卖得铜器，银入手，各在妓家去嫖。林、陈窥见彩，即躲之。林子达曰：“他在院中取乐，必不便动。我与你往县去告，差捕兵缉拿，恕不漏网。”二人入县告准，陈爷差捕兵六名，同林、陈往院中去捕缉。彩与得二人正与妓笑饮，陈四指与捕兵，俱擒锁之。再到赖富家来，富方出门他适，遇见亦被捉获。三人同拿到官，陈爷审问，将三人夹敲，受苦不过，只得招认。彩曰：“彼时搬箱上船，其重非常。疑是金银，三人方起意谋之，将尸丢落于江。开其箱看，尽是铜物，只得现银一拾五两，悔之无及。铜物沿途卖讫，银已分散。今其事败，是我等自作自受，甘认死罪。”陈爷将三人各打五十板，即拟典刑，赃追与罗逵、林子达领归。二人叩首而去。

At that time Li Cai and Weng Jide had sold the copperware, had the silver in hand, and had each gone to a brothel to spend it. Lin and Chen caught sight of Cai and hid. Lin Zida said: “He is taking his pleasure in the house; he will not be in a hurry to move. You and I will go to the county to file a charge, and have constables sent to seize them, so they cannot slip the net.” The two went into the county; the charge was accepted. Magistrate Chen sent six constables, who went with Lin and Chen to the brothel to arrest them. Cai and Jide were just laughing and drinking with the prostitutes; Chen Si pointed them out to the constables, and both were seized and locked. They then went to Lai Fu’s house; Fu was just going out elsewhere, met them, and was also caught. The three were taken together before the official. Magistrate Chen examined them, put them in the ankle-press and beat them; they could not bear the pain and had to confess. Cai said: “At the time we moved the chests on board they were extraordinarily heavy. We thought it was gold and silver, so the three of us formed the intent to kill them, and threw the bodies in the river. We opened the chests and looked: all copperware, only fifteen taels of ready silver. Regret came too late. The copper was sold along the way; the silver has already been split. Now the affair is out; we brought it on ourselves, and willingly admit a capital crime.” Magistrate Chen had each of the three given fifty strokes, then sentenced them to death; the stolen goods were recovered and given to Luo Kui and Lin Zida to take home. The two kowtowed and left.

按：溪河本险危之地，舵公多蠢暴之徒。若带实银在身，须深藏严防。或带铜器铅锡等物，镇重类银，须明与说之，开与见之，以免其垂涎，方保安全。不然，逐金丸以弹雀，指薏苡为明珠，其不来奸人之睥睨者几希。若维仇之能报，犹幸子达之得其根脚也。使非因写船者以穷其舵公，何以歼罪人而殄厥慝乎？然诛逆何如保躬？死偿何如生还？故出行而带重物者，宜借鉴于斯而慎之密之，其永无失矣。

Note: Streams and rivers are dangerous places by nature, and helmsmen are often coarse, violent men. If you carry ready silver on you, you must hide it deep and watch it strictly. If you carry copper, lead, tin, and the like—heavy things that resemble silver—you must tell them plainly, open the loads and show them, so they will not drool, and only then will you be safe. Otherwise it is like shooting sparrows with gold pellets, or letting coix seeds pass for pearls: few will escape a villain’s greedy eye. As for Siwei’s feud being avenged, it was still luck that Zida got the man’s origin. If they had not used the man who booked the boat to track down the helmsman, how could they have wiped out the guilty and cut off the evil? But punishing the wicked is no match for keeping yourself safe; a death repaid is no match for coming home alive. So those who travel and carry heavy goods should take a lesson from this, be careful and keep things close, and they will never come to grief.

带镜船中引谋害

Mirrors on the Boat Invite a Murder Plot

熊镐，章富人，乃世家子也。力足扼虎，兼习棍棒。尝月夜挟二婢往后园，遇一虎跳墙入，即退入家，各持钢叉、大杖出。虎对面扑来，镐以叉抵，顺放于地，急打一下。虎复再扑，镐又叉放之，再打一下，虎遂回身而去。镐从后赶打，虎为之倒。疾呼二婢曰：“速来助。”二婢各以大杖以鏖之，虎立死杖下。时称之曰“打虎镐四官”。

Xiong Hao of Zhangfu was a son of a hereditary official house. He was strong enough to hold a tiger, and had also practiced staff and club. Once on a moonlit night he took two maids to the back garden and met a tiger that had jumped over the wall. He went back into the house at once; each took a steel fork or a heavy staff and came out. The tiger sprang at them face to face; Hao blocked it with the fork, pinned it to the ground, and struck once. The tiger sprang again; Hao again pinned it with the fork and struck once more; the tiger turned and left. Hao chased it from behind and beat it; the tiger fell. He quickly called the two maids: “Come help at once.” Each maid used a heavy staff and they laid on together; the tiger died under the staves. People at the time called him “Fourth Master Hao the Tiger-Beater.”

后思遍游各胜处，故脱兄云：“将出外买卖。”兄阻之曰：“汝刚而无谋，莫思赚钱，还恐生祸。”镐曰：“老仆满起有力多智，与我同去，何妨？”兄不能阻。镐带百餘金行，曰：“吾出外相机置货，虽不得利，岂折本乎？有谁人欺得我者！”及游浙粤，有货可买者，仆满起曰：“此价甚廉，买归必得利。”镐曰：“吾远到此，未遍览此中景致，若遂置货，安能轻身自由？”仆累禀几次，皆不见听，知其志在浪游，不思利也，后只任之，主饮亦饮，主行亦随。不半年，本去三分之二矣。起复曰：“不归将无盘缠。”镐曰：“本虽少，亦要置些货归，可当远回人事相送者。”又挨两月，到湖州，起又催归。镐曰：“买何物好？”起曰：“笔墨上好。”镐曰：“不在行不会拣择，恐受人亏。亦须更买甚物，与母嫂及我妻者？银本已折，省他辈多口。”起曰：“细缎、镜好。”镐曰：“细缎无多本，不是这般客。不如买十两笔墨，十两镜罢。”起曰：“亦好。”催趱买归，只两小箱。镐曰：“此货甚妙，又简便易带。”

Later he wanted to tour all the famous places, so he told his elder brother, as a pretext: “I am going out to trade.” His brother stopped him: “You are headstrong and have no cunning. Do not think of making money; I fear you will only make trouble.” Hao said: “The old servant Man Qi has strength and much wit; if he goes with me, what harm?” His brother could not stop him. Hao took a little more than a hundred taels and set out, saying: “I will go out and buy goods as the chance comes. Even if I make no profit, how should I lose the capital? Who can take advantage of me?” When they toured Zhejiang and Guangdong, and there were goods worth buying, the servant Man Qi said: “This price is very cheap; if we buy and take it home we will surely profit.” Hao said: “I have come a long way and have not yet seen all the sights here. If I buy goods at once, how can I travel light and free?” The servant reported it several times and was never heard; he knew his master’s mind was on idle touring, not on profit, and afterward only let him be: when the master drank, he drank; when the master went, he followed. In less than half a year two thirds of the capital was gone. Qi said again: “If we do not head home we will have no travel money.” Hao said: “Though the capital is small, we still must buy some goods to take back, to serve as gifts after a long journey.” They delayed another two months and reached Huzhou; Qi again urged him to head home. Hao said: “What should we buy?” Qi said: “Brushes and ink are best.” Hao said: “I am not in that trade and cannot choose; I fear I will be taken advantage of. We also need to buy something for Mother, my sister-in-law, and my wife. The capital is already lost; let us spare them extra talk.” Qi said: “Fine silks and mirrors would be good.” Hao said: “We have not much capital for silks; we are not that kind of merchant. Better buy ten taels’ worth of brushes and ink and ten taels’ worth of mirrors.” Qi said: “That will do too.” They hurried the purchase for the return; there were only two small chests. Hao said: “These goods are excellent, and also light and easy to carry.”

及到江边搭船，舵公见财主威仪，家人齐整，奈何行李只两小箱。及抬入船中，觉箱中镇重，想必尽是银也。故以言动问曰：“客官从何来，亦不多买些货物？”镐以本少，恐客商见轻，故谎言：“吾家兄敝任在湖广，吾从任中归，未买得甚货。”舵公曰：“原来是大舍。”又见家人伏侍恭敬，每呼主为相公，使用皆大手面，不与诸商一类，以此益信为真官舍，船中人皆敬让之。及到岸，诸商都搬起船。舵公独留熊大舍曰：“船中客官多，未能伸敬。念备一杯酒，敬请大舍。”即上岸，多买佳肴美酒，夜间劝饮，甚是殷勤，熊镐宽心放饮。舵公又苦劝家人酒。满起心知其非好意，初诈推不饮，后难禁其劝，亦饮数杯，推醉去睡。熊舍凭舵公劝饮，真醉不醒事。起俟其睡熟，即起对舵公曰：“吾非真醉。今将近家，心中忧闷，吃酒不下耳。此相公酒色之徒。大相公在任中，将几百两银打发他归。在路上嫖用都尽，只带得几把笔，几面镜归，与侄子辈作人事耳。明日大老爷归，必责我不能谏阻。世有此人，见酒如糖，又好夸口，怎么谏他？我试开两箱与你看，其中哪有厘银？”即取锁匙开两箱，惟笔与镜，并无银两。起取两面镜送舵公，曰：“一路来多蒙照顾，各送一镜与你用。”舵公曰：“主物不可擅送人。”起曰：“拿一半去，他也理不得，到家后那晓得数。”复锁住箱，与舵公去睡，起一夜提防。次日上岸，熊曰：“难得舵公如此好意，再偿他银一钱。”

When they hired a boat at the river, the helmsman saw a rich man’s bearing and servants in good order, yet the luggage was only two small chests. When they were lifted into the boat he felt how heavy the chests were, and thought they must be all silver. So he probed with words: “Where have you come from, sir? And you did not buy more goods?” Hao, because his capital was small, feared other merchants would look down on him, so he lied: “My elder brother holds a humble post in Huguang. I am returning from his post and did not buy much.” The helmsman said: “So you are a young gentleman.” He also saw the servants wait on him with respect, always calling their master “young sir,” and that he spent in a grand style, not of a kind with ordinary merchants; for this he believed still more that he was truly a young gentleman of an official house, and everyone on the boat deferred to him. When they reached shore, the other merchants all moved off the boat. The helmsman alone kept Young Gentleman Xiong, saying: “There were many guests on the boat; I could not show you proper respect. I have prepared a cup of wine; please let me invite you.” He went ashore, bought many fine dishes and good wine, pressed them to drink at night, most attentive; Xiong Hao drank with an easy heart. The helmsman also hard-pressed the servant to drink. Man Qi knew in his heart it was not kindly meant. At first he pretended to refuse; later he could not stop the urging, drank several cups, then pretended to be drunk and went to lie down. Young Xiong let the helmsman press him, and was truly drunk and knew nothing. When Qi saw he was sound asleep, he got up and said to the helmsman: “I am not truly drunk. We are near home now; my heart is heavy, and I cannot get the wine down. This young master is a man of wine and women. The elder master, in his post, sent him home with several hundred taels. On the road he spent it all on whoring; he is only taking a few brushes and a few mirrors home as gifts for the nephews. When we get home tomorrow, the old master will surely blame me for not stopping him. There are men like this in the world: they see wine as sugar, and love to boast—how can I check him? Let me open the two chests and show you: is there a speck of silver in them?” He took the key and opened both chests: only brushes and mirrors, no silver at all. Qi took two mirrors and gave them to the helmsman, saying: “All along the way you have looked after us; I give each of you a mirror to use.” The helmsman said: “A master’s things must not be given away.” Qi said: “Take half; he will not be able to make it out, and once we are home he will not know the count.” He locked the chests again, went with the helmsman to sleep, and kept watch all night. The next day they went ashore. Hao said: “It is rare for a helmsman to be so kind; give him another qian of silver.”

及归家，起曰：“可数过镜，勿令有失。”镐检过曰：“更失两面。”起曰：“吾将这两面镜，换你我两颗头归。主人尚未知乎？”镐曰：“你何狂言？”起将船中劝饮事，一一叙之，曰：“彼非欲谋害，将别之人，何如此更费酒馔，若殷勤乎？”镐惊曰：“是也！非尔知事，险丧二命耳！”一家人闻之皆喜，赏满起。

When they reached home, Qi said: “Count the mirrors, so none are missing.” Hao checked and said: “Two are gone.” Qi said: “I traded those two mirrors for your head and mine, to get us home. Does the master still not know?” Hao said: “Why this wild talk?” Qi told the affair of the drinking on the boat, item by item, and said: “If he did not mean to murder us, why would he go to such extra expense on wine and food for people he was about to part from, and be so attentive?” Hao was startled: “So it was! If you had not known how to act, we would nearly have lost two lives!” The whole household heard and was glad, and rewarded Man Qi.

按：镐本膏粱之子，以纵性为快，以夸口为高，哪知世路之险？若非满起心明，轻以二命付鱼腹耳。凡远行者，主若疏满，得一谨密家人，亦大有益。故旅以丧童仆为厉，以得童仆为吉。圣人系旅之义大矣哉。

Note: Hao was a pampered son of a wealthy house; he took indulging his nature as pleasure and boasting as distinction, and did not know how dangerous the world’s roads are. If Man Qi had not had a clear mind, two lives would have been lightly given to the fishes’ bellies. Whenever you travel far, if the master is careless and slack, getting one close, careful servant is also a great benefit. So in the Travel hexagram, losing a servant is an omen of trouble, and gaining a servant is auspicious. How great is the Sage’s teaching on Travel!

行李误挑往别船

Luggage Carried onto the Wrong Boat

陆梦麟，江西进贤人。往福建海澄县买胡椒十餘担，复往芜湖发卖。有一客伙，将硼砂一担对换，餘者以银伐之。次日，叫店家写舵公陈涯四船，直到建宁。诸货都搬入船，只一仆詹兴，挑实落行李一担，跟梦麟同行。途中陡遇一乡亲，动问家中事务，语喇喇不能休。乃命仆先担行李上船，再来此听使用。仆挑往别船去，收在船舱已讫，再来寻主，尚与乡亲谈叙未决。见仆来，即差之别干，始辞乡亲到船。查行李未见，即将家人打骂；又坐舵公偷去，状告本县胡爷，言舵公盗他卖胡椒银一百餘两，以店家祝念九作证。舵公诉：“船中有客商十数伙，哪见他仆挑行李上船？”胡爷拘来审问，同船众商都谈，未曾见挑甚行李。胡爷曰：“船不漏针。别货物都在，独行李有银便会失落？”将舵公敲夹，不认，曰：“是他仆詹兴见囊中有银，自盗去以陷我；或错担别船去，以致有失。小人虽夹死难招！”胡爷又审詹兴曰：“想是你错认别人船为己船，忙中有失，非你背主，好好招来免夹。”詹兴不认，乃夹敲一百。受苦不过，只得招认：“是主人路遇乡亲谈话，我自担上船去，藏入船舱讫，再回听主差唤。及到船，并未见行李。是我一时错认，以致有失。恐主人加罪于我，我故不敢承。望老爷救小人一命。”胡爷将詹兴责三十板，劝梦麟曰：“是你自错。凡出外为商，银物不可离身。当担行李时，须叫詹兴看守，待你到船，然后差别干；纵错上别船，亦不会失。今若此，是你命该失财，岂可以怨仆乎？”各发出免供。

Lu Menglin was a man of Jinxian in Jiangxi. He went to Haicheng County in Fujian, bought more than ten loads of pepper, then went to Wuhu to sell it. A fellow merchant swapped a load of borax; the rest was paid in silver. The next day he had the innkeeper book Helmsman Chen Yasi’s boat, going all the way to Jianning. All the goods were moved onto the boat; only one servant, Zhan Xing, carried a load of the real luggage and followed Menglin. On the way they suddenly met a fellow townsman; they asked about family affairs, and the talk rattled on and would not stop. So he ordered the servant to carry the luggage onto the boat first, then come back here and wait for orders. The servant carried it onto another boat, stowed it in the cabin, then came back to find his master, who was still talking with the townsman and had not finished. When he saw the servant come, he sent him on another errand, then took leave of the townsman and went to the boat. He checked and did not see the luggage, so he beat and cursed the servant; he also pinned the theft on the helmsman, and filed a charge with Magistrate Hu of the county, saying the helmsman had stolen more than a hundred taels of silver from the pepper sales, with innkeeper Zhu Nianjiu as witness. The helmsman stated: “There are a dozen-odd merchants on the boat; who saw his servant carry luggage on board?” Magistrate Hu summoned them to hear the case; all the fellow merchants on the boat said they had not seen any luggage carried on. Magistrate Hu said: “A boat does not lose so much as a needle. The other goods are all here; only the luggage, which had silver, happens to be missing?” He put the helmsman in the press and beat him. The man would not confess, and said: “It was his servant Zhan Xing who saw there was silver in the bundle, stole it himself to trap me; or he carried it onto the wrong boat, and so it was lost. Even if you press this little man to death I will not confess!” Magistrate Hu then examined Zhan Xing: “I think you mistook another man’s boat for your own, and in the hurry something was lost; it was not that you turned on your master. Confess properly and you will be spared the press.” Zhan Xing would not confess, so he was pressed and beaten a hundred blows. He could not bear the pain and had to confess: “My master met a fellow townsman on the road and was talking. I carried it onto a boat myself, stowed it in the cabin, then went back to wait for my master’s orders. When we reached the boat, the luggage was not to be seen. I mistook it for a moment, and so it was lost. I feared my master would add to my punishment, so I dared not admit it. I beg Your Honor to spare this little man’s life.” Magistrate Hu had Zhan Xing given thirty strokes, and advised Menglin: “This was your own mistake. Whenever you go out to trade, silver and goods must not leave your person. When the luggage was to be carried, you should have told Zhan Xing to watch it, and only sent him on other errands after you reached the boat; even if it went onto the wrong boat, it would not have been lost. Now that it has come to this, your fate was to lose the money—how can you resent the servant?” They were each sent out and spared further depositions.

按：货物上船，须不离人看守。要防舵公侵盗，又要得智仆为吉也。故雏仆之挑行节，银物所系，须跟在身边，托在实落，方无所失。若先令挑去，错寄别船，安能无失哉？然麟徒知叙旧之谈，致仆误丧其财而干讼者，何其愚也！诸商鉴此，可为后戒。

Note: When goods go onto a boat, you must not leave them unwatched. You have to guard against the helmsman stealing, and you also need a smart servant if you are to be lucky. So when a greenhorn servant carries the travel bundle—the thing the silver and goods hang on—he must follow at your side, and you must leave it only where it is secure, and then nothing will be lost. If you first tell him to carry it ahead, and it is wrongly left on another boat, how can there be no loss? Yet Menglin only knew how to talk over old times, and caused the servant to lose the money by mistake and drag them into a lawsuit—how foolish! Merchants should look at this and take it as a warning.

脚夫挑走起船货

A Porter Runs Off with Goods Fresh off the Boat

建城溪边，凡客船到岸，众脚夫丛集，求雇担代挑入城。有老成客，必喝退众夫，待船货齐收上岸，都数纪定，然后分作几担，叫几名脚夫，自相识认，乃发入城，急令人跟行其后，方保无失。若雏家到，众脚夫不管物件检齐否，即为收拾上担，及急跟夫去，多致遗物在船中未尽收。

By the creek at Jianning city, whenever a merchant boat reaches shore, porters swarm and beg to be hired to carry loads into the city. An experienced traveler will always shout the crowd of porters back, wait until the boat’s goods are all brought ashore and counted and recorded, then divide them into several loads, call several porters he can recognize, send them into the city, and at once have someone follow behind; only then is there no loss. If a greenhorn arrives, the porters, without waiting to see whether the things have been fully checked, pack them onto the poles at once; and if you hurry after the porters, you often leave things still on the boat not yet brought in.

有侯官县一田秀才，出外作馆。年冬归，得束金四十餘两，衣被物件亦十餘两，共作两大笼。经过建城，欲入拜乡亲，命一脚夫挑笼先行。田乃儒家，从后缓步随之。脚夫见其来迟，一步紧一步，趱入城门；人闹攘处，更是疾行，遂挑入曲巷逃走。田从后虽叫“止步”，哪能止得！入城曲巷多歧，何处可寻？次日，往府吕巡捕呈之。

There was a licentiate named Tian of Houguan County who had gone out to tutor. At year’s end he was heading home; he had more than forty taels of teaching silver, and clothes and bedding worth another ten-odd taels, packed as two large baskets. Passing Jianning, he wanted to go in and call on a fellow townsman, and ordered a porter to carry the baskets ahead. Tian was a Confucian scholar, and followed slowly behind. The porter, seeing him come late, walked faster and faster, hurried in at the city gate; in the crowded press he went still quicker, then carried the load into a winding alley and fled. Though Tian called from behind, “Stop!” how could he stop him? Inside the city the winding alleys forked many ways—where could one search? The next day he presented the matter to Prefectural Constable Lü.

吕捕衙是精明官，以脚扶拐物，须用脚夫查之。即叫二差人来：“你认定这田相公，今午后穿白长衫在船中。行李到，必有脚夫挑走，你二人从后密跟，到他家拿来。”再对秀才曰：“你今再讨假行李一担，在十里外搭船来府，照前日到岸时，叫脚夫来挑。你穿白长衫去，此两差人易认。若已在旁，你故意缓行，任此脚夫挑去，必能拿得前脚夫来。”秀才未会其意。

Constable Lü was a sharp official. To catch a porter who had made off with goods, you must use porters to find him. He at once called two runners: “You mark this Licentiate Tian. This afternoon he will wear a white long gown on a boat. When luggage arrives, a porter will surely run off with it; you two follow secretly behind and seize him at his house.” He then said to the licentiate: “Today get a dummy load of luggage, board a boat ten li out, and come to the prefecture. When you reach shore as you did the other day, call a porter to carry it. Wear a white long gown so these two runners can know you. If they are already beside you, walk slowly on purpose and let this porter carry it off; they will surely catch the earlier porter.” The licentiate did not yet see his meaning.

即日下午，备行李从十里外搭船到，见此两美人在旁，各相认得，故叫脚夫挑行李，从后缓随。脚夫果然挑走。二公差迤跟到家拿住，曰：“吕爷叫你。”脚夫黄三不知来历，只得随往。吕爷曰：“你缘何挑走秀才行李？”黄三惊曰：“只暂寄我家，便欲送还。”官止喝：“打五板！”田秀才方到衙。吕爷叮咛黄三曰：“今日饶你罪。这田相公昨日被脚夫挑走一担笼，限你两日代根究来；若寻不出，定坐你赔。”黄三曰：“河下挑夫，两日换一班。昨日不是我辈。”吕爷曰：“你即根究昨日的。”

That same afternoon he prepared luggage, boarded a boat ten li out, and arrived. He saw the two runners beside him; each knew the other, so he called a porter to carry the luggage and followed slowly behind. The porter indeed ran off with it. The two runners followed at a distance to his house and seized him, saying: “Constable Lü wants you.” The porter, Huang San, did not know why, and had to go along. Constable Lü said: “Why did you run off with the licentiate’s luggage?” Huang San, startled, said: “I was only going to leave it at my house for a while, then send it back.” The official merely shouted, “Give him five strokes!” Licentiate Tian then reached the yamen. Constable Lü instructed Huang San: “Today I spare you. Yesterday a porter ran off with one load of this Tian’s baskets. I give you two days to track him down; if you cannot find him, you will certainly be made to pay.” Huang San said: “The porters on the river change shifts every two days. Yesterday was not our shift.” Constable Lü said: “Then you track down yesterday’s men.”

黄三密访两日，不能得。第三日，公差来拿。到半途，见一脚夫柳五，将银三钱换钱用，随即买鱼肉等归家。黄三再拿到衙，禀曰：“并访不得。只今遇柳五换钱，多买鱼肉，事有可疑。”捕衙立差四人，同田秀才、黄三直往柳五家搜。只一间小房，搜果见赃。拿到捕衙，柳五供曰：“银物现在。前五日未敢出门，今日止用银三钱换钱，买物作欢。拐盗是实。”捕衙发打二十板，曰：“你二脚夫拐资客货，各该拟徒。但黄三捕出柳五，以此赎罪，再打十板释放，以惩后日。姑念柳五穷汉，只拟不应罪，纳完发放。”再叫田秀才具领状来，尽将原物领去。不数日，拿得真赃正犯，非有治才，安能如此哉！

Huang San inquired in secret for two days and could not find him. On the third day runners came to seize him. Halfway, he saw a porter, Liu Wu, changing three qian of silver into cash, then at once buying fish, meat, and such to take home. Huang San had him seized as well and taken to the yamen, and reported: “I could find nothing. Only just now I met Liu Wu changing money and buying a good deal of fish and meat; the matter looks suspicious.” The constable at once sent four men, who went with Licentiate Tian and Huang San straight to Liu Wu’s house to search. There was only one small room; they searched and indeed found the loot. They took him to the constable’s yamen. Liu Wu stated: “The silver and goods are here. For the past five days I dared not go out. Today I only used three qian of silver to change money and buy things to make merry. The theft is real.” The constable ordered twenty strokes, and said: “You two porters made off with a traveler’s goods; each of you ought to be sentenced to penal servitude. But Huang San caught Liu Wu, so that redeems his crime; give him ten more strokes and release him, as a warning for later days. I will be lenient to Liu Wu as a poor fellow: sentence him only for doing what he ought not, and when the fine is paid let him go.” He then told Licentiate Tian to file a receipt, and all the original goods were given back to him. In a few days they had caught the true thief with the true loot. Without a talent for governing, how could it have been done like this?

按：脚夫挑走货物，处处有之，故出行最宜慎防。若吕捕衙之发奸得捕盗不遗余力者，全在以脚夫查脚夫一着。所谓“以蛮夷攻蛮夷”是也。又谚云“贼拿贼，针挑刺”，亦此意也。雇夫者可以为戒，捕盗者可以为法。

Note: Porters running off with goods happen everywhere, so when you travel you should most carefully guard against it. As for Constable Lü’s exposing the fraud and catching the thief with no effort spared, the whole thing lay in that one move of using a porter to find a porter. This is what is meant by “using barbarians to fight barbarians.” The proverb also says, “Set a thief to catch a thief; use a needle to pick out a splinter”—the same idea. Those who hire porters may take it as a warning; those who catch thieves may take it as a method.

第十三類 詩詞騙

Category 13 · Swindles by Verse

伪装道士骗盐使

Posing as Daoists to Swindle the Salt Inspector

唐寅，字伯虎，又字子畏，南京吴趋里人也。中弘治戊午南京解元，因事被黜之。后遂放浪不羁，流留花酒。善诗文，画极工。与文征明、文征仲、祝希哲等为友，皆极一时之名流也。日游平康妓家，滑稽为乐，随口成文。有一皂隶，携纸一张求画。伯虎援笔，画螺蛳十餘个，题诗于上云：“不是蝤蛑不是蛏，海味之中少此名。千呼万呼呼不出，只待人来打窟臀。”众皆大笑。偶一日出，见县前枷一和尚。众人请曰：“可将此和尚作诗一首。”伯虎询知和尚被枷缘由，援笔题于枷上曰：“皂隶官差去采茶，不要纹银只要赊。县里捉来三十板，方盘托出大西瓜。”知县送客出来见之，问是何人所作，或以伯虎对，即将和尚释之。其捷于口才，大约类此。

Tang Yin, courtesy name Bohu, also Ziwei, was a man of Wuqu Lane in Nanjing. He placed first in the Nanjing provincial examination in the wuwu year of the Hongzhi reign (1498), then lost his degree over a scandal. After that he lived unbridled, lingering among flowers and wine. He was skilled at poetry and prose, and his painting was especially fine. He was friends with Wen Zhengming, Wen Zhengzhong, Zhu Xizhe, and others—all famous men of that generation. He spent his days in the pleasure houses, amusing himself with jokes and composing off the cuff. A yamen runner once brought a sheet of paper and asked for a painting. Bohu took up the brush, painted a dozen or so snails, and wrote on it: “Not a swimming crab, not a razor clam; among seafood this one barely has a name. Call a thousand times, ten thousand times, it will not come out; it only waits for someone to smack its rump.” Everyone laughed hard. One day he went out and saw a monk in a cangue in front of the county yamen. People asked him to make a poem on the monk. Bohu asked why the monk had been cangued, then wrote on the cangue: “A runner, sent on official business, went to pick tea; he wanted no fine silver, only credit. The county seized him for thirty strokes; on a square tray they served up a big watermelon.” The magistrate, seeing guests out, noticed it, asked who had written it, was told it was Bohu, and promptly released the monk. His ready tongue was generally of this kind.

一日，与祝希哲等十数辈，携装游维扬，日与妓者饮酒，声色为乐。将及一月，资用殆尽。希哲曰：“黄金用尽，作何计策乎？”伯虎曰：“无妨！当今盐使者，资财巨万。我和你二人，可假扮女贞观道士以化之。”二人即扮道士。值盐使者升堂，二人俯伏阶下，云：“女贞观道士参见。”盐使者大怒曰：“岂不闻御史台风霜凛凛耶？是何道者，敢此无状！”将挞之。二人徐对曰：“明公以小道为游方觅食者耶？小道遍游天下，所交者皆极海内名流。即如吴邑唐伯虎、文征明、祝希哲辈，无不与小道折节为友。凡诗词歌赋，应口辄成。明公如不信，愿奏薄技，惟明公所命。”盐使者乃指堂下石牛为题，命二人联诗一首。伯虎应声即吟云：“嵯峨怪石倚云边，”哲云：“抛掷于今定几年。”虎云：“台藓作毛因雨长，”哲云：“藤萝穿鼻任风牵。”虎云：“从来不食溪边草，”哲云：“自古难耕陇上田。”虎云：“怪杀牧童鞭不起，”哲云：“笛声斜挂夕阳烟。”

One day, with Zhu Xizhe and a dozen others, he packed up and traveled to Yangzhou. Day after day they drank with courtesans and took their pleasure in music and beauty. After nearly a month their money was almost gone. Xizhe said, “The gold is spent—what’s the plan?” Bohu said, “No matter. The salt inspector these days is worth tens of thousands. You and I can pose as Daoist priests of Nüzhen Abbey and solicit a donation from him.” The two dressed as Daoists. When the salt inspector held court they prostrated themselves at the foot of the steps and said, “Daoist priests of Nüzhen Abbey present themselves.” The salt inspector was furious: “Have you not heard that a censor’s court is severe as frost? What kind of Daoists dare this insolence!” He was about to have them beaten. The two answered calmly: “Does Your Honor take us for wandering priests out begging meals? We have traveled the whole empire; those we keep company with are all the most famous men in the land. Even Tang Bohu, Wen Zhengming, and Zhu Xizhe of Wu county set aside their rank to befriend us. Poetry, lyrics, songs, rhapsodies—we finish them the moment we open our mouths. If Your Honor does not believe us, we offer a modest sample; command us as you please.” The salt inspector pointed to a stone ox below the hall as the topic and ordered them to compose a linked verse. Bohu answered at once: “A towering strange rock leans against the clouds.” Zhe: “Thrown down—who can tell how many years by now.” Hu: “Moss on the terrace makes its hair, grown by the rain.” Zhe: “Vines thread its nose, left for the wind to tug.” Hu: “It has never eaten grass beside the stream.” Zhe: “From of old it could never plow the ridge fields.” Hu: “No wonder the herdboy’s whip cannot raise it.” Zhe: “The sound of his flute hangs aslant in the sunset haze.”

盐使者览毕，霁色问曰：“诗则佳矣。将欲何为？”二人曰：“顷者女贞观圮坏。闻明公宽仁好施，愿捐俸金修葺，以成胜事，亦且不朽。”盐使者大悦，即檄吴兴二县，可给库银五百与之。二人见盐使者应允，速夜赴吴兴，假为道士说关节行状，对吴兴二县云：“今有盐使者修葺女贞观，此系盛举，可依数与之，不可宽缓。”吴兴二县果如数与之。二人得银大悦，曰：“不将万丈深潭计，安得骊龙项下珠？”复往维扬，聚交游十数辈，于妓者家欢呼剧饮，纵其所乐。不十数日，五百之金费用殆尽。后盐使者按临吴兴，束衣冠往女贞观，则见其倾圯如故。召吴兴二县责之，二县对曰：“日前唐伯虎与祝希哲从维扬来，极称明公兴此盛举，小知县即依数与之矣。”盐使者怅然，知为二人所骗，但惜其才，故亦不究。

The salt inspector finished reading, his expression cleared, and he asked, “The poem is fine. What do you want?” The two said, “Nüzhen Abbey has lately fallen into ruin. We hear Your Honor is generous and fond of giving. If you would give from your salary to repair it, it would complete a worthy deed, and the merit would not fade.” The salt inspector was delighted and issued a dispatch to the two counties of Wuxing: they were to issue five hundred taels from the treasury to them. Seeing that he had agreed, the two hurried that very night to Wuxing, and, still posing as Daoists, they cited their official backing and told the two counties: “The salt inspector is repairing Nüzhen Abbey. This is a grand undertaking. Give the sum as ordered; do not delay.” The two counties paid it in full. The two men, delighted with the silver, said, “If we had not risked the ten-thousand-fathom pool, how would we have got the pearl under the black dragon’s chin?” They went back to Yangzhou, gathered their dozen companions, shouted and drank in the courtesans’ houses, and did as they pleased. In fewer than ten days the five hundred taels were gone. Later the salt inspector came through Wuxing on tour, put on his robes and cap, and went to Nüzhen Abbey—only to find it still in ruins as before. He summoned the two magistrates and rebuked them. They answered, “The other day Tang Bohu and Zhu Xizhe came from Yangzhou and praised Your Honor for raising this grand work; we magistrates paid the sum as ordered.” The salt inspector was dismayed. He knew he had been taken in by the two men, but he valued their talent, so he did not pursue it.

按：唐伯虎、祝希哲，皆海内一时名家也。但以不得志于时，遂纵于声色，青楼酒肆中，无不闻其名。然非口若悬河，才高倚马，何以能倾动使院？此之骗，可谓骗之善矣。独计当今冠进贤而坐虎皮者，咸思削民脂以润私橐，敛众怨以肥身家，其所以骗民者何如！乃一旦反为唐、祝所骗，亦可为贪墨者一儆。但其知而不究，亦可谓有怜才之心者矣。

Note: Tang Bohu and Zhu Xizhe were both famous masters of their generation throughout the land. Blocked in their careers, they gave themselves over to music and beauty; there was no brothel or tavern that had not heard their names. Yet without tongues as fluent as a river in flood, and talent ready to compose at a moment’s notice, how could they have shaken the inspector’s yamen? This swindle may be called a swindle well done. Think of the men who wear official caps and sit on tiger skins today: they all think of scraping the people’s fat to grease their private purses, gathering the crowd’s resentment to fatten their own households—and how they cheat the people! Then one day they themselves are taken in by Tang and Zhu, which may serve as a warning to the greedy. That the inspector knew and still did not pursue it may also be called a heart that pities talent.

陈全遗计嫖名妓

Chen Quan’s Scheme to Sleep with a Famous Courtesan

金陵陈全者，百万巨富也。其为人风流潇洒，尤善滑稽。凡见一物，能速成口号。尝与本地院妓往来，唯一妓最得意。夏间瓜初出，院妓将瓜皮二片，放于门限内，诈令一人慌忙叫全云：“某姐姐偶得危病，要你一相见方瞑目。”全即乘马速至，慌忙进门，脚踹瓜皮跌倒。众妓鼓掌大笑云：“陈官人快做一口号，不得迟！”全即答曰：“陈全走得忙，院子安排定。只因两块皮，几丧我的命。”众妓欣然，遂会饮而罢。

Chen Quan of Jinling was a man of enormous wealth. He was dashing and easy in his ways, and especially good at jokes. Whatever he saw, he could turn into an improvised verse at once. He used to keep company with the local courtesans; one of them was his particular favorite. In summer, when the first melons came in, the house put two pieces of melon rind just inside the threshold and had someone run to Quan in a panic: “Sister So-and-so has suddenly taken a mortal illness; she wants to see you once before she can close her eyes.” Quan mounted at once and rode hard. He rushed in, stepped on the rind, and fell. The courtesans clapped and laughed: “Master Chen, make a verse at once—no delay!” Quan answered: “Chen Quan came in a rush; the house had it all arranged. Just because of two pieces of rind, I nearly lost my life.” The women were delighted; they drank together and left it at that.

又一日，与众妓游湖，见新造一船。众妓云：“速作一口号，勿迟！”全即答曰：“新造船儿一只，当初拟采红莲。于今反作渡头船，来往千千万万。有钱接他上渡，无钱丢在一边。上湿下漏未曾干，隔岸郎君又唤。”众妓皆欢然叹服。凡游戏口号，类如此。

Another day he was out on the lake with several courtesans and saw a newly built boat. They said, “Make a verse at once—don’t lag!” Quan answered: “A brand-new little boat; at first it was meant to pick red lotuses. Now it is a ferry at the crossing, coming and going by the thousands. Those with money it takes aboard; those without it tosses aside. Wet above, leaking below, never dry—and from the far bank another gentleman is calling.” The courtesans all laughed and admired him. His joking verses were generally of this kind.

彼时，浙江杭州有一名妓，号花不如。姿态甚佳，且琴棋诗画，无不通晓。但身价颇高，不与庸俗往来，唯与豪俊交接。每宿一夜，费银六七两方得。全闻之，欲嫖此妓，因而骗之。故令十餘家丁，陆续运船到杭，彼与二三家丁先往。到花不如家，即令家人扛抬皮箱一个———下面俱系纸包砖石，上面一重，俱是纸包真银，每十两为一封———入花不如卧房内，当面开箱取银，赏赐妓家诸役甚厚，奉不如白金十两。不如与众役俱大欢喜，以为此大财主也，所得必不资矣。不如问曰：“客官贵处？”全曰：“金陵。”又问曰：“高姓？”答曰：“姓浪。”又问曰：“尊号？”答曰：“子遂。”不如整盛席相款。子遂不去，只在彼家。过两日，又一家人来报云：“某号船已到。”子遂云：“餘货只放船内，但扛抬皮箱进姐姐家来。”如是者三四次，皮箱有五六个在不如卧房内矣。子遂见不如带珠，云：“你这珠俱不好。我有大珠数百颗，个个俱圆，候此号船到，我去取与你。”

At that time Hangzhou in Zhejiang had a famous courtesan called Hua Buru. She was a great beauty, and she was versed in the qin, chess, poetry, and painting. But her price was high. She did not mix with ordinary men, only with the rich and distinguished. A night with her cost six or seven taels. Quan heard of her and wanted to sleep with her, so he set out to cheat her. He had a dozen or more household men send boats on to Hangzhou one after another, while he and two or three servants went first. At Hua Buru’s house he had a leather chest carried into her bedroom—the bottom all paper packets of brick and stone, only the top layer paper packets of real silver, ten taels to a packet. In front of everyone he opened the chest, took out silver, tipped the house servants very generously, and presented Buru with ten taels of silver. Buru and the servants were all delighted, thinking this was a great rich man and that what they would get would be beyond counting. Buru asked, “Where is the gentleman from?” Quan said, “Jinling.” “Your surname?” “Lang.” “Your style name?” “Zisui.” Buru laid out a rich feast. Zisui did not leave; he stayed in her house. After two days another servant came to report: “Boat number such-and-such has arrived.” Zisui said, “Leave the rest of the goods on the boat; just carry the leather chests into Sister’s house.” This happened three or four times, until five or six chests were in Buru’s bedroom. Zisui saw Buru wearing pearls and said, “These pearls of yours are all poor stuff. I have several hundred large pearls, every one of them round. When this next boat arrives I will fetch them for you.”

将及月餘，子遂欲心已足。有一家人来报云：“某号船到。”子遂对不如言曰：“此号船不比前船，俱是实落宝货，须我自去一看，兼取大珠与你。其皮箱数个，安顿在你卧房，你须照管，我午后方能进来。但叫你家丁一人，并头口一个，同我去。”不如遂令一人跟随，并驴仔一个，与子遂同去。

After more than a month Zisui had had his fill. A servant came to report: “Boat number such-and-such has arrived.” Zisui said to Buru, “This boat is not like the earlier ones. It is all genuine treasure. I must go look myself, and fetch the large pearls for you as well. The several leather chests are in your bedroom; you must watch them. I cannot come back until afternoon. Just send one of your men and one mount with me.” Buru sent a man and a donkey to go with Zisui.

行至半路，子遂慌忙言曰：“我钥匙一把，安放在你姐姐房内，一时起身，未及带来，你要去取来。”其人即回取。子遂云：“且止！要有我亲手字去，你姐姐方肯把钥匙交付与你。不然，取不来。”子遂乃下驴入纸店，写一口号云：“杭州花不如，接着金台浪子遂，着了人，赔了驴。从今别后，哪得明珠？”封识与那人回。不如开封视之，知被骗矣。忙开皮箱一看，俱系砖石。子遂预令家人买舟俟候，一到河边，即上舟回京。后不如细访，亦知是南京陈全，然已无如之何矣。

Halfway there Zisui said in a hurry, “I left a key in your sister’s room. I set out in a rush and did not bring it. You must go back and fetch it.” The man turned to go. Zisui said, “Wait! You will need a note in my own hand, or your sister will not hand the key over to you. Otherwise you will not get it.” Zisui got off the donkey, went into a paper shop, and wrote a verse: “Hangzhou’s Hua Buru took in Jintai’s Lang Zisui; she fell for the man and lost a donkey. From this parting on, where will she get bright pearls?” He sealed it and sent the man back. Buru opened it, saw she had been cheated, and hurriedly opened the chests—all brick and stone. Zisui had already told his men to hire a boat and wait; as soon as he reached the river he boarded and headed back to the capital. Later Buru inquired carefully and learned it was Chen Quan of Nanjing, but there was nothing she could do.

按：妓家尝是骗人，轻者丧家，重者丧身，未尝有被人骗者；况花不如高抬身价，选拣孤老，其骗人财尤难计算。岂知有陈全之术，又有神出鬼没者乎！赔人赔驴，悔无及矣。此虽陈全之不羁，亦足供笼络院之一笑云。

Note: Brothels are used to cheating people. The lighter cases ruin a household; the heavier ones cost a life. One never hears of them being cheated themselves. Hua Buru, moreover, put her price high and picked her patrons; the wealth she took from men is past counting. Who would have thought Chen Quan had a method still more uncanny—here one moment, gone the next? She lost a man and a donkey, and regret came too late. This was Chen Quan’s lack of restraint, yet it is also good for a laugh at how the pleasure houses that ensnare others can themselves be played.

第十四類 假銀騙

Category 14 · Counterfeit-Silver Swindles

设假元宝骗乡农

Planting Fake Ingots to Swindle a Country Farmer

昔有一人，本农家者流也。辛苦耕田，服食淡薄，而性甚悭吝，家颇充裕。外地有骗棍到此地方，知这乡农性贪识惘，遂探其某日当在某处耕田，预将假元宝二个重一百两，埋藏其处。俟乡农正在力耕之时，贼棍故意在其山畔，作左寻右寻状。乡农问曰：“你这人在此寻甚么？”棍云：“我在此寻些东西，你问我则甚？”乡农只得默然。棍又认此树，复认彼树，如有所失状。乡农又曰：“你这人好笑，只管满山认树何为？”棍曰：“实不相瞒，我先父往岁曾被流贼所劫，亦同入伙。后来银子甚多，孤身难带，将银埋在各处，留下一帐登记，欲再来取，不幸死矣。今我依帐来寻此处树下的，不知哪个树是。幸遇你在此，可来助我寻。若寻得，分些与你不妨。”乡农遂带锄同寻。果在一树下，寻得元宝二个。棍佯作喜甚之态，说：“此若寻得，则他处皆可寻了。我实肯分些与你，只是此处无槌凿。”又曰：“此银我无贮藏所在，不如去你家下，代我寻完，分数个元宝谢你，尊意如何？”乡农云：“甚好。”但我与你素不相识，一旦至家下来往，岂不招人疑猜？”棍云：“当诈称是何亲故方好。”乡农云：“有了。我有一妻舅，六七岁时曾卖外江客人，至今并无下落。只认作我妻舅，回来看取姊姊、姊夫，有何不可？”遂将妻父、妻母姓名形状，一一对棍说讫。

There was once a man, a farmer by birth. He worked his fields hard, ate and dressed simply, and was terribly tight with money, so his household was fairly well off. A con man from out of town came to this district, and learning that the farmer was greedy and dull-witted, he found out which day the farmer would be plowing a certain field and buried two fake yuanbao ingots there ahead of time, a hundred taels in weight. While the farmer was hard at his plowing, the crook deliberately wandered the hillside nearby, pretending to search left and right. The farmer asked, “What are you looking for here?” The con man said, “I am looking for something. What is it to you?” The farmer had to keep quiet. The con man then studied one tree, then another, like a man who had lost something. The farmer spoke again: “You are a strange one—what do you mean by going all over the hill checking trees?” The con man said, “I will not hide it from you. Years ago my late father was captured by roving bandits and joined their gang. Later he came into so much silver that he could not carry it alone, so he buried it in various places and left an account book recording the spots, meaning to come back for it—but he died, poor man. Now I have come with the book to find the one under a tree here, and I cannot tell which tree it is. Lucky for me you are here. Come help me look; if we find it, I do not mind sharing some with you.” The farmer took his hoe and searched with him. Sure enough, under one tree they found two yuanbao. The con man feigned great delight and said, “If we have found this one, all the other spots can be found. I am truly willing to share it with you—only we have no hammer or chisel here.” He went on, “I have nowhere to keep this silver. Why not go to your house? Help me finish the search, and I will thank you with several ingots. What do you say?” The farmer said, “Fine with me. But you and I have never met. If you suddenly start coming and going at my house, will people not grow suspicious?” The con man said, “We should say I am some relative or old friend.” The farmer said, “I have it. I had a brother-in-law, my wife’s brother, who was sold at six or seven to a merchant from outside the province, and nothing has been heard of him since. We will pass you off as my wife’s brother, come back to see his sister and her husband. Why not?” And he told the con man, one by one, the names and looks of his wife’s father and mother.

遂领至家下，叫妻子出来见舅。其妻相见，问：“弟弟面貌，如何与我不相类？”棍应云：“弟出外省，那边风水不同，以此不类。”其妻又问云：“我父何名，形状何如？母何名，形状何如？”其棍对言不差。又问：“我叔何名，形状何如？”棍应曰：“我小时出去，只记得父母，记不得叔了。”妻遂信之。杀鸡烹鲜，设为盛馔，以待其弟。乡农兄弟诸人，各设席相待甚厚。棍对姊夫曰：“我要些零碎银用，可在你家取过十五六两，与我杂用。”乡农遂取己真银十餘两，与棍用。

He led him home and called his wife out to meet her “brother.” When she saw him she asked, “Why does my brother’s face look nothing like mine?” The con man answered, “I went away to another province; the water and soil there are different, so I do not resemble you.” She asked again, “What was my father’s name, and what did he look like? My mother’s name, and her looks?” The con man answered without a mistake. She asked, “And my uncle—his name and looks?” He answered, “I left home as a small child; I remember only my parents, not my uncle.” The wife believed him. She killed a chicken and cooked fresh fish, laying out a fine feast for her “brother.” The farmer’s brothers and the rest of the family each gave him a generous meal in turn. The con man said to his “brother-in-law,” “I need some loose silver for spending. Let me take fifteen or sixteen taels from your house for my sundry expenses.” The farmer then took out more than ten taels of his own genuine silver and gave it to the con man.

过数日，棍将帐与姊夫查，更有元宝十餘个，在某山某庵中。其庵无人居住，姊夫带饮食二盒，挑至庵中。时庵中棍已预令二贼在彼伺候，即将乡农背缚于柱中。其二贼抽出牛尾尖刀，再三要杀之。棍佯劝云：“我受姊夫厚款，吃得他兄弟鸡鱼多，勿杀我姊夫。”三贼将饮食吃了即去。其乡农叫天不应，入地无门。至次日午后，一牧童至，乡农叫救命，得解缚归家。妻子问曰：“何待今日方归？舅何不回？”乡农应曰：“勿说他，勿说他。”至今被人骗者，俗语曰“勿说他”。
