A few days later the con man went over the account book with his “brother-in-law”: there were still a dozen or more yuanbao in a chapel on such-and-such a hill. The chapel stood empty. The “brother-in-law” packed two boxes of food and drink and carried them there on a pole. Two accomplices the con man had posted in the chapel were already waiting, and they bound the farmer backward against a pillar. The two drew ox-tail knives and made again and again as if to kill him. The con man pretended to plead for him: “My brother-in-law has treated me generously, and I have eaten plenty of his family’s chicken and fish. Do not kill my sister’s husband.” The three crooks ate the food and left. The farmer cried to heaven and got no answer; there was no door into the earth. Not until the afternoon of the next day did a herdboy pass by. The farmer shouted for help, was untied, and made his way home. His wife asked, “Why are you only back today? Why did my brother not return?” The farmer answered, “Do not speak of him. Do not speak of him.” To this day, when someone has been swindled, the common saying is, “Do not speak of him.”

近有江源地方一人，被一棍亦如此骗。其妻有智，即以其元宝凿来与他，知是锡。遂将此棍捆打，勒其供状，始释之。苟非其妻有识，亦蹈前乡农覆辙也。彼时悔之，宁不晚乎！

Not long ago a man in Jiangyuan was taken in by a con man in just this way. His wife was sharp: she had the yuanbao chiseled open at once and saw it was tin. They bound the crook and beat him, forced a written confession out of him, and only then let him go. Had his wife not been so clever, he would have gone the way of the earlier farmer. To regret it only then—would that not have been too late?

按：此乡农心苦力勤，啬用薄奉以致富，幸矣！何乃为贪心所使，落贼牢笼，以致失财被辱，反不如江源之妇之智哉！然末世滋伪，奸宄百出，近有丢包贼骗人甚多。更江淮间，又有扯遂法，尤难防检。贼止问你一句，你若答应一句，即被他迷。此妖术也，害人尤多。世道人心，一变至此极乎！偶因前事，遂备述之，以为出途者警。

Note: This farmer worked himself hard, scrimped on food and clothing, and so grew rich—a fortunate man. Why did he let greed drive him into the crooks’ cage, to lose his money and be shamed, ending up less wise than the woman of Jiangyuan? But in these latter days deceit flourishes and villains appear in a hundred shapes. Lately “dropped-bundle” thieves have cheated a great many people, and in the country between the Yangzi and the Huai there is another trick, called “the pull,” that is even harder to guard against: the thief only has to ask you one question—answer him once and you are spellbound. It is sorcery, and it has harmed even more people. Have the ways of the world and the hearts of men really sunk to this? Coming across these stories, I have set them down in full as a warning to all who travel.

冒州接着漂白皛

Accepting Bleached Fake Silver at the Maozhou Fair

钱天广，福建安海人也。时买机布，往山东冒州药王会卖。会期四月十五日起，二十五日止。天下货物，咸在斯处交卸。无牙折中，贸易二家自处。

Qian Tianguang was a man of Anhai in Fujian. Once he bought loom-woven cloth and took it to sell at the Medicine King Fair in Maozhou, Shandong. The fair ran from the fifteenth of the fourth month to the twenty-fifth, and goods from all over the empire were unloaded and traded there. There were no brokers to set a middle price; buyer and seller dealt directly with each other.

一棍以漂白银来买布。每五两一锭，内以真银，如假银一般，色同一样。棍将丝银先对。广以铁槌凿打，并无异样。打至十餘锭，通是一色。广说：“不须再凿槌打。”棍遂以漂白出对，共银六百餘两，内只有细丝银一百餘两，餘者皆假也。银交完讫，布搬去了。广收其银，检束行李，与乡里即雇骡车，直到临清，去买回头货物。取出其银，皆假银也。那时虽悔不及，然广不甚动情，只说云“是我方承得此会，他人出外贸易，从此止矣。”人慨斯人量大，有此大跌，后必有大发也。棍虽脱骗得金数百，然天理昭昭，子孙必不昌隆。盖假银天下处处有之，故录此以为后人之提防，勿蹈天广之覆辙也。按：棍之用假银，此为商者最难提防，必得其梗概，方能辨认。余于壬子秋，在书坊检得一小本仔，辨说银之真假，甚是明白。故录之，以为江湖诸君览之，则假银若一入眼，灼然明白。略陈其一二于左：

A con man came to buy cloth, paying with bleached silver. The ingots were five taels each, and genuine silver was mixed in with the fake, all identical in color. The con man first put out fine-thread silver for checking. Guang hammered and chiseled it and found nothing amiss. After he had tested more than ten ingots, all uniform, Guang said, “No need to hammer and chisel any more.” The con man then brought out the bleached silver to settle the payment: over six hundred taels in all, of which only a hundred-odd taels were genuine fine-thread silver—the rest was all fake. The silver was handed over, and the cloth was carried away. Guang put the silver away, packed his bags, and with his fellow townsmen hired a mule cart straight to Linqing to buy goods for the trip home. When he took out the silver, it was all fake. Regret was useless by then, but Guang did not take it too hard. He only said, “It happened to be my turn to catch it at this fair. May it end with me, and no other traveling merchant suffer the same.” People marveled at his magnanimity: after such a great fall, he was bound to rise high. The crook may have gotten away with several hundred taels, but Heaven’s principle is clear—his descendants will not prosper. Fake silver is found everywhere in the empire, so I record this as a precaution for those who come after: do not repeat Qian Tianguang’s mistake. Note: Of all the traps a merchant faces, fake silver is the hardest to guard against; you must grasp the general outline before you can spot it. In the autumn of the renzi year I came across a little booklet in a bookshop that explained how to tell true silver from false, very clearly. I transcribe it here for traveling merchants to read, so that fake silver is plain the moment it meets the eye. I give a few of its points below.

夫元宝者，坑淘出而原宝。今之官解钱粮，亦倾煎如坑淘出厚色，而成元宝也。俗云“圆宝”是也。

A yuanbao is the “original treasure” just as it comes washed from the mine pits. Today the government’s shipments of tax silver are likewise melted and refined to the rich color of pit-washed silver and cast into yuanbao. In common speech it is called “round treasure.”

松纹，与细丝一样，其皆足色也。

Pine-grain (songwen) is the same as fine-thread (xisi); both are silver of full fineness.

摇丝，色未甚足。银泻入，以手摇动而成丝也，曰摇丝。

Shaken-thread (yaosi): the fineness is not quite full. The silver is poured in and shaken by hand so that threads form—hence “shaken-thread.”

水丝，又名曰干丝，自七成、八成、九成、九五止，通名曰水丝。

Water-thread (shuisi), also called dry-thread (gansi): everything from seven-tenths, eight-tenths, and nine-tenths up to nine-and-a-half tenths fine goes by the name water-thread.

画丝，即水丝。泻出而无丝，以铁锥画丝于其上，曰画丝。

Drawn-thread (huasi) is water-thread. It is poured without threads, and an iron awl draws threads on the surface—hence “drawn-thread.”

吹丝，即九程水丝。银一入，口含吹筒即吹之以成丝也，曰吹丝。

Blown-thread (chuisi) is nine-tenths water-thread. The moment the silver is poured, the maker holds a blowpipe in his mouth and blows it into threads—hence “blown-thread.”

吸丝，以湿纸盖其上，中取一孔，以银从孔泻下，吸以成其丝也，曰吸丝。今人以铁箔盖于上，亦中取一孔，银从孔泻下，亦吸以成丝也。盖吸丝自七成起，九五止。九五者，亦看得足色也。

Sucked-thread (xisi): wet paper is laid over the top with a hole in the middle; the silver is poured through the hole and sucked into threads—hence “sucked-thread.” Nowadays people lay an iron foil on top, again with a hole in the middle, and the silver poured through it is likewise sucked into threads. Sucked-thread runs from seven-tenths up to nine-and-a-half, and the nine-and-a-half can pass for full fineness.

茶花，以纹银九钱，入铅一钱，入炉中锅内不用一毫之硝，明倾取出，以把淡底填于脚。后泻银于内，铅方不露，而自成其粗丝也，曰茶花。

Tea-flower (chahua): take nine qian of fine silver and one qian of lead, put them in the furnace pan without a speck of niter, and pour it out openly. A pale base is packed into the feet, and the silver is then poured in so the lead does not show, while coarse threads form of themselves—hence “tea-flower.”

鼎银，即汞银也，又曰水银。以纹银五钱，以汞五钱半，入铁鼎中，倾其色通红于内，取出候冷，拿出其银，止有一两，拆汞五分，可打之而成，或造之以成饼。以银薄贴于外，以墨微洒之，以掩其太白。更能造酒器，及诸项首饰。能拔银丝，亦犹细丝者，只是色略青些。更有赤脚汞银，文银三钱，铜丝二钱，汞五钱半，如同前，倾煎取出，不能打造，亦如同水丝一般。若辨汞银，其色脚嫩，上面银薄贴，色不同赤脚者。然色赤而带嫩，终不如水丝色老。此上古所传。造此换人，亦发家数千。子孙继迹不肖，而家即萧条。害众成家，终不悠久。

Tripod silver (dingyin) is mercury silver, also called quicksilver. Take five qian of fine silver and five and a half qian of mercury in an iron tripod, and pour while the inside glows red. Take it out, let it cool, and remove the silver: you get only one tael, having lost five fen of mercury. It can be hammered into shape or cast into cakes. Silver leaf is pasted on the outside, with a light sprinkling of ink to hide the too-white color. It can also be made into wine vessels and all kinds of ornaments, and it can be drawn into silver wire much like fine-thread, only slightly greener in color. There is also “barefoot” mercury silver: three qian of fine silver, two qian of copper wire, and five and a half qian of mercury, treated the same way—melted, poured, and taken out. It cannot be worked, and it looks much like water-thread. To spot mercury silver: the color at the feet is soft, and the silver leaf pasted on top does not match the barefoot kind’s color; reddish yet soft, it never has the aged look of water-thread. This lore has been handed down from ancient times. Men who made this silver to pass off on others amassed fortunes of thousands, yet the sons and grandsons who followed in their footsteps proved worthless, and the family soon fell into decline. Harming the public to build a family fortune never lasts.

铜，以铜嵌四旁，而后以银泻下，藏其铜于中，曰铜。辨之虽看其丝，终不如细丝之明。其丝粗而带滞碍，即可疑而凿之，方露其铜。

Copper-core (tong): copper is set around the four sides and silver is poured over it, hiding the copper in the middle—this is called “copper.” You can look at the threads, but they are never as bright as fine-thread’s. If the threads are coarse and sluggish, suspect it and chisel it open: the copper will show.

铁碎，以铁碎先放于内，然后以银泻诸，适均入其银内，包藏铁于其中。至低者亦有九成，九五者有丝。

Iron-bits (tiesui): broken iron is placed inside first, and then silver is poured so that it takes the iron in evenly, wrapping it in the middle. The lowest still comes out at nine-tenths; at nine-and-a-half it even has threads.

或以铜碎如前，名曰包销银。至低者亦有九成；九五有丝，九成无丝。

The same is done with bits of copper; this is called wrapped-solder silver (baoxiao). The lowest is still nine-tenths. At nine-and-a-half it has threads; at nine-tenths, none.

钞仔铜，用铜一两，入银三分，入炉中。以白信石如硝抽入，泻入中。取出铗四旁者三四分重片，中心者又入炉中倾。再铗，如此者数次。然后用银末，以碗极细，用酸砒草捣汁，入硼砂三分，以罐仔同煮。后放前银末三分，入砒草汁内。以前铜入罐中，以筋炒之，取出，以白水洗去其砒草汁，其色甚白。有一人问曰：“铜中只用银三分，后又以银末三分，何能使银相交于外？”其人对曰：“世间宝物，惟金银为至宝。若先不以银三分入铜倾煎，则后用银末亦为煎煮，必不能入。先以银三分入内，则后用此银末煮之，自然相应也。”故造假银，俗曰“神仙”。然辨此铜，当认银色，乃死鱼白，无青白之色。再看其脚，有两样：或用胭脂点，或用石点。须在点脚及死鱼白处辨之处，则真、赝了然。

Chaozai copper: put one tael of copper and three fen of silver into the furnace. White arsenic, used like niter, is drawn in and poured into the middle. Take it out and clip pieces of three or four fen from the four sides; the center goes back into the furnace to be melted again. Clip again, and repeat this several times. Then take silver powder ground extremely fine in a bowl, pound sour-arsenic herb for juice, add three fen of borax, and boil them together in a small pot. Then put the first three fen of silver powder into the herb juice. Put the copper into the pot, stir it with chopsticks, take it out, and wash off the herb juice with clean water: the color is very white. Someone asked, “You use only three fen of silver in the copper, and then another three fen of silver powder—how can that make silver appear on the outside?” The answer: “Of all the world’s treasures, gold and silver are the most precious. If you do not first melt three fen of silver into the copper, boiling it later with silver powder will never take. With the first three fen of silver inside, the later boiling with silver powder naturally bonds.” That is why making fake silver is popularly called “the immortals’ art.” To spot this copper, look at the color of the silver: it is a dead-fish white, with no blue-white sheen. Then look at the feet, which are of two kinds—some dotted with rouge, some dotted with mineral pigment. Judge it by the dotted feet and the dead-fish white, and true and false will be plain.

漂白，用银倾煎细丝一样，只是甚热而壁乃薄，而后以，去其下面者，只留上面其薄者，中以白铜倾一无壁，以前上面安于其上，下面用银箔合其下，用焊焊之。后用滓槌槌其脚，为风锅无二。虽以凿凿开，必不能辨。如辨此，则当时烧焊之际，以火烧去其青青自然之色，如死鱼之白，故曰漂白。以此辨之，灼然明白矣。

Bleached (piaobai): silver is melted and poured just like fine-thread, only very hot, so the walls come out thin. Then the lower part is removed and only the thin top is kept. White copper is poured into the middle with no wall of its own; the saved top is set over it; silver foil is joined underneath; and the whole is soldered together. The feet are then worked with a dross hammer until it looks no different from a wind-pot ingot, one cast whole in the furnace. Even chiseling it open may not reveal it. The way to tell: during the soldering, the fire burns away the natural bluish sheen and leaves a dead-fish white—hence “bleached.” Judge it by this and it is perfectly plain.

煎饼银法，每铅一钱，销铜一分。若九成银一两，可用铅一两；八成可用铅二两，七成可用铅三两，灰堤中用炭装炉，慢扇其火，煎至铅化。若过后，必急扇其火，待油珠大如豆者，即以盖盖之，煞出止九五色。如待金花灿烂，煞出即结布于上，曰布心饼，又曰焦心饼。下面蟹眼回珠，二面皆白，即松纹足色。

The method for refining cake silver: for every qian of lead, melt in one fen of copper. For one tael of nine-tenths silver you may use one tael of lead; for eight-tenths, two taels of lead; for seven-tenths, three taels. Pack charcoal into the furnace inside an ash dike, fan the fire gently, and refine until the lead melts. After that you must fan hard; when the oil beads grow as big as beans, cover the pot. Take it out then and it stops at nine-and-a-half fineness. If you wait until golden flowers blaze before taking it out, a crust forms on top—called cloth-heart cake, or scorched-heart cake. Below are crab-eyes and returning pearls, and both faces are white: that is pine-grain at full fineness.

九成饼，亦出炉白。上乃鸡爪面，下面脚亦白。

Nine-tenths cake also comes out of the furnace white, with a chicken-claw face on top and white feet below.

八成饼，出炉略黑，必用天砂擦之方白。上面蚤斑之痕，剪开略白。

Eight-tenths cake comes out of the furnace slightly black; it must be rubbed with heaven-sand to turn white. There are flea-spot marks on top; snip it open and it is slightly white inside.

七成饼，出炉墨黑，亦用砂擦及用盐梅洗之方白，其剪口带赤。

Seven-tenths cake comes out of the furnace ink-black; it too must be rubbed with sand and washed with salted plums to turn white, and the snipped edge carries a reddish tinge.

六成比七成犹不同些。

Six-tenths differs a little further from seven-tenths.

五成即梅白饼。

Five-tenths is the plum-white cake.

盐烧饼，二钱五分银出一两，取出以盐烂水，调上一重在其饼上，入火烧之，取出以锤打去一重铜皮；又用盐烧之，再锤打。如此者数次，则外面铜去而自然白，曰盐烧。

Salt-burned cake: two qian five fen of silver yields one tael. Take it out, mix a brine of rotten salt, spread a coat over the cake, and put it in the fire; take it out and hammer off a layer of copper skin; salt-burn it again and hammer again. After several rounds the copper on the outside is gone and it turns white of itself—this is called salt-burn.

白铜倾者即白盐烧。

What is poured from white copper is white salt-burn.

三铗饼，底是足色饼，用如纸薄，中用白饼熔一饼于中。上面用银入炉中倾出细丝，入铅二三钱，取出，泻入炭锅，成一饼样，亦用甚薄，盖于其上。然后用焊焊成一饼，铗去其四旁者，中间的饼对面剪铗，尽可瞒人。辨之其饼厚，上下皆真银，中间色目异样。知者以银面于杉木擦之，即见三样色。

Three-clip cake: the bottom is a full-fineness cake, thin as paper; in the middle a white-copper cake is melted in; for the top, silver is melted in the furnace and poured as fine-thread with two or three qian of lead added, then taken out, poured into a charcoal pan to form a cake, also very thin, and laid over the top. The whole is then soldered into one cake and the four sides clipped away. Snip and clip the middle of the cake face to face, and it can still fool people. To tell it: the cake is thick; top and bottom are genuine silver, but the middle is a different color. Those in the know rub the silver face on fir wood, and three different colors appear at once.

车壳即灌铅，以松纹细丝面，以落锥落一孔，然后以割仔入其内割之，尽取其囊中者，留其银壳。后用铅灌其内填满，再用银打一尖仔尖之，又以铁錾仔錾之，如风锅一般。然辨此银，要看其两面之痕处，即见明白。

Turned-shell (cheke) is lead-filled silver. On a pine-grain fine-thread face, a hole is sunk with a drop-awl; then a small cutter is worked inside to hollow it out, emptying the belly and leaving a silver shell. Lead is poured in to fill it; a silver point is hammered in to plug it; and an iron graver works it over until it looks like a wind-pot ingot. To tell this silver, look at the seams on both faces and it will be plain.

倒茅饼，先以上号白信石，用熔成罐不洧水者，以盐泥固济，入信石于内。打二炷香升灯盏上，轻清者听用。以银七钱、铜三钱五分熔。将起炉时，以前信石七分入银内，将盖盖之。取出天砂擦之，其面上亦鸡爪面，如九成银一般。辨之九成出炉自白，不待砂擦；然此饼铗口带黄，九成饼铗口自白。以此辨之朗然。

Inverted-thatch cake (daomao): first take top-grade white arsenic, put it in a crucible that will not leak, and seal it with salt mud. Burn it for two sticks of incense beneath a lamp dish, and keep the light, clear sublimate for use. Melt seven qian of silver with three qian five fen of copper. Just as you take it from the furnace, put seven fen of the arsenic into the silver and cover it. Take it out and rub it with heaven-sand: the face shows the same chicken-claw marks as nine-tenths silver. To tell them apart: nine-tenths comes from the furnace white on its own, with no need of sand-rubbing; and this cake’s clipped edge has a yellow tinge, while a nine-tenths cake’s clipped edge is white of itself. Judge by this and it is plain.

更有铁线饼、江山白、华光桥、神仙饼、糁铜饼、倒插铅，其餘奇巧假银数十样，非言语笔舌所能形容。知者引申触类，观此思过半矣。有等游惰好闲、不务生理、受磨丧心、用此假银苟计衣食，以度时光，此犹穷徒，故不足责；然今贪黜之辈，家颇殷足，尚换此银，用以毒众，自图富厚，以遗子孙。不知丧心悖理，岂有善报，子孙其能昌乎！凡四民交易，止可用七成以至细丝，更低者不可用也。如昧心欺人，不唯阴遣之罪难偿，而阳报之网亦不漏矣。

There are also iron-wire cake, Jiangshan white, Huaguang Bridge, immortal’s cake, sprinkled-copper cake, inverted-inserted lead, and dozens of other ingenious fakes that words and brush cannot fully describe. Those who know can reason from one case to the whole class: study this and you have grasped more than half. There are idle loafers who will not ply a trade, ground down by hardship until they lose all conscience, who use this fake silver to scrape together clothes and food and pass their days—these are poor wretches and not worth blaming. But nowadays greedy, shameless men whose households are already well off still pass this silver to poison the public, scheming riches for themselves to leave to their sons and grandsons. They do not see that men who deaden their conscience and defy Heaven’s principle can have no good reward—how could their descendants prosper? In all dealings among the four classes of people, only silver from seven-tenths up to fine-thread should be used; anything lower must not circulate. If you deaden your conscience to cheat others, not only will the debt of unseen punishment be hard to repay—the net of open retribution will not let you slip through.

第十五類 衙役騙

Category 15 · Swindles by Yamen Runners

入聞官言而出騙

He Overheard the Official's Words, Then Went Out to Swindle

里有寡婦，富蓋鄉鄰。止生一子甘澍，年方弱冠，恪守祖業，不敢生放。鄉人路五两問之借銀谷，皆不肯，心恨之。歸與妻胡氏謀，要賴他强姦。妻許曰：“可。”又托心友支九为干证，即往分巡道处告。道亲提审，先問胡氏曰：“甘澍因何到你家？”胡氏曰：“他家豪富，终日无图，只是姦淫人婦女。知我男人未在家，无故来调戏我；不从便强抱亲嘴，骂他不去。支九来邀我夫贩货，甘澍方走去。”再問支九：“你往路五家何干？”支九曰：“小的與路五都挑贩为生，因邀他买货，听得（里）面婦人喊骂，甘澍走出。”又問甘澍曰：“你因何與婦人角口？”甘澍曰：“并无到他家，哪有角口？問路五左右鄰便知。”左右鄰都称：“甘澍寡婦之子，素不敢非为。外间并未聞姦情，此是装情捏他。”路五执曰：“他万金巨富，岂不能买两个干证？”左右鄰曰：“我鄰近不知，他支九隔越一街，岂不是买来作证？”道曰：“路五贫民，何能买人作证？”将左右鄰并甘澍各责二十，定要問做强姦。甘澍出而惧甚，思无解释。

In the district there was a widow, the richest of all her neighbors. She had only one son, Gan Shu, just come of age, who kept strictly to the family estate and dared not put money out at interest. A villager named Lu Wu twice asked him for a loan of silver and grain; both times Gan Shu refused, and Lu Wu bore him a grudge. Lu went home and plotted with his wife, née Hu, to pin a rape on Gan Shu. His wife agreed: "All right." He also enlisted his close friend Zhi Jiu as a witness, and they went straight to the circuit inspector's office to lodge the charge. The inspector took up the case himself. First he asked Hu, "Why did Gan Shu come to your house?" Hu said, "His family is rich and he idles all day; he does nothing but debauch other men's wives. Knowing my husband was not at home, he came for no reason and made advances on me; when I refused, he seized me and forced kisses on me. I cursed him and still he would not leave. Only when Zhi Jiu came to invite my husband out to buy goods did Gan Shu finally go." Next he asked Zhi Jiu, "What business did you have at Lu Wu's house?" Zhi Jiu said, "Lu Wu and I both make our living carrying goods for sale. I went to invite him to buy stock, heard a woman inside shouting curses, and saw Gan Shu come out." Then he asked Gan Shu, "Why were you quarreling with the woman?" Gan Shu said, "I never set foot in their house—how could there be a quarrel? Ask Lu Wu's neighbors on either side and you will know." The neighbors all said, "Gan Shu is a widow's son and has never dared misbehave. None of us around here has heard of any affair; this is a story cooked up to frame him." Lu Wu shot back, "His family is worth ten thousand—why couldn't he buy a couple of witnesses?" The neighbors said, "We live right next door and know nothing; this Zhi Jiu lives a whole street away—isn't it plain he was bought to testify?" The inspector said, "Lu Wu is a poor man—how could he buy testimony?" He had the neighbors and Gan Shu each given twenty strokes, and was bent on convicting him of rape. Gan Shu left the court in terror, with no idea how to clear himself.

晚堂退后，道已封门，在后堂周旋闲行，沉默思想，忽自言曰：“错矣！错矣！”又周行数次，遂拂衣而入。适有防夫涂山，在外窥道举动，聞其言错，想必是审此姦情一事也。夤夜越墙而出，扣甘澍歇家门。歇家开门延入。甘澍正忧闷无计。涂山曰：“你今日事要关节否？”澍曰：“甚关节可解？正要求之。”涂山曰：“道爷适有妻舅到，三日内即要打发起身，唯此最灵。若投他，明日即复审，更大胜矣。”澍曰：“如此得可好。须銀几何？”涂山曰：“此翻自案事，不比别人情，须百金方可。”澍曰：“百金我出，只要明日复审。”涂山曰：“舅爷今酒席尚未散，吾当即入言之。”澍與歇家送出。道大门已封，涂山复从居旁民家越墙而入。

When the evening session ended, the inspector had the gates sealed. He strolled about the rear hall, thinking in silence, then suddenly said to himself, "A mistake! A mistake!" He paced around several more times, then flicked his sleeves and went in. Now a watchman named Tu Shan was outside, spying on the inspector's every move. Hearing him say "a mistake," Tu Shan guessed it must be about this rape case. In the dead of night he climbed out over the wall and knocked at the door of the inn where Gan Shu was lodging. The innkeeper opened up and let him in. Gan Shu was sitting in gloom, out of ideas. Tu Shan said, "About today's case—do you want to work an inside connection?" Gan Shu said, "What connection could clear me? That is exactly what I am desperate for." Tu Shan said, "His Honor happens to have his wife's brother visiting, and within three days the man must be seen off with his parting gifts. This channel works like a charm. If you go through him, there will be a rehearing tomorrow, and you will win bigger still." Gan Shu said, "If that can be done, good. How much silver will it take?" Tu Shan said, "This means reversing a case on his own docket—not like an ordinary favor. It will take a hundred taels." Gan Shu said, "A hundred taels I will pay, so long as the case is reheard tomorrow." Tu Shan said, "The uncle's banquet has not yet broken up; I will go in and speak to him at once." Gan Shu and the innkeeper saw him out. The yamen's main gate was already sealed, so Tu Shan climbed back in over the wall by way of a neighboring house.

次日，道出早堂，即出牌复审强姦事。甘澍大喜，以为果验也。下午再审甘澍曰：“路五曾問你措借否？”澍曰：“他两次問借銀谷，我皆不肯，因此仇恨，妆情诬我。”再审胡氏曰：“甘澍未到你家，哪有强姦事？将拶起！”路五边未用銀，一拶即紧。胡氏难忍，即吐实“未有强姦。只措借不肯，故妆情告他。”又将路五、支九各打三十，将甘澍全解无罪。涂山即跟出索銀。甘澍曰：“吾乐與之。”涂山自索谢，澍另以十两與之。涂山以銀入道卸起，再出索添谢，又得十两。当时以舅爷关节之力，岂知出道之自悔，而銀尽为涂山所风騙乎！

The next day the inspector opened morning court and at once issued an order for the rape case to be reheard. Gan Shu was overjoyed, sure the connection had worked. That afternoon Gan Shu was examined again: "Did Lu Wu ever press you for a loan?" Gan Shu said, "Twice he asked to borrow silver and grain and I refused both times; that is why he hates me and made up this charge." Hu was examined again: "Gan Shu never entered your house—how could there be a rape? Bring the finger-squeezers!" Lu Wu's side had spent no silver, so the squeezers were tightened in earnest. Hu could not bear the pain and blurted out the truth: "There was no rape. He refused us the loan, so we made up the charge." Lu Wu and Zhi Jiu were each beaten thirty strokes, and Gan Shu was cleared and released as innocent. Tu Shan followed him out to collect the silver. Gan Shu said, "I pay it gladly." Tu Shan then asked for a thank-you for himself, and Gan Shu gave him another ten taels. Tu Shan carried the silver into the yamen, stowed it away, came back out to beg a further token of thanks, and got ten taels more. At the time Gan Shu credited it all to the power of the uncle's pull—how was he to know it sprang from the inspector's own second thoughts, and that every tael had been conned away by Tu Shan, who had merely caught wind of the case?

按：衙役皆以騙养身供家，丰衣足食，其騙何可枚举！蓋事事是騙，日日是騙，人人是騙。虽罄南山竹，何能悉之；虽包拯再生，何能察之？予素不入公庭，此中情弊，希所知聞。此其偶得于真见者，故述其弊窦如此。然衙中虽人人是姦徒，事事是騙薮，吾唯早完公课，百忍不讼，虽贪吏悍卒，其如我何！故曰：机虽巧，不蹈为高；鸩虽毒，不饮为高；衙役虽騙，不入为高。纵有无妄之灾，必有明官能昭雪之者。何也？官皆读书人，明者多而昏者少也。无奈在衙人役，各以阴云霾雾蔽之耳。故唯忍小忿，不入衙为高也。

Note: Yamen underlings all feed and clothe their households by swindling—how could their tricks ever be numbered? Every affair is a swindle, every day a swindle, every man a swindler. Use up every bamboo on the southern hills and you still could not write them all down; bring Bao Zheng back to life and even he could not see through them. I have never been one to enter a court of law, and of the abuses within I have heard little; this one I happened to witness with my own eyes, so I record exactly where the fraud crept in. Yet though every man in the yamen is a knave and every business a nest of swindles, I need only pay my public dues early and endure a hundred wrongs rather than go to law—then what can greedy clerks and bullying runners do to me? Hence the saying: a snare may be cunning—the wise course is not to step in it; poison may be deadly—the wise course is not to drink it; yamen men may be cheats—the wise course is not to enter their gates. Even if some undeserved calamity should strike, there is bound to be a clear-sighted official to right the wrong. Why? Officials are all men who have read the books; the clear-headed are many and the muddled few. The pity is that the underlings around them screen them in dark cloud and fog. So swallow small angers and stay out of the yamen—that is the wise course.

故拟重罪释犯人

Deliberately Proposing a Heavy Sentence to Free the Prisoner

富民元植者，家温行谨，奕世良善，偶與鄉权贵有隙。鄉贵素善叶推官，乃吹毛求疵，砌元植之恶十餘件。叶推官为之送访，按院即批與叶审。叶提元植，谕之曰：“汝之恶迹，我已备晓，罪在有定。只汝家殷富，不許央关节。若有关节，罪有加无减。且收入监，候拘到被害，即听审定罪。”叶推官素廉正，从来不纳分上。今元植既承面诫，越不敢展转，只惶惧待罪耳。

There was a well-to-do commoner named Yuan Zhi, comfortably off and careful in his conduct, from a family decent for generations. By chance he fell out with a powerful man of the district. That man had long been friendly with Judge Ye, so he went hunting for flaws and cobbled together more than a dozen charges of Yuan Zhi's "crimes." Judge Ye forwarded them for investigation, and the investigating censor approved the case and assigned it to Ye for trial. Ye had Yuan Zhi brought in and told him, "I already know your misdeeds in full; the punishment is fixed by statute. But your family is rich, so hear this: you are not to beg any inside connections. If there is any back-door pull, your punishment will be increased, not reduced. Take him to jail for now; once the alleged victims have been brought in, the case will be heard and sentence passed." Judge Ye had always been honest and upright and never accepted private intercession. Now that Yuan Zhi had been warned to his face, he dared even less to scheme or stir, and only waited in dread for his sentence.

适眷亲易鄉官，素與植相善，知其事属仇陷，默地代诉于太府，托转释于四尊。太府乘间缓颊及之。叶四尊大怒，歸取元植，责之曰：“我叫你不得投分上，反央太爷来讲。这样刁恶人，定要拟你谪戍！”元植茫不知来历，叩头曰：“老爷素不纳关节，一府通知；又蒙钧旨面谕，怎敢央太爷？实不知事从何来。”叶爷曰：“且入监去，定是军罪。”元植出查，方知事出易鄉官，自以己意代释，并不使植知也。植思无处可解，寻其用事凌书手，密商曰：“能为我减军入徒，当以厚礼谢。”凌书曰：“能出百金，为汝计之。”植許曰：“可。”以銀封讫。

Now it happened that a kinsman by marriage, the retired official Yi, had long been on good terms with Yuan Zhi. Knowing the case was an enemy's frame-up, he quietly pleaded Yuan Zhi's cause to the prefect and asked him to put in a word with Judge Ye for a release. The prefect found an opening and spoke gently on his behalf. Judge Ye was enraged. He went back, had Yuan Zhi fetched, and berated him: "I told you not to send in any private word—and you went and got His Honor the prefect to speak for you! A vicious schemer like you—I will certainly sentence you to garrison exile!" Yuan Zhi had no idea where this had come from. He kowtowed: "Your Honor has never accepted back-door appeals—the whole prefecture knows it. And you instructed me to my face; how would I dare beg the prefect? I truly do not know how this arose." Judge Ye said, "Back to jail. It will be a military sentence for certain." Yuan Zhi came out and made inquiries; only then did he learn that the matter had come from the retired official Yi, who had spoken for his release on his own initiative, never letting Yuan Zhi know. Yuan Zhi could think of no way out. He sought out Ling, the scribe who had the judge's ear, and said in secret, "If you can get my military exile reduced to penal servitude, I will thank you with a rich gift." Scribe Ling said, "Put up a hundred taels and I will work it out for you." Yuan Zhi agreed, and sealed up the silver.

叶爷果唤凌书手作招曰：“须寻一军律拟来。”凌书故以绞罪拟上。叶爷命改招，只可拟军。凌书过一日再以绞罪拟曰：“访单中惟謀死亲弟一件最重，正合绞罪。餘某条某条，只是徒罪，并无合军律者。”叶爷寻思：“有对头之状，尚不轻入人绞，况是拿访，而可绞人？”曰：“造化了他，只拟徒罢。”后拟上三年徒。元植欣然纳赎，凌书遂安受百金之贿。在叶爷，宁知其外受金而内拟人重罪乎？故衙役之欺官，虽神君不及察也。

Judge Ye did in fact call in Scribe Ling to draft the sentence: "Find a military-exile statute and propose it." Ling deliberately proposed strangulation. Judge Ye ordered the draft changed—only military exile would do. A day later Ling again proposed strangulation: "On the investigation list, the heaviest count is scheming to kill his own younger brother, which fits the strangulation statute exactly. The remaining counts, this one and that one, are only servitude offenses; none of them fits a military statute." Judge Ye thought it over: "Even on a complaint with a named accuser I would not lightly send a man to the cord—still less on a warrantless investigation like this." He said, "Lucky for him. Just propose servitude." In the end the sentence proposed was three years' penal servitude. Yuan Zhi gladly paid the redemption money, and Scribe Ling pocketed the hundred-tael bribe at his ease. As for Judge Ye—how was he to know that outside his clerk had taken gold, while inside he was deliberately proposing too heavy a sentence? So it is when yamen underlings deceive their official: even a magistrate with the insight of a god would not catch it.

按：善有旌奖，恶有拿访，此朝廷激劝一大机权也。今旌者多由攒刺之巧，访者或由权贵之嗾，其虚实益相半耳。然犹幸有拿访一途，可以少惕刁顽，稍为良民吐气。特被访者出入于問官之心，高下于权书之手，其情得罪当者亦少矣。当官持权者，或遇大故重情，必虚心详审，明察沉断，庶可杜姦欺之一二耳。

Note: The good are commended and the wicked arrested for investigation—this is one of the court's great levers for encouraging virtue and warning vice. Nowadays those commended often owe it to clever toadying and string-pulling, and those investigated are sometimes set upon at the instigation of the powerful; false and true run about half and half. Still, we should be glad this avenue of investigation exists: it can give the cunning and obstinate a scare and let honest folk breathe a little easier. The trouble is that the investigated man's fate rises or falls in the examining judge's mind, while his sentence is pitched high or low in the scheming clerk's hand—so few in the end receive the punishment their case truly merits. When those who hold office and wield power meet a grave case with heavy stakes, they must empty their minds, examine every detail, see clearly and decide with firm resolve; only then can they stop one or two of the cheats.

吏呵罪囚以分责

A Clerk Berates a Convict to Split the Beating

传包孝肃为官，清廉明察，用法无私，诈不得以巧辨售，罪不得以权贵免，又不纳分上。故人称之曰：“关节不到，有阎罗包老。”

It is told that when Bao Xiaosu held office he was clean-handed and clear-sighted, and applied the law without favor: no cheat could talk his way out with clever arguments, no guilty man could escape through powerful friends, and he accepted no private intercession. So people said of him, "Where no back-door appeal can reach, there stands old Bao, dread as King Yama of the underworld."

适有富豪子犯姦情真，知难逃洞察，预與一老胥謀曰：“包爷精明，察事如神。我所犯情真，干证又直证，罪实难逃。若重罚犹可输纳；唯痛责实是难堪。有何计可以减责，必不惜厚费图之。”老胥曰：“明日若当责时，你奔近案前，强辩求申。我从旁呵斥，为你分责，或可减你一半。此外，别无策可图也。”

It happened that a rich man's son was truly guilty of an illicit affair. Knowing he could not escape that piercing insight, he plotted in advance with a veteran clerk: "Lord Bao is sharp and sees through a case like a god. My offense is real, and the witnesses testify straight against me—there is no escaping conviction. A heavy fine I could still pay; it is the painful beating I cannot bear. If there is any scheme to reduce the strokes, I will spare no expense to work it." The veteran clerk said, "Tomorrow, when the beating is about to be given, run up close to the bench and argue stubbornly, begging to be heard. I will bawl you out from the side and split the beating with you; perhaps we can cut your share by half. Beyond that, there is no scheme to be had."

次日，包公审得真情，发怒要打富子四十。富子奔近案（前），哓哓申辩不已。老胥从旁大声呵之曰：“速去受责，何须許多说话，罪岂赦你！”包公见之，大恨此吏揽权起威，恐后日窃势騙人，外必生事，即先责老胥二十板，偏减去富子二十，欲使威不自胥出，不知正落其謀中也。老胥遂得厚赂，而包公漠不知之。

The next day Lord Bao found the facts true, flared with anger, and ordered the rich son forty strokes. The rich son ran up to the bench and kept arguing, loud and endless. The veteran clerk bellowed from the side, "Go take your beating at once! What need for all this talk—do you think your sentence will be pardoned?" Lord Bao saw this and bitterly resented the clerk for grabbing authority and putting on a show of power. Fearing the man would one day steal his master's influence to swindle people and make trouble outside, he first gave the veteran clerk twenty strokes of the board—and, perversely, knocked twenty off the rich son, wanting to show that awe did not issue from any clerk. He did not know he had fallen squarely into their trap. The veteran clerk collected a fat bribe, and Lord Bao never knew a thing about it.

按：吏为姦，皆是知本官性情，而变幻用之。老胥知包公严明，岂容胥吏招权？故旁呵犯人，包公必责吏而故恕犯人，以见胥吏之无权，欲外人不畏慑之。岂知于难减责之中，故分责以取其贿，又孰从而察之？公且受胥騙，况后之为官者哉！

Note: When clerks work their frauds, it always begins with knowing their own official's temperament and turning it to their use. The veteran clerk knew Lord Bao was strict and clear-sighted and would never let underlings gather power. So he shouted at the prisoner from the side, certain that Lord Bao would beat the clerk and deliberately spare the prisoner—to prove that clerks hold no power and keep outsiders from standing in awe of them. Who would have guessed that, inside a beating that could hardly be reduced, he was splitting the strokes to collect a bribe? And who could possibly detect it? Even Lord Bao was swindled by a clerk—what hope is there for the officials who come after him?

第十六類 婚娶騙

Category 16 · Marriage Swindles

妇嫁淘街而害命

A Woman Marries a Street Cleaner and Takes His Life

京城有房八者，为人痴蠢，以淘街为生，家止一老母。一日，房八淘街，往小河边洗。靠晚来有一妇人，身穿麻衣，旁立看淘洗讫，谓房八曰：“我将往娘家，今晚不能到，暂借你家一歇。”房八曰：“我家歇不得，何不往客店歇？”妇人曰：“客店人丛杂，（住）宿不便。你家有何人？”房八曰：“家有老母。”妇人曰：“有母便可同歇。”

In the capital there was a man named Fang Ba, dull-witted, who cleaned the streets for a living. His household was only an old mother. One day Fang Ba was cleaning the streets, then went to a small river to wash. Toward evening a woman in hemp mourning clothes stood by, watched him finish washing, and said, “I am going to my mother’s house. I cannot get there tonight. Let me rest a night at yours.” Fang Ba said, “My house is no place to stay. Why not an inn?” The woman said, “Inns are crowded with mixed company; lodging is inconvenient. Who is in your house?” Fang Ba said, “I have an old mother.” The woman said, “If there is a mother, we can stay together.”

房八引至家，妇人把银与籴米买酒菜，夜间三人同食。妇人问：“曾娶媳否？”房母答：“家下仅能度日，哪得银娶媳？”妇人曰：“我前夫死已葬讫，家无亲人。今收拾家财，将回娘家，奈娘家又远。看你儿子孝善，偶然相遇，亦似天缘，意欲为你媳妇，以供奉朝夕，何如？”房母曰：“你虽好意，只恐儿不能供三口人。”妇曰：“我亦带有些少银本，谅勤治女工，亦足自给。”房八喜曰：“我算命今年当招好妻。一人自有一人禄，何患不能供？”是晚，遂成亲同宿。一夜之间，叙尽风流。男称前未娶，今如渴而得浆；女称久失偶，今如热而遇凉。二人交爱，真如鱼水。房母亦大喜天赐贤媳。

Fang Ba led her home. The woman gave silver to buy rice, wine, and dishes, and at night the three ate together. She asked, “Has he taken a wife?” The mother said, “We can barely get through the day. Where would we get silver for a daughter-in-law?” The woman said, “My former husband is dead and already buried. I have no kin at home. I have gathered the household goods to go back to my mother’s, but her house is far. I see your son is filial and kind. Meeting by chance seems Heaven’s match. I would like to be your daughter-in-law and serve you morning and night. How would that be?” The mother said, “You mean well, but I fear my son cannot feed three mouths.” The woman said, “I have also brought a little silver capital. If I work diligently at needlework, it should be enough.” Fang Ba was pleased: “My fortune this year says I should get a good wife. Each person has each person’s allotted living. Why worry we cannot feed ourselves?” That night they became husband and wife and slept together. In one night they gave themselves over to every pleasure of love. The man said he had never married, and now it was like thirst meeting drink; the woman said she had long been without a mate, and now it was like heat meeting cool. They loved each other truly like fish in water. The mother too was overjoyed at Heaven’s gift of a worthy daughter-in-law.

次日，妇以银六钱与夫籴米买菜蔬。第三日，问婆母曰：“何不做身衣服穿？”婆称无银。妇又出银六钱，叫夫在汪客大布店买之。房八既得妻，又前后得银作家，心中扬扬喜色，往汪店买青布二端归。妇各将剪去三尺，故持尺量曰：“此是剪剩之布，未成全匹。何被人瞒也？可持去与换。有好银买布，他何得如此亏人？”房八听妻言去换。汪店言：“我家哪有零布？是你自剪起胡赖我。”二人各争一场。汪客令家人再以二端与之。及持归，妇背地以剪刀刺破几叶，后将展开，又曰：“如何又换两匹碾烂布？这布店好可恶！他欺你纯善，故敢诳你。今次不换，可放言骂他，怕他什么！”房八被妻激，忿忿往说：“你以破布诳我。”汪客说：“你买一匹布，来换许多次，店中哪有此功夫！”不换与他。房八便纵言秽骂。汪客怒，喝令家人扯打一顿，后以两匹布打开看明，掷与之。房八执布归，言被打之恨。妻怒，顿足曰：“有银买布，反招他打。他靠财势，可拼命与他作对。吾与婆能替你申冤。”又激夫到店凶泼。汪店家人又群起痛打，带重伤而归。妇哭曰：“必往告保辜状。”遂往御史处告准。归买好酒好菜，劝夫多饮，方可散血。夫被其劝，酩酊大醉。夜乘醉，紧绑其手足，以沙塞口鼻，至三更死已久，解其绑绳。妇故喊曰：“你儿身冷了，硬了，莫非是死？”吓得婆起看，儿已死。二人相对哭尽哀，复往御史处补状。差官检验收贮，遍体都有重伤。汪客惊惶无措。

The next day the woman gave her husband six qian of silver to buy rice and vegetables. On the third day she asked her mother-in-law, “Why not have some clothes made to wear?” The old woman said she had no silver. The woman again put out six qian and told her husband to buy cloth at Merchant Wang’s large cloth shop. Fang Ba had got a wife and, before and after, silver to keep house; his face was full of high spirits. He went to Wang’s shop, bought two bolts of blue cloth, and came home. The woman cut three chi from each, then deliberately took a ruler and said, “This is leftover cut cloth, not a full bolt. How were we cheated? Take it back and exchange it. We paid good silver for cloth; how can they short us like this?” Fang Ba listened to his wife and went to exchange it. Wang’s shop said, “When did we ever have remnant cloth? You cut it yourself to cheat us.” The two argued. Wang told his men to give him two more bolts. When he brought them home, the woman, behind his back, stabbed several folds with scissors, then spread them out and said again, “How is it you exchanged two more bolts of crushed, ruined cloth? This shop is hateful! They see you are simple, so they dare lie to you. This time if they will not exchange, curse them out loud. What is there to fear?” Fang Ba, stirred up by his wife, went in a rage and said, “You palmed broken cloth off on me.” Wang said, “You buy one bolt and come to exchange it many times. What shop has time for that?” He would not exchange. Fang Ba then let fly with filthy abuse. Wang grew angry, ordered his men to drag him and beat him, then opened the two bolts, looked them over, and threw them at him. Fang Ba took the cloth home and told of the beating with hate. The wife stamped her foot in anger: “We paid silver for cloth and instead got a beating. He leans on money and power. Risk your life against him. Your mother and I can plead your wrong.” She stirred her husband up again to go to the shop and raise a violent scene. Wang’s household men rose together and beat him badly; he came home with heavy wounds. The woman wept: “We must file a complaint to hold them liable for the wounds.” She went to the censor and had the plaint accepted. Coming home she bought good wine and dishes, and urged her husband to drink heavily, so the bruising would disperse. Urged by her, he got dead drunk. That night, while he was drunk, she bound his hands and feet tight, stuffed sand in his mouth and nose, and by the third watch he had long been dead. She untied the ropes. Then she deliberately cried, “Your son’s body is cold and stiff—can he be dead?” The frightened mother rose to look: her son was dead. The two wept together in full mourning, then went back to the censor to supplement the plaint. Officers examined and impounded the body: there were heavy wounds all over. Wang the merchant was terrified and at a loss.

过三日将审，妇与婆到汪客店曰：“我夫被你打死已的，只我婆年老，我一妇人难独供膳，把你偿命亦无益。你能出银三百两，与我供奉婆婆，叫婆具息免检罢。”汪客闻言心喜，令人担议，许出银二百两，与房母养赡。房母依妇言，自具息言：“身贫老，儿死妇寡，莫能存命。凭亲邻劝谕，着汪出银一百两，与氏养赡，免行检验。”官准息，将汪客打二十，又罚一大罪，令房八妻领银而归。过两日，妇窃银二百两，夜间逃去，不知所往。房母再欲告，汪客又重出二十两与之，以息其事。

After three days, just before the hearing, the woman and the mother went to Wang’s shop and said, “My husband was beaten to death by you; that is certain. Only my mother-in-law is old, and I am one woman who can hardly feed us alone. If you pay with your life it does us no good. If you can put up three hundred taels for me to keep my mother-in-law, have her file a withdrawal and skip the inquest.” Wang heard this with a glad heart, sent people to bargain, and agreed to put up two hundred taels for Fang’s mother to live on. The mother, following the woman’s words, herself filed a withdrawal: “I am poor and old; my son is dead and the wife a widow; we cannot survive. By the urging of kin and neighbors, let Wang put up one hundred taels for my support, and waive the inquest.” The official allowed the withdrawal, had Wang given twenty strokes, fined him a large penalty besides, and had Fang Ba’s wife take the silver home. After two days the woman stole the two hundred taels and fled by night, no one knew where. The mother wanted to sue again; Wang put up another twenty taels to quiet the matter.

按：此妇是大棍之妻，查得房八止此老母，故遣妇假与为妻，激其与富店殴争，然后加力打死，则房母必告，必可得银，然后拐银而逃。是断送人一命，而彼得厚利也。世之奸险至此，人可痴心而犯其机阱乎！

Note: This woman was the wife of a veteran con man. They had found that Fang Ba had only this old mother, so they sent the woman to pretend to be his wife, stirred him up to brawl with a rich shop, then finished him off themselves. The mother would surely sue, they would surely get silver, then they would make off with the silver and flee. So they threw away a man’s life and took a fat profit. When the world’s treachery has come to this, can anyone still be simple-hearted enough to walk into the trap?

媒赚春元娶命妇

A Matchmaker Cons a Provincial Graduate into 'Marrying' a Titled Lady

福建春元洪子选在京将纳妾，媒数引看，多未称意。适有崔命妇者，年近三十，犹绰约如处子。以为夫除服，入寺建醮。二棍套定，一为媒先引洪春元到寺亲看。洪见其容貌秀雅，言动庄重，大是快意。媒曰：“既称意，须与其大伯言之，此妇是伯主婚。”径引春元到其家，先袖钱五十文，入付其小家僮曰：“有一春元来尊府看大厦，托讨三杯茶与吃。”再出邀春元曰：“他大伯在外即回，可入厅坐。”少顷，一棍称为伯，从外入。三人叙礼，复坐，小仆捧茶出。媒曰：“令弟妇欲改造，此福建春元欲求娶，敬问礼银若干？”伯曰：“路太远些，恐弟妇外家不允。”媒曰：“他目今受官，即叫令弟舅同到任，亦何惮远？况他世家宦族，姻眷满朝，即在京亦多人看顾，此不可错过。但老爷尚未得见令弟妇。”伯笑曰：“舍弟妇人品德行女流第一，往日亦不肯令人见。今日除服，在某寺建醮，往彼处看之易矣。”媒曰：“尊府所出，亦不须看。但问何时肯去，及礼银若何？”伯曰：“她除服了，亦不拘时去。礼银须一百以上，她首饰妆奁，亦有五六十两。”旋引媒起，密曰：“我上贺须四十两，莫与弟妇知，其身资可减些。”再复入坐，复曰：“明日若交银，可在花园馆中。家中有俗忌，不交银也。”媒曰：“须请令弟舅同见为好。”伯曰：“彼来自多称说，待娶后即通未迟。”便送媒与春元出。媒曰：“适间伯与我言，须上贺银四十两，其身资可减些。彼不欲弟妇知，欲在园交银。”

Hong Zixuan, a Fujian provincial graduate, was in the capital about to take a concubine. Matchmakers led him to look several times; most did not please him. There happened to be a titled lady of the Cui house, nearly thirty, still graceful as a maiden. Her mourning for her husband being over, she had gone to a temple to have rites performed. Two crooks had the scheme set: one, as matchmaker, first led Graduate Hong to the temple to look for himself. Hong saw that her looks were refined and her speech and bearing serious, and was greatly pleased. The matchmaker said, “Since she suits you, we must speak to her husband’s elder brother; this woman is given in marriage by the elder brother.” He led the graduate straight to the house, first tucked fifty cash in his sleeve, went in, and gave them to a young page: “A graduate has come to look at your fine residence; please ask for three cups of tea for him.” He came out again and invited the graduate: “Her elder brother-in-law is out and will be back at once. Come sit in the hall.” Shortly a con man, posing as the elder brother, came in from outside. The three exchanged courtesy, sat again; the young servant brought tea. The matchmaker said, “Your younger brother’s wife wishes to remarry. This Fujian graduate seeks to take her, and respectfully asks how much betrothal silver.” The “elder brother” said, “The road is rather far; I fear her natal family will not agree.” The matchmaker said, “He will soon take office, and can have her brother go with him to the post; why fear the distance? Besides, he is of an official family for generations, with in-laws filling the court; even in the capital many will look after her. This must not be missed. Only his lordship has not yet seen your younger brother’s wife.” The elder brother smiled: “My younger brother’s wife is first among women in character and conduct, and in the past would not let people see her. Today her mourning is over; she is having rites at such-and-such a temple. To go there and look is easy.” The matchmaker said, “From a house like yours, there is no need to look. Only ask when she will go, and what the betrothal silver should be.” The elder brother said, “Her mourning is over; she can go anytime. The betrothal silver must be a hundred or more; her jewelry and trousseau are also worth fifty or sixty taels.” He then led the matchmaker aside and said privately, “My congratulatory gift must be forty taels; do not let my brother’s wife know. The bride-price can be cut a little.” He sat down again and added, “If the silver is handed over tomorrow, do it in the garden lodge. There is a household taboo; we do not take silver in the house.” The matchmaker said, “Better invite her brother to meet as well.” The elder brother said, “If he comes he will talk too much. After the marriage it will not be too late to send word.” He then saw the matchmaker and the graduate out. The matchmaker said, “Just now the elder brother told me the congratulatory silver must be forty taels, and the bride-price can be cut a little. He does not want his brother’s wife to know, and wants the silver handed over in the garden.”

次日，媒引春元及二管家同往园馆，又去邀崔家大伯同一小仆挟天平至。媒曰：“要叫一人写礼书。”伯曰：“亡弟未在，何用婚书？”媒曰：“京城交易，不比共府作事，只记一帐，亦有凭据。”伯曰：“吾自写何如？”媒曰：“最好。”即取纸与。写到财礼处，伯曰：“六十两。”媒曰：“减些，只四十。但要安顿令弟妇有好处，不必多索银。”伯曰：“两项可都一样。”媒曰：“易说的。”写完了，媒曰：“婚书放在我手，看对银。”先对四十两作一总封，又对二十两付与伯收。伯起曰：“吾取四十两，财礼任你家中面交；不然，亦不消说。”媒曰：“再加十两。”伯亦不肯。媒顾春元曰：“何如？”春元曰：“凑起四十两在你手，到他家交与妇人。”媒曰：“婚书并银，都要在我手，一同家中，两相交付。”伯曰：“我的非今日言明，要背交，昨已议定了。若事不成，岂能赖得？”媒惟取四十两并婚书在手，同春元回店，雇人去接亲。媒以婚书付春元曰：“事已定矣，不消带去。”只同两管家，领十餘人至崔家，先入厅旁坐。媒曰：“吾叫大伯来。”脱身去矣。

The next day the matchmaker led the graduate and two stewards to the garden lodge, and also went to invite the Cui “elder brother,” who came with a young servant carrying a balance. The matchmaker said, “We should call someone to write the betrothal letter.” The elder brother said, “My late brother is not here; what use is a marriage letter?” The matchmaker said, “A transaction in the capital is not like doing business in your own prefecture. Even a simple account is still evidence.” The elder brother said, “What if I write it myself?” The matchmaker said, “Best.” Paper was brought. When they came to the betrothal silver, the elder brother said, “Sixty taels.” The matchmaker said, “Cut it; only forty. Only settle your brother’s wife in a good place; no need to demand much silver.” The elder brother said, “The two sums can be the same.” The matchmaker said, “Easy enough.” When it was written, the matchmaker said, “Leave the marriage letter in my hand while we weigh out the silver.” First they weighed out forty taels as one sealed packet, then weighed out twenty taels and gave them to the elder brother. The elder brother rose: “I will take the forty taels. The betrothal gift can be handed over at the house in her presence; otherwise there is nothing to discuss.” The matchmaker said, “Add another ten taels.” The elder brother would not. The matchmaker looked at the graduate: “What do you say?” The graduate said, “Make up forty taels in your hand, and at their house hand them to the woman.” The matchmaker said, “The marriage letter and the silver must both be in my hand; we go to the house together and both sides deliver.” The elder brother said, “Mine is not a matter of today’s talk; it was to be handed over behind her back, as we agreed yesterday. If the thing falls through, how could I keep it?” The matchmaker only took the forty taels and the marriage letter in hand, went back to the inn with the graduate, and hired men to fetch the bride. The matchmaker gave the marriage letter to the graduate and said, “The matter is settled; no need to take it along.” He went with only the two stewards and a dozen men to the Cui house and sat first at the side of the hall. The matchmaker said, “I will call the elder brother,” and slipped away.

崔家见许多人来，出问曰：“你辈何干？”管家对曰：“来接亲。”崔家人曰：“你走错门了，接甚亲？”管家曰：“媒人引我来，怎会错？”崔家人曰：“哪位是媒？”管家曰：“媒去叫你大伯。”崔家人曰：“有甚大伯？”管家曰：“是你家交银主婚的。”崔唾其面曰：“你一伙小辈该死的！此是崔爷府中，你信何人哄，在此胡说？”管家曰：“昨同洪相公在你家吃茶，许议亲事，已在花园交银了，今反退悔。我岂怕你的，难道脱得我银去？”崔家人曰：“谁把茶你吃，谁受你银？我家哪有出嫁的人？”管家曰：“你前日在寺中建醮的娘子要嫁。”崔家人曰：“啐！那是我主母，曾受朝廷诰命，谁人娶得！我去禀巡爷，把这伙棍徒锁去！”两管家见媒人请大伯不来，心中不安，各逃回店。崔家人尾其后，查是春元洪子选强婚，即往府尹告强娶命妇事。洪春元闻告，始知被棍脱，即逃出京去。及府尹差人来提，回报已无期走矣。府尹曰：“他自然要走，怎敢对得！”遂为立案存照，以俟后提。

The Cui household saw so many people coming and asked, “What is your business?” The stewards said, “We have come to fetch a bride.” The Cui people said, “You have the wrong door. What bride?” The stewards said, “The matchmaker led us. How could we be wrong?” The Cui people said, “Which of you is the matchmaker?” The stewards said, “The matchmaker went to call your elder brother-in-law.” The Cui people said, “What elder brother-in-law?” The stewards said, “The one in your house who took the silver and gives the bride in marriage.” The Cui people spat in their faces: “You pack of lowborns, you deserve to die! This is Lord Cui’s residence. Who fooled you into talking this nonsense here?” The stewards said, “Yesterday we drank tea in your house with Master Hong, agreed on a marriage, already handed over silver in the garden, and now you go back on it. Are we afraid of you? Think you can make off with our silver?” The Cui people said, “Who gave you tea, who took your silver? Who in our house is to be married out?” The stewards said, “The lady who was having rites in the temple the other day wants to remarry.” The Cui people said, “Pah! That is our mistress, who holds an imperial patent of rank. Who can marry her! We will report to the patrol officer and have this pack of crooks locked up!” The two stewards, seeing the matchmaker did not come back with the “elder brother,” grew uneasy and each fled to the inn. The Cui people followed, found it was Graduate Hong Zixuan forcing a marriage, and went to the prefect to charge him with forcibly marrying a titled lady. When Hong heard of the charge he knew he had been swindled, and fled the capital. When the prefect sent men to bring him in, they reported he had already gone, no telling when. The prefect said, “Of course he would run. How would he dare face it!” So the case was filed for the record, to be taken up later.

按：此棍巧处，在见崔家主仆皆在寺，乃哄其家小仆进茶；又云大伯欲背索上贺，在园交银，故可行其骗。洪春元既失银，又着走。又府尹信其强娶，为之立案。在外娶妾，信然难哉！作事何可不审实也？

Note: The clever part of this trick was seeing that the Cui master and servants were all at the temple, then tricking a young page of the house into serving tea; and saying the elder brother wanted a congratulatory gift in secret, with the silver handed over in the garden—that is how the cheat could be worked. Graduate Hong lost his silver and had to flee besides. And the prefect believed it was a forced marriage and filed the case. Taking a concubine away from home is truly hard! How can one act without checking the facts?

异省娶妾惹讼祸

Taking a Concubine in Another Province Brings a Lawsuit

广东蔡天寿者，为人慷慨仗义，年四十无子。其妻泼甚，弗容娶妾。一日，贩广锡三十餘担，往苏州府卖，与牙人萧汉卿曰：“我未得子，意欲在此娶一妾，亦有相因的否？”汉卿曰：“有银何怕无当意女子？”即领去看几个室女。汉卿曰：“我年过四十，此女皆年纪不相宜，吾不娶也。”

Cai Tianshou of Guangdong was open-handed and stood by his word. At forty he had no son. His wife was a shrew and would not let him take a concubine. One day he carried more than thirty loads of Guangdong tin to Suzhou to sell, and said to the broker Xiao Hanqing, “I have no son. I would like to take a concubine here. Is there anyone suitable, at a reasonable price?” Hanqing said, “If you have silver, why fear there is no girl to your liking?” He led him to look at several unmarried girls. “I am past forty,” Cai said; “these girls’ years do not match. I will not take them.”

忽有荡子国延纪，家有寡母邓氏，年三十三岁，容貌端好。夫死，遗家资千金，被延纪赌荡罄空，更欠赌银二十餘两，逼取无办。乃与棍商议，诈称母为妻，欲嫁以偿债。媒传于汉卿，领天寿看之，年貌合意，议身资银四十餘两。延纪曰：“氏系过江出身，恐外家阻挡，不与嫁远。其银可封牙人手，待临行上船，我叫人送到船来，人与银两相交付。”牙人以为可。

There happened to be a wastrel named Guo Yanji. His household had a widowed mother, née Deng, thirty-three, of decent looks. When her husband died he left a thousand taels; Yanji gambled it all away, and still owed more than twenty taels in gambling debts, which were being pressed with no way to pay. He plotted with a crook to pass his mother off as his wife and marry her out to pay the debt. Word reached Hanqing through a matchmaker. He led Tianshou to look; age and looks suited, and they agreed on a bride-price of a little over forty taels. Yanji said, “She comes from across the river. I fear her natal family will block it and not let her marry far. Seal the silver in the broker’s hand. When you are about to board, I will have her sent to the boat, and person and silver can be exchanged.” The broker thought this would do.

临行，延纪自雇轿，诈称母舅家接母。上船后，始知子将己脱嫁于客，心中甚怒，只忍气问曰：“夫既以我嫁人，何必相瞒，且娶我者是谁？”天寿应曰：“是不才。”妇曰：“看君谅是个富翁，我亦无恨。但我因夫赌荡，衣资首饰悉藏母家。我同你去取，亦且令母家得知。”天寿信之，与邓氏偕往。氏入诉其子背将己嫁之事。其兄邓天明发怒曰：“哪有子敢嫁母者！是何客人敢斗胆而娶？”出将天寿乱打。邓氏救止曰：“谅客人亦不知情，只不孝延纪，胆大该死！”天明即具状告县。邹爷准状，差拘延纪，逃走不出；先拘汉卿、天寿到。邹爷审出大怒，将婚主、媒人各责二十，以天寿收监，着汉卿讨延纪。数月，终不能拿，累被拿限拷打。天寿投分上释监，邹爷竟不许。人教天寿曰：“贼要贼拿，赌钱要赌钱人拿。何不许银与赌棍人拿？”不数日，贼指延纪所在，差人拿到。邹爷审出延纪以子嫁母与远客作妾，责四十板，拟重典，身资银追入官；汉卿、天寿各拟杖惩；其母取氏，着兄邓天明领归供养，任自择嫁，批照付之。

When it was time to leave, Yanji himself hired a sedan and pretended her natal uncle’s house was fetching her. Only after she was on the boat did she learn her son had married her off to a traveling merchant. She was very angry, but swallowed it and asked, “Since my husband has married me off, why hide it? And who is taking me?” Tianshou answered, “This unworthy man.” The woman said, “You look to be a man of means; I bear no grudge. But because my husband gambled, my clothes and jewelry are all stored at my mother’s house. Come with me to fetch them, and also let my mother’s house know.” Tianshou believed her and went with Deng. She went in and told how her son had married her off behind her back. Her elder brother Deng Tianming burst out: “Since when does a son dare marry off his mother! What traveling merchant has the gall to take her?” He came out and beat Tianshou wildly. Deng stopped him: “I expect the guest did not know. It is only the unfilial Yanji who deserves to die for his daring!” Tianming filed a plaint at the county. Magistrate Zou accepted it, sent runners for Yanji, who fled and would not come out, and first had Hanqing and Tianshou brought in. Magistrate Zou, having heard the facts, was furious, beat the man who had taken her and the go-between twenty strokes each, put Tianshou in jail, and ordered Hanqing to produce Yanji. For several months they could not catch him; Hanqing was repeatedly summoned on deadline and beaten. Tianshou sent in a private word for release from jail; Magistrate Zou would not allow it. Someone taught Tianshou: “It takes a thief to catch a thief, a gambler to catch a gambler. Why not put up silver and have gambling crooks seize him?” In a few days the thieves pointed out where Yanji was, and the runners brought him in. Magistrate Zou found that Yanji, a son, had married his mother off to a far-traveling guest as a concubine, gave him forty strokes, proposed a heavy statute, and had the bride-price silver confiscated; Hanqing and Tianshou were each sentenced to the staff as a warning; the mother, née Deng, was given into her brother Deng Tianming’s care to support, free to choose another marriage, and a written order was issued to that effect.

按：为嗣娶妾，礼律不禁，特当娶于附近小户。若出外省，慕色而娶，多酿后患。若此类者，可为炯戒矣。

Note: Taking a concubine for the sake of an heir is not forbidden by ritual or law. Only, one should take her from a nearby small household. If you go to another province and marry out of lust for looks, you often brew later trouble. Cases of this kind may serve as a stark warning.

青蛙露出谋娶情

A Frog Gives Away a Plot to Marry

徐州人陈彩，家资巨富，机智深密，有莽、操之奸。年三十岁，妻妾俱无子。邻舍潘，常借彩银，出外为商。彩往家，见其妻游氏美貌绝伦，遂起不良心。邀同本往瓜州买绵花，发广州等处卖。货收完，二人同归。路经西关渡，此幽僻之处，往来者稀。上渡，以篙撑船。彩暗忖：“此机可乘。”从后将一推落江。奔起水面，彩再以篙指落深渊。浸死之后，彩故叫渔翁捞其尸，以火焚之，裹骨归家。

Chen Cai of Xuzhou was enormously rich, deep and close in his schemes, with the treachery of a Wang Mang or a Cao Cao. At thirty, neither wife nor concubine had given him a son. A neighbor surnamed Pan often borrowed Cai’s silver to go out trading. While Cai was at the Pan house he saw that Pan’s wife, née You, was unmatched in beauty, and an evil thought rose in him. He invited Pan to put in capital together, buy cotton at Guazhou, and sell it in Guangzhou and elsewhere. When the goods were sold they traveled home together. The road passed the West Gate ferry, a lonely place with few coming and going. They boarded, and a pole was used to push the boat. Cai thought in secret, “This chance can be used.” From behind he gave a shove and sent Pan into the river. When Pan broke the surface, Cai again used the pole to push him into the deep. After he had drowned, Cai deliberately called fishermen to drag up the body, had it burned, wrapped the bones, and headed home.

彩穿白衣，见父母先大哭，而后报凶情。家大小都恸，乃细问身死因由。彩曰：“因过西关渡，上渡撑船，与篙并入水中。水深急，力不能起，遂致浸死。我雇人捞尸，焚骨而归。”言毕，潘家又哭。彩用将所卖帐薄并财本一一算明，交还之父母。满家反怀其德，哪知彩之设计谋死也。

Cai put on white mourning, saw the parents, first wept loudly, then reported the disaster. The whole household wailed, then asked in detail how he had died. Cai said, “Crossing at the West Gate ferry, he boarded and poled; he went into the water with the pole. The water was deep and swift; he had not the strength to rise, and so drowned. I hired men to recover the body, burned the bones, and came home.” When he had finished, the Pan house wept again. Cai then accounted for the sales ledger and the capital one by one and returned them to the parents. The whole family even felt grateful to him. How could they know Cai had plotted the death?

至半年后，父潘玉年老，有二幼孙不能抚养，欲以媳招人入赘，代理家事，与彩商议。彩曰：“入赘事久远，必得得当人方可；不然，家被他破害，后悔何及？依彩愚见，小心支持守节，勿嫁人为尚。”彩言虽如此，而中藏机械甚深。后有议入赘者，玉亦与彩议，彩皆设机破之。因先贿游氏之外家。

Half a year later the father, Pan Yu, was old, with two young grandsons he could not raise. He wanted to have his daughter-in-law take a man in as a live-in husband to manage the house, and discussed it with Cai. Cai said, “A live-in marriage is a long affair; you must get the right man. Otherwise he will ruin the house, and regret will come too late. In my foolish view, the better course is to keep the household carefully and hold to widowhood, and not marry.” So Cai spoke, while the scheme he hid was very deep. Later, when anyone was proposed as a live-in husband, Yu also discussed it with Cai, and Cai always found a way to break it off. Meanwhile he first bribed You Shi’s natal family.

布谋已定，自言于玉曰：“吾与令郎至知，本无自赘之理。但事有经权，试与尊叔自筹之。”玉曰：“尊见何如？”彩曰：“吾欲以叔产业悉付我理，请叔族亲议立文书，递年几多供应尊叔夫妇食用，几多供应祭坟纳役，餘者悉付叔存之，以备二孙婚娶；令媳与我为次室。况我拙荆颇贤，必无妒恚之患。后倘得产男女，必不亏她。是令媳得所归，而公家亦有所付托矣。”媳曰：“古云‘宁作贫人妻，莫作富人妾。’我夫与他为友，我嫁他为妾，似不好观瞻。请公公再详。”玉曰：“难得此人家富忠厚，况又代我理家。我不劳而坐享衣食，餘剩者又存与孙婚娶。文字有我族人为证，何等安妥，不必再疑。”海军家大小皆以为然，游氏父母亦同声曰“可”，游氏只得听命。

When the plot was set, he said to Yu himself, “I was on the closest terms with your son; there is no reason I myself should move in. But sometimes one must weigh principle against what the moment requires. Try thinking it through yourself.” Yu said, “What is your view?” Cai said, “I would have your property all put in my charge. Please have your clan kin draw up a document: so much each year to supply you and your wife with food and use, so much for ancestral graves and labor service; the rest to be kept for you, to provide for the two grandsons’ marriages. Let your daughter-in-law be my second wife. My own humble wife is quite worthy; there will surely be no jealousy. Later, if there are sons or daughters, I will not short her. So your daughter-in-law will have a place, and the family will have someone to rely on.” The daughter-in-law said, “The old saying is, ‘Better a poor man’s wife than a rich man’s concubine.’ My husband was his friend; if I marry him as a concubine, it will not look well. Please, Father-in-law, consider again.” Yu said, “It is hard to find a house so rich and honest, and he will manage for us besides. I sit and enjoy clothes and food without labor; the remainder is saved for the grandsons’ marriages. The writing is witnessed by my clansmen. How secure that is; no need to doubt further.” The Pan household, old and young, all thought it right; You Shi’s parents also said together, “Agreed.” You Shi had no choice but to obey.

不觉嫁后二十餘载，生有二子，又养一长孙。前二子皆已娶媳，亦生二孙。彩之正室前十年已故，游氏与夫极和顺。一日，大雨如注，天井水满。忽有青蛙浸于水中，跃起庭上。彩以小竹挑入水中去，如此者数次。彩平时是谨密之人，是日天牖其衷，暗忖游氏恩情已久，谅谈前情，妻必不怨，不觉漏言曰：“你前夫亦似此青蛙。若不生计较，安得与你成夫妇？”游氏曰：“计较若何？”彩曰：“昔时见你貌无双，要得同床伴我眠。心生一计同贸易，过渡踢他落波心。你夫奔起浮水面，再将篙指落深渊。连奔连指两三次，亦如青蛙此状情。”游氏惊号，大骂曰：“你这狼子野心贼，当千刀万剐！哪有人如此狠心者！”彩被妻骂，无一语可应之。游氏哭奔于路，高声叫曰：“我前夫被这贼谋死，谋我为妾！我必经官告论，为前夫报仇！”左邻右舍皆卒听惊骇。彩叫二子强抬游氏入家，皆跪下苦劝曰：“看家中大小之面，勿说此话。”游氏指骂二子曰：“你父奸谋子岂昌，无端造恶忒强梁。险邪暗害同曹贼，天牖其衷自说扬。呈官告论清奸孽，斩他首级振纲常。我夫虽然归黄土，九泉之下也心凉。”

Unawares, more than twenty years after the marriage, she had borne two sons and raised an eldest grandson. The two earlier sons had both taken wives and also had two grandsons. Cai’s principal wife had died ten years before; You Shi and her husband were extremely harmonious. One day rain poured down; the courtyard filled with water. Suddenly a frog, soaked in the water, leaped onto the hall. Cai used a small bamboo to poke it back into the water, several times like this. Cai was ordinarily a close-mouthed man, but that day Heaven opened his heart. He thought You Shi’s affection had lasted long; if he spoke of the past she would surely not resent it, and without noticing he let it slip: “Your former husband was like this frog. If I had not made a plan, how would I have become husband and wife with you?” You Shi said, “What plan?” Cai said, “Back then I saw your looks had no equal, and wanted you in the same bed to sleep with me. I formed a plan and we traded together; at the crossing I kicked him into the waves. Your husband broke the surface; I again used the pole to push him into the deep. Twice or three times he rose and I poked him down—just like this frog.” You Shi cried out in shock and cursed him loudly: “You wolf-hearted thief, you deserve a thousand cuts! Who is so vicious as this!” Cai, cursed by his wife, had not a word to answer. You Shi ran weeping into the road and shouted, “This thief murdered my former husband and plotted to take me as a concubine! I will go to the officials and avenge my former husband!” Neighbors on both sides all heard in shock. Cai had his two sons force You Shi into the house; they all knelt and pleaded, “Think of the faces of everyone in the house; do not say this.” You Shi pointed and cursed the two sons: “Your father’s treacherous plot—how can sons flourish? He did evil without cause, too violent by far. He harmed his partner in trade with dark, crooked treachery; Heaven opened his heart and he told it himself. I will take it to the officials and clear the wicked crime, cut off his head to set the bonds of duty straight. Though my husband has gone back to yellow earth, under the Nine Springs his heart will be at peace.”

长子潘槐、次子潘杨闻游母出，路扬陈彩谋杀其父之事，与潘族众来问其详。游氏见二子并小叔，恸哭甚而言曰：“当你父在日，出外为商，尝问这贼借本。他见我先时有貌，即起歹意。邀你父出外贸易，归西关渡，踢你父于江中。奔起水面，复以篙指落深渊。如此者数次，因此浸死。”众等曰：“何以知之？”游氏曰：“适间大雨，天井水溢，有一青蛙被浸，跃起庭上。贼以竹打，抽下数次，蛙因打困浸死。天不容奸，他见此蛙，因自道其故，所以知之。儿可去告，我来作证。”槐、杨闻言，捶胸号天，大哭曰：“这仇不共戴天，扯来打死他！”直入内堂，将陈彩揪打。彩家理亏，自然不敢对敌。彩怒曰：“我纵谋人，罪有明条，岂该你打！”游氏曰：“他罪不容诛，若未经官，错手打死，则仇未报反成人命。”方闹嚷间，潘家族众倏集百餘人，中有无藉者欲掠其家。游氏曰：“物是我的。贼犯法当死，非他所有。我不出证其罪，汝众何得掠我财物？”

The eldest son, Pan Huai, and the second, Pan Yang, heard their mother You go out into the road proclaiming that Chen Cai had murdered their father, and came with the Pan clan to ask the full story. You Shi, seeing her two sons and her husband’s younger brother, wept bitterly and said, “When your father was alive and went out trading, he often borrowed capital from this thief. He saw that I had looks then, and an evil thought rose. He invited your father out to trade; returning, at the West Gate ferry, he kicked your father into the river. When he broke the surface, he again used the pole to push him into the deep. Several times like this, and so he drowned.” They asked, “How do you know?” You Shi said, “Just now in the heavy rain the courtyard overflowed. A frog was soaked and leaped onto the hall. The thief beat it with bamboo and poked it down several times; the frog, worn out by the beating, drowned. Heaven will not abide treachery. He saw this frog and told the reason himself; that is how I know. You sons can go and charge him; I will come as witness.” Huai and Yang heard this, beat their breasts, cried to heaven, and wept: “This feud cannot share the same sky. Drag him here and beat him to death!” They went straight into the inner hall, seized Chen Cai, and beat him. Cai’s household, in the wrong, naturally dared not fight back. Cai said in anger, “Even if I plotted against a man, the law has a clear article. How should you beat me?” You Shi said, “His crime will not be paid even by death. If we have not gone through the officials and kill him by a wrong blow, then the feud is not avenged and we have made a homicide case instead.” In the midst of the uproar more than a hundred of the Pan clan gathered; among them some riffraff wanted to loot the house. You Shi said, “The goods are mine. The thief has broken the law and should die; they are not his. If I do not come forward to prove his crime, how may you lot plunder my property?”

游氏与二子抱牌急告本县。魏爷准其状，差拿陈彩到官，无半语推辞，一一招认。魏爷打彩三十板，立拟典刑，即申上司讫。游氏并二子槐、杨各讨保，候解两院。是日，县看者何止数百人，皆曰：“此妇原在潘家，处中户；今处于陈，万金巨富，驱奴使婢，先作妾而今作正室；况年已久，生子及孙。徇情者初谈及此，未免哽咽喉干，吞声忍气而罢。今径呈之公庭，必令偿前夫命，真可谓女流中节侠，行出乎流俗者也！”

You Shi and the two sons clutched the plaint and rushed to charge him at the county. Magistrate Wei accepted the plaint, sent runners for Chen Cai, who did not deny a single word and confessed to every point. Magistrate Wei gave Cai thirty strokes, at once proposed capital punishment, and reported it up. You Shi and the two sons Huai and Yang were each released on surety, to await transfer to the two provincial courts. That day, those watching at the county numbered no fewer than several hundred. They all said, “This woman was originally in the Pan house, a middling household; now she is with Chen, worth ten thousand in gold, ordering slaves and maids about, first a concubine and now the principal wife; and the years have been many, with sons and grandsons. A woman who followed feeling, at the first talk of this, would likely choke, swallow her voice, and let it go. Now she has gone straight to the public court and will have the former husband’s life paid for. She may truly be called a woman of honor among her sex, acting beyond the common run!”

两院倒案已毕，陈彩正典刑已定。彩托禁子叫游氏并二子来狱中嘱咐。游氏不肯去见，只叫二子往见之。彩嘱二子传命曰：“我偿潘之命已定，她之怨已酬，而结怨之恩已报矣，何惜见我一面？我有后事欲以付托。”游氏曰：“我与他恩谊绝矣，有何颜再见他？”二子入狱中回话，彩大怒曰：“我在狱受尽苦楚，不日处决。她在家享受富贵，是他潘家物乎，陈家物乎？”言毕，二子以父言传于母。游氏曰：“我在你父家二十餘载，恩非不深，但不知他机谋甚巧。今已泄出前情，则你父实我仇人，义当绝之。你二人是我毛里天性，安忍割舍？你父不说富贵是他家的，我意已欲还潘家；今既如此说，我还意已决。当你母已死，勿复念也。”二子曰：“母亲为前夫报仇，正合大义，我父不得生怨。须念我兄弟年幼，方赖母亲教育，万勿往他家也。”游氏不听，召集陈门亲族，将家业并首饰等项交割明白，空身而还潘家，甘处淡泊，人皆服其高义，羡潘之有妻，仇终得报；叹子彩之奸谋，祸反及身也。

The two courts had finished reviewing the case; Chen Cai’s capital sentence was fixed. Cai asked a jailer to call You Shi and the two sons to the prison to give last instructions. You Shi would not go to see him; she only told the two sons to go. Cai charged the two sons to pass word: “I am to pay Pan’s life; that is fixed. Her grievance is paid, and the debt of that old wrong has been settled. Why begrudge seeing me once? I have later affairs I wish to entrust.” You Shi said, “All kindness and duty between him and me are cut. What face have I to see him again?” The two sons went into the jail and answered. Cai said in a great rage, “I suffer every misery in jail and will be executed any day. She enjoys wealth at home—is it the Pan house’s goods, or the Chen house’s?” When he had finished, the two sons passed their father’s words to their mother. You Shi said, “I was in your father’s house more than twenty years; the kindness was not shallow. I only did not know how clever his scheme was. Now that the past has come out, your father is in truth my enemy, and by right I must cut him off. You two are of my flesh and blood; how could I bear to give you up? Your father need not say the wealth is his house’s; I already meant to go back to the Pan house. Now that he has spoken so, my mind to return is fixed. Take your mother as already dead; think of me no more.” The two sons said, “Mother avenging her former husband is exactly great duty; our father has no call to resent it. Only think that we brothers are young and still depend on Mother’s teaching. By all means do not go to that other house.” You Shi would not listen. She gathered the Chen clan, handed over the estate, jewelry, and the rest clearly, and went back empty-handed to the Pan house, content to live simply. People all admired her high sense of duty, envied Pan for having such a wife, so that the feud was at last repaid, and sighed that Cai’s treacherous plot had brought the disaster back on himself.

第十七類 姦情騙

Category 17 · Illicit-Affair Swindles

用银反买焙纸妇

Using Silver to Win the Woman Drying Paper

宗化人羽崇，家资殷富，性最好淫，常以银穀生放于乡下。乡人唯早午晚在家食饭，午前后都往耕田，并无男子在家。崇偏于半午前往人家取帐，遇单居妇女，千方挑之，多与通好。

Yu Chong of Zonghua was a man of ample means, and lecherous by nature. He often put silver and grain out at interest in the countryside. Villagers were home only for the morning, noon, and evening meals; before and after midday they were all out in the fields, with no men left in the houses. Chong made a point of going to collect debts in the middle of the morning. When he found a woman alone, he tried every way to seduce her, and he often got her into bed.

人有问之者曰：“凡妇人与初相见，面生情疏，茫不相识，怎好开口便通野话？倘怒骂起来，后何以登其门？”崇曰：“凡撩妇人，临机应变，因事乘机。或以言挑，或以利诱，或以势压，或以恳求，何止一端，全在察其心情而投中之。或无可入机者，试与之讲梦，说我昨夜梦一所在去，宛似你家一般，某物在此，某物在此。又梦与你相交，一夜快活，醒来乃是一梦。今日到此，全与梦中相同。如此且笑且说，讲了一遍，看她言貌或喜或怒，或不睬，或应对，或疑猜，便可以言投入。彼若发骂，我只说梦；彼若不拒，我便可取事矣。“我尝往一所在取帐。男子另一处造纸，两妯娌对焙纸。其伯姆半宿妇人，其婶子极是少美，我欲挑之。若半声推拒，隔焙便闻，何以动手？我生一计，包银一钱，作一块，密密轻轻与说曰：‘我欲挑你伯姆，把此一钱银送你；再一包五分，託你代送与伯姆，替我说个方便’。妇人接四包银，把自己包开看过，见银作一块，心中有些喜意，答曰：‘你爱她你自与她说，自然是替，我不好替说。’我便曰：‘若爱只是爱你，但恐你不肯，故託你通伯姆。’罢不应，我便搂之，默然应承。只隔焙幹事，那边全不知。若不如此，反生计较。彼恐伯姆知之，怎肯默然应允？惟先说挑伯姆，彼心道：‘那边可干事，我这边密密幹，亦何妨？’故不劳而成也。”

Someone asked him: "When you first meet a woman you are strangers, with no feeling between you. How can you open your mouth with indecent talk? If she flies into a rage and scolds you, how will you ever enter her door again?" Chong said: "Seducing a woman depends on reading the moment and using whatever the situation offers. You may bait her with words, tempt her with profit, press her with rank, or plead—there is more than one way. The whole art is to watch her mood and hit it. If there is no opening, try telling her a dream: say that last night you dreamed you went to a place that looked just like her house, this thing here, that thing there; and you dreamed you were with her all night, then woke and found it was a dream; and today you arrive, and it is exactly as in the dream. Tell it with a smile. Watch whether her face is pleased or angry, whether she ignores you, answers, or grows suspicious—then you know how to speak. If she scolds, I say it was only a dream. If she does not refuse, I can proceed. I once went to collect a debt. The men were making paper elsewhere; two sisters-in-law were drying paper facing each other. The elder brother's wife was a woman of middle years; the younger brother's wife was very young and pretty. I wanted to seduce the younger one. If she made even half a sound of refusal, it would be heard across the drying rack—how could I act? I thought of a plan. I wrapped one qian of silver in a lump and said quietly: 'I want to seduce your sister-in-law. I'll give you this one qian; here is another packet of five fen—please pass it to her and put in a word for me.' The woman took four packets of silver, opened her own packet, saw the silver in a lump, and was somewhat pleased. She answered: 'If you like her, tell her yourself. Of course I'll help, but I shouldn't be the one to speak.' I said: 'If I like anyone, I like you. I only feared you would refuse, so I asked you to speak to your sister-in-law.' She did not answer. I embraced her, and she silently consented. We did it with only the drying rack between us; the other side never knew. If I had not done it this way, she would have thought twice. She would have feared her sister-in-law would know, and would never have silently agreed. Only by first saying I wanted the sister-in-law would she think: 'If that side can do it, I can do it quietly here too—what's the harm?' So it was done without effort."

按：妇人不爱淫者亦爱财，但深畏人知，故不敢为。唯点壮其心，谓人不能知，彼便敢妄为耳。既许从你，彼之遮盖，自然更谨密矣。此羽崇骗姦机巧之一节也。然世情鬼魅，有许多深奸隐慝，何能尽述为戒？特择其近闻者如此。

Note: Women who do not love lust still love money, but they deeply fear being found out, so they dare not act. Only when you give them the nerve by saying no one can know will they dare to go ahead. Once they have agreed to you, they will cover themselves even more carefully. This is one of Yu Chong's tricks for cheating a woman into bed. But worldly wickedness has many deep, hidden schemes—how can they all be told as warnings? I only pick what I have recently heard.

和尚剪绢调佃妇

A Monk Cuts Silk to Seduce a Tenant's Wife

寿山寺，田粮五百石，分为十二房。僧皆富足，都锦衣肉食，饮酒宿娼，更甚俗家。每管寺十餘年，银多欲归，先约家中定姻，在外蓄发为头陀，鬓发可缚网巾，即回娶妻当家矣。每兄去弟来，父亡子继，据为己业，并无异色人得参入。或有畏受家累，不思归俗者，辄择村中愚善佃客有无妻者，出银与代娶。僧先宿一个月，后付与佃客，供不时往宿，僧来则僧之妻，僧去则佃之妇。故谚云：“非僧奸佃妇，乃佃奸僧老婆。”即此俗也。或生子，有全月可认者，则属某；或交错无可辨者，则僧与佃分，各得其一，待十餘岁，即领为侍者，实则亲子也。故僧家云：“灭灯传道，寄姓传宗。”即此也。

Shoushan Temple had a grain tax of five hundred shi, split among twelve monastic households. The monks were all well off: silk clothes, meat, drinking, and visits to prostitutes—worse than laypeople. After managing the temple for ten-odd years, when they had enough silver and wanted to head home, they first arranged a marriage in the family, then grew their hair as itinerant monks. When the hair was long enough to bind with a hairnet, they headed home, married, and kept house. Brothers succeeded brothers; sons succeeded fathers. They treated it as family property; no outsider could join. Some who feared family burdens and did not want to return to lay life would pick a simple, kind tenant in the village who had no wife, and pay silver to marry one for him. The monk slept with her first for a month, then handed her to the tenant, and came back to sleep with her whenever he liked: when the monk came she was the monk's wife; when he left she was the tenant's wife. Hence the saying: "It is not the monk who seduces the tenant's wife—it is the tenant who sleeps with the monk's woman." That was the custom. If a child was born and the month made paternity clear, it belonged to that one; if mixed and unclear, monk and tenant split, each taking one. After about ten years they were taken as attendants—actually their own sons. Hence the monks said: "Put out the lamp to pass on the teaching; lodge the surname to continue the line." That is what they meant.

有一僧，往乡取苗租，其佃户柔懦。见其妇美貌，每挑之便骂，不睬。后冬十月，故买匹好绢，问此妇借剪刀，剪下二尺，曰：“将送人作鞋面。”餘者寄此妇手。两日后复来取绢，借剪刀又剪二尺，将往送人，餘者仍寄之。妇曰：“送甚人，何不全拿去？”僧曰：“只消许多，可长享用。”妇曰：“我代收藏，亦当剪二尺与我。”僧曰：“你若要，便全匹与你，这两尺亦与你，不消送那人矣。”妇曰：“果真乎？”僧曰：“惟恐你不受，我久有意送矣。”两下遂成云雨佳会。僧曰：“你往日骂我，今日何有这好意？”妇曰：“我冬间要做一身衣服，送母亲寿，故不得已从你。后日决不肯矣。”僧曰：“那二尺更要一次。”妇曰：“二尺任你送别人。”僧曰：“取多辞少，你好歹。”

One monk went to the village to collect the field rent. The tenant was timid. Seeing that the wife was pretty, the monk made advances whenever he could; she always cursed him and paid him no mind. Later, in the tenth month of winter, he deliberately bought a bolt of good silk, asked this woman to lend scissors, cut two chi, and said, "I'll give this to someone for shoe uppers." He left the rest in her hands. Two days later he came again for the silk, borrowed the scissors, cut another two chi to give someone, and left the rest with her again. The woman said, "Who are you giving it to? Why not take it all?" The monk said, "This much is enough; it will last a long time." The woman said, "If I'm storing it for you, you should cut two chi for me too." The monk said, "If you want it, I'll give you the whole bolt, and these two chi as well—no need to give that other person." The woman said, "Truly?" The monk said, "I only feared you wouldn't take it; I've long meant to give it to you." Then the two of them had sex. The monk said, "You used to curse me; why so kind today?" The woman said, "This winter I need a set of clothes as a birthday gift for my mother, so I had no choice but to go along. After this I certainly will not." The monk said, "Those two chi will cost one more time." The woman said, "Give the two chi to someone else." The monk said, "You take a lot and refuse a little—that's a fine way to behave."

及事完了出房，僧曰：“我要禾稿绞一索用。”妇取付之。僧将稿慢慢绞索。妇催快去，僧曰：“在外何妨？”少顷，佃客回，问曰：“你作索何用？”僧曰：“我有绢大半匹要卖，令正说要造衣，送令岳母寿，以你养的猪，作一两二钱还我绢，将此索牵去。”佃客骂妻曰：“我猪要养，何换此无用绢？急取还他去！”妇取起二尺，将大匹丢出还之，曰：“舍与你！”僧曰：“我还你是价，也不亏你，有甚舍与我？”僧见其取起二尺，知她终是爱财，次月复买蓝绢半匹，并前绢送与之。妇骂曰：“秃骡该入螺蛳地狱，我岂睬你！”僧曰：“正为你常骂我，故意取回，弄你受气。不然，我岂悭吝的？你说要一身衣服送寿，前日止一件衣，今敬剪一件下，成就你事，何故又骂？”妇拒不允，僧再三哀求，只前已有情了，终拒不得，复为受之。后遂通往来，难禁断矣。

When they were done and came out of the room, the monk said, "I need rice straw to twist a rope." The woman fetched some. The monk twisted the straw slowly. She urged him to leave quickly. The monk said, "What's the harm in being outside?" Shortly the tenant returned and asked, "What's the rope for?" The monk said, "I have most of a bolt of silk to sell. Your wife said she needs it to make clothes for your mother-in-law's birthday, and would pay with a pig you raised, one tael two qian for the silk; this rope is to lead the pig away." The tenant cursed his wife: "I need to raise that pig; why swap it for useless silk? Give it back at once!" The wife picked up the two chi, threw the large piece back, and said, "Take it as a gift!" The monk said, "I was paying you a fair price; I wasn't cheating you. What's this 'gift'?" Seeing she had kept the two chi, he knew she still loved money. The next month he bought half a bolt of blue silk and brought it with the earlier silk. The wife cursed: "Bald mule, you belong in snail hell—as if I'd give you the time of day!" The monk said, "Precisely because you always curse me, I deliberately took it back to make you angry. Otherwise would I be stingy? You said you needed a set of clothes for the birthday; last time it was only one garment. Now I have respectfully cut another so you can finish the job—why curse again?" She refused. He pleaded again and again. Since they had already been intimate, she could not hold out in the end, and accepted again. After that they kept meeting; it was hard to stop.

按：此妇性本烈，只为爱其绢，遂至玷身。所谓“枨也欲，焉得刚”是也。人家唯禁止僧道来往，便是好事。若入寺，若拜佛，若子寄僧道姓，此皆耻事，切宜戒之。勿图无影福田，而蹈无穷污垢也。

Note: This woman was fierce by nature; only because she loved the silk did she stain herself. As the saying goes, "When desire is in you, how can you stay firm?" Households should simply forbid monks and Daoists from coming and going—that is already a good thing. Entering temples, worshipping Buddha, giving a child a monk's or Daoist's surname—these are all shameful; be sure to avoid them. Do not chase phantom merit, only to step into filth without end.

地理寄妇脱好种

A Geomancer Leaves His Wife to Steal a Fine Bloodline
