有鲁地理，看山颇精。要图一好地，自葬父。寻至宁城，得一佳风水，落在杨乡官坟祠后，既难明买，又难盗葬。闻杨乡官已故，两公子亦欲求地葬父。鲁地理即以此地献，引二公子来看，果好穴情山。不费买，坐向又大利，即用葬父，将银三十两谢地理。鲁客不能谋其地，因欲脱其种。乃租杨公子花园门下住家，用银娶一美妇为妻，与居两个月，对妻曰：“我要出外行地理，难计归程。家下若欠缺薪米，已托主人公子看顾你。此是我恩人，因得他银，故能娶你。我已远出，这两公子若调戏你，随你从他。若与他有情，后日扶持你必厚。但他家多奴仆，切不可与他通。若轻自身，公子必看贱你，后自取困穷，谁来周济你？”又去托两公子：“见得要远出行，地理家下些少，望相周济，归时一一奉还。”

There was a geomancer named Lu, quite skilled at reading mountains. He wanted a fine gravesite to bury his own father. Searching as far as Ningcheng, he found excellent fengshui behind the ancestral shrine of the retired official Yang. It was hard to buy openly and hard to bury in secretly. He heard that Official Yang was already dead, and the two young masters also wanted a site to bury their father. Geomancer Lu offered them this site, led the two to look, and it was indeed a fine burial spot. No need to buy, and the orientation was very auspicious. They buried their father there and paid the geomancer thirty taels. Lu could not get the site himself, so he wanted to make off with their seed. He rented a house at the garden gate of the Yang sons, used silver to marry a beautiful woman, lived with her two months, then told his wife: "I must go out to practice geomancy; I cannot say when I will return. If the household lacks firewood and rice, I have asked the young masters to look after you. They are my benefactors; because I got silver from them I could marry you. While I am far away, if these two young masters make advances, you may go along. If you have an affair with them, they will support you generously later. But they have many servants—never sleep with the servants. If you cheapen yourself, the young masters will look down on you, and you will end up poor with no one to help." He also went and asked the two young masters: "I have to travel far; the household I leave behind has little. Please help out, and when I return I will repay everything."

公子常往花园，见其妇美，已是动心。地理才去两日，大公子即来其家，调戏其妻。这妇人已承夫嘱，慨然与通，情意好甚。后月餘，次公子亦来戏之，亦从。半年后，鲁地理归，见家中米菜充足，问妻曰：“公子来否？”妻曰：“两人都来，我都纳之。”鲁地理曰：“与这好人交，亦不羞辱你。有吃有穿，有人陪你睡，早晚有人看顾，我虽出外亦安。”妻笑曰：“食用还强你在家时，只你不要吃醋。”地理曰：“是他银娶的，又代我供你，何须妒？但两人迭来，恐你惹毒疮。须与他定一月一个，可无生疮。”

The young masters often went to the garden, saw that the wife was beautiful, and were already stirred. Two days after the geomancer left, the elder came to the house and made advances. The woman, already instructed by her husband, readily consented, and they were very fond. After a month or so, the younger also came and made advances; she consented too. Half a year later Geomancer Lu returned, saw rice and vegetables plentiful, and asked his wife: "Did the young masters come?" The wife said: "Both came; I took them both." Lu said: "To be with such good men is no shame to you. You have food and clothes, someone to sleep with, people looking after you morning and evening; I can rest easy even when away." The wife laughed: "The food is even better than when you were home—just don't get jealous." The geomancer said: "It was their silver that married you, and they supply you in my place—why be jealous? But if both come in turn, you might catch the pox. Set it as one month each, and you won't get the pox."

再次又出外，公子又来。妇人曰：“你两位不时来，恐我成毒疮。须定单月大公子，双月小公子方好。”公子曰：“你说极是。自今某月属某，菜米一应他供给。”不觉经四年，已生两男子，皆两公子血脉矣。鲁地理将命与人推，皆云后当大富贵。因携妻与子辞两公子而归，二人各赠有厚程。后二子长成，皆登科第，实杨姓之风水，被其暗漏去而不知也。

He went out again; the young masters came again. The woman said: "You two come at odd times; I might get the pox. Better: odd months the elder, even months the younger." The young masters said: "You're quite right. From now on such-and-such month belongs to such-and-such, and vegetables and rice are all supplied by that one." Before they knew it, four years had passed; two boys were born, both the young masters' blood. Geomancer Lu had their fortunes told; all said they would later enjoy great wealth and rank. He took wife and sons, took leave of the two young masters, who each gave generous gifts for the journey. Later the two sons grew up and both passed the examinations—truly the Yang family's fengshui, leaked away in secret without their knowing.

按：富贵家子弟，多有好淫人妻小者。或致生子，其风水不无分去。观此地理之脱种，后人可鉴矣。

Note: Sons of rich families often lust after other men's wives. If children result, their fengshui is inevitably split away. Look how this geomancer made off with the seed—later generations can take warning.

有一富家子，往佃户家取租，见其妇美，累挑之。妇不敢从，密报于婆。婆曰：“他富家子，若与他有子，后日亦讨得吃。”富子后又挑之，妇即允，与入房中解衣。富子曰：“往时累说不从，今何故便肯？”妇曰：“已对婆婆说过了。”富子曰：“你婆要拿奸么？”妇曰：“非也！婆曰傍你富家种，若有儿，亦讨得吃。”富子一闻漏种话，猛然自省曰：“不可！不可！”连说四句“不可”，因转言曰：“我非真欲奸，只爱你生得好，故与耍耳。今送银三钱，与你买粉，我不污你也。”淫情已动，驰归家，夜与妻交。其夜受胎，后生一男，长中进士，官受知县。

A rich family's son went to a tenant's house to collect rent, saw that the wife was pretty, and propositioned her again and again. She dared not consent, and secretly told her mother-in-law. The mother-in-law said: "He's a rich man's son; if you have a child by him, later we'll have something to eat." The rich son propositioned her again; the woman agreed, and they went into the room to undress. The rich son said: "Before you always refused; why agree now?" The woman said: "I've already told Mother-in-law." He said: "Does she want to catch us in the act?" The woman said: "No. She said to lean on your rich-family seed; if there's a son, we'll have something to eat." At the words about leaking the seed, he suddenly came to himself: "Must not! Must not!" He said "Must not" four times, then changed his words: "I didn't really mean to; I only liked your looks, so I was playing. Here's three qian of silver for powder; I won't stain you." Lust already aroused, he hurried home and slept with his wife that night. She conceived that night; later a boy was born, grew up, passed the jinshi examination, and became a magistrate.

初上任日，天晴日朗，忽见官堂四大柱上，各有两个“不可”金字，心中忧曰：“此必不可任此官也。”谨慎做一季官，便推病辞官养亲，匆然归。父惊问故，答曰：“因上任日，见四个‘不可’金字，恐非吉光，故辞官归养。”父曰：“养亲官在亦可。”经一夜，父思到大喜，呼其子曰：“你见四‘不可’金字，此大吉兆，你官必高也。我少年时，挑一佃妇，已允矣。临行事时，他说要傍我好种，我猛省起，连说四句‘不可’，遂不肯苟合。其夜归后即生汝，此天报我不淫人妇之德。若是凶兆，何故是金字？又何故四个‘不可’，与我昔言相应也？此是好兆矣。”儿曰：“是也。”随即写书托同年，次年复起官。后官至侍郎，一门贵盛。

On the first day of taking office, the sky was clear. Suddenly he saw on each of the four great pillars of the hall the two golden characters "Must not." He worried: "I must not take this post." He served carefully for one season, then claimed illness, resigned to care for his parents, and hurried home. His father asked in alarm. He answered: "On the day I took office I saw four golden 'Must not'; I feared it was not an auspicious sign, so I resigned to come home and care for you." The father said: "You can care for parents while in office too." After a night, the father thought it over and was overjoyed. He called his son: "Those four golden 'Must not' are a great auspicious sign; your office will be high. When I was young I propositioned a tenant's wife; she had already agreed. At the moment of the act she said she wanted to lean on my good seed. I suddenly came to myself and said 'Must not' four times, and would not go through with it. That night after I returned, you were conceived. This is Heaven rewarding me for not defiling another man's wife. If it were an ill omen, why golden characters? Why four 'Must not,' matching what I once said? This is a good sign." The son said: "Yes." He immediately wrote to a classmate from his examination year; the next year he was reinstated. Later he rose to vice minister; the whole family flourished.

看此节，可见富贵家子弟不可漏种于人矣。有乡官知县，生四男，皆为秀才，聪明俊伟。一日，乡官卒。地理为择一葬地，风水甚佳，曰：“六年两科内，四位公子当尽登科第。”六年后，地理来取谢。三长公子都中去为官，独四公子在家款待地理，敬问曰：“承先生许我四人皆发科，今三位兄果中矣。论才学，我更高于兄，独不中，何故？”明日，地理同四公子再登坟细看，曰：“论此地，虽几兄弟皆当中，其间不中者，必有故。”公子恳问何故，地理曰：“令先尊几岁生你？”公子曰：“先父生我时年六十，后七十四岁卒，今又六年矣。”又问曰：“令堂当时几岁？”公子曰：“其时三十岁。”地理摇头曰：“我知之矣。”公子曰：“先生知何缘故？”地理曰：“休怪我说。公子必欲中，须问太夫人，你是何人血脉？”公子会其意，夜设盛席，慢慢劝母醉饮。至二更后，吩咐亲人并奴婢等各先睡。四下无人，公子跪曰：“儿有所禀，不敢言。不知母亲愿我中否？”母曰：“三哥子都中了，我愿你中极切。有甚好歹事，但说无妨。”公子曰：“地理说我不是爹爹亲血脉，故不中。必须知谁实生我，方可中。”母本爱幼子，静夜又无人，酒后又醉了，不觉吐言曰：“地理果高见。彼时你父已六十，衙中某门子后生标致，我实与他生你。”公子已得实，次日谋于地理。地理曰：“须到彼处谋门子骸骨来，附葬旁，来科即中矣。”公子依言，往取而葬之，次科果中。

This shows that sons of rich families must not leak their seed to others. A retired official who had been a magistrate had four sons, all licentiates (xiucai), clever and handsome. One day the official died. A geomancer chose a burial site of excellent fengshui and said: "Within six years and two examination cycles, all four young masters will pass." Six years later the geomancer came for his thanks. The three elder sons had all passed and become officials; only the fourth was home entertaining the geomancer. He respectfully asked: "You promised all four of us would pass. My three brothers have indeed. In talent I'm even above them; I alone haven't passed—why?" The next day the geomancer and the fourth son climbed the grave and looked carefully. The geomancer said: "By this site, all the brothers should pass; if one hasn't, there must be a reason." The son pressed him. The geomancer asked: "How old was your late father when he had you?" The son said: "Father was sixty when I was born; he died at seventy-four; that's another six years now." He asked again: "How old was your mother then?" The son said: "Thirty." The geomancer shook his head: "I know now." The son said: "What do you know?" The geomancer said: "Don't take offense. If you must pass, you must ask the old lady whose blood you are." The son understood. That night he set a rich feast and slowly got his mother drunk. After the second watch he told relatives and servants to go to bed first. With no one around, he knelt: "I have something I dare not say. Does Mother want me to pass?" The mother said: "Your three brothers all passed; I want you to pass most of all. Whatever it is, good or bad, just say it." The son said: "The geomancer says I'm not Father's own blood, that's why I don't pass. I must know who really fathered me before I can pass." The mother loved her youngest; it was a quiet night, no one else, and she was drunk—she let it out: "The geomancer sees truly. At that time your father was already sixty. A certain page in the yamen was young and handsome; I actually had you with him." The son had the truth. The next day he consulted the geomancer. The geomancer said: "You must go there, get the page's bones, and bury them beside the grave; next cycle you'll pass." The son did so, fetched and buried them; next cycle he indeed passed.

看此节，可见暗中杂种，人不及知，故有共风水而贵贱悬隔者，其中不无难言处也。

This shows how mixed blood can be hidden, unknown to anyone. So people may share the same fengshui and still be worlds apart in rank—there is always something hard to speak of.

又解某之父，血衰无子。其母夏月热甚，着单裙睡于床。家蓄有猴公，往奸之。惊醒欲推去，猴欲啮欲抓，推去不得。睡熟神旺，不觉淫情动，即有孕。解父归，妻与言被猴奸之故，曰：“此异物，须杀之。”猴既奸后心亏，走于后门大桃树上，不肯下。解父故与妻戏于树下。猴见人色喜，方下树来。解父槌杀之，即埋于桃树下。后解某生，极聪明伶俐，但跳跃倒地，若猴状。解母心知为猴种也。以无别子，故不杀之。八岁父死，地理为择葬曰：“此地极佳，当出神童才子。此子虽不才，但三年后可登高第。”过三年后，地理复来。解母曰：“汝说三年后此子知变，今轻狂如前，奈何？”地理再往坟细看，归问曰：“此子是安人亲生的，抑妾生乎？”解母曰：“此子非亲生，是邻家丫头与猴生的，欲弃之，我以无子，故血抱以养。”地理曰：“欲此子成器，须得猴骨在，附葬此冢之旁，后日还昌你家。”解母往树下掘之，其骨犹在。特与地理曰：“邻人尚留骨在，当如何处？”地理教择吉日葬之。再三年，果举神童，后为一代名人。（此闻其乡陈地理所传。）

Also: a certain Xie's father had failing vitality and no son. In the summer heat his mother slept on the bed in a thin skirt. The household kept a male monkey; it came and raped her. She woke and tried to push it off; the monkey would bite and claw, and she could not push it away. When she fell back asleep, her vitality stirred; lust arose without her noticing, and she conceived. When Xie's father returned, his wife told him of the monkey rape and said: "This is a freak; it must be killed." After the rape the monkey felt guilty, went to a big peach tree at the back gate, and would not come down. Xie's father pretended to sport with his wife under the tree. The monkey saw them making love, was pleased, and came down. The father clubbed it to death and buried it under the peach tree. Later this Xie was born, extremely clever, but he leapt and tumbled like a monkey. The mother knew in her heart he was monkey-seed. Having no other son, she did not kill him. At eight his father died. A geomancer chose a burial and said: "This site is excellent; it will produce a child prodigy and a man of talent. This boy isn't much now, but in three years he can rise to high rank." After three years the geomancer returned. Mother Xie said: "You said after three years this boy would change; he's still as wild as before—what now?" The geomancer went back to the grave, looked carefully, returned, and asked: "Is this boy your own child, madam, or a concubine's?" Mother Xie said: "He's not my own; a neighbor's maid had him by a monkey; they wanted to abandon him; I had no son, so I took him in and nursed him." The geomancer said: "If you want this boy to amount to something, you need the monkey's bones buried beside this grave; later it will still prosper your house." Mother Xie went under the tree and dug; the bones were still there. She told the geomancer: "The neighbors still have the bones; what should we do?" The geomancer told her to pick an auspicious day to bury them. After another three years he was indeed recommended as a child prodigy, later a famous man of his generation. (This was heard from Geomancer Chen of that district.)

看此节，可见风水之效，捷如影响。人家得好地者，子孙宜守礼法，不可淫欲败德，致漏脉于人也。

This shows that the effect of fengshui is as swift as shadow and echo. Families that get a good site should have their descendants keep rites and the law, not ruin their virtue with lust and leak the bloodline to others.

奸人婢致盗失银

An Affair with a Maid, Then the Silver Is Stolen

宁城一人，姓李名英，年二十餘岁，聪明脱洒，惟耽酒色。常买夏布，往苏州阊门外，寓牙人陈四店。其店兼卖白酒。邻家林廷节，常遣婢京季来买酒。季年方十八，国色娇媚。李英爱之，因而调戏成奸，买簪圈等送之。同店多有谏其勿惹祸者。英与季两少相爱，情深意美，哪肯割断？后廷节察知季与英有奸，呼季责曰：“你与李客私通，我姑恕汝。可密窥英银藏于何处，偷来置些衣装与你，后得享用。”

In Ningcheng there was a man named Li Ying, a little over twenty, clever and uninhibited, only addicted to wine and women. He often bought summer cloth, went outside Chang Gate in Suzhou, and lodged at broker Chen Si's shop. The shop also sold white liquor. Neighbor Lin Tingjie often sent the maid Jing Ji to buy wine. Ji was just eighteen, a rare beauty. Li Ying liked her, teased her into an affair, and bought hairpins and bangles for her. Others in the shop often warned him not to court disaster. Ying and Ji were young and in love, with deep feeling—who would cut it off? Later Tingjie found out that Ji and Ying were having an affair. He called Ji and scolded her: "You've been privately with Guest Li; I'll let you off this once. Secretly watch where Ying hides his silver, steal it, and I'll get you some clothes to enjoy later."

一日，英饮酒娼家。季潜开英房，盗去银一百餘两。及英回店，知银有失，向店主逼取。客伙吴伦曰：“你房内有银，不可远饮娼家；即饮亦宜早归。今荡饮致失，何干主人事？今午见京季入你房中，必此女偷去。你可告于官，我与店主为证。”

One day Ying was drinking at a brothel. Ji quietly opened Ying's room and stole over a hundred taels. When Ying returned to the shop and found the silver gone, he pressed the shopkeeper to make good. Fellow guest Wu Lun said: "If you have silver in your room, you shouldn't drink far off at brothels; even if you drink you should come back early. You caroused and lost it—what's it to the shopkeeper? This noon I saw Jing Ji go into your room; it must be this girl who stole it. You can report it to the officials; I'll witness with the shopkeeper."

英待两日，季不来店，乃告于府。廷节诉英欺奸伊婢，情露惧告，先以失银诬抵。本府张爷审问于证，吴伦、陈四证曰：“亲见季入英房，盗去银是实。”张爷诘曰：“客人房、室女床，二者岂容妄入？季入英房，汝等见何不阻？”伦曰：“英与季私通，亦是实，故英未在店，开门而盗。”张爷审出此情，知银系季偷是的；奈廷节乃府庠生，季考取之第二，只依廷节所诉，断曰：“既有奸情，则失银系是抵饰。以英不合欺奸侍婢，虚词抵赖。陈四为牙，知有奸情，何不谏英早改？待事败而犹偏证，各拟杖惩。”

Ying waited two days; Ji did not come to the shop. He sued at the prefecture. Tingjie claimed Ying had seduced his maid; the affair exposed, fearing a lawsuit, Ying had first used the lost silver as a false countercharge. Prefect Zhang questioned the witnesses. Wu Lun and Chen Si testified: "We saw Ji go into Ying's room with our own eyes; it is true she stole the silver." Prefect Zhang pressed: "A guest's room and a maiden's bed—neither should be entered lightly. If you saw Ji enter Ying's room, why didn't you stop her?" Lun said: "Ying and Ji having an affair is also true; so when Ying wasn't in the shop, she opened the door and stole." Prefect Zhang found this out and knew the silver was indeed stolen by Ji; but Tingjie was a student of the prefectural school, ranked second in the last exam, so he followed Tingjie's complaint and ruled: "Since there was an illicit affair, the lost silver is a covering excuse. Li Ying was wrong to seduce a serving maid and then lie. Chen Si as broker knew of the affair; why didn't he advise Ying to stop sooner? After it blew up he still testified one-sidedly. Each is sentenced to beating."

按：此审李英甚枉。特为客旅，宜谨慎自持，岂有奸人侍婢而不取祸者！今店中多有以妻女引诱客人，成奸后赖其财本者。切宜识透此套，勿入其骗可也。

Note: This judgment was very unjust to Li Ying. Especially as a traveling merchant, one should be careful and self-restrained; who has an affair with someone's maid and does not bring disaster? Nowadays many shops use wives and daughters to lure guests; after the affair they cheat them of their capital. See through this trick; do not fall into it.

姦牙人女被脱骗

An Affair with a Broker's Daughter Ends in a Swindle

经纪廖三号龙潭者，有女名淑姬，年方二八，尚未配人。容如月姊，貌赛花仙，真个女子中班头，绝世无双者。客人张鲁，年二十餘岁，磊落俊雅，颇谙诗书，浪迹江湖。一日，买闽笋数十担，在廖三店中发卖。不时遇风，都放帐未收。日久，见其女丰姿娇媚，日夜想慕，不能安枕。奈廖三家中人众，难以动手。而女亦时于门后偷眼觑鲁。鲁以目挑之，女为俯首作娇羞态。二人情意已通，只阳臺路隔，鹊桥难渡矣。

Broker Liao San, style Longtan, had a daughter named Shuji, just sixteen, not yet betrothed. Her face was like the moon goddess, her looks rivaling a flower immortal—truly first among girls, unmatched in the world. The merchant Zhang Lu, a little over twenty, handsome and elegant, somewhat learned in poetry and books, drifted from place to place. One day he bought several dozen loads of Fujian bamboo shoots and sold them at Liao San's shop. Because of frequent delays, the lot had been sold on credit and the silver was not yet in. Over time, seeing the daughter's charming looks, he longed day and night and could not sleep. But Liao San's household was crowded, hard to act. The girl also sometimes peeked at Lu from behind the door. Lu made eyes at her; she lowered her head in a shy pose. Their feelings had already met; they only lacked a way to come together.

一日，廖三家中早起炊饭，与商人上乡讨帐。张鲁心喜，乘机潜入其房，与廖女成姦。偷情之后，时有私会。

One day Liao San's household rose early to cook, and he went with merchants to the countryside to collect accounts. Zhang Lu was glad, slipped into her room, and they had sex. After that they sometimes met privately.

其母知之，与夫商议曰：“吾女几多豪门求婚，未肯轻许。今被鼠客所玷，须密捕杀之，以消其恨。”廖三曰：“不可。凡妻与人私通，当场捉获，併斩呈官，律方无罪。今女与人通姦，併杀则不忍；单杀客人，被罪不至死，岂死无后话？现今笋帐已完，其银皆在我手。密窥女与姦时，当场捉之，打他半死，以锁繫住，勒其供状，怕他不把笋银献我，彼时亦何说？”妻然之。

The mother found out and discussed it with her husband: "How many rich families have sought our daughter, and we wouldn't lightly agree. Now she's been stained by a rat of a guest. We should secretly catch and kill him to quench the hate." Liao San said: "No. If a wife commits adultery, catching them in the act and killing both, then presenting them to the officials, the law finds you not guilty. Now it's a daughter in an affair: killing both I can't bear; killing only the guest—her crime isn't a capital one, so if he dies there will be later trouble. Right now the bamboo-shoot accounts are settled; the silver is all in my hands. Secretly watch when the girl is with him, catch them in the act, beat him half to death, lock him, force a confession—won't he hand over the shoot silver? Then what can he say?" The wife agreed.

未数日，张鲁果堕其术。鲁曰：“此是我不良。银须以一半还我便罢，不然吾不甘心。”廖三不允，鲁遂告于府，批刑馆。吴爷审出实情，问淑姬：“曾许配人否？”对曰：“未配。”又问鲁：“曾娶否？”鲁已有发妻，乃诳曰：“发妻已死，尚未再娶。”吴爷断曰：“汝二人既未成婚，须断合之，以所勒银准作财礼。”廖三曰：“奸人室女而得成婚，后何以儆？”吴爷曰：“汝牙家常以妻女赖人姦而脱其银，吾岂不知？若不配合，须将汝女官卖，将银究论；张鲁合惩通姦之罪耳。”鲁曰：“一女子安值财礼一百餘两？须判一半还我，准与其女为奁。”吴爷曰：“为商而嫖花街柳巷，尚宜有节；主人室女，岂容欺姦？”鲁且感且哭，尽丧其本，止得一女，又无盘缠可带，即转嫁银三十两而归。

Within days Zhang Lu fell into the trap. Lu said: "This was my fault. Give me back half the silver and we'll drop it; otherwise I won't rest." Liao San refused. Lu sued at the prefecture; it was sent to the judge. Judge Wu found out the truth and asked Shuji: "Have you been betrothed?" She said: "Not yet." He asked Lu: "Are you married?" Lu already had a wife, but lied: "My first wife is dead; I haven't remarried." Judge Wu ruled: "Since neither of you is married, they should be matched; the silver extorted will count as the bride-price." Liao San said: "If a man who seduces a virgin then gets to marry her, how will later people be warned?" Judge Wu said: "You brokers often use wives and daughters to trap guests into affairs and cheat them out of their silver—do I not know? If they aren't matched, I'll have your daughter sold by the government and the silver investigated; Zhang Lu will still be punished for the affair." Lu said: "How is one girl worth over a hundred taels of bride-price? Order half returned to me, counting as her dowry." Judge Wu said: "A merchant who visits brothels should still have limits; how can he seduce the host's virgin daughter?" Lu was both grateful and in tears; he lost all his capital, got only a girl, and had no travel money to take her, so he married her off for thirty taels and headed home.

按：牙家纵容妻女与客人成姦，后脱其财本，此常套也。惜此女不知，为父母作货；张鲁亦不知，而落此套中。犹幸吴爷断与成婚，虽失利，犹得妇也。惜其财本稀少，不得同此女归耳。后之为商者，断合事本难期望，则脱姦宜慎防之。

Note: Brokers letting wives and daughters have affairs with guests, then cheating them of their capital—this is a common trick. Pity this girl did not know she was merchandise for her parents; Zhang Lu also did not know, and fell into the trap. Luckily Judge Wu ordered them married; though he lost the money, he still got a wife. Pity his capital was so thin he could not take the girl home. Later merchants: expecting a court-ordered marriage is hard; better guard carefully against being cheated through an affair.

第十八類 婦人騙

Category 18 · Swindles by Women

哄婶成奸骗油肉

Luring a Sister-in-Law into Adultery to Cheat Them Out of Oil and Meat

两妯娌并坐，适有卖油者过。婶石氏曰：“家下要油用，奈无银可买。”姆左氏曰：“先秤油来，约后还银未迟。”石氏叫入买油，秤定二斤矣。曰：“男人未在家，过两日来接银。”后两日卖油者来，婶曰：“无银，何以处？”姆曰：“再约三日。”婶以此言退之去。又三日，婶曰：“你教我先秤油，今竟无银。你讨些借我还。”姆曰：“你肯依我教，还他何难？”婶曰：“我凡事常依你，把甚物还？”姆曰：“我看卖油后生俊俏，你青年貌美，和他相好一次，油何消还？”婶曰：“恐你后日说。”姆曰：“是我教你，怎敢说？我避在房中，你自去为之。”

Two sisters-in-law were sitting together when an oil seller passed by. The younger, Shi, said, “We need oil in the house, but we have no silver to buy it.” The elder, Zuo, said, “Have him weigh out the oil first; paying later is soon enough.” Shi called him in and bought two jin. She said, “The men aren’t home; come back in two days for the silver.” Two days later the oil seller came. The younger said, “There’s no silver—what now?” The elder said, “Set another three days.” The younger used this to send him away. After three more days the younger said, “You told me to have the oil weighed first, and now there’s no silver. Lend me some to pay him.” The elder said, “If you’ll do as I teach you, paying him is easy.” The younger said, “I always do as you say—what should I pay him with?” The elder said, “That oil-seller lad is handsome, and you’re young and pretty. Have a fling with him once—why pay for the oil at all?” The younger said, “I’m afraid you’ll talk later.” The elder said, “I taught you myself—how would I dare talk? I’ll hide in the inner room; you go do it.”

少顷，卖油者到。石氏思无计可退，强作笑脸，出迎曰：“两次约你接银，奈何无可措办，不如把我还你罢。”卖油者见其眉开眼笑，亦起淫心曰：“你家内有人，莫非哄我？”石氏曰：“丈夫去耕田，伯姆在邻家绩麻。因无人，故与你耍言。”卖油者放心，与入房去。左氏听已拴房门，即密出，将两半篓油倾起，把两半篓水注入，再到房门密听。婶曰：“完了，起去。”卖油者曰：“与我停停。”左氏手持麻筐，跳身出大门外，故扬言曰：“今日尚未午，何耕田的回了？”卖油者闻人言，忙出挑油，恰相遇于门外。左氏问曰：“婶婶油还你否？”卖油者连应曰：“还了！还了！”即挑过一村卖。左氏知其必再来，站在大门候。

Shortly the oil seller arrived. Shi had no way to put him off. She forced a smile, came out to meet him, and said, “Twice I set a date to pay you, and I still can’t raise the money. Better I pay you with myself.” The oil seller saw her beaming, and lust rose in him: “There are people in your house—are you tricking me?” Shi said, “My husband’s in the fields; my sister-in-law is next door spinning hemp. There’s no one here, so I was teasing you.” The oil seller was reassured and went into the room with her. Zuo heard the door bolted, slipped out, poured the oil from both half-baskets, filled both with water, then went back to listen at the door. The younger said, “Done—get up.” The oil seller said, “Stay with me a bit.” Zuo, hemp basket in hand, jumped out the main gate and deliberately called out, “It’s not even noon—how is the field-worker back already?” The oil seller heard, hurriedly came out with his carrying-pole, and met her at the gate. Zuo asked, “Did my sister-in-law pay you for the oil?” The oil seller quickly said, “Paid! Paid!” and carried it off to sell in the next village. Zuo knew he would come back, and waited at the main gate.

近午，卖油者向前。左氏曰：“你尚在此？我婶婶的弟挑桶来打涝，见油一担在宅，家并无人，只婶房有人笑话，疑与卖油人有奸，将油倾在桶去，把半篓水注满，归报其母，母子径来拿奸。及来时，挑油的已去，正在此猜疑。叵知你在此，必拿你作对。”卖油者便行。左氏扯住曰：“我报你知，你须谢我。”卖油者曰：“明日寄两斤油与你。”

Near noon the oil seller came back. Zuo said, “You’re still here? My sister-in-law’s brother came with buckets to fetch water, saw a load of oil in the house and no one around, only laughing from her room, suspected she was having an affair with the oil seller, poured the oil into his buckets, filled the half-baskets with water, went home and told his mother. Mother and son came straight to catch them in the act. By the time they got here the oil carrier was gone; they’re still guessing about it. If they knew you were here they’d surely take you as the culprit.” The oil seller started to go. Zuo grabbed him: “I tipped you off; you ought to thank me.” The oil seller said, “Tomorrow I’ll send you two jin of oil.”

过数日，果寄油来。姆又变说，持与婶曰：“前日我在门站，卖油者复从门前过。我故耍之曰：‘婶婶说油银未还，你适间慌忙说还了，必有缘故。我在此等报叔叔。’卖油者心虚，许我两斤油，今果寄来。此是你换来的，须当补你。”婶曰：“似此半时光景，也是四斤油用，多谢指教。”姆曰：“你若依我，更有别享用处。”

After several days he did send the oil. The elder changed the story, took it to the younger, and said, “The other day I was standing at the gate when the oil seller passed again. I teased him: ‘My sister-in-law says the oil silver still isn’t paid; you hurriedly said it was paid—there must be a reason. I’m waiting here to tell Uncle.’ He felt guilty, promised me two jin; now he’s sent it. This is what you traded for; I ought to make it up to you.” The younger said, “In that half hour I got four jin of oil to use; thanks for the teaching.” The elder said, “If you listen to me, there are more good things to enjoy.”

少顷，有人叫卖肉。姆婶二人叫入，各秤二斤，吩咐再来接银。三日，屠子来接。伯姆秤银七分还之，婶的再约两日。至期，屠子来。伯姆曰：“你依前日套子还他，我方便入房内去。”石氏出，笑对屠子曰：“借你肉，无银可还。今日无人在家，不如把我肉还你。”屠子见其美貌，嬉嬉笑曰：“我只要你腰间些些肉。”石氏曰：“全身都允你，何惜些些？”屠子搂抱入房干事。伯姆潜出，把一担肉都搬入讫，默坐在肉箩边。

Soon someone called selling meat. The two called him in, each weighed two jin, and told him to come back for the silver. In three days the butcher came to collect. The elder weighed out seven fen of silver to pay him; the younger’s was set for two more days. When the time came the butcher arrived. The elder said, “Pay him with the same trick as before; I’ll conveniently go into the room.” Shi came out, smiled at the butcher, and said, “I borrowed your meat and have no silver to pay. Today no one’s home; better I pay you with my meat.” The butcher saw how pretty she was, grinned, and said, “I only want a little of the meat around your waist.” Shi said, “The whole body is yours—why begrudge a little?” The butcher pulled her into the room and got down to business. The elder slipped out, moved the whole load of meat inside, and sat silently by the meat baskets.

屠子与石氏欢罢而出，问曰：“我肉在哪里去？”左氏曰：“叔叔挑与里老去了。”屠子曰：“何得偷我肉？”左氏曰：“你好大胆！叔叔归，见肉担在此，入房门来又闭住，只闻你两人嘻嘻笑话，知是你奸他妻，叫我看住房门。我不好听你动静，故坐在此。你且略坐，停会偷肉的便来了。”屠子挑起空箩便走。左氏扯住曰：“把一肉刀与我做当头。”屠子曰：“托你方便，明日送两斤肉与你。”左氏放手，屠子飞步奔去。

The butcher finished with Shi and came out: “Where has my meat gone?” Zuo said, “Uncle carried it over to the village elder.” The butcher said, “How can you steal my meat?” Zuo said, “You’ve got nerve! Uncle came back, saw the meat load here, the bedroom door shut, heard you two laughing, knew you were with his wife, and told me to watch the door. I didn’t like listening to you, so I sat here. Sit a bit; the meat thief will be back soon.” The butcher picked up the empty baskets and started to run. Zuo grabbed him: “Give me a meat knife as pledge.” The butcher said, “Thanks for the help; tomorrow I’ll send you two jin of meat.” Zuo let go; the butcher fled at a run.

婶埋怨曰：“都是你教我干此事。今丈夫知道，怎么是好？”姆曰：“你不该把师父摊出来。只要你肯食肉，此事何难遮盖？”婶曰：“有甚计策，快说出来。”姆入房，拖一腿肉出，又入拖一腿，曰：“你食肉乎？你报丈夫乎？”婶曰：“你偷肉，不该惊死我。”姆曰：“我惊那人；不惊他去，怎得他肉？”

The younger complained: “It was you who taught me to do this. Now my husband knows—what then?” The elder said, “You shouldn’t have laid your teacher out in the open. If you’ll just eat the meat, this is easy to cover up.” The younger said, “What’s the plan? Quick, say it.” The elder went in, dragged out one leg of meat, then another: “Will you eat the meat, or tell your husband?” The younger said, “You stole the meat; you shouldn’t have scared me to death.” The elder said, “I scared him; if I didn’t scare him off, how would we get his meat?”

两妯娌将肉煮来，把酒对吃。婶曰：“真是一日不识羞，三日吃饱饭。”姆曰：“不是如此说，是半时得快活，一月吃酒肉。”二人呵呵，饱吃一顿，餘者烟干后食。

The two cooked the meat and sat drinking wine across from each other. The younger said, “Truly: one day of shamelessness, three days of full meals.” The elder said, “Not like that—half an hour of pleasure, a month of wine and meat.” They laughed, ate their fill, and smoked the rest for later.

后数日，屠子经过，左氏出，支肉二斤，屠子速行。左氏曰：“亏我婶娘前日被一餐粗打，也该送二斤与她。”屠子将一片丢来，曰：“托你转上，我不得暇。”左氏手提两吊肉，入对婶娘说知，又将来作乐。婶曰：“我会养汉，不如你会光棍。”以后奸门一开，不可胜记。

A few days later the butcher passed by. Zuo came out and took two jin of meat; the butcher hurried on. Zuo said, “Thanks to my sister-in-law taking a beating the other day—you should send her two jin too.” The butcher tossed a piece: “Please put in a word; I haven’t time.” Zuo carried two strings of meat in, told the younger, and they made merry again. The younger said, “I can keep a lover; I’m no match for you as a sharp.” After that, once the door to adultery was open, more followed than can be recorded.

按：石婶不过呆妇人，左姆乃狡猾巧妇；若是男子，当为大棍。遇此巧妇，愚者何不落其圈套？故不唯男子当择交，妇人尤当与良女相伴也。

Note: Aunt Shi was only a dull woman; Elder Zuo was a cunning, clever one. Had she been a man, she would have been a master of the game. Meeting such a clever woman, how would a fool not fall into her trap? So not only should men choose their company; women especially should keep company with good women.

爬灰复骗奸姻母

Incest with a Daughter-in-Law, Then Tricking Her Mother

乡间有一殷实村老，谷豆满仓，鸡鸭成群。只极是村恶，不知礼体。娶一田家女为媳妇，年少貌美，便思爬灰。只怕老妈严厉约束，家法整肃，积年不敢发。

In the countryside there was a well-off old villager, granaries full of grain and beans, chickens and ducks in flocks. He was extremely rustic and coarse, with no sense of propriety. He married a farm girl as daughter-in-law, young and pretty, and thought of sleeping with her. He only feared the old lady’s strict control; household rules were tight, and for years he dared not act.

一日，老妈邻家请饮，村老便调戏其妇。拒不从，遂行强抱。其妇喊起骂出，去外家只十里，便徒步奔告于母。母系村妇，惫赖愤怒，同女来。这村老见媳妇奔告外家，忙叫老妈回，以实情吐告，商量何以抵对。老妈心忖：“亲家村鲁，必不来，惟姻母惫赖必来。”已思有计笼之，故反言要老公曰：“恭喜你，喜事到矣！”村老曰：“往事已错，何须再题。你往日常能干，我凡事皆听你。今须救我，忽致破家。”老妈曰：“何止破家，你该死矣！我今救你来，你越胆大。若听我言，事过再勿起此野意，不但救你，且有好事抬举你。”村老曰：“不愿抬举，只救得这一遭，再不敢起恶意。若再有此，天诛地灭！”老妈曰：“既肯誓过，饶你这遭。你取银四两，作二锭，伏在外客房中，覆大下。若姻母来，我叫她在房来洗澡。你听其洗完，从下出，以两锭银付她两手。她必定拿住，推拒你不得，你便强奸一次。走出外去，事便息矣。”村老曰：“若奸她，则挑她女是真矣。”老妈曰：“你勿管，后事在我身。”村老依言，藏入大去。

One day the old lady was invited next door to drink. The old villager then teased the daughter-in-law. She refused; he forced an embrace. She shouted and cursed her way out. Her natal home was only ten li away; she ran on foot to tell her mother. The mother was a village woman, vicious and furious, and came with her daughter. The old villager, seeing the daughter-in-law had run to tell her natal family, hurriedly called the old lady back, told her the truth, and asked how to answer. The old lady thought: “The girl’s father is rustic; he won’t come. Only the girl’s mother, who’s vicious, will surely come.” She already had a plan to trap her, so she turned and congratulated her husband: “Congratulations! A happy event is here!” The old villager said, “What’s done is wrong; don’t bring it up again. You’ve always been capable; I listen to you in everything. Save me now; don’t let the family be ruined.” The old lady said, “Not only ruin—you’ll die! I save you now and you only get bolder. If you listen to me, never raise this wild idea again after this; I’ll not only save you but have a happy affair to promote you.” The old villager said, “I don’t want promoting; just save me this once. I won’t dare raise a bad thought again. If I do, may Heaven strike me and Earth destroy me!” The old lady said, “Since you’ll swear, I’ll spare you this once. Get four taels of silver, make two ingots, hide in the outer guest room, under the big tub. When the girl’s mother comes, I’ll tell her to bathe in that room. After she’s done washing, you come out from under and put the two ingots in her two hands. She’ll certainly grab them and can’t push you off; then you force her once. Walk out, and the matter will rest.” The old villager said, “If I do her, then teasing her daughter is proven true.” The old lady said, “Don’t worry; the rest is on me.” He followed, and hid under the tub.

少顷，姻母到。老妈出外笑迎曰：“有劳贵步，未曾备轿迎得。”姻母便骂曰：“你家没伦理爬灰老贼，奸我女儿！”老妈故惊曰：“恰才哄我说，媳妇私煮炒吃，被他打骂，因逃归，乃有此恶事？我要和这老狗死！”大声大口，骂恨更切，姻母无待开口矣。因曰：“停会我、你、儿、媳四人，揪住打死，以大粪灌其口，使不为人。”即令媳妇把大鸡鸭宰，设盛馔待姻母。先大骂一场，后待茶果讫，曰：“走路身热，可讨水与洗澡，再好食午。”送姻母入房中洗，老妈入后厨房助妇整酒。及洗讫，下一人出，以银两锭付姻母，两手抱住便奸。及喊叫女儿、亲母，并无人应。其人曰：“她在厨房远，怎叫得知？”赤身难拒，又爱惜两槽银，哑口受奸。事讫，村老曰：“我就是亲家，你勿信女儿说。这成奸也是前缘，我本躲避你，谁知你送来洗澡，反先与你相好，从今再不望你女儿矣。”言罢走出。

Soon the girl’s mother arrived. The old lady came out smiling: “Sorry to trouble your honored steps; we didn’t prepare a sedan to meet you.” The mother cursed: “Your household has no ethics, you old thief who sleeps with his daughter-in-law—you raped my daughter!” The old lady pretended shock: “Just now he fooled me, said the daughter-in-law cooked a stir-fry on the sly, he beat and cursed her, so she fled home—there’s this evil thing? I’ll die with this old dog!” She cursed loudly and bitterly, even more fiercely, so the mother had no need to speak. Then: “In a bit, you, I, son, and daughter-in-law—the four of us—will grab him and beat him to death, pour nightsoil down his mouth, so he’s not fit to be a person.” She had the daughter-in-law kill big chickens and ducks and set a rich meal for the mother. First a round of loud cursing, then tea and fruit, then: “Walking makes you hot; fetch water for a bath, then we’ll eat the noon meal better.” She sent the mother into the room to wash; the old lady went to the back kitchen to help the daughter-in-law prepare the wine. After the washing, a man came out from under, put two ingots in the mother’s hands, held her, and forced her. She shouted for her daughter and the old lady; no one answered. He said, “They’re far off in the kitchen; how would they hear?” Naked, hard to resist, and she coveted the two ingots of silver; she silently endured it. When it was done, the old villager said, “I’m the in-law; don’t believe your daughter’s talk. This affair was also predestined. I was hiding from you; who knew you’d be sent to bathe, and we’d get along first. From now on I won’t look to your daughter again.” Then he walked out.

姻母入厨，见女与老妈方在排馔，想叫时必是不闻，遂午间从容笑饮，不说及爬灰事矣。

The mother went to the kitchen, saw daughter and old lady still arranging the dishes, thought they must not have heard her shouts, so at noon she drank and smiled at her ease, and did not mention the incest.

席罢辞归，老妈再三苦留，女亦曰：“我叫你来做身主，你只要人酒吃，何这等老懵？”姻母曰：“我妇人自身不能作主，怎能做得你主？你公公不是好人，你妈妈贤德。只姑媳不相离，自无恶事矣。”老妈留之不得，以食品果仪厚赠之，欢喜送别而去。凄风骤雨之景色，倏化为光风化日之风景，皆能妇调停之力，———亦一大棍也。

After the meal she took her leave. The old lady repeatedly urged her to stay; the daughter also said, “I called you to stand up for me; you only want people’s wine to drink—how can you be so old and muddled?” The mother said, “I as a woman can’t even stand up for myself; how can I stand up for you? Your father-in-law isn’t a good man; your mother-in-law is virtuous. If mother-in-law and daughter-in-law don’t part, there will be no more evil.” The old lady couldn’t keep her, gave generous gifts of food and fruit, and happily saw her off. The scene of a bitter wind and sudden rain suddenly became a bright breeze and fair sun—all by this capable woman’s mediating power. She too was a master of the game.

按：妇人不可轻易往外亲之家。若彼狡妇与昵夫套合，中多有被其污秽者，谁则知之？若此村老妇之弄姻母，虽一时解纷之巧计，亦彼自知妇人性皆流水，可以利暗诱奸暗陷，必不敢张胆明言也。后人其鉴之。

Note: Women should not go lightly to the homes of relatives by marriage. If a cunning woman and her intimate husband set a trap, many are defiled in it—who would know? As with this old village woman tricking the in-law mother: though it was a clever one-time way to settle a quarrel, she also knew that women’s nature is all like flowing water. They can be secretly lured with profit into a hidden trap of illicit sex, and will never dare speak of it openly. Later people, take warning.

佃妇卖奸脱主田

A Tenant’s Wife Sells Sex to Cheat the Landlord Out of His Land

乡间有一佃户，欠主人苗三冬，算该本息银五两零。冬间，主人来收租，佃母与子谋曰：“苗带今年共欠三冬，明年必起田去，一家无望矣。我看主人富家子弟，必好风月，不如把媳妇哄他奸，拿住必可赖得苗去。”佃曰：“这事可。母亲可与媳妇言。”佃母曰：“还有一件，须要与他奸完了，然后拿住，他方甘心；若未成奸便拿，他是主人，怎肯受屈？又难赚他银矣。”佃曰：“亦可。”佃母方与媳妇言。妇曰：“你子心下实何如？”佃母曰：“我与儿说过了。任你事完成，然后拿他，方抵得苗去。”三人商议已定。

In the countryside there was a tenant who owed the landlord three winters of grain rent, calculated as over five taels principal and interest. In winter the landlord came to collect. The tenant’s mother plotted with her son: “With this year’s, we owe three winters of grain; next year he’ll surely take the land back, and the whole family will have no hope. I see the landlord is a rich family’s son—he must like romance. Better have the daughter-in-law lure him into sex, catch him, and we can write off the rent.” The tenant said, “That can work. Mother, speak to the daughter-in-law.” The mother said, “One more thing: he must finish the act, then we catch him, so he’ll submit. If we catch him before it’s done, he’s the landlord—why would he accept being wronged? And it’ll be hard to cheat him of silver.” The tenant said, “That’s fine too.” The mother then spoke to the daughter-in-law. The wife said, “What’s your son’s true mind?” The mother said, “I’ve spoken with my son. Let you finish the business, then catch him, so it can offset the rent.” The three agreed.

次日饭后，佃推往岳丈家借银来还苗。佃母又吩咐媳妇曰：“主人来无菜，我往上村讨斤肉，再往叔家取个鸡来。苗有还否，须做一东道与主人吃。你须备火炉与主人向。”主人在外已闻。二人去后，妇拾火炉出煽火。主人问曰：“你妈妈哪里去？”妇曰：“去讨菜。”又问曰：“你丈夫何去？”妇曰：“到我娘家去借银还你苗，未知有否？”主人曰：“不消问你娘借，只消问你借。”妇曰：“我若有银，早送来还了。”主曰：“昨夜早同我睡，便与你对苗去。”妇曰：“睡可当得银，今夜来陪你。”主人便起曰：“不待夜间，今日喜得无人，就耍去。你夫借得银来，我背地秤三钱与你买布；若无银，且宽限你明年还。”妇人即允，同入房去。佃户从密处窥见，悄悄出候房门外。只听房内二人欢话，心中自然焦燥，恨不得即打进去。半晌久，主人曰：“起去罢。”妇曰：“从容无妨。”知其完了，在房外高声喝曰：“你和甚人讲话？”打入门去。二人忙不能躲。佃户喝曰：“嗳也！你这贼奸我妻！”便在床上揪下打。妻忙起穿衣，来拿夫手曰：“你嫁我，我不在你家！”佃户曰：“这花娘也要打死！”三人滚作一团，也不能打得。佃母适携肉、鸡从外归，问曰：“何为？”佃曰：“主人奸我妻，我在床上拿住，我要打死这两个。”母指主人曰：“你好人家子孙也，不该干此事。不如讨银与我媳赔丑罢。”主人曰：“便对三年苗与你。”佃取妇脚带，系住主颈曰：“我不肯。”出外取刀磨曰：“定杀死他！”母出外抢刀，曰：“他是官家舍，白的是银，黄的是金，要得他几多。若杀死他，我你也不得安生。”再入劝主曰：“我儿性子不好，你再写田契与他。”主人曰：“亦可。”佃母取纸与写契。佃户立旁，勒要更写毗连田，共凑二十铺，作价二十两。主人亦写与之。佃母再与子商曰：“本意只抵赖苗，不意多得二十两。今晚你须避开，再令媳妇陪他一宵，方服得他心，可保无事。”佃曰：“已得娶妻之本，就让他一宵。”

Next day after the meal, the tenant pretended to go to his father-in-law’s to borrow silver to pay the rent. The mother also told the daughter-in-law: “When the landlord comes there’s no dish; I’ll go to the upper village for a jin of meat, then to uncle’s for a chicken. Whether the rent can be paid or not, we should make a feast for the landlord. You prepare a brazier for him to warm himself.” The landlord outside already heard. After the two left, the wife brought out the brazier and fanned the fire. The landlord asked, “Where did your mama go?” The wife said, “To get dishes.” He asked again, “Where’s your husband?” The wife said, “To my mother’s to borrow silver to pay your rent; I don’t know if he’ll get it.” The landlord said, “No need to borrow from your mother; just borrow from you.” The wife said, “If I had silver I’d have sent it long ago.” The landlord said, “If you’d slept with me last night, I’d have settled the rent with you.” The wife said, “If sleeping can count as silver, come tonight and I’ll keep you company.” The landlord stood: “Don’t wait for night; luckily there’s no one today—let’s play now. If your husband borrows the silver, I’ll secretly weigh out three qian for you to buy cloth; if there’s no silver, I’ll extend you to next year.” The woman agreed; they went into the room. The tenant, watching from a hidden place, quietly came out and waited outside the door. Hearing the two talking happily inside, he was naturally burning, and wished he could burst in. After a long while the landlord said, “Get up.” The wife said, “No hurry.” Knowing it was done, he shouted outside, “Who are you talking with?” and burst in. The two were too hurried to hide. The tenant shouted, “Ai! You thief—you’ve raped my wife!” and dragged him off the bed to beat him. The wife hurriedly dressed, grabbed her husband’s hands: “You married me; I won’t stay in your house!” The tenant said, “This hussy should be beaten to death too!” The three rolled in a heap; he couldn’t really beat them. The mother just then came back with meat and chicken: “What’s this?” The tenant said, “The landlord raped my wife; I caught them on the bed; I’ll beat these two to death.” The mother pointed at the landlord: “You’re a son of a good family; you shouldn’t do this. Better pay silver to my daughter-in-law to make up for the shame.” The landlord said, “I’ll write off three years of grain rent.” The tenant took the wife’s foot-binding, tied the landlord’s neck: “I won’t agree.” He went out, took a knife, and sharpened it: “I’ll kill him for sure!” The mother rushed out, snatched the knife: “He’s from an official family; white is silver, yellow is gold—you can get plenty from him. If you kill him, you and I won’t live in peace either.” She went back in and urged the landlord: “My son’s temper is bad; write him a land deed too.” The landlord said, “All right.” The mother fetched paper for him to write a deed. The tenant stood by and forced him to also write the adjoining fields, totaling twenty pu, priced at twenty taels. The landlord wrote that too. The mother conferred with her son again: “We only meant to write off the rent; unexpectedly we got twenty taels extra. Tonight you should stay away, let the daughter-in-law keep him company one night, so his heart submits and we can keep things safe.” The tenant said, “I’ve already got back the capital of marrying a wife; I’ll yield him one night.”

半午后，方整酒出。佃欲请人陪，佃母曰：“不可，只我老人自陪。”三人同坐，主人只索饭吃回去。佃母曰：“适间儿子蠢性，千万勿怪，我自陪你。”叫儿子先吃饭，往母舅家，故说借银，相添买田。儿去讫，佃母呼妇出陪。主人曰：“你母子妆套弄我，明日必告官理论。”佃母发誓曰：“我若套弄你，我即死在今日！”佃妇泣曰：“若告我便缢死。”主人见妇泣，翻料其非套，曰：“我不管你有套否，今晚更与我睡一夜，便当送你。”佃母连声应曰：“凭媳妇。”妇曰：“拼定陪你。男人若有言，嫁我便是。”主人被此瞒过，只宿一宵而去，安然无后话。

After mid-morning they finally set out the wine. The tenant wanted to invite people to sit with them. The mother said, “No; only I, an old person, will sit with him.” The three sat; the landlord only wanted rice so he could head home. The mother said, “Just now my son was stupid-tempered; please don’t take offense; I’ll keep you company.” She told her son to eat first and go to his maternal uncle’s, pretending to borrow silver to help buy land. The son left. The mother called the wife out to sit. The landlord said, “You mother and son set a trap for me; tomorrow I’ll sue at the yamen.” The mother swore, “If I set a trap for you, may I die today!” The wife wept: “If you sue I’ll hang myself.” The landlord saw her weep and flipped to thinking it wasn’t a trap: “I don’t care if you had a trap or not; sleep with me one more night tonight and I’ll let you off.” The mother quickly said, “Up to the daughter-in-law.” The wife said, “I’ll keep you company for sure. If my man says a word, just marry me off.” The landlord was fooled by this, stayed only one night and left; there was no later trouble.

按：佃母极狡猾，安排圈子已定，又令奸须过手，又令再陪一夜，方得主人心凉。不然，主佃之分，岂空套可笼？此佃母一狡棍也，述与后人知防。

Note: The tenant’s mother was extremely cunning. The trap was already set; she also required the act to be finished, then required another night of company, so the landlord’s heart would cool. Otherwise, given the landlord-tenant distinction, could an empty trap hold him? This tenant’s mother was a cunning crook; this is told so later people know to guard against it.

三妇骗脱三匹马

Three Women Make Off with Three Horses

荆南道上，人多畜马，以租行客，日收其利。有三妇轻身同行，遇马夫牵回马三匹，三妇各租乘一匹。末婶曰：“伯母善乘马者先行，我二人不善乘者随后。”行不十里，末婶叫马夫扶下马小宜。马夫紧抱以下，有讨趣之意。末婶曰：“你讨我便宜。”马夫曰：“不敢！要紧挟些，方不跌。”末婶曰：“看你亦知趣。我久无丈夫的，亦不怕你挟。”马夫曰：“既不怕，前有小茅房，再同我相抱一抱何如？”妇曰：“要赶路。今晚在你家借歇，何如？”马夫曰：“无三铺床。”妇曰：“伯母两人同榻，我只傍床。”马夫曰：“你要傍我床，我不索你租马银。”妇人曰：“人比马价，你又讨便宜。”马夫曰：“两有便宜事，可不好干。”

On the Jingnan roads, people often keep horses to rent to travelers, collecting a daily profit. Three women traveling light met a horse-handler leading back three horses; each woman rented one. The youngest said, “Auntie who rides well, go first; we two who don’t ride well will follow.” Before ten li, the youngest called the handler to help her down to relieve herself. He held her tightly as he lowered her, with a hint of taking advantage. The youngest said, “You’re taking liberties.” The handler said, “I wouldn’t dare! I have to hold a bit tight or you’ll fall.” The youngest said, “I see you know how to play. I’ve long had no husband; I’m not afraid of your holding.” The handler said, “If you’re not afraid, there’s a little thatched hut ahead; how about another hug?” The woman said, “We have to hurry. Tonight we’ll lodge at your house—how about that?” The handler said, “I haven’t three beds.” The woman said, “The two aunts can share a couch; I’ll just stay by your bed.” The handler said, “If you’ll stay by my bed, I won’t charge you horse rent.” The woman said, “A person compared with a horse’s price—you’re taking advantage again.” The handler said, “When both sides get something, isn’t it good to do?”

两人正在此私约，前面次伯母坠马。妇指马夫曰：“快去扶我小姆。”马夫行且回顾曰：“不要哄我。”妇曰：“小姆若跌坏，怕她不在半路歇？我你事一定成矣。”马夫忙奔前去。

While they were privately arranging this, the middle aunt ahead fell off her horse. The woman pointed: “Quick, go help my younger aunt.” The handler went, looking back: “Don’t trick me.” The woman said, “If the younger aunt is badly hurt, won’t she have to rest halfway? Then our business is sure to happen.” The handler hurried forward.

次母跌在路盘坐，挪脚曰：“跌伤了脚，又跌伤了腿。”马夫扶起上马，曰：“须赶路。”次姆曰：“我跌坏了，前去须买补损膏药贴，只好随路歇，赶不得梢头。你前去叫我大姆少待。”因挨延北两遭，前马去不止十餘里。马夫向前去追，后二妇跃马加鞭奔回。

The middle one sat cross-legged on the road, rubbing her foot: “I hurt my foot, and my leg too.” The handler helped her onto the horse: “We must hurry.” The middle one said, “I’m hurt; ahead we’ll have to buy bruise plaster. We can only rest along the way—we can’t make the destination. You go ahead and tell my eldest aunt to wait a little.” By delaying twice like this, the front horse was more than ten li ahead. The handler went forward to catch up; the two women behind whipped their horses and raced back.

马夫前去赶不上，心忖曰：“任她前去，且在此等后二妇来，她自然要等齐同歇矣。”俟久不至，心又忖曰：“想必后路买膏药来。”因问行路人曰：“两妇人骑马的，到哪里了？”路人曰：“两妇人跑马如飞，此去不止二十里了。”马夫又问曰：“骑马是来此的，是去的？”路人曰：“是下去的。你快赶也来不及了。”马夫心无主意，慌忙走回原所，再问路人，皆云：“马去已远。”马夫追回十里，天已晚。再问行人，云：“不见妇人马矣。”三马从两路脱去，前后不能两追，马夫惟怅悒而归。

The handler couldn’t catch the one ahead, and thought: “Let her go on; I’ll wait here for the two behind; they’ll naturally wait to lodge together.” He waited a long time; they didn’t come. He thought again: “They must be buying plaster on the back road.” He asked a passerby: “The two women on horses—where have they gotten to?” The passerby said, “The two women are racing like the wind; they’re more than twenty li that way.” The handler asked again: “Were they riding this way or going away?” The passerby said, “Going back down. Even if you hurry you won’t catch them.” The handler had no plan, hurried back to the original place, asked more people; all said, “The horses are already far.” He chased back ten li; it was already dark. He asked travelers again; they said they hadn’t seen the women or horses. The three horses had been taken off on two roads; he couldn’t chase both front and back. The handler could only head home dejected.

按：此巧脱处全在后妇小宜，与马夫私谈，以惑其心，以缠其时；次又中妇跌马，彼疑真不善骑者，又缠多时，则前马稳脱矣，故赚其前追，又安能及？后两妇奔回，彼唯疑跌伤来迟，岂料反奔而回乎？然亦马夫太痴：安有中途一遇，便许与你歇？马夫有何标致而妇恋之？其言太甘，其中必毒。故就其甜言处，便知是棍也。以妇人而有此高手，世道几何不鬼魅哉！

Note: The clever getaway all hinged on the rear woman relieving herself, talking privately with the handler to confuse his mind and waste his time; then the middle woman falling, so he thought she truly couldn’t ride, wasting more time, so the front horse got away steadily. Thus they lured him to chase ahead—how could he catch up? The two behind raced back; he only thought the injured one was slow—how would he guess they were racing the other way? But the handler was too stupid: who, meeting on the road, would at once promise to lodge with you? What looks did the handler have that women would cling to him? Words too sweet have poison in them. From the sweet talk itself, you know it’s a swindle. When women have this kind of mastery, how can the world not be full of ghosts?

尼姑撒珠以诱奸

A Nun Scatters Prayer Beads to Lure a Woman into Adultery

白鉴妻向氏，大有姿色。鉴专好酒，与妻不甚绸缪。为王军门公干，差之上京。妻向氏在家开纸马店，常遣婢兰香接钱交易。夫去日久，向氏时出店看人。有宁朝贤见之，爱其美，注目看之；向亦不避。朝贤归，与心友曹知高谋，欲诱此妇。曹曰：“若骗妇人，须用一女人在内行事，方易成就。古云‘山贼攻山贼，水寇擒水寇’。此中法华庵尼姑妙真，常往来各家，汝去托之，其事易矣。”朝贤闻教大喜，即寻法华庵来，见了妙真，以银二两送之，托其通纸马店内白鉴之妻；若事成之后，再有重谢。尼姑曰：“此也不难，你三日后来讨回音。”宁再三嘱之而去。

Bai Jian’s wife, née Xiang, was very attractive. Jian loved wine and was not very intimate with his wife. On business for Commander Wang, he was sent to the capital. Wife Xiang kept a paper-offering shop at home, and often sent the maid Lanxiang to take money and handle sales. The husband gone a long time, Xiang sometimes came out to the shop to watch people. Ning Chaoxian saw her, liked her looks, and stared; Xiang did not avoid it. Chaoxian went home and plotted with his close friend Cao Zhigao to lure this woman. Cao said, “To trick a woman you need a woman working inside, then it’s easy. The old saying: it takes a mountain bandit to fight mountain bandits, a water pirate to catch water pirates. Nun Miaozhen of Fahua Convent often visits various houses; go ask her, and the matter will be easy.” Chaoxian was delighted, went to Fahua Convent, saw Miaozhen, gave her two taels, and asked her to make contact with Bai Jian’s wife in the paper-offering shop; if it succeeded, there would be further heavy thanks. The nun said, “Not hard; come in three days for a reply.” Ning charged her again and again and left.

尼姑将手中数珠剪断绳子，捻定在手，往白鉴店前转行几次，不见向氏，空回了。次日又往，见向氏在店坐。尼姑故将断绳珠撒放满地，多有滚在污泥去者，俯躬满地捡之。向氏见，叫之入，以水与洗，又净手讫。尼姑再三拜谢而去。

The nun cut the string of the prayer beads in her hand, held them, walked back and forth in front of Bai Jian’s shop several times; didn’t see Xiang, and went back empty. The next day she went again and saw Xiang sitting in the shop. The nun deliberately scattered the broken-string beads all over the ground; many rolled into the mud; she bent to pick them up all over. Xiang saw, called her in, gave her water to wash, and she cleaned her hands. The nun thanked her again and again and left.

至明日，尼姑买糕、果、饼、面四品，叫人往向氏家谢。向氏喜，遣人请尼姑来吃素酒。席间，向氏问曰：“你几岁出家？”尼姑曰：“我半路出家。”向曰：“因何事出家？”尼曰：“因嫁个人，好赌钱饮酒，终日在外，有夫与无夫同，故誓愿出家。”向氏叹气一声道：“招这人不如勿嫁。”尼见他动心，又问曰：“娘子如何叹气？”赂曰：“我病亦似你。今嫁个人，只好饮酒，从来不要妻子，一年不欢会几次。今又奉差远去，似无夫一般。”尼知此妇有春怨，即乘机曰：“男人心歹者多。唯我庵前宁朝贤，当日爱妻如命，只其妻没福而死。今央我择再娶，谁妇人遇此者，真日日得欢喜也。”向氏听了，口中不语。尼亦不好再调，酒完而去。

The next day the nun bought four items—cakes, fruit, pastry, and noodles—and sent someone to Xiang’s to thank her. Xiang was pleased, and sent someone to invite the nun for a vegetarian drink. At the table Xiang asked, “How old were you when you left home?” The nun said, “I left home halfway through life.” Xiang asked, “Why?” The nun said, “I married a man who loved gambling and drinking, always out; having a husband was like having none, so I vowed to leave home.” Xiang sighed: “Better not marry than marry such a man.” The nun saw she was moved, and asked, “Why does Madam sigh?” Xiang said, “My affliction is like yours. I married a man who only likes drinking, never wants a wife; in a year we don’t happily meet more than a few times. Now he’s been sent far away; it’s like having no husband.” The nun knew this woman had spring longing, and seized the chance: “Many men’s hearts are bad. Only Ning Chaoxian in front of my convent loved his wife like his life; only she had no luck and died. Now he asks me to choose a remarriage; any woman who meets him would truly be happy every day.” Xiang heard and said nothing. The nun did not dare tease further; after the wine she left.

第三日，朝贤整饰衣冠，来庵问回音，妙真曰：“事有九分成了。凡妇人与夫和顺者，极难挑动。昨向氏请我，知她心中恨夫；又别夫日久，但有机会，便可到手。今须讨银与我，办一盛席，请来用好酒灌醉，必在我床睡，你便解开衣，慢慢行事，恣你所为矣。但醒来之时，须备镯甸簪珥类送之，可买其心，方可长久相交。”宁听了拜下：“若如此，死生不忘。今再送银五两，你速作席请来。”

On the third day Chaoxian dressed up and came to the convent for a reply. Miaozhen said, “Nine-tenths done. Women who get along with their husbands are very hard to move. Yesterday Xiang invited me; I learned she resents her husband; and she’s been apart a long time; given a chance, she’s within reach. Now I need silver; I’ll prepare a rich feast, invite her, pour good wine till she’s drunk; she’ll sleep in my bed; you undo her clothes and slowly proceed, do as you like. But when she wakes, have bracelets, hairpins and such ready to give her, to buy her heart, so you can have a long affair.” Ning bowed: “If so, I won’t forget in life or death. Here’s five more taels; quickly make the feast and invite her.”

妙真遣人买好肴好酒，叫厨子整治丰洁，先遣人去请，后自到家邀行。向氏欢喜，同兰香打轿而来。见酒席十分美盛，曰：“你还请何人？”妙真曰：“专请娘子，并无别客。”向氏曰：“一人亦不消如此破费，怎吃得许多？”妙真曰：“我无亲骨肉，多感娘子知己，愿结为姊妹，当个知心人。”向氏笑曰：“我和你知心，不能相爬痒痛。”饮了几杯，问曰：“此酒香而甜，其价必贵。”尼曰：“是前日宁大官送的，亦不识其价。”又劝饮。向氏曰：“酒甜吃得下，只恐易醉。”尼曰：“若醉暂在我房少睡，醒后回去不迟。不知娘子尊量，饮几许方醉。”向氏曰：“夜间恐睡不着，常可饮一瓶。若不饮酒，如何得睡？”尼曰：“若自家人在家，只吃他一杯，便可睡矣。”向氏曰：“我和你说知心话，虽醉只半夜便醒。丈夫在家，只是贪酒，再不要干事。我醒来极是难熬，哪止得我渴想？”妙真曰：“似此有老公的，与我无有的一般。我日间犹过了，只夜来过不得，惟怨前生未修种也。”向曰：“的是如此。今日须极醉，求一夜可忘却。”少顷醉倒，遣兰香先回看家，旋在尼床少睡。

Miaozhen sent people to buy good food and wine, had a cook prepare it richly and cleanly, first sent someone to invite, then went herself to fetch her. Xiang was glad, and came with Lanxiang by sedan. Seeing the feast so lavish, she said, “Who else did you invite?” Miaozhen said, “Only Madam; no other guest.” Xiang said, “For one person you needn’t spend so much; how can we eat so much?” Miaozhen said, “I have no blood kin; I’m grateful Madam is a kindred spirit; I want to become sisters, a true confidante.” Xiang laughed: “You and I are confidantes, but we can’t scratch each other’s itches.” After a few cups she asked, “This wine is fragrant and sweet; it must be expensive.” The nun said, “Master Ning sent it the other day; I don’t know the price.” She urged more drink. Xiang said, “It’s sweet and goes down, but I fear I’ll get drunk easily.” The nun said, “If drunk, sleep a while in my room; head home after you wake, no rush. I don’t know Madam’s capacity—how much before you’re drunk?” Xiang said, “At night if I can’t sleep, I can often drink a bottle. If I don’t drink, how can I sleep?” The nun said, “If one’s own man is at home, one cup from him and you can sleep.” Xiang said, “I’ll tell you a confidante’s truth: even drunk I wake by midnight. Husband at home only greedily drinks, never wants to do the deed. When I wake it’s hard to bear—how can that quench my thirst?” Miaozhen said, “So having a husband is the same as my having none. Daytime I can still get through; only nights I can’t; I only resent I didn’t plant merit in a past life.” Xiang said, “Exactly so. Today I must get very drunk, hoping for one night of forgetting.” Shortly she collapsed drunk. They sent Lanxiang home first to watch the house; Xiang then slept a while on the nun’s bed.

朝贤向氏睡，即来解其衣带，如死去而暖的一样，凭他恣意恋战，其味甚美。少歇又一次，亦不醒。朝贤双手搂定妇人睡，直到半夜醒来，衣已脱去。觉有男子在身边，又觉腰间爽快，浑身通泰，低声问道：“你是何人？”朝贤道：“心肝，我想你几时，今日方才得偷两次，还要明日和我一好。”向氏曰：“你谋既就，切不可与外人知。”朝贤曰：“只尼姑知道，除外何人得知？”

While Xiang was asleep, Chaoxian came and undid her clothes and sash. She was like one dead yet warm; he did as he pleased, and the flavor was very fine. After a rest, once more; still she did not wake. Chaoxian slept holding her with both arms until midnight. She woke; her clothes were already off. She felt a man beside her, and a pleasant ease at her waist, her whole body comfortable. Softly she asked, “Who are you?” Chaoxian said, “Darling, how long I’ve wanted you; today I finally stole two times; tomorrow you must be good with me too.” Xiang said, “Your plot has succeeded; just don’t let outsiders know.” Chaoxian said, “Only the nun knows; who else would know?”

又睡到天微明，向氏起。朝贤以镯甸与之，又抱亲嘴。两人兴浓，再战一次，携手出门。妙真已在候，忍笑不住，曰：“好酒也！”向氏曰：“好计也！”朝贤曰：“好姻缘也！”妙真曰：“既有此好，何以谢我？”紧抱贤曰：“亏我脚酸也，要和我好为谢。”贤曰：“力尽耳，今夜不忘谢。”向氏曰：“从今夜夜都让谢你。”朝贤曰：“后会可长，谢亦可长。”从此常与向氏往来，皆由尼姑此番之引诱也。

They slept till faint dawn; Xiang rose. Chaoxian gave her bracelets, and kissed her. Both still eager, they did it once more, and went out hand in hand. Miaozhen was already waiting, and could not hold her laugh: “Good wine!” Xiang said, “Good scheme!” Chaoxian said, “Good match!” Miaozhen said, “Since it’s this good, how will you thank me?” She tightly hugged Chaoxian: “My legs ache from the work; be good with me as thanks.” Chaoxian said, “I’m spent; tonight I won’t forget to thank you.” Xiang said, “From tonight every night we’ll yield the thanks to you.” Chaoxian said, “Meetings can last; thanks can last too.” From then on he often visited Xiang, all from this lure by the nun.

按：妇人虽贞，倘遇淫妇引之，无不入于邪者。凡妇之谨身，唯知耻耳，唯畏人知耳。苟一失身之后，耻心既丧，又何所不为？故人家唯慎尼姑、媒婆等，勿使往来，亦防微杜渐之正道也。

Note: Even a chaste woman, if led by a wanton woman, will enter into evil. A woman’s guarding her person is only knowing shame, only fearing people will know. Once she’s lost herself, shame is gone—what won’t she do? So households should be careful with nuns, matchmakers, and the like; don’t let them come and go. This is the right way to stop trouble at the start.

第十九類 拐帶騙

Category 19 · Abduction

刺眼刖腳陷殘疾

Stabbing Out Eyes and Severing Feet to Cripple Children

浙中有等棍，常於通衢僻路，專候人家子女十數歲者，或迷路失歸，必拐帶去。擇其女有姿色，又絕聰明者，賣落院為娼。稍愚鈍者，刺瞎其雙眼，教之唱叫路歌曲；又或刖去足掌，致其拐腳。其刖足之法，每於隆冬極寒時，以麻扎幼童足肚，置腳掌於冷水中。浸得良久，以柴木指之曰：「痛否？」童應曰：「痛。」則又浸，及至冷極血凝，指亦不知痛，則以利刃刖斷其足掌，然後用藥敷之。後驅此雙瞽者、拐腳者，叫乞於道，每日責其丐錢米。多者與之飽食，少者痛酷捶打，令乞者方肯哀丐。晚复聚宿舟中。棍得其錢米，置美衣美食，在舟中歌唱為樂。暇或登岸，又四出拐帶，極為民害，而人不知。

In Zhejiang there is a breed of crook that haunts busy thoroughfares and lonely byways, watching for other people’s children of ten years or so. Any child who loses the way home is sure to be carried off. They pick out the girls who are pretty and quick-witted and sell them into brothels as prostitutes. The duller ones they blind in both eyes and teach to sing begging songs along the road; or they cut off the front of a foot to leave the child lame. Their method of cutting the foot is this: in deepest winter, when the cold is most bitter, they bind a child’s calves with hemp and set the feet in cold water. After a long soak they prod them with a stick of firewood and ask, “Does it hurt?” If the child answers, “It hurts,” they soak them longer, until the cold has congealed the blood and the prodding no longer hurts; then they slice off the front of the foot with a sharp blade and dress the wound with medicine. Afterward they drive these blind and crippled children onto the roads to beg, demanding a daily take of money and rice. Those who bring back plenty get a full meal; those who bring back little are savagely beaten, so the young beggars learn to plead piteously. At night they all gather to sleep in a boat. The crooks pocket the money and rice, dress in fine clothes, eat well, and amuse themselves singing on board. In idle hours they go ashore and fan out to kidnap more children. They are a monstrous plague on the people, and no one knows it.

一日，有小丐婆唱叫於路。居旁一老婦曰：「此丐婆好似李意五之女，其聲音亦似，只目瞎耳。」丐婆曰：「吾父正是李意五，吾有哥名鴉兒。五年前我往外婆家，不識路，被人引去，刺瞎兩眼，每日遣出叫化。有錢米歸，則有食；丐得稀少，便痛打無食，極是苦楚無奈。你聲音似我鄰居王二姆一般，千萬叫我娘與哥來認我，超度我出此地獄，你陰功如天。」王二姆聽其敘來歷皆真，收留入家曰：「你母今年已死，你兄遷居上巷，即遣人去喚來。」彼此皆相認得，遂具狀告於縣，批與主簿審。差人船中提二棍到。棍即用銀賄主簿；又用銀二十兩買其兄李鴉兒曰：「你令妹是他人拐帶，我收與眾乞合夥，非我刺她眼。況今已雙瞽，亦無人娶，不如與丐子為伴，亦不虧她衣食。」兄與官都得銀了，拘審時哥不堅認，主簿仍斷與棍去。棍引到船，撐入湖心痛打，以儆他丐，使後不敢漏洩。李丐婆叫屈連天，淒楚不忍聞。

One day a little beggar girl was singing for alms on the road. An old woman who lived nearby said, “This beggar girl looks just like Li Yiwu’s daughter—and her voice is like hers too, only the girl is blind.” The beggar girl said, “My father is Li Yiwu, and I have a brother named Ya’er. Five years ago I was on my way to my grandmother’s and did not know the road; someone led me away, stabbed out both my eyes, and has sent me out begging every day since. If I come back with money and rice, I get to eat; if I collect too little, I am beaten and given nothing—my misery is past bearing. Your voice sounds just like my neighbor Auntie Wang Er. I beg you, call my mother and brother to come claim me and lift me out of this hell; your hidden merit will be as high as Heaven.” Auntie Wang Er heard her story, and every detail rang true. She took her into her house and said, “Your mother died this year, and your brother has moved to Upper Lane; I will send for him at once.” Brother and sister knew each other at sight, and they filed a complaint at the county yamen. The case was handed to the registrar for trial, and runners dragged two of the crooks off the boat. The crooks at once bribed the registrar with silver, and for another twenty taels they bought off the brother, Li Ya’er, saying: “Your sister was kidnapped by someone else; I took her in to join my band of beggars—it was not I who blinded her. Besides, she is blind in both eyes now and no one will marry her. Better she stay with the beggars as a companion; she will not lack for food or clothing.” The brother and the registrar had both taken the silver, so at the hearing the brother would not stand by his identification, and the registrar awarded the girl back to the crooks. They took her to the boat, poled out to the middle of the lake, and beat her savagely as a warning to the other beggars never to let the secret out. The beggar girl Li cried her innocence to high heaven; the anguish of it was unbearable to hear.

船到向鄉官後門，聞溪中叫死聲，甚可憐，遣二家人去牽其船來，問打何人。眾丐指曰：「打李丐婆。」鄉官問：「因何打？」丐婆不敢說，只苦情求救。鄉官令引丐婆異處，再問曰：「你因何被這等苦打？明說來，我便救你。」李丐婆一一敘其前由。向鄉官聞情淒愴，不勝發忿，即鎖住四棍，並引眾丐入見太府，代陳其冤苦。太府亦切恨之，將四棍各打三十，曰：「此罪雖凌遲碎剮未足懲其罪，可鎖於府前，令眾人共毆之，以洩其忿。」眾人知此棍情，都來手毆石打，四棍一時皮破血吐，立刻盡死。後瞽目、拐腳眾丐，各問其鄉貫，家有人者，令其收養；無親屬者，各送入養濟院。人盡感向鄉官之仁，能除此四孽棍。

The boat happened to come by the back gate of a retired official named Xiang. Hearing cries of mortal agony from the creek—pitiful beyond words—he sent two servants to haul the boat in and asked who was being beaten. The beggars pointed and said, “They are beating the beggar girl Li.” The official asked why. The girl dared not say; she only begged in her misery to be saved. He had her led apart from the others and asked again: “Why were you beaten so cruelly? Tell me plainly, and I will save you.” The girl Li told the whole story from the beginning. Hearing it, Xiang was grief-stricken and could not contain his fury. He clapped the four crooks in chains on the spot, led the whole band of beggars in to see the prefect, and laid out their wrongs on their behalf. The prefect too burned with hatred. He had each of the four given thirty strokes and declared: “For a crime like this, even death by slow slicing would not be punishment enough. Chain them in front of the prefecture gate and let the crowd beat them, to vent the people’s rage.” Everyone who knew what these crooks had done came to strike them, with fists and with stones; in moments the four were torn and vomiting blood, and all died on the spot. Afterward, the blind and crippled beggars were each asked their native place. Those with families were sent home to be cared for; those without kin were placed in poorhouses. The people were all grateful for Xiang’s humanity in rooting out these four fiends.

按：人家子女幼稚，不可令其單行，亦不可帶金銀鐲錢。若偶遇此等棍，悔何可及！其防於未失之先可也。今後官府遇瞎拐群集處時，遣人查其居止，及提問一二瘸瞎緣由，或訪得此等棍，則除一棍勝去一狼虎也，功德高於浮屠矣。

Note: People with young children must never let them go about alone, nor let them wear gold or silver bangles. Should a child chance to meet such crooks, regret comes too late. Guard against the loss before it happens. Henceforth, whenever officials come upon a place where blind and crippled beggars gather, they should send men to find out where they lodge and question one or two of them about how they came to be lame or blind. If crooks like these are unearthed, then removing one crook is better than removing a wolf or a tiger—and the merit of it is higher than any pagoda.

太監烹人服精髓

A Eunuch Cooks Boys and Eats Their Marrow

朝廷往往聽言利之臣，命太監四出抽分，名為徵商抑末以重農本，實則商稅重而轉賣之處必貴，則買之價增，而買者受其害；商不通而出物之處必賤，則賣之價減，而賣者受其害。利雖僅取及商，而四民皆陰耗其財，以供朝廷之暗取，尤甚於明加田稅也。且徵榷之利，朝廷得一，太監得十，稅官得百，巡卒得千，是民費千百金，以奉朝廷之一金，益上者少而損下者無涯矣。然巡卒、稅官之實溪壑，猶是普天率土之民得飽暖也。特不耕不織而魚肉下民，不免坐蠹天地間服食。若太監攘剝既多，崇聚盈溢，視錦繡如敝葉，視金玉如瓦礫。服食器用，皆與天子同；指使承順，如奉天子同。人間福分享受，無不窮極；獨恨不能淫樂女色，所少者此耳。嘗命左右，訪有復生陽物之方，購以萬金。

The court time and again heeds ministers who preach profit and sends eunuchs out across the country to levy duties on goods—in the name of “taxing merchants and curbing the secondary trades to strengthen agriculture, the root.” In truth, when taxes on trade are heavy, goods grow dear wherever they are resold: prices rise for the buyer, and the buyer suffers. When trade stops moving, goods grow cheap where they are produced: prices fall for the seller, and the seller suffers. The profit may seem to come only from merchants, but all four classes of the people are silently bled of their wealth to feed the court’s hidden exactions—worse than openly raising the land tax. Worse still, of every levy taken, the court gets one share, the eunuch ten, the tax officials a hundred, and the patrol runners a thousand. So the people pay out hundreds or thousands of taels to deliver a single tael to the court: little gained above, measureless harm below. And yet, when runners and tax clerks stuff their bottomless gullets, at least it is folk of this same realm who get fed and warmed. But men who neither plow nor weave and yet make fish and meat of the common people are nothing but grubs, battening on the food and clothing of the world. As for the eunuchs: having plundered and squeezed until their hoards pile high and overflow, they look on brocade as dead leaves and on gold and jade as rubble. Their dress, their food, their vessels all match the Son of Heaven’s, and the minions at their beck and call wait on them as they would wait on the Son of Heaven. Of every blessing and pleasure the human world holds, they enjoy the uttermost; their one regret is that they cannot have the pleasure of women—that alone is denied them. One of them once bade his attendants hunt for a method to regrow the male organ, with ten thousand taels as the reward.

有方外道士，利得其金也，私臆懸度，謂「古方云『土以土補，木以木補，人以人補』，意必食人可補人也。」妄去獻方云：「烹童男膾肝脯肉，食其精髓，則精液充滿，陽物復生，可姦婦生子矣。」閩高閹信之，先售以百金，候服有驗，再來領萬金。由是，命牙爪往窮鄉僻邑，買貧民幼童，詐云「高衙欲養為子，日後富貴無窮。」貧民信之，多賣以博眼前重利，且希望後日富貴。後先買者，難以稽數。但鬻子之家，有託人往​​查己子者，並無聲息；即衙中走僕，亦不知內之養子若何也。原來買之幼童，盡養以錦衣美食。廚子能烹調一童以進食，賞銀十兩，深禁其秘密。每殺一童，廚子提刀追趕，眾童各涕泣奔呼，候其走熱氣揚，則執其肥者烹之。

A wandering Daoist, greedy for that gold, made a wild guess of his own: “The old formulas say earth is mended with earth, wood is mended with wood, man is mended with man—so eating human flesh must mend a man.” He went recklessly and offered up a recipe: “Boil a young boy, mince the liver, dry the flesh, and eat the essence and marrow. Then the vital fluid will be replenished, the organ will grow back, and you can lie with women and father sons.” Eunuch Gao of Fujian believed him. He paid a hundred taels up front; if the dose proved effective, the man could return for the ten thousand. From then on he sent his henchmen into poor villages and remote counties to buy young boys from destitute families, lying that “the Gao household wants to raise them as sons, and later they will have wealth and honor without end.” The poor believed it, and many sold their sons for the heavy profit in hand, hoping besides for riches to come. How many were bought in all is impossible to reckon. But some of the families who had sold sons sent people to ask after their boys—and there was never any word. Even the servants who ran errands in the yamen had no idea how the adopted sons inside were faring. In fact, the purchased boys were all kept in brocade and fed the finest food. Any cook who could prepare a boy for the table was rewarded with ten taels, and the secret was guarded under the strictest ban. Each time a boy was to be killed, the cook chased the children with a knife while they ran weeping and screaming; when they had run themselves hot and their blood was up, he seized a fat one and cooked him.

內有一童十二歲，跪廚子涕泣哀告，叩頭求救。廚子亦淚曰：「吾怎能救你，吾亦不奈何墮此也。」有頃，外人傳某鄉官相拜。廚子曰：「憑你命，吾放你出去，外有鄉官相拜，你扯其衣死哀求救；肯帶你去，則你可生，我代你死罷。你可傳知外人，切勿將子賣入太監府也。」此幼童直奔至鄉官前，哀告：「廚子要殺我！」太監即令查拿廚子斬首，———彼恨其縱出此童也，笑顏諭幼童入後。幼童死扯鄉官衣求救。鄉官疑有緣故，為之帶出。幼童歷敘內中殺諸童之由，鄉官不勝嗟嘆。思起本未得諸童買來之由，又無廚子證據，亦不敢留養此童，遣其出外別投生。此童後流丐於建郡等處。人問其太監府之事，多能言其中之富貴，皆非人世所有也。自後方知太監之食人，始不肯以子賣之。近年，高閹以罪去。其鬻子之父母，累十百候於途，並不見一幼子與姦閹生去者，無不墮淚，痛其子之必遭烹也。

Among the boys was one of twelve who knelt before the cook, weeping and pleading, knocking his head on the ground and begging for his life. The cook wept too: “How could I save you? I fell into this against my own will as well.” Presently word came from outside that a certain retired official had come to pay a call. The cook said: “I will leave it to your fate: I am letting you out. A retired official is calling—clutch his robe and wail for your life. If he will take you away, you may live, and I will die in your place. And tell the people outside: never sell a son into a eunuch’s house.” The boy ran straight to the official and cried out, “The cook is going to kill me!” The eunuch at once ordered the cook seized and beheaded—he hated him for letting the boy slip out—and with a smiling face told the boy to go back inside. The boy clung for dear life to the official’s robe, begging to be saved. The official suspected there was more behind it and took the boy out with him. The boy recounted in full how the children inside were being slaughtered, and the official sighed without end. But reflecting that he had never learned how the boys had been bought in the first place, and that with the cook dead there was no witness, he dared not keep the boy himself; he sent him out to find some other way to live. The boy later drifted as a beggar around Jian Prefecture and thereabouts. When people asked him about the eunuch’s household, he could tell them much of the wealth and splendor within—things this world has never seen. Only then did people learn that the eunuch ate human flesh, and only then did they refuse to sell him their sons. In recent years Eunuch Gao was removed for his crimes. The parents who had sold their sons waited along the road in scores and hundreds, and not one saw a young son come out alive with that vile eunuch. There was none who did not shed tears, grieving that his boy must surely have been cooked.

按：貧民賣子，極為至愚。若不能養，何不若鳳陽府父子俱丐，猶可骨肉相保。必不得已，唯可賣之富戶為僕。固不可供太監之啖，亦不可賣入庵寺為行童侍者，其賤尤在乞丐下也。國家置閹尹，以供掃除傳命耳。至使握利權，享用已極，更思生陽物淫婦人，為不可必得之事。雖食人而可為汝欲扶已朽之軀，曾不惜人渾全之命乎？是可忍也，孰不可忍也！孟子曰：「善戰者服上刑」，猶為強兵而殺人也；此為何事而視人命如草菅乎？王法若明，當不令此閹得生還矣。

Note: For poor people to sell their sons is folly of the deepest kind. If you cannot raise them, why not do as they do in Fengyang Prefecture, where father and son beg together? At least flesh and blood stay together. If there is truly no other way, sell them only to a rich household as servants. You must never feed them to a eunuch’s appetite, nor sell them into convents and temples as boy servants and attendants—that degradation is lower than beggary itself. The state keeps eunuchs to sweep the palace and carry messages, nothing more. To let them grasp the power of profit, glut themselves on every luxury, and then dream of regrowing the organ so they can debauch women—a thing they cannot possibly have! Even if eating human flesh could feed your lust and prop up your rotting carcass, have you no pity for another person’s whole and unbroken life? If this can be endured, what cannot be endured? Mencius said, “Those skilled in war should suffer the heaviest punishment”—and that was for killing to make an army strong. What cause is this, that human lives should be treated as weeds? If the law of the land were truly enforced, this eunuch would never have been allowed to return alive.

第二十類 買學騙

Category 20 · Buying School Places

诈面进银于学道

A Staged Face-to-Face Bribe to the Education Commissioner

凡学道出巡，各处棍徒云集追随，专体探富家子有谋钻刺者，多方献门路，以图蛊骗。或此路不售，后一帮又生一端以投，年年有堕其术者。但受骗之家羞以告人，故后次人又蹈之。

Whenever the education commissioner goes on tour, crooks from every quarter gather and follow him, hunting for rich men’s sons who want to buy influence. They offer openings of every kind, hoping to cheat them. If one pitch does not take, another gang comes along with a different angle; every year some fall for it. But the families who are cheated are too ashamed to tell anyone, so the next people walk into the same trap.

有一学道，考选至公，不纳分上。忽一棍自言能通于道者，人不之信。棍曰：“此道爷自开私门，最不喜人央分上前途。唯对手干者，百发百中，但人不敢耳。如真肯干者，但要现钱，彼当面接之，可稳保成就。”赵甲问曰：“从何处献之？”棍曰：“候退堂后，先用手本开具某县某人银若干，求取进学。彼肯面允，便进上银；如不允，银在我手，彼奈我何？”赵甲曰：“我要在旁亲看。”棍曰：“自然与你亲看。学道的二门，其缝阔一寸，从外窥之，直见堂上，任你看之。”赵甲曰：“若道肯亲手接银，吾敢投之。”即写手本，以手帕包银二百两作一封。

There was an education commissioner, scrupulously fair in his examinations, who took no private requests. Suddenly a con man claimed he could get through to the commissioner; no one believed him. The con man said, “This commissioner opened a private door himself. He hates it when people send introductions through someone else. Only those who deal with him face to face succeed every time—people just don’t dare. If you truly mean to do it, all you need is ready money; he’ll take it in person, and success is as good as guaranteed.” Zhao Jia asked, “Where do we present it?” The con man said, “Wait until he leaves the hall. First use a petition listing such-and-such county, so-and-so, so many taels of silver, asking to be admitted to the school. If he agrees in person, then hand over the silver; if he doesn’t, the silver is still in my hands—what can he do to me?” Zhao Jia said, “I want to stand beside you and watch for myself.” The con man said, “Of course you’ll watch for yourself. The second gate of the education yamen has a crack an inch wide; peeking from outside you can see straight into the hall—watch as you please.” Zhao Jia said, “If the commissioner will take the silver with his own hands, I’ll dare to go in.” He wrote a petition and wrapped two hundred taels in a handkerchief as one packet.

下午出堂，往道前候之。棍曰：“要二包过门银。”甲付与之。将退堂之际，棍以银与手本挨入堂去，嘱甲曰：“才封门时，即要在门缝来看。”及道退堂后，甲于二门缝中看，见道仍旧纱帽圆领而出。棍先以手本高递上，一门子接进。道展看了，笼入袖中去。棍又高擎一封银上，道顾门子，门子接上银。道一看即转身，门子随后捧银包而入。棍趋至二门，隔门谓甲曰：“好了，好了，事已妥矣！你见否？”甲应曰：“我亲见了，果是自接。”棍曰：“今夜不能出，我你须在门内外宿矣。”甲曰：“但得事妥，不吃晚饭亦好。”

That afternoon they went out and waited in front of the commissioner’s office. The con man said, “I need two packets of silver to get through the gate.” Jia paid him. As the session was about to end, the con man took the silver and the petition and squeezed into the hall, telling Jia, “The moment they seal the gate, come look through the crack.” After the commissioner left the hall, Jia looked through the crack in the second gate and saw a man still in gauze cap and round-collared robe come out. The con man first held the petition high; a yamen attendant took it in. The commissioner opened it, looked it over, and tucked it into his sleeve. The con man then held a packet of silver high; the commissioner glanced at the attendant; the attendant took up the silver. The commissioner glanced at it and turned; the attendant followed him in, carrying the silver packet. The con man hurried to the second gate and said through the door, “It’s done, it’s done! Did you see?” Jia said, “I saw it myself; he did take it with his own hands.” The con man said, “I can’t go out tonight; you and I will have to sleep on either side of the gate.” Jia said, “If the business is settled, going without supper is fine.”

次日开早门，棍与甲方同出，即到甲店拜贺。甲大设席待之。棍曰：“高取后须厚谢我。”甲曰：“加一谢是定规，不加亦不减矣。”此为信棍之戒。

The next morning when the early gate opened, the con man and Jia went out together and went to Jia’s inn to offer congratulations. Jia laid on a big feast. The con man said, “After you place high, you must thank me generously.” Jia said, “Ten percent as thanks is the fixed rule; I won’t add a bit, and I won’t subtract a bit.” Let this be a warning against trusting crooks.

后揭晓日，本生无名。棍查不见踪，方知前受银之道，乃此棍先与宿衙人套定，盖妆假道也。

When results day came, this candidate’s name was not on the list. The con man was nowhere to be found. Only then did they realize that the “commissioner” who had taken the silver was this same con man: he had first arranged with the night-duty yamen men to dress up as a fake commissioner.

二门望入堂上，虽可亲见，终是路遥，哪见得真？故落此棍骗而不知。若真道自接银，何必衣冠出？何必堂上递手本？又何必堂上交银？独不可私递手本乎？况堂上有宿衙人役，岂私受银之地？此村富不识官体，故以目见为稳，不知与你目见，正所以骗你也。

Looking from the second gate into the hall, though you can see it yourself, the distance is still great—how can you see clearly? So they fell for this swindle without knowing it. If a real commissioner were taking silver himself, why come out in full dress? Why present a petition in the hall? Why hand over silver in the hall? Couldn’t the petition be passed privately? Besides, the hall has night-duty staff—how is that a place to take silver in secret? This rustic rich man did not know official form, so he took seeing with his own eyes as proof; he did not know that letting you see with your own eyes was exactly how they cheated you.

乡官房中押封条

A Sealing Strip Applied in a Retired Official’s Inner Room

富人钱一，欲为子买进学。歇家孙丙，有意骗之，与之言曰：“此中李乡官，原与学道同僚，二人极相得。今若说一名进学，此断可得。吾试与商议之。”钱一曰：“可。”孙丙往匠铺，见两挂箱一样，用钱三钱买其一，又以银二分定后只，嘱曰：“我停会引人来买，更出三钱，不可别换。”又买两把一样的锁。后以挂箱与锁付李乡官家人曰：“你可秤定二百两石头，装在挂箱内，外加锁之，放在你家主房内。少顷，我领人央你老爷说进学，以二百两好银与你封。你把放银的箱收入，换放石的箱出来，然后将这银与我均分。”李家人许曰：“可。”

Rich man Qian Yi wanted to buy his son a place in the school. Innkeeper Sun Bing meant to cheat him, and said, “There’s a retired official named Li here who was once a colleague of the education commissioner; the two are very close. If we speak for one name to enter the school, we can certainly get it. Let me try discussing it with him.” Qian Yi said, “All right.” Sun Bing went to a carpenter’s shop, saw two identical chests, bought one for three qian, and put down two fen of silver to reserve the second, telling them, “In a bit I’ll bring someone to buy it; he’ll pay another three qian—don’t swap it for a different one.” He also bought two identical locks. Then he gave a chest and lock to Official Li’s servant: “Weigh out two hundred taels of stones, put them in the chest, lock it from the outside, and put it in your master’s room. Shortly I’ll bring someone asking your master to speak for school admission, with two hundred taels of good silver for you to seal. You take in the chest with the silver, bring out the chest with the stones, then split this silver with me.” Li’s servant agreed: “All right.”

孙丙领李家人来，对钱一说：“我面见李老爷了，他道此事容易，只把现银对于他家人看过锁住，送到他家加封条，仍以银箱付还我，以锁匙付他收。待有名进学之后，将原银谢他，不得开箱再换。”钱一曰：“在你家借一挂箱来用。”孙丙曰：“新锁有，挂箱可往街买之。”领钱一家人，以银三钱，往铺买到。钱一将银二百两同李家人、孙丙三面对定，收入挂箱中，外加锁定。

Sun Bing brought Li’s servant and told Qian Yi: “I saw Lord Li in person. He said this is easy: just have the ready silver shown to his servant, checked and locked, sent to his house for a sealing strip, then the silver chest is returned to me and the key given to him to keep. After the name is on the list for the school, the original silver is his thanks; the chest must not be opened and swapped.” Qian Yi said, “I’ll borrow a chest at your place.” Sun Bing said, “I have a new lock; the chest can be bought on the street.” He led one of Qian’s people, paid three qian, and bought it at the shop. Qian Yi, with Li’s servant and Sun Bing looking on, checked two hundred taels of silver, put them in the chest, and locked it from the outside.

孙丙负银，同钱一到李乡官家，求加封条。李乡官推病，在厅左房内坐。李家人持箱入门边曰：“银已看对阴白，只讨一封条。”李乡官曰：“既看明白，还他自收，来接封条。”李家人仍以银箱出，再领出一封条，对三面封讫。钱一解锁匙，付李家人收。孙丙复负银箱归，交与钱一自收藏，皆谓事极妥矣。

Sun Bing carried the silver and went with Qian Yi to Official Li’s house, asking for a sealing strip. Official Li claimed illness and sat in the room to the left of the hall. Li’s servant held the chest at the doorway: “The silver has been checked; it’s good yin-white silver.
