We only ask for one sealing strip.” Official Li said, “Since it’s been checked clearly, give it back for him to keep, and come get the sealing strip.” Li’s servant still brought the silver chest out, then brought out a sealing strip, and sealed it before the three of them. Qian Yi handed over the key to Li’s servant. Sun Bing carried the silver chest back and gave it to Qian Yi to keep; all thought the matter perfectly secure.

及揭晓，钱一子无名。孙丙曰：“事不成，银现在，可速收拾归，免得李家人来索轿价。”钱一既失望，怏怏而归。及到半路，叫匠人开锁启视，则皆石头矣。惊异复回，大闹歇家曰：“你何通同骗我？”孙丙曰：“我与你当面干事，何处是骗你？若三面共开挂箱，犹怪得李家；今去半日，私自开箱，我那知中间是银是石？”钱一明知是孙、李合骗，只事无凭证，谅是难取，但辱骂歇家一场而归。此为信乡官之戒。

When the results were posted, Qian Yi’s son had no name on the list. Sun Bing said, “It didn’t work; the silver is still here. Pack up and head home quickly, lest Li’s people come asking for sedan-chair fees.” Qian Yi, disappointed, headed home dejected. Halfway there he called a carpenter to open the lock and look: all stones. Shocked, he went back and made a scene at the inn: “How did you collude to cheat me?” Sun Bing said, “I did the business with you face to face; where was the cheat? If the three of us had opened the chest together, you could still blame the Li household; now after half a day’s travel you opened the chest in private—how would I know whether inside was silver or stone?” Qian Yi knew perfectly well that Sun and Li had cheated him together, but there was no proof; he figured the silver would be hard to recover, so he only cursed the innkeeper roundly and headed home. Let this be a warning against trusting retired officials.

按：两挂箱共样，本是难辨。但加封条，只须在外封之，何必持入内禀乃请封条乎？向令当时若告，追究卖挂箱之家，问两箱何以一样，或能证出孙丙先买其一，后领人买一。或遇明官，便可从中勘出换包之骗矣。

Note: Two chests of the same pattern are hard to tell apart to begin with. But adding a sealing strip only needed to be done outside; why take it inside to report and then request the strip? If at the time one had filed a complaint, investigating the shop that sold the chests, asking why two were alike, one might have proved that Sun Bing first bought one, then led someone to buy the other. Meeting a clear-sighted official, the swap-the-package cheat could have been found out from this.

诈封银以砖换去

Sealed Silver Swapped Out for Bricks

建宁府郝天广，世家巨富，有几所庄，多系白米。时建宁无价，其智家罗五闻省城米价高腾，邀主人带二仆，以米十馀船装往省粜。时宗主王爷发牌考延、建二府，各有告示，将考儒童。米才上船，有一客人带二仆，来搭船往省。船中暇坐，问其何干。答曰：“王爷家来投书者。”后又谈及可夤缘之事，天广有长子出考，言甚合意；只宗主前考甚公，并无私窦，未敢深信其事。

Hao Tianguang of Jianning Prefecture came from an old rich family, with several estates, mostly of white rice. At the time Jianning had no good price; his steward Luo Wu heard rice prices in the provincial capital were soaring, and invited his master, with two servants, to load ten-odd boats of rice to sell in the capital. Just then Commissioner Wang had issued a notice examining Yan and Jian prefectures; there were proclamations that boy-students would be examined. The rice had just been loaded when a guest with two servants came to catch a boat to the capital. Sitting at leisure on the boat, they asked what his business was. He answered, “I’ve come from Commissioner Wang’s household to deliver a letter.” Later they talked of things one might pull strings for. Tianguang had an eldest son sitting the exam; the talk suited him well. Only, the commissioner had examined very fairly last time, with no private opening, so he did not quite dare believe it.

至省中，棍辞别去，曰：“王爷有公子在学，必共看卷。试与谈尊府事，倘许诺，我再出回你诺；若不出，则事不谐耳，再亦无信。”天广曰：“是也。”密遣一仆，踪迹棍所去处，果入学道衙去。数日后出来曰：“事谐矣。可将银对定，以我皮箱藏之，外加封条，银仍与你自收掌。后有名进学，即以皮箱银交出与我。”天广思“银虽对定，仍是我藏，有何不可？”即依言对讫，不知此棍有甚法。银明是天广自投自锁，棍只加封票一条而去，再约曰：“今夜间公子或可潜出，我与之同看过，事即美矣。”连候数夜不来，天广以皮箱开看，其内尽是砖石，前银已被赚去矣。此为封银防换之戒。

At the provincial capital the crook took his leave: “Commissioner Wang has a young master in the school; they are sure to look at papers together. I’ll try talking about your honorable house; if he agrees, I’ll come out and bring you the yes; if I don’t come out, the matter isn’t on, and there will be no further word.” Tianguang said, “Right.” He secretly sent a servant to track where the crook went; indeed he went into the commissioner’s yamen. After several days he came out: “It’s arranged. Weigh out the silver, hide it in my leather case, add a sealing strip outside; the silver stays in your own keeping. After the name is on the list for school, you hand the leather case of silver over to me.” Tianguang thought, “Though the silver is checked, I still keep it—what’s wrong with that?” He followed the instructions and weighed it out, not knowing what method this crook had. The silver was clearly put in and locked by Tianguang himself; the crook only added one sealing ticket and left, and further agreed: “Tonight the young master may slip out; I’ll look it over with him, and the matter will be fine.” They waited several nights; he did not come. Tianguang opened the leather case: all bricks and stones; the silver had been cheated away. Let this be a warning: when sealing silver, guard against a swap.

按：买进学、买帮补，甚至买举人，此事处处有之，岁岁有之，而建宁一府，叠遭骗害为甚。盖建郡民富财多，性浮轻轻故也。虽累受骗，而继起营买者未已，此光棍途中常以逢考建宁为一桩好生意也。特其封银法，至今人看不破。明以银与之同封，复还我收，及棍去后开之，则皆砖石矣；或以为有一遁银法。如此神矣哉，上智难防也！唯明鉴乎此，勿信为上。若急欲买进，可勿封银，须以榜上有名为定。若只信其漏报，虽至三四次见金榜矣，亦未可以银付之，方可防其脱也。

Note: Buying a school place, buying a stipend, even buying a juren—this happens everywhere, every year, and Jianning Prefecture has been cheated over and over, worse than anywhere. The people of Jian commandery are rich, with much wealth, and light and flighty by nature. Though they have been cheated again and again, those who then rise to try buying still don’t stop; so sharps on the road often treat the Jianning exams as a good line of business. The method of sealing silver, especially, people still cannot see through to this day. Clearly you seal the silver together, it is given back for you to keep, and after the crook leaves you open it—all bricks and stones. Some think there is a method of making silver vanish. How miraculous—even the wisest can hardly guard against it! Only see this clearly; not believing is best. If you urgently want to buy entry, don’t seal silver; take a name on the posted list as the settlement. If you only believe his leaked reports, even if you see the golden list three or four times, you still must not hand over silver; only then can you guard against being cheated out of it.

空屋封银套人抢

Sealing Silver in an Empty House, Then a Staged Robbery

骗局多端，唯仕进一途，竟奔者多，故遭骗者众。棍尝有言，唯虚名可骗实利，唯虚声可赚实物。盖仕进之人，求名之心胜，虽掷重利不暇顾惜，遂入棍术中而不及察。

Swindles have many forms; only the path of official advancement has so many rushing down it, so many are cheated. Crooks have a saying: only an empty name can cheat you of solid profit; only empty sound can earn solid goods. Because people seeking office hunger so fiercely for a name, they throw down heavy sums without time to care, and so fall into the swindler’s art without examining it.

有一巨富家子，欲营谋进学，所带管家者极为能干。往省考大续，寓一歇家中，令其求关通之路。数日内，以门路投者，更进迭来。管家者窥其行径，穷其来历，皆察其言事不相应，踪迹不分明，多与歇家有套同情弊，悉拒却之，不信其哄。后一棍妆为仆价，言语迟钝，举动村朴，自方跟一罢职乡官，与宗主有旧，来此打秋风。引管家去见乡官，果似贫薄小官样。面酌定一名进学，只谢银一百两亦肯讲，只要现银，来伊店封。管家曰：“在我店封。”乡官曰：“事宜慎密，你店内人众，传扬不便。此下有一所空房，是顾秀才的，前欲在彼借寓，以借什物不便，故迁在此。可与我小价在彼处封定，最是稳当。”管家强求乡官来所住店看封为妥。乡官曰：“汝更有疑，我只小价一人，任你多用人来同封。”管家回以“外人不可与知，只同本主去。”果只村仆一人在，把银出对定。忽有棍数人，打开门入曰：“汝辈买秀才，吾拿去出首！”将三人打倒，银尽抢去。

A very rich family’s son wanted to scheme his way into the school; the steward he brought was extremely capable. Going to the provincial capital for the student examination, they lodged at an inn and told him to seek a connecting path. Within days, people offering openings came one after another. The steward watched their conduct, traced their origins, saw that their words and their business did not match, their tracks were unclear, and many were in collusion with the inn—he refused them all and did not believe the talk. Later a crook dressed as a servant, slow of speech, rustic in manner, claiming he followed a dismissed retired official who had old ties with the commissioner and had come here to seek gifts. He led the steward to see the official; indeed the man looked like a poor minor official. They agreed face to face on one name for the school; even a hundred taels as thanks he would discuss, but it had to be ready silver, sealed at his inn. The steward said, “Seal at my inn.” The official said, “The matter should be discreet; your inn has many people, inconvenient if word spreads. Below there’s an empty house, Xiucai Gu’s; I wanted to lodge there before, but borrowing furnishings was inconvenient, so I moved here. You and my little servant can seal it there; that’s most secure.” The steward strongly asked the official to come to the inn they stayed at to watch the sealing. The official said, “If you still doubt, I have only one little servant; you may send as many people as you like to seal together.” The steward replied, “Outsiders mustn’t know; I’ll only go with my master.” Indeed only the rustic servant was there; they brought out the silver and checked it. Suddenly several crooks broke in the door: “You people are buying a xiucai; we’ll seize you and report it!” They knocked the three down and robbed all the silver.

村仆爬起，做烦恼样。管家起，挈其手曰：“不须恼，此银亦不多，同在我店再封。”村仆不肯去。富子曰：“事已错矣，何可再干？”管家曰：“我自有处。”强邀村仆再来，一面令富子速收拾回家。管家雇募店中人，将自己当作儒士，与村仆对锁，送入县中，口告被脱抢之故。县官曰：“你不合买进学，与者受者各有其罪。况被棍抢银，与乡官家人何干？”管家曰：“抢银者即此棍之伙。但穷究此银出，情愿追入官；更愿大罚，与此棍同罪。”县官再差人去叫乡官，早已走了。县官曰：“此果是棍。”严刑拷打。棍仆受刑不过，愿赔一半。追完，管家又告愿全追，甘与同配驿。棍仆死不肯摊出同伙；又累受刑，无可追。乃将棍仆拟徒，管家者只拟杖发归。此为封银防抢之戒。

The rustic servant crawled up, putting on a distressed look. The steward got up, took his hand, and said, “Don’t worry; this silver isn’t much; come to my inn and we’ll seal again.” The servant wouldn’t go. The rich son said, “The matter has already gone wrong; how can we do it again?” The steward said, “I have a way.” He forced the servant to come again, and at the same time told the rich son to pack up and head home quickly. The steward hired people from the inn, passed himself off as a scholar, locked himself together with the rustic servant, sent them into the county yamen, and orally reported being robbed. The county magistrate said, “You shouldn’t have bought a school place; giver and receiver each have their crime. Besides, being robbed by crooks—what’s that to the retired official’s servant?” The steward said, “The robbers were this crook’s gang. Only thoroughly investigate where this silver went; I’m willing to have it recovered for the government; I’m also willing to be heavily fined, same crime as this crook.” The magistrate then sent people to summon the retired official; he had already fled. The magistrate said, “This is indeed a crook.” Harsh torture. The servant-crook couldn’t bear the punishment, and was willing to repay half. After it was recovered, the steward again sued, wanting the whole recovered, willing to share penal exile to a post-station. The servant-crook would rather die than name his gang; tortured again, nothing more to recover. So they sentenced the servant-crook to penal servitude, the steward only to beating and sent home. Let this be a warning: when sealing silver, guard against robbery.

按：管家虽有能，终落棍所脱抢。特既抢后，即能拿棍仆同解，甘与同罪，终能追其一半，棍亦无所利。若富子自己，必不肯与棍同罪，而一抢之后，无如之何矣。或曰：管家顶认儒士，若官考之何如？曰：央分上之人，已是天才，官何须考？即考不得，亦无妨也。

Note: Though the steward had ability, he still fell to the crooks’ robbery. But after the robbery he could seize the servant-crook and go in together, willing to share the crime, and in the end recover half; the crooks also got no profit. If it had been the rich son himself, he surely wouldn’t share a crime with the crook, and after one robbery there would be nothing to be done. Some say: the steward posed as a scholar; what if the official examined him? Answer: a man asking for private introductions is already a genius; why would the official need to examine him? Even if he failed an exam, it wouldn’t matter.

诈秋风客以揽骗

Posing as a Gift-Seeker to Collect Bribes

简学宪最廉明。考大续时，有秋风客到，寓于开明僧舍。次日，有一棍带三仆来，亦与同寓。内中相拜，自称彼系县堂亲眷，亦来打秋风者。外则炫耀冠服，仆从扔卫更盛，每与（冠）盖往来寺中。尝有生儒遇之，辄误指曰：“此学道乡亲也。”又见简道亲回拜，又请酒，偕真秋风客往，而棍专外影窃其名，以欺诳人。简公是严明人，不数日，真秋风客已打发行矣，唯棍在寺。其外棍伙故四下传扬曰：“学爷乡亲在某寺。”生儒中亦甚传之，多有求取大续者，只无人可担当银。棍背套学道衙中书手、皂隶来，过付银封于其家。人既信是真秋风客，又衙门有身役人与同事，银封其家，亦复何虑？棍客动云，彼要说十名，每名要三百两。当赴场人众，各务兢趋，数日已满十人之数。共日封于各书、皂之家，明白交付，共银三千两。背地各瓜分已讫。

Education Commissioner Jian was most honest and clear-sighted. During the student examination, a visitor who had come to seek gifts arrived, lodging at the monks’ quarters of Kaiming Temple. The next day a crook with three servants also lodged there. They exchanged visits; he claimed to be a relative of the county magistrate, also come to seek gifts. Outwardly he displayed caps and robes; his servants and attendants were even more splendid; he often came and went through the temple with the pomp of official canopies. Students who met him often mistook him: “This is the education commissioner’s hometown relative.” They also saw Commissioner Jian’s relative return the visit and invite him to drink; he went along with the real gift-seeker, while the crook made a specialty of stealing that name from the outside to deceive people. Lord Jian was a strict, clear-sighted man; within days the real gift-seeker had been sent off; only the crook remained at the temple. His outside gang deliberately spread word around: “The commissioner’s hometown relative is at such-and-such temple.” Among the students it was much passed on; many seeking the examination had no one to take charge of the silver. The crook had secretly arranged with clerks and runners of the education office to come, and pass sealed silver at their homes. People already believed he was the real gift-seeker, and yamen staff with posts were in on it; silver sealed at their homes—what further worry? The guest-crook kept saying he would speak for ten names, three hundred taels each. With many going to the examination, each competing to rush, within days the ten were full. That day they sealed it at various clerks’ and runners’ homes, delivered plainly, three thousand taels in all. Behind the scenes they had already split it.

但思后日无名，不能回复诸人，银亦何以得去？乃雇一人，往学道出首：“见得衙门书、皂某某等，外同客棍招揽生童银若干两，封于某某等家。”简准状，即出白牌，提拿客棍风火至急，秋风棍即乘机逃去。又拿在衙书皂拶夹，皆不肯招，各打三十，革役。又差人往衙役家搜缉。凡有名与列钻刺者，闻踪迹露出，唯恐指名逮捕，各各四散，走回本县，银都弃撇，不敢来问。由是棍得安享所分之银。书皂虽革役，无赃可据，后复陆续谋入。唯一时受夹打，彼刑用在衙人役，亦仅如搬戏；而所得之多，奚止偿失也！此为信秋风客之戒。

But they thought: later, when there is no name on the list, they can’t answer people—and how can the silver get away? So they hired someone to go to the education office and report: “Clerks and runners so-and-so of the yamen, together with an outside guest-crook, solicited so much silver from students, sealed at so-and-so’s homes.” Jian accepted the complaint, issued a plain warrant to seize the guest-crook with the utmost haste; the gift-seeking crook then used the chance to flee. Then they seized the yamen clerks and runners, put them to the squeezers and presses; none would confess; each was beaten thirty strokes and dismissed. Then they sent people to search the yamen men’s homes. Anyone named as having drilled for a place, hearing their tracks were out, feared being named and arrested, scattered in all directions, fled back to their counties, abandoned the silver, and dared not come ask. Thus the crook could enjoy in peace the silver he’d split. The clerks and runners, though dismissed, had no stolen goods that could be proven; later they gradually schemed their way back in. They only suffered squeezing and beating for a time; using punishment on yamen staff is also only like putting on a play; and what they got was more than enough to repay the loss! Let this be a warning against trusting gift-seekers.

按：此棍称学道乡亲，而学道既已来拜，又请吃酒，则的是乡亲矣。况书、皂皆有身役人，为之翼护，人孰疑之？不知真乡亲已去，而此乃其托名者。彼衙门人唯利是图，所敛既多，何惜数十之板？况其顶头银仍在，虽革役，乌足以惩之。今人谓衙役知法，不知侮法者正是知法之人。唯蹈实地行实事，以真学问博真功名，勿萌侥幸，勿图钻刺，棍骗何从入哉？彼遭骗者，皆惰学不肖之徒，自取灾眚者也。

Note: This crook claimed to be the education commissioner’s hometown relative, and the commissioner had already come to visit and invited him to drink, so he truly seemed a hometown relative. Besides, clerks and runners all had posts, shielding him—who would suspect? They didn’t know the real relative had gone, and this was one borrowing the name. Those yamen men only seek profit; having collected so much, why begrudge a few dozen strokes? Besides, the silver they paid to buy their posts is still there; even dismissed, how is that enough to punish them? People today say yamen runners know the law; they don’t know that those who abuse the law are exactly people who know the law. Only walk on solid ground, do solid things, win a true name with true learning; don’t sprout hopes of a lucky break, don’t plot to buy influence—how would swindles get in? Those who are cheated are all lazy, unworthy students who bring disaster on themselves.

银寄店主被窃逃

Silver Left with an Innkeeper, Who Steals It and Flees

有三棍合帮，共骗得银三百两，未肯遂分，更留合装骗棚，以图大骗。先遣一人过省，离会城两日之府，用银七十两买屋，内系土库，城外铺舍开一客店。又用银五十两娶一妻，买一婢，又买一家奴。更有数十两在手上，调度供家。人见其店有家着奴婢，食用丰足，多往宿其店。此府相近省城，往年文宗考科举不及，常调邻府生童到此合考，以便往返，每富家生童择店，必居于此。

Three crooks in a gang, having cheated three hundred taels together, wouldn’t split yet; they kept the silver to set up a swindle house together, hoping for a bigger haul. First they sent one man across the province, to a prefecture two days from the provincial capital, and spent seventy taels to buy a house; inside was an earthen storehouse; outside the wall they opened shop premises as a guest inn. Another fifty taels went to marry a wife, buy a maid, and buy a house slave. Several tens of taels more were on hand to run the household. People saw the inn had a family, slaves and maids, and food and living in plenty; many lodged there. This prefecture was near the provincial capital; in past years, when the education commissioner’s exam for the provincial examination could not cover all the prefectures, they often transferred students from neighboring prefectures here for a joint exam, convenient for coming and going; every rich student choosing an inn was sure to stay here.

壬子科六月，科期已迫，复调外两府生员来此选考。本店住建郡三个秀才，皆系巨富。一日，有客儒人品丰厚，衣冠鲜整，泊船城外，入此店来，密问店主曰：“你识科举秀才中有大家者乎？”店主曰：“我店中三位都富家，你问何干？”客儒曰：“有好事与他讲。”店主曰：“甚好事，何不对我说？”客儒曰：“你不在行，只好与秀才讲。”店主出，向三秀才曰：“此客先生问科举秀才何人最富，有好事对他说。我问他何事，又不肯言。列位试问其说何事。”三人共入叙礼，问曰：“老丈问富家，小弟等家皆万金，有何好事说？”客曰：“列位肯计较中否？”三秀才曰：“中都肯计较，兄有何门路？”客曰：“我亦不能为力，亦不识门路。但果肯计较者，各备银一千两，来此店对过封定，付还你收，自有指示的路。”三人约四日后家中取银来对，客儒辞去。三人密遣人跟随客去，见其下船，船中止一家人。归报如此，三秀才喜曰：“此必大主考的人，可信也。”店主出问：“适间说何事？”三秀才曰：“此未必然事，若事可成，自有大抬举你。”

In the sixth month of a renzi year, the exam date was pressing; they again transferred students from two outer prefectures here for selection. In this inn stayed three xiucai from Jian commandery, all very rich. One day a visiting scholar, impressive in person, clothes fresh and neat, moored outside the wall, came into this inn, and secretly asked the innkeeper, “Do you know among the exam xiucai any from great families?” The innkeeper said, “The three in my inn are all rich; why do you ask?” The guest said, “I have a good matter to discuss with them.” The innkeeper said, “What good matter? Why not tell me?” The guest said, “You’re not in this line; I can only speak with xiucai.” The innkeeper went out and told the three: “This gentleman asked which exam xiucai is richest; he has a good matter to tell. I asked what, he wouldn’t say. You gentlemen try asking what he says.” The three went in, exchanged courtesies: “You asked about rich families; our families all have ten thousand taels; what good matter?” The guest said, “Would you gentlemen care to plan for passing?” The three said, “We all care to plan for passing; what opening do you have, brother?” The guest said, “I also can’t do anything, and don’t know an opening. But if you truly will plan, each prepare a thousand taels, come to this inn, check and seal it, give it back for you to keep; there will be a path pointed out.” The three set four days to fetch silver from home to check; the visiting scholar left. The three secretly sent someone to follow; they saw him go down to a boat, only one servant on the boat. They reported this; the three were glad: “This must be a man of the chief examiner; he can be trusted.” The innkeeper came out: “What did you just discuss?” The three said, “This isn’t yet a sure thing; if it can be done, we’ll see you well rewarded.”

四日后，三家人都取银到。客儒应期来问，各答“银都齐备。”客曰：“今夜对明封定。”三秀才言：“银多夜间不便，明日入店主内庭去对。”客曰：“店主恐不密事，不知外客房中封更密。”三秀才曰：“明日临时相商。”客辞去。夜饭后，店主出曰：“列位与此客议封银事，客人难防。这门壁浅薄，若夜间统人来劫，可要提防。依我说，可藏入我城门内，你外间好心关防，可保安稳。”三秀才曰：“是也。”共将六皮箱银都寄入店主家内去。家主瞒过妻婢，将银尽从后门藏出，与棍伙夤夜逃去。唯嘱其妻曰：“明日三秀才问我，只说早间出去寻人，少刻即归。”

After four days, three servants all brought silver. The visiting scholar came as agreed and asked; each said, “The silver is all ready.” The guest said, “Tonight we’ll check it clearly and seal it.” The three said, “There’s much silver; night is inconvenient; tomorrow we’ll go into the innkeeper’s inner court to check.” The guest said, “The innkeeper may not keep the matter secret; sealing in the outer guest rooms might be more secret.” The three said, “Tomorrow we’ll discuss at the time.” The guest left. After supper the innkeeper came out: “You gentlemen and this guest discussed sealing silver; the guest is hard to guard against. These door-walls are thin; if at night they bring people to rob, you must watch out. As I see it, you can hide it in my place inside the city gate; you keep good watch outside; that can be secure.” The three said, “Right.” Together they entrusted six leather cases of silver to the innkeeper’s house. The master of the house, hiding it from wife and maid, took all the silver out the back door and fled by night with the crook gang. He only told his wife: “Tomorrow if the three xiucai ask for me, just say I went out early to find someone; I’ll be back shortly.”

次日，客儒欣欣喜色来对银。秀才曰：“银付店主收藏，今早出外，少待即归。”等到午间，店主不回。客辞归船。午后又遣家人来问，又以店主未归答之。至第三日午间，问店主妇取皮箱，妇答云：“并未见甚箱。”及出溪边寻客船，亦不见矣。再问店妇取，苦执未见。任入搜之，竟不见踪。问：“店主果何去？”妇云：“前夜已出，教我如此应你。”三人正慌。

The next day the visiting scholar came in good spirits to check the silver. The xiucai said, “The silver was given to the innkeeper to keep; he went out this morning and will be back shortly.” They waited till noon; the innkeeper didn’t return. The guest went back to the boat. After noon they sent a servant to ask; again they were told the innkeeper was not back. On the third day at noon they asked the innkeeper’s wife for the leather cases; she said, “I haven’t seen any cases.” They went to the creek to find the guest’s boat; it was also gone. They asked the wife again; she stubbornly insisted she hadn’t seen them. They searched inside as they liked; no trace. “Where did the innkeeper really go?” The wife said, “He left the night before last, and told me to answer you this way.” The three were in a panic.

适此三棍脱得银去，已出境外。晚投宿一店，店主见其来晚，提其六箱皆重，疑是窃贼，明日将集众擒之。三棍闻其动静，次早天未明，只挑得四箱去，以二箱寄店。店主越疑是贼，出首于官。太府将银逐封开之，内封有一合同文书，称某人买举人者。太府提某生员到，不敢认。太府以甘言赚之，乃招认，即收入监。后又投分上解释，再骗去银四百两方免申道。又没入店主之屋，及官卖其妻婢，并箱内一千都追入库。彼四箱被棍挑去者，幸得落名，不受再骗。是府官亦一棍也。此为信店家一戒。

By then these three crooks, having made off with the silver, were already out of the territory. At night they lodged at an inn. The innkeeper saw they came late, hefted the six cases, all heavy, and suspected they were thieves; the next day he would gather people to seize them. The three heard the stir; next morning before dawn they carried off only four cases and left two at the inn. The innkeeper was more sure they were thieves, and reported to officials. The prefect opened the silver packet by packet; inside one seal was a contract document saying so-and-so was buying a juren. The prefect summoned that student; he dared not admit it. The prefect lured him with sweet words; he confessed, and was put in jail. Later he sought introductions to get the matter settled, and was cheated of another four hundred taels before they would refrain from reporting it to the commissioner. They also confiscated the innkeeper’s house, sold his wife and maid by official order, and recovered the thousand taels in the cases into the treasury. The four cases the crooks carried off luckily left those students unnamed, so they were not cheated again. Thus the prefectural official was also a crook. Let this be a warning against trusting innkeepers.

按：店主有家眷，最可凭者。彼肯代藏银，孰不信之？谁知其妻妾皆买下以装棍棚者。彼骗得厚利，则弃此而去，别娶妻妾，享大富贵矣。以有眷属之店尚不可信，世路之险，一至于此！人若何不务实而可信棍以行险哉！

Note: An innkeeper with a family is the most trustworthy thing. If he’s willing to store silver for you, who wouldn’t believe? Who knew wife and concubine were all bought to set up the swindle house? Having cheated a thick profit, they abandon this and go, marry other wives, and enjoy great wealth. Even an inn with dependents still can’t be trusted—the world’s roads are this dangerous! Why don’t people do solid work, instead of trusting crooks to walk a dangerous path?

第二十一類 僧道騙

Category 21 · Monk and Daoist Swindles

和尚认牝牛为母

A Monk Claims a Cow Is His Mother

夏六月间，一行脚僧过于路，见小竖牧一伙牛。内有黄牝牛，大而肥。牧竖伸左脚与之舐，牝牛舐之；又与右脚与舐。僧问曰：“此牛何为舐你脚？”牧竖曰：“此牛最驯熟，吾甚爱之。我脚多汗咸，故牛爱舐。”僧知牛爱舐咸味，密此牛，系索长者家所畜的。

In the sixth month of summer a traveling monk was on the road and saw a herd-boy watching a group of cattle. Among them was a yellow cow, large and fat. The boy held out his left foot for it to lick, and the cow licked it; then he held out his right foot the same way. The monk asked, "Why does this cow lick your feet?" The boy said, "This cow is very tame. I am very fond of it. My feet sweat and taste of salt, so the cow likes to lick them." The monk understood that cattle like a salty taste, and he quietly noted that this cow was one kept by Elder Suo.

次日，僧取浓盐汁，厚涂头脸及遍身手足等处，寻到索长者家，跪门涕泣曰：“愿赐慈悲心，超度我母子。”索老曰：“我不会说法念经，怎能超度人？”僧曰：“我先母在生，不肯修斋布福。今已死七年，知冥中必受罪谴，奈家贫不能功果追荐。因慕目连救母，情愿削发从师，专求度母。前月得遇善知识，指老母在长者家投生为黄牛母，敬来求超度。”索老曰：“我栏有四头牝牛，知何牛是？”僧曰：“愿同往看。畜物更有灵性，母子相见，必有恩爱情在，自与别的不同。”索老与僧同到栏前，放出群牛。僧见大牝牛到，即揭下袖蒂帽，涕泣跪向前曰：“此是吾母也！”牝牛嗅其咸味，以舌遍舐其头脸，若怜惜状。僧愈加流涕，又自剥去衣服，牛遍舐其身，不忍去。索老看见果异，真似母之爱子，但不能言耳。问曰：“既是你前生之母，今须何以超度？”僧曰：“我若有银，当以半价买去养，奈贫僧衣钵罄空。愿长者权舍贫僧，牵往山庵，日采草煮粥喂养。待其谴罪完满，天年数终，贫僧当收埋，念经卷超度，庶来世转身为人，不堕畜生道矣。”长者怜其词情恳切，曰：“吾舍与你去。”僧叩头拜谢，牵此牛往三日路外，付出庵寄养。

The next day the monk took thick salt brine and coated his head, face, whole body, hands, and feet with it, then found Elder Suo's house. He knelt at the gate weeping and said, "Please show compassion and deliver my mother and me." Elder Suo said, "I cannot preach or recite sutras. How could I deliver anyone?" The monk said, "While my late mother was alive she would not keep vegetarian fasts or give alms. She has been dead seven years, and I know she must be suffering punishment in the underworld, but our family is poor and I cannot afford the rites to save her. Admiring how Mulian saved his mother, I shaved my head and followed a teacher, seeking only to deliver her. Last month I met a wise teacher who said my old mother had been reborn as a yellow cow in the elder's house, so I have come respectfully to ask that she be delivered." Elder Suo said, "I have four cows in the pen. How would one know which cow it is?" The monk said, "Please let me go and look with you. Beasts have even more spiritual nature. When mother and child meet, there will surely be affection, different from the others." Elder Suo went with the monk to the pen and let the cattle out. When the monk saw the large cow arrive, he took off his cap, knelt forward weeping, and said, "This is my mother!" The cow smelled the salt and licked his head and face all over, as if pitying him. The monk wept still more, then took off his clothes; the cow licked his whole body and would not leave. Elder Suo saw that it truly was strange, just like a mother's love for her child, only it could not speak. He asked, "If this is your mother from a former life, what must be done now to deliver her?" The monk said, "If I had silver, I would buy her at half price and raise her, but this poor monk's robe and bowl are empty. I ask the elder to grant her to me for now. I will lead her to a mountain hermitage, pick grass each day, and cook gruel to feed her. When her punishment is complete and her natural span ends, I will bury her and recite sutras to deliver her, so that in the next life she may be reborn as a human and not fall into the animal path." The elder pitied the earnestness of his words and said, "I grant her to you. Take her." The monk kowtowed in thanks, led the cow three days' journey away, and left her at a hermitage to be boarded.

至十月，天气寒凉，叫屠子来宰，以一半分与，卖得价银一两五钱；一半僧自留，做成干粮，收藏衲袄中各处。径到索长者厅前，结双趺而坐。长者出曰：“何僧敢升厅而坐？”僧曰：“你颇认得我么？”长者曰：“不知你是何人，怎么认得？”僧曰：“亦自然觉得面熟么？”长者曰：“并无相会，何处面熟？”僧长叹曰：“你本来灵性且尽丧，何故不识故人色相！”长者曰：“何为是故人？”僧曰：“昔佛印点醒东坡，远公唤回乐天，非苏、白二公之故人乎？你前生与我同修，因尘心未断，复来享此人福。我今特来度你，急宜丢手尘债，再去勤修，庶不废前生功行也。”长者曰：“你安能识得前生？”僧曰：“我功行高你一倍。你今且享半生福禄，我又加半生苦修，何难知三生事因？”长者曰：“你今生若何苦修？”僧曰：“从前苦修且休提，现今已辟谷三年矣。”长者始惊曰：“你能辟谷，在我家辟一月何如？”僧笑曰：“三年于是，何有一月？”长者曰：“亦服茶汤乎？”僧曰：“清茶滚水，日一瓯耳。”长者留之，扫一空室与坐，早进瓯茶，夜进瓯滚水。

When the tenth month came and the weather turned cold, he called a butcher to slaughter her. He gave the butcher half, which sold for one tael and five qian of silver; he kept the other half himself, made it into dried provisions, and hid them in various places in his patched robe. He went straight to Elder Suo's hall and sat down in full lotus. The elder came out and said, "What monk dares come up into the hall and sit?" The monk said, "Do you happen to recognize me?" The elder said, "I do not know who you are. How would I recognize you?" The monk said, "Don't I look familiar?" The elder said, "We have never met. Where would the familiarity come from?" The monk sighed at length: "Your original spiritual nature is already lost completely. Why do you not recognize an old friend's form?" The elder said, "What old friend?" The monk said, "In the past Foyin woke Dongpo, and Master Yuan called Letian back. Were they not old friends of Su and Bai? In a former life you practiced with me. Because your worldly heart was not cut off, you came back to enjoy these human blessings. I have come specially to deliver you. You should quickly drop worldly ties and go cultivate hard again, so as not to waste the merit of your former life." The elder said, "How can you know former lives?" The monk said, "My merit is twice yours. You are now enjoying half a lifetime of fortune, and I have added half a lifetime of bitter practice. Why would it be hard to know the causes of the three lives?" The elder said, "How have you practiced bitterly in this life?" The monk said, "Never mind former bitter practice. I have already gone without grain for three years." The elder was startled at last: "If you can go without grain, how about doing it for one month in my house?" The monk laughed: "Three years already—what is one month?" The elder said, "Do you still take tea and hot water?" The monk said, "Clear tea or boiling water, one bowl a day." The elder kept him, swept an empty room for him to sit in, and sent in a bowl of tea in the morning and a bowl of boiling water at night.

连坐七日，再请出，答对如常。长者惊服，问曰：“我当如何修？”僧曰：“只弃家长往，自有修行善方。”长者曰：“妻寡子幼，产业付谁？此事不能。其次修何如？”僧曰：“唯有舍施，修寺奉佛，来生亦受福报。现今庐山一庵，化人独力修造。倘捐五百金，一完修之，亦一大功德也。”长者依言，遣仆同僧送五百金往，交付与住持明白，留仆住数日，送归报主。后僧分住持银二百五十两而去。其以辟谷动富翁，则私食所带之干粮耳，宁有人而真辟谷者！

He sat on like that for seven days, then was invited out again, and answered as usual. The elder was amazed and convinced, and asked, "How should I cultivate?" The monk said, "Just leave the household and go far away; the proper way to practice will present itself." The elder said, "My wife would be left a widow and my children are young. Who would the property be given to? This I cannot do. What would the next-best cultivation be?" The monk said, "Only giving. Repair temples and serve the Buddha, and in the next life you will also receive a blessed reward. Right now there is a hermitage on Mount Lu that one donor is being asked to rebuild alone. If you give five hundred taels and finish the repair in one stroke, that too is a great merit." The elder followed his words, sent a servant with the monk to deliver five hundred taels, handed them over clearly to the abbot, had the servant stay several days, then sent him back to report to his master. Later the monk split two hundred fifty taels of the abbot's silver and left. As for moving the rich man by fasting from grain, he was privately eating the dried provisions he had brought. As if anyone truly lives without grain!

按：此僧脱牛，犹其小者，转卖之可也。名为生前母而宰食之，罪浮于天矣。至用为干粮而诈称辟谷，其骗亦大。虽半舍入庵，亦是好事，僧若得劝缘功。然周急赈贫，自当施于邻里，何必投入于庵？此愚人信福田利益之过也，亦未读傅奕公《高识传》矣。

Note: This monk's making off with the cow was still the small part; he could have resold her. To call her his mother from her former life and then slaughter and eat her—the crime towers over Heaven. Then using her as dried provisions and falsely claiming to fast from grain: that swindle too was large. Though half the gift went into the hermitage, that was still a good deed, and the monk got the credit for soliciting it. But relieving the urgent and aiding the poor should be given to neighbors. Why must it be thrown into a hermitage? This is the fault of fools who believe in the profits of the field of merit—and of not having read Master Fu Yi's Gaoshi zhuan.

服孩儿丹诈辟谷

Taking Infant Elixir to Fake Fasting from Grain

有僧自称能辟谷者，富家多召而试之。连七八日不食一粒，或间二三日，服滚汤一瓯而已。传名甚广，人争以金帛舍之。一乡官见褚县尊，偶道及此，称世间有此高僧，真仙佛再生于世出。褚公最正大，素不信僧道辈，曰：“人受此色身，哪能断绝食色？假托辟谷者，不过暗藏干粮，以哄惑愚民耳。明理君子，何可信此辈？若果能辟谷，彼将远遁深山，唯恐名落人间，何必浪游市里？受人施舍金帛，将何所用？”乡官被褚公一驳，似乎己为信邪，更欲取信其言，乃曰：“老父母不信，可召而试之，方知晚生言非妄矣。”

There was a monk who claimed he could live without grain. Rich families often summoned him to test it. For seven or eight days running he would not eat a single grain, or every two or three days he would take only one bowl of boiling water. His name spread widely, and people competed to give him gold and silk. A local official, seeing Magistrate Chu, happened to mention this, saying the world had such an eminent monk, truly an immortal or Buddha born again. Master Chu was most upright, and had never believed monks and Daoists. He said, "People receive this physical body. How can they cut off food and sex? Those who pretend to live without grain only hide dried provisions in secret, to fool the ignorant. How can a gentleman who understands principle believe this sort? If they could truly live without grain, they would flee far into the deep mountains, fearing only that their names might fall among men. Why would they wander the markets? And if they accept gifts of gold and silk, what would they use them for?" The local official, rebuked by Master Chu, seemed as if he himself believed heresy, and wanted still more to make his words believed. So he said, "If Your Honor does not believe, you may summon him and test him, and then you will know this junior's words are not false."

褚公即差人唤至，令搜其身。别无夹带，唯持二十四个弥陀珠，诛之带入。扫一净室，布床席与坐，外遣人轮番密窥。日遣人明开门一视，出仍锁门。两日内，果结双趺而坐，容貌如故。第三日开视，见脸有乏汗，求滚水饮。褚公命与之，复出锁门。密窥者来禀曰：“僧以一弥陀珠调水饮讫，容貌复好。”后每两日进滚汤一碗，密窥者辄禀云：“以珠调吃。”

Master Chu at once sent men to call him in, and ordered his person searched. He had nothing else hidden on him, only twenty-four Amitabha beads, which they allowed him to bring in. They swept a clean room, spread a bed and mat for him to sit on, and outside sent people in rotation to watch in secret. Each day they sent someone to open the door openly and look once, then lock it again on leaving. For two days he did sit in full lotus, his looks unchanged. On the third day they opened and looked: his face had a weary sweat, and he asked for boiling water to drink. Master Chu ordered it given, then they went out and locked the door again. The secret watchers came and reported: "The monk mixed one Amitabha bead in the water and drank it, and his looks recovered." After that, every two days they sent in one bowl of boiling water, and the secret watchers always reported, "He mixed a bead and swallowed it."

经十一日，召之出，取其弥陀珠视之，止十九枚在手耳。褚公收其珠，命收入轻监不许揽动，听彼静坐，以候发落。密嘱禁子曰。“铁容僧道人入见，两日后必问你乞食。你问其弥陀珠何以做？做来以水调之，与此珠一样，后重赏你。”

After eleven days they summoned him out, took his Amitabha beads and looked: only nineteen were still in his hands. Master Chu confiscated the beads, ordered him put in light detention and not to be disturbed, and let him sit quietly pending sentence. He secretly instructed the jailer: "Do not allow monks or Daoists to come in and see him. After two days he will surely beg you for food. Ask him how the Amitabha beads are made. If he makes some that, mixed with water, are the same as these beads, I will reward you heavily afterward."

次日，僧即问禁子求食。禁子曰：“你教我作珠方法，便与你食。”僧曰：“此药极难得。你但与我食，出外多以银谢你，不必问此方。”禁子不与之食，三日饿倒，面青黄无人色矣。

The next day the monk asked the jailer for food. The jailer said, "Teach me the method for making the beads, and I will give you food." The monk said, "This medicine is extremely hard to get. Just give me food. When I get out I will thank you with much silver. No need to ask for this formula." The jailer gave him no food. After three days he collapsed from hunger, his face green-yellow, with no human color left.

褚公提出，审曰：“我早知此珠是孩儿丹矣。你供出制造方法来，免汝一死。”僧诈作将死形状，不敢应。褚公笑曰：“众看此辟谷僧，在褚爷前辟三日谷，即饿死矣。此丹妇人胎内孩子，必须谋死孕妇，剖其婴孩，以作此丹。不知你害死多少命，以造此恶业，你怎敢说出口？我岂求汝方乎？若打死你，罪还轻。”命衙前搭起一台，以十九枚珠发出，将四个调与众百姓看，以滚水调之，满碗都是膏液。有敢饮者，又香又甜，只饮两口，一日亦饱。后十五枚发与医生治补损。然后缚此僧在台上凌迟之。褚公曰：“县令为民父母，岂忍杀人？但为众冤泄恨矣。”众皆称快，而乡官后亦永不信僧道矣。

Master Chu had him brought out and examined him, saying, "I knew from the start that these beads were infant elixir. Confess the method of manufacture, and I will spare your life." The monk pretended to be at the point of death, and dared not answer. Master Chu laughed: "Everyone look at this monk who lives without grain. Before Magistrate Chu he went three days without grain and is about to starve to death. This elixir is a child from a woman's womb. You have to plot to kill a pregnant woman and cut out the infant to make this elixir. Who knows how many lives you have destroyed to make this evil work—how dare you speak the formula aloud? As if I wanted your recipe? If I beat you to death, the punishment would still be light." He ordered a platform built in front of the yamen, brought out the nineteen beads, and had four mixed for the common people to see: mixed with boiling water, the whole bowl became a thick paste. Those who dared drink found it both fragrant and sweet; two mouthfuls and they were full for a day. The remaining fifteen were given to doctors to treat injuries and deficiencies. Then they bound this monk on the platform and put him to death by slicing. Master Chu said, "A county magistrate is father and mother to the people. How could he bear to kill? But this is to vent the hatred of the many who were wronged." All called it satisfying, and afterward the local official never again believed monks and Daoists.

按：此诈辟谷者，多是藏干粮，其服孩儿丹者少。此粮非藏于身，恐人搜也。都寄于丐乞者之身，有人试之，则密以干粮付。又有服松毛竹叶者，松毛用羊蹄草同吃，竹叶用嫩蕨同吃，皆滑而可食。僧亦尝以此惑人，谓彼能服此。然从古有辟谷之说者，乃仙方，非人间所有也。曾见有遇异人授辟谷者，述之于左。

Note: Those who fake fasting from grain mostly hide dried provisions; those who take infant elixir are few. This food is not hidden on the body, for fear people will search. It is all entrusted to beggars; when someone tests them, they secretly pass the dried provisions over. There are also those who take pine needles and bamboo leaves: pine needles eaten together with yangti herb, bamboo leaves eaten together with tender ferns, all slippery and edible. Monks have also used this to fool people, saying they can live on these. But from ancient times, the methods of living without grain that exist are immortal recipes, not things of the human world. I once heard of a man who met an extraordinary person who taught him to live without grain; I record it below.

武夷山有贫民结庐于岩曲，仅容床灶，垦山种茶，卖以供食。积十数年，所开茶山，岁可收鬻三四金，每日力作不息，唯大寒、暑甚、风雨，终日寂坐岩庐下，不识经典，亦不通往来。

On Mount Wuyi a poor man built a hut in a bend of the cliffs, barely room for a bed and a stove. He opened the mountain and planted tea, selling it to feed himself. After ten-odd years the tea slopes he had opened could be harvested and sold for three or four taels a year. Every day he labored without rest; only in severe cold, extreme heat, or wind and rain would he sit silent all day under the cliff hut. He did not know the scriptures, and had no dealings with others.

忽一日一道人过其庐，谓曰：“汝耕山劳苦，何不以茶山付人代耕，岁收一金，以买衣资？吾授汝辟谷方，则不须买米，不劳耕山，可安坐自足矣。”山民曰：“吾尝间修行人有辟谷方，若肯教我，愿拜师父求学。”道士曰：“你性子恬静，尽可修行。今后唯早晨煎清泉二罐，煎至半落，以两罐合煎作一罐，早午晚各饮二瓯。饮后澄心息想，以舌抵上颚，合口闭目，终日静坐。或天清神爽，爰出游，行则慢步闲观，随意所适，不拘半午，不拘片时。凡行住坐卧，只从心不拂。或山果草实可食者，遇着稍食一二不妨，但不可有意寻求。如此便可辟谷矣。记之不可轻易传人。”山民依此行之，一年果不食一黍，颜如金黄，轻健如常。同山旁居人，常不见其籴米，或过其庐，亦无锅甑。问之，答曰：“近年学得辟谷方。”居人转相传异。有拜之求方者，辄逃避不授，曰：“师嘱勿轻传泄。”次年传于远近，多有来册拜访者。或赍粮宿其居庐看守，至匝月，果唯见饮滚水，饮后静坐，寂无一为，亦无闲谈。不知者或穷问之，或与谈修养，微笑而起，出游山径，迨午晚归，复暖滚水而饮。凡人之来者不迎，去者不送，亦无半语讯问人。人问之，有可答者，随口答一二句。问其餘闲事，则摇首不应，若有厌烦之意，唯自去静坐。凡言动应酬，总是付之无心而已。第二年后，名益著。富家贵人，多备鞍轿迎者，坚逃不往。富贵人身往劝逼之，后亦遍往诸家，所到不食人一物，唯向空室静坐，若一木佛，然有言动而已。

Suddenly one day a Daoist passed his hut and said, "You till the mountain and labor hard. Why not give the tea slopes to someone else to till, take one tael a year to buy clothes, and let me teach you a method of living without grain? Then you need not buy rice or labor at tilling, and can sit at ease and be sufficient." The mountain man said, "I have heard in passing that practitioners have methods of living without grain. If you are willing to teach me, I will bow to you as master and ask to learn." The Daoist said, "Your nature is quiet; you are quite able to cultivate. From now on, in the morning only boil two jars of clear spring water down by half, then combine the two jars and boil them into one. Morning, noon, and evening drink two bowls each time. After drinking, still the mind and rest thought, press the tongue to the upper palate, close the mouth and shut the eyes, and sit quietly all day. Or if the sky is clear and the spirit fresh, then go out walking: walk slowly and look at leisure, following whatever suits you, whether half a day or only a moment. In walking, standing, sitting, and lying down, simply follow the mind and do not go against it. Or if there are mountain fruits and plant seeds that can be eaten, if you come upon them it does no harm to eat one or two, but you must not seek them on purpose. In this way you can live without grain. Remember this, and do not lightly pass it to others." The mountain man followed this and practiced it. After one year he indeed did not eat a single grain of millet; his face was gold-yellow, and he was light and strong as usual. People living on the same mountain nearby often did not see him buy rice; or if they passed his hut, there was also no pot or steamer. When they asked, he answered, "In recent years I have learned a method of living without grain." The neighbors passed the wonder from one to another. Those who bowed and asked for the method he would always flee and not teach, saying, "My teacher ordered me not to leak it lightly." The next year it spread near and far, and many came with visiting cards to pay respects. Some brought grain and lodged in his hut to watch, for a full month: they indeed saw him only drink boiling water, then sit quietly after drinking, silent, doing nothing at all, and with no idle talk. Those who did not understand might question him hard, or talk with him of cultivation; he would smile, rise, and go walk the mountain paths, returning at noon or evening, then warm boiling water and drink again. Of people who came he did not welcome them; of those who left he did not see them off; he also did not ask people half a word. If people asked him, and there was something he could answer, he would answer one or two sentences offhand. If they asked about other idle matters, he would shake his head and not answer, as if annoyed, and only go sit quietly himself. All speech, movement, and social response he simply met with an empty mind. After the second year his name was still more famous. Rich houses and noble men often prepared saddles and sedan chairs to receive him; he firmly fled and would not go. When the rich and noble went in person to urge and press him, he later also went around to the various houses. Wherever he arrived he would not eat a single thing of theirs, only sit quietly in an empty room, like a wooden Buddha, except that he still spoke and moved a little.

经二年半后，有潭阳富人礼迎之，虔奉更肃，若敬神明。时进茶果，稍为食些少。后备清茶精饭，苦劝之食，坚辞不能，不得已为食一瓯。少顷饥甚，服滚汤。又饥饿不能禁，又索食。富人欢喜，肃进之。连三日内，皆一日五食，仅能止饥。山民自惊疑，急求归山，依旧服汤静坐，不免肚饥。后只得复食，三餐如寻常人矣。

After two and a half years a rich man of Tanyang received him with ceremony, serving him still more solemnly, as if honoring a god. From time to time he sent in tea and fruit, and the man ate a little. Later he prepared clear tea and fine rice and bitterly urged him to eat. He firmly declined that he could not; then, unable to help it, he ate one bowl. In a short while he was very hungry, and took boiling water. He was hungry again and could not bear it, and asked for food once more. The rich man was glad and served him solemnly. For three days running he ate five times a day, and only then could stop the hunger. The mountain man was startled and doubtful himself, and urgently asked to return to the mountain. He took water and sat quietly as before, but could not avoid a hungry belly. Later he had no choice but to eat again, three meals like an ordinary person.

按：山民所遇之道士，明是仙人。若辟谷三年完满，必有超度矣。惜哉为名所累，致人迎奉，致人逼食，而自毁前功。此劝食之愚。富人彼意，欲虔奉之，以分生佛之福，岂诚心奉道哉！此山民既为所误，而彼福亦安在也？且堕百劫之罪，来生必与山民结一大仇矣。观此，则辟谷乃仙方，不徒在服滚水静坐也。不然，后仍服之坐之，而何谷不能辟哉？则今之托辟谷索人钱米者，真盗贼僧道也。真辟谷者，敢令人知乎？

Note: The Daoist the mountain man met was clearly an immortal. If the three years of living without grain had been completed, there would surely have been deliverance. A pity he was burdened by fame, which brought people to receive and serve him, brought people to force him to eat, and he destroyed his former achievement himself. This was the folly of urging him to eat. That rich man's intent was to serve him devoutly in order to share a living Buddha's blessings. Was that a sincere heart serving the Way? This mountain man had already been harmed by it, and where then was that man's blessing? He will also fall into the sin of a hundred kalpas, and in the next life he is bound to form a great enmity with the mountain man. Looking at this, living without grain is an immortal method, not merely a matter of drinking boiling water and sitting still. Otherwise, afterward he still drank it and sat as before—why then could he no longer fast from grain? So those today who plead fasting from grain in order to demand people's money and rice are truly thief-monks and Daoists. Would anyone who truly lives without grain dare let people know?

信僧哄惑几染祸

Believing a Monk's Talk, He Nearly Came to Ruin

徽州人丁达，为人好善喜舍。一日，与友林泽往海澄买椒木，到临清等处发卖，货已卖讫。此处有一寺，内有名僧号无二者，年近三十餘，相貌俊雅，会讲经典，善谈因果，风动多少良家子弟，往寺参拜，常有被其劝化，削发出家者。时丁达邀林泽去谒无二。林泽曰：“你素性好善，闻此僧巧嘴善言，累诱人削发为僧。你若见之，被其哄惑，何以归见父母？”达曰：“劝在彼，从在我。我自有主，彼何能拿？”若要往拜之。见无二举动闲雅，谈及因果之事，达被打动，尽舍其财本，入寺拜无二为师，欲削发为僧。泽怒曰：“未到此处，我早言之。今果被哄惑，何以为人？”再三苦谏不听。泽自回去，达在寺修行。

Ding Da of Huizhou was a man who liked goodness and liked to give. One day he went with his friend Lin Ze to Haicheng to buy pepper and timber, and sold it at Linqing and other places; the goods were already sold off. In that place there was a temple, and in it a famous monk styled Wuer, nearly thirty-odd, of handsome looks, able to lecture on the scriptures and good at talking of karmic cause and effect. He stirred any number of sons of good families to go to the temple and pay respects; often they were converted by him and shaved their heads to leave home. At the time Ding Da invited Lin Ze to go visit Wuer. Lin Ze said, "You have always liked goodness. I hear this monk has a clever mouth and good talk, and has repeatedly lured people to shave and become monks. If you see him and are fooled by him, how will you return home and face your parents?" Da said, "Persuasion is on his side; following is on mine. I have a mind of my own. How can he take hold of me?" And he insisted on going to pay respects. He saw that Wuer's manner was easy and refined, and when they talked of karmic matters Da was moved. He gave away all his trading capital, entered the temple, bowed to Wuer as his teacher, and wanted to shave his head and become a monk. Ze was angry: "Before we even reached this place I already said it. Now you have indeed been fooled. How can you still be a man?" He bitterly advised him again and again; Da would not listen. Ze went back by himself, and Da stayed in the temple to cultivate.

过二年后，僧无二因有董寡妇入寺烧香，容貌甚美，亦信善好念弥陀。带一使女，十七岁，国色娇媚，到寺亦参拜。无二以巧言劝诱，寡妇亦心服，即拜无二为师，欲削发为尼，暂在寺宿几夜。其丫头常往无二房送果吕，无二欲心难制，以白金十两戏之。丫头收其银，与之通情。无二又思及其嫠妇，夜潜入其房，候董氏熟睡，欲强奸之。董氏坚贞不从，喊曰：“何人无理，敢来奸盗？”言未数声，无二以手巾紧勒其颈，须臾而死。

After two years the monk Wuer, because Widow Dong came to the temple to burn incense—she was very beautiful, and also believed in goodness and liked to recite Amitabha. She brought a maid, seventeen, strikingly beautiful and charming, who also paid respects at the temple. Wuer used clever words to persuade and lure; the widow's heart also submitted. She at once bowed to Wuer as her teacher, wanted to shave her head and become a nun, and stayed a few nights in the temple for the time being. Her maid often went to Wuer's room to deliver fruit. Wuer could not restrain desire, and used ten taels of silver to seduce her. The maid took the silver and had relations with him. Wuer then thought also of the widow. At night he slipped into her room, waited until Mistress Dong was in a deep sleep, and tried to rape her. Mistress Dong was chaste and firm and would not comply. She cried out, "Who is this, so lawless, daring to come rape and steal?" Before she had spoken many sounds, Wuer tightened a handkerchief around her neck, and in a moment she was dead.

次日，使女去报知董氏之子李英。及到寺，无二已先逃走矣。但无二久出名，各处人多认得。李英雇人遍处缉拿，不两日拿到送县。王爷即点民兵百餘，围绕其寺。时寺僧已四散逃命，无僧可拿。王爷再命焚其寺，将无二责了四十，问典刑之罪。

The next day the maid went and reported to Mistress Dong's son Li Ying. By the time they reached the temple, Wuer had already fled first. But Wuer had long been famous, and people in all places mostly recognized him. Li Ying hired men to hunt him everywhere; in less than two days they caught him and sent him to the county. Magistrate Wang at once mustered a hundred-odd militia and surrounded the temple. By then the temple monks had already scattered in all directions to save their lives; there was no monk who could be seized. Magistrate Wang further ordered the temple burned, had Wuer beaten forty strokes, and sentenced him to death.

达悔财本俱丧，无颜回家。后家中已知达逃回，叫人寻觅归家，发长方敢出。此愚人信僧之明鉴也。

Da regretted that his capital was all lost, and was too ashamed to return home. Later his family learned that Da had fled back, sent people to find him and bring him home, and only when his hair had grown long did he dare go out. This is a clear mirror of fools who believe monks.

按：寺门藏奸，僧徒即贼，此是常事，亦往往有败露者。人不目见，亦多耳闻，何犹不知戒？而妇人入寺，男子出家，真大愚也。董虽死犹幸完节；丁达虽幸逃生而财本已丧。使当时与无二并获，何分清浊？必并死狱中矣。故邪说引诱人者，无论士农工商，皆当勿信而远之可也。

Note: Temple gates hide villains, and monks are thieves. This is a common matter, and there are also often cases that come to light. If people have not seen it with their eyes, they have also often heard it with their ears. Why do they still not know to take warning? And women entering temples, men leaving home—truly great folly. Dong, though she died, was still fortunate to keep her chastity complete; Ding Da, though fortunate to escape with his life, had already lost his capital. If at the time he had been taken together with Wuer, how would they have told clean from dirty? He would surely have died in prison with him. So those who use heretical talk to lure people, whether scholar, farmer, artisan, or merchant, should all not believe it, and keep far from it.

僧似伽蓝诈化疏

A Monk Who Looked Like the Temple Guardian Fakes a Subscription

天元寺年久倾颓，住持僧完朗有意修之，恐工费浩大，非有大力者发愿独任，未易举手。忽一日，游方僧若冰来寺投宿，身干魁梧，面方而黑，目圆耳长，宛似本寺伽蓝形象。完朗一见心喜，夜设斋款待，甚加勤敬。次日，僧若冰曰：“宝刹非兴旺，何如此肯接待十方？”完朗曰：“兴我寺者，在尊宿一臂之力，敢大有所托。”若冰曰：“山家缘薄，怎能相助？”完朗曰：“此寺须五百金方可全修，虽化些少众缘，亦不济事。看尊相，极似我本寺伽蓝，托你择巨富家，若化其全修，待彼在允否间，约其来寺亲看，我自有方法纳之。”

Tianyuan Temple had been dilapidated for years. The abbot Wanlang meant to repair it, but feared the labor and cost would be huge: unless some person of great power vowed to take it on alone, it would not be easy to raise a hand. Suddenly one day the wandering monk Ruobing came to the temple to lodge. He was tall and sturdy, with a square black face, round eyes and long ears, exactly like the image of this temple's guardian (Qielan). Wanlang was delighted at the sight, set a vegetarian feast that night to receive him, and treated him with extra diligence and respect. The next day the monk Ruobing said, "This precious monastery is not flourishing. Why then are you willing to receive monks from all quarters like this?" Wanlang said, "The one who will raise my temple depends on your reverence's helping hand. I dare greatly to rely on you." Ruobing said, "This mountain household's karmic ties are thin. How can I help?" Wanlang said, "This temple needs five hundred taels before it can be fully repaired. Even collecting a little from many donors will not serve. Looking at your honored appearance, you extremely resemble our temple's guardian. I ask you to pick a hugely rich family. If you collect from them for the full repair, wait until they are hovering between yes and no, arrange for them to come to the temple and look in person, and I will have a method of my own to land them."

若冰会意，前去大江边，有柴商财本巨万。若冰备干粮在身，直到柴排厅中，朗讽一经，结趺而坐，高叫曰：“化缘！”柴商荆秀云命手下以钱与之。僧全不视，曰：“吾非化小可钱钞。贫僧与施主有夙缘，要化千金。”秀云作色曰：“化千金何用？”僧曰：“此去二百里，有天元寺。前创时，施主有缘在，故今生大富。近年颓坏，须五百金修理，又须五百金为香灯田，后可保长久，则施主功德远大矣。”秀云曰：“你为寺化疏，前生与此寺何缘？”僧曰：“寺本我居食之地，非有缘，得久居乎？”秀云不睬之。僧在柴排坐三日不去，手下人以饭与食，亦食；不与，亦不食。又过四日，秀云曰：“吾舍三百相助，你更去化别人。”僧曰：“有缘者不能化，无缘者何劳空说？”秀云曰：“你把疏簿来，我题三百两。”僧曰：“疏簿在寺中。三百亦不够用，不须题？你图今生享福，只施五百两。若布来世津梁，非千金不可。”秀云曰：“吾不信今生来生，你且领三百两去，好心修造。不足者，岂无别善人相助？”僧曰：“吾哪要银？你自送与住持僧。”秀云曰：“吾十日后送到寺来。”僧遂合掌，念“阿弥陀佛”一声而去。归对完朗详说其事，又约“十日后柴商且来，吾远避之。”完朗大喜，早备茶果斋品以待。

Ruobing understood. He went on to the edge of the great river, where there was a timber merchant with capital in the tens of thousands. Ruobing packed dried provisions on his person, went straight into the timber-raft hall, loudly chanted a sutra, sat in lotus, and called out, "Collecting alms!" The timber merchant Jing Xiuyun ordered his men to give him cash. The monk did not look at it at all, and said, "I am not collecting paltry cash. This poor monk has a former-life tie with the donor, and wants to collect a thousand taels." Xiuyun changed color: "What use is collecting a thousand taels?" The monk said, "Two hundred li from here is Tianyuan Temple. When it was first founded, the donor had a tie there, and so in this life you are greatly rich. In recent years it has fallen into ruin. It needs five hundred taels for repairs, and another five hundred taels for incense-and-lamp fields; afterward it can be kept long, and then the donor's merit will be far and great." Xiuyun said, "You are collecting a subscription for the temple. What tie did you have with this temple in a former life?" The monk said, "The temple is originally the place where I live and eat. Without a tie, could I dwell there long?" Xiuyun paid him no attention. The monk sat on the timber raft three days and would not leave. The men gave him rice, and he ate; if they did not give it, he also did not eat. After four more days Xiuyun said, "I will give three hundred to help. You go collect from others besides." The monk said, "If I cannot collect from the one with the tie, why waste empty talk on those without a tie?" Xiuyun said, "Bring the subscription book. I will write three hundred taels." The monk said, "The subscription book is at the temple. Three hundred is also not enough. No need to write it. If you only seek to enjoy blessings in this life, just give five hundred taels. If you would pave a way for the next life, nothing less than a thousand taels will do." Xiuyun said, "I do not believe in this life and the next. You take three hundred taels and go, and kindly do the building. If it is not enough, will there be no other good people to help?" The monk said, "Why would I want the silver? You send it yourself to the abbot." Xiuyun said, "I will send it to the temple in ten days." The monk then put his palms together, recited "Amitabha Buddha" once, and left. He returned and told Wanlang the matter in detail, and also arranged, "In ten days the timber merchant will come. I will stay far away." Wanlang was greatly pleased, and early prepared tea, fruit, and vegetarian dishes to wait.

至第十日，秀云果带银同两仆来。完朗知是柴商，肃迎待茶毕，问曰：“施主高姓？”秀云曰：“姓荆。”完朗曰：“施主从哪里来？”秀云曰：“前约宝刹中化疏僧，今敬从江上来。”完朗沉吟曰：“山寺未曾化疏。”秀云曰：“十七日前，有僧在柴楼中坐七日，我许他今日来。”完朗曰：“本寺僧此半月内并无人出外者，必方僧诈托也。”即命作斋相待。秀云心疑怪：“若方僧诈托，何不前日即领银去？”只存在心，遍寺闲游。到伽蓝祠去，举头看伽蓝宛似前日僧形象。两仆亦指曰：“此伽蓝好似前日僧。”秀云看越惊异，心疑是伽蓝化为僧，以劝我修寺。即以祈曰：“前日僧若是你变的，求一圣。”即打一圣。又曰：“三百金已带来，祈保今年大利。”再一圣，又得一阳。又曰：“三百不够，若要五百，求一圣。”又得一阴。”又祝曰：“我心中已悟。若更要五百两香灯，求一圣。”果掷一圣。秀云拜谢讫，来就斋席，谓完朗曰：“须用银几何？”完朗曰：“久有意要修。前日叫匠人估计，要五百两方够，故不敢举。”秀云曰：“我前日许过三百两，今现送在此，明日更送二百两来添。若修完备，再舍五百两买置香灯田，永远奉佛。”完朗闻言大喜，合掌下拜。后依约舍完，若冰密分二百两而去。

On the tenth day Xiuyun indeed brought silver and came with two servants. Wanlang knew it was the timber merchant, received him with ceremony, finished serving tea, and asked, "What is the donor's honored surname?" Xiuyun said, "Jing." Wanlang said, "Where has the donor come from?" Xiuyun said, "I previously arranged with the subscription monk of this precious monastery. Now I have respectfully come up from the river." Wanlang pondered: "This mountain temple has not been collecting subscriptions." Xiuyun said, "Seventeen days ago a monk sat in my timber loft for seven days. I promised him I would come today." Wanlang said, "In this past half month none of this temple's monks have gone out. It must be a wandering monk falsely claiming it." He at once ordered a vegetarian meal prepared. Xiuyun thought it strange in his heart: if a wandering monk was falsely claiming it, why did he not take the silver that day? He only kept it in mind, and wandered the whole temple at leisure. He went to the guardian shrine, raised his head, and looked: the guardian was exactly like the monk of the other day. The two servants also pointed and said, "This guardian looks very like the monk from before." Xiuyun looked and was still more amazed, and suspected in his heart that the guardian had transformed into a monk in order to urge him to repair the temple. He then prayed: "If the monk of the other day was you transformed, grant one holy cast." He at once threw the blocks and got a holy sign. He said again, "I have already brought three hundred taels. Pray that this year there be great profit." He threw once more, and got a yang. He said again, "Three hundred is not enough. If you want five hundred, grant one holy cast." He again got a yin. He prayed again: "I have already understood in my heart. If you further want five hundred taels for incense-and-lamp, grant one holy cast." Indeed he threw one holy sign. Xiuyun bowed his thanks, came to the vegetarian feast, and said to Wanlang, "How much silver must be used?" Wanlang said, "I have long meant to repair it. The other day I had craftsmen estimate: it will take five hundred taels to be enough, so I dared not start." Xiuyun said, "The other day I promised three hundred taels. I now deliver them here, and tomorrow I will send two hundred more to add. When the repair is complete, I will further give five hundred taels to buy incense-and-lamp fields, to serve the Buddha forever." Wanlang heard this and was greatly pleased, put his palms together and bowed. Later Xiuyun gave it all as agreed, and Ruobing secretly took two hundred taels as his split and left.

按：僧貌似伽蓝，故凑成此巧，亦可谓奇。然是人作成此套，何尝真有伽蓝化身乎！故富而能舍，本是善行；若谓真佛化缘而施舍者辄有福报，此两个装骗僧，岂能福人乎？吾不信也。

Note: The monk's looks resembled the temple guardian, so this trick was pieced together; one may also call it remarkable. But it was people who made up this setup. When was there ever a true guardian incarnation? So being rich and able to give is originally a good deed. If one says that a true Buddha collecting alms, and those who give, always have blessed reward—could these two monks who staged a swindle bless anyone? I do not believe it.

诈称先知骗绢服

Faking Foresight to Cheat a Man Out of a Silk Robe

东阳江达涧，父遗产万金。因为本府库吏累累浸润剥削，破去家产强半；又好男风，尝畜美好小仆，陪侍出入。有日，江之梁友遇其小仆，问曰：“前日为你相公买两匹青绢都长，做长衫必有剩。”小仆曰：“裁工不善做，先做一领太长，穿不得；后一领做得恰好。”梁曰：“长的可裁短，何妨？”仆曰：“他也不要得，已藏在书房大箱中去。”———原来江多衣服，其穿后不用的，都投入此箱。梁曰：“新服何忍弃，叫把与我修短服之。”仆曰：“你要问他讨，箱中第三件便是这新服。”

Jiang Dajian of Dongyang inherited ten thousand taels from his father. Because he served as a prefectural treasury clerk and was repeatedly squeezed and exploited, more than half his family property was wrecked. He also liked male favorites, and often kept handsome young servants to attend him going out and in. One day Jiang's friend Liang met his young servant and asked, "The other day two bolts of dark silk bought for your master were both extra long. Making a long robe, there must have been leftover." The servant said, "The tailor was no good at the work. The first one he made was too long and cannot be worn; the later one was made just right." Liang said, "The long one can be cut shorter. What harm?" The servant said, "He does not want it either. It has already been put away in the big chest in the study." Jiang had many clothes; those he no longer used after wearing them were all thrown into this chest. Liang said, "How can one bear to throw away a new robe? Tell him to give it to me to alter shorter and wear." The servant said, "If you want to ask him for it, the third piece in the chest is this new robe."

适一僧在旁闻得，素知江达涧肯施舍，即诈称方僧，入江相公廨中抄化。江以两文钱施之。僧曰：“吾看满衙之中，皆有怨气，唯相公府中祥光满室，后日必有好官职，前程远大。吾将化你一件好服，以结个缘。”江曰：“我无好服。”僧曰：“你有一件穿不得的，舍与我好。”江故曰：“衣皆可穿，哪有穿不得的？”僧曰：“是一件新青绢太长的，在书房大箱中，第三件该舍与我，吾为尔消灾延寿。不然，你眼下有多少是非到。”江心异之，开大箱中看，果有两件在上，新绢服第三。便疑此僧先知，持出舍与之，问曰：“既舍此服，可免是非否？”僧曰：“我试你有善心否。今果肯施，便转灾成福矣。”

A monk standing nearby happened to hear this. He had long known that Jiang Dajian was willing to give, so he pretended to be a wandering monk and went into Master Jiang's yamen quarters to collect alms. Jiang gave him two cash. The monk said, "I look through the whole yamen: there is an air of grievance everywhere. Only in your honor's residence does auspicious light fill the rooms. Later you will surely have a good official post and a great career ahead. I will collect one good robe from you, to form a tie." Jiang said, "I have no good robes." The monk said, "You have one that cannot be worn. Give it to me; that will be good." Jiang therefore said, "The clothes can all be worn. How would there be one that cannot be worn?" The monk said, "It is one new dark-silk robe that is too long, in the big chest in the study. The third piece should be given to me. I will dispel disaster for you and extend your life. Otherwise you have much trouble arriving right before your eyes." Jiang thought it strange, opened the big chest and looked: indeed there were two pieces on top, and the new silk robe was the third. He then suspected this monk had foresight, took it out and gave it to him, and asked, "Having given this robe, can I be spared the trouble?" The monk said, "I was testing whether you have a good heart. Now you are indeed willing to give, so disaster has already turned into fortune."

按：今僧皆庸人，何能前知？其称已往事者，多得于传闻；说未来事者，皆涉于矫诬。观此僧欺江相公之事，则今之称善知识者，皆此类也。江相公之易欺如此，家安得不败？世之信僧引诱者，可以此为鉴。

Note: Monks today are all ordinary men. How can they know the future? When they speak of past events, they mostly get them from hearsay; when they speak of future events, they are all false claims. Looking at how this monk cheated Master Jiang, those today who call themselves wise teachers are all of this type. Master Jiang was so easy to cheat—how could the household not fail? Those in the world who believe monks' lures may take this as a warning.

第二十二類 煉丹騙

Category 22 · Alchemy Swindles

深地炼丹置长符

Refining Elixir in a Deep Pit, with Long Talisman Stakes

古有炼丹之说，点铁成金，盖仙方，非人世有也。世所传炼丹之术，用好纹银三两，杂诸铅、汞辰、朱砂药物在炉同炼，每次须炼四十九日。至四十日后，须两人轮番守炉，昼夜不得暂时离守。丹成，可得九两，内除三两银本，要三两买药物，每次只出三两。一年可炼四次，共可得十二两，仅足供两人食用。故真得此方者，亦不屑为。其炼出丹银，亦可经煎，每次渐渐亏少，复归于无。但此银第二次不可为银母，若再炼，须另以纹银为母。此相传真方，费心费工，甚不易为。若云游方士，托炼丹为名以行骗者，用砒霜、雄黄诸物，炒好银为灰砂，假称曰“丹头”；然后将此与好银同煎，仍煎成银，彼便道“丹药可点成银”，此个个是弄假行骗之套子。

Since ancient times there has been talk of refining elixirs, of touching iron and turning it into gold. That is an art of immortals, not something the human world possesses. The elixir-refining method handed down in the world uses three taels of fine silver of the best grade, mixed with lead, mercury, cinnabar, and other ingredients, all refined together in a furnace. Each round must be fired for forty-nine days. After the fortieth day, two men must take turns watching the furnace; day and night they may not leave it for a moment. When the elixir is done, you get nine taels. Out of that, subtract the three taels of silver capital, and three more taels go to buying ingredients, so each round yields only three taels of profit. You can refine four times a year, making twelve taels in all, barely enough to feed two men. So even someone who truly possesses this formula would not deign to practice it. The elixir silver it produces can stand up to remelting, but each time it shrinks a little, until it dwindles back to nothing. And this silver cannot serve as mother silver a second time; to refine again, you must take fresh fine silver as the mother. This is the genuine formula as it has been handed down: it costs care and labor and is very hard to carry out. As for wandering alchemists who make elixir refining their pretext for swindling: they use arsenic, realgar, and such substances to cook good silver down to ash and grit, and falsely call it "elixir seed." Then they melt this together with good silver, and it melts back into silver, whereupon they say, "The elixir can transmute into silver." Every one of these is a put-up job, a fraud and a swindle.

有一道士，自称能炼丹者。先以银灰明煎出些与人看，人多疑信相半。一富人独信之，请至家炼。道士曰：“炼丹乃仙术，家中多秽浊，恐不能成。可于僻地开坑一丈四尺深，下仅可容一床一炉，在此处烧炼四十九日。一百两银母，可炼出三百两矣。”富人依言，于后门凿一坑，广八尺，深一丈四尺。道士入坑去，命用银十两买铅、汞辰、朱砂等来，先炼丹头，三日已讫。富人付银百两与炼，日吊下三餐饭与食，道人又命讨一手握的坚实圆木七只，每只三尺五寸长，作符；用大棕索一条，交横缚柴符上，日以大斧摧打柴符。富人每日往坑上看。至三十餘日，柴符渐渐打下，只有一尺在上，心料银将成矣。道士知一月之久，防守者必懈，夜以索一头系裹银药，一头系在腰，将七只长符，每二尺打一符于上，扳援而升，将银吊起，夤夜逃去。次早送饭，下无人接。以烛照之，不见道士矣。梯下看之，银都窃去，方知彼蹈符而上，明白被其窃骗也。

There was a Daoist priest who claimed he could refine elixirs. First he took some silver ash and openly smelted a little silver out of it to show people; most were half doubtful, half convinced. One rich man alone believed him and invited him home to refine. The Daoist said, "Refining elixirs is an immortal art. A house is full of foulness and impurity; I fear it cannot succeed there. You should dig a pit in a secluded spot, fourteen feet deep, the bottom just big enough to hold a bed and a furnace, and do the firing there for forty-nine days. A hundred taels of mother silver can be refined into three hundred taels." The rich man did as he said and dug a pit by the back gate, eight feet across and fourteen feet deep. The Daoist went down into the pit, had him spend ten taels of silver buying lead, mercury, cinnabar, and the rest, and first refined the elixir seed, which was finished in three days. The rich man handed over a hundred taels of silver for refining, and each day lowered three meals down to him. The Daoist also told him to find seven solid round logs, each thick as a hand's grip and three and a half feet long, to serve as talismans. He took a thick coir rope, bound it crosswise around the wooden talismans, and every day had them hammered with a great axe. The rich man went to look down at the pit every day. After thirty-some days, the wooden talismans had been driven down little by little until only a foot remained above ground, and he reckoned the silver was nearly ready. The Daoist knew that after a full month the watchers were bound to grow slack. That night he tied one end of the rope around the wrapped silver and ingredients and the other end around his own waist; then he took the seven long talisman stakes, drove them into the pit wall one every two feet, climbed up by hauling on them, pulled the silver up after him, and fled in the dead of night. The next morning, when the meal was sent down, no one below took it. They shone a candle down: the Daoist was gone. They lowered a ladder and went down to look: the silver had all been stolen. Only then did they realize he had climbed out by way of the stakes; plainly, they had been robbed and swindled.

按：深坑煅炼，使人不疑其逃。然用符用索，已早为出坑之计。其使人不疑处，即其脱身处也。后人鉴此，尚以炼丹为可信否？

Note: Firing the furnace in a deep pit kept people from suspecting he would flee. But the talisman stakes and the rope were his escape plan, laid well in advance. The very thing that kept people from suspecting was his way out. Let those who come after take warning from this: can elixir refining still be believed?

信炼丹贻害一家

Believing in Elixir Refining Brings Ruin on a Whole Family

方士以炼丹脱剥，受骗者历来无算，故明人皆能灼见其伪，拒绝不信。有一邴道士，术极高，拐一脚，明言已得真传炼丹术，不肯轻易为人炼。其法以丹头与人，任其以铜铅同煎，皆成银。彼自用则不须炼，但随手取出，都是银。或见人疾苦者，在手掌一捻，即取银与之；或衣袖中随来，亦是银。多肯施舍与贫人，由是，人称为“半仙”。有用银器皿设盛席待之者，食毕，令取一米桶置席上，以手取银器，件件收入桶中。及看，则空桶无一物，明言我收去不还矣。人以善言求取，则云：“已在你家内原藏器之所。”视之，果在。若恶言强取，则终不见。此谓得五鬼搬运之法。如此累显奇术，皆足骇动人。

Alchemists use elixir refining to fleece people. Those cheated have been beyond counting through the ages, so clear-eyed people can all see through the fraud and refuse to believe it. There was a certain Daoist named Bing, a master of the art, lame in one leg, who said openly that he had received the true transmission of elixir refining and would not lightly perform it for anyone. His method was to give people elixir seed and let them melt it with copper or lead: it all turned to silver. When he wanted silver himself, he did not need to refine at all; whatever he drew from his hand was silver. If he saw someone in hardship, a twist of his palm would produce silver to give them; or it would come out of his sleeve, and that too was silver. He was generous in giving to the poor, and so people called him the "Half Immortal." Once a host set out silver vessels and a lavish feast to entertain him. When the meal was over, Bing had a rice bucket brought and set on the table, picked up the silver vessels piece by piece, and dropped them into the bucket. When they looked, it was an empty bucket with nothing in it. He said openly, "I have taken them away, and I am not giving them back." If they asked for them politely, he would say, "They are already back in your house, in the place where the vessels were kept." They looked, and there they were. If they demanded them rudely, the vessels were never seen again. This was called having mastered the Five Ghosts' art of transport. Marvels like this, displayed again and again, were enough to stagger and sway people.

有富人尧鲁信之，延至于家，朝夕参拜，敬礼备至，愿学其术。道士安然受拜，未肯遂传之。每日坐享其敬，饮醉而睡，睡醒而游，全不以其敬礼为意。但有甚术，凡拜之者便倾心悦服，与共席饮酒，使称颂其道。尧鲁一家老幼婢仆，皆尊敬之。唯鲁妻辛氏始终不信，累劝夫宜绝此邪人。后邴道士知之，以银二钱与其家小仆，曰：“你主母梳头时，可取她梳下头发一根与我。”小仆早晨取与之。道士得此发即作法，至半上午，辛氏中心只爱与道士通，谓婢曰：“今日我心异也。”至午益甚，又曰：“今日心中大异。”至半下午，心不能自禁，明谓侍婢曰：“吾往日极恶邴道士，今日何爱他好？你看我脸上何如？”婢曰：“你似欲睡模样。”至晚饭后，辛氏思与道士云雨之意极切，只恨一家人在旁耳。又强制住，密谓婢曰：“你今须紧跟我，或入道士房去，你须打我两掌，批我面皮，切不可忘。”及上床睡后，夫已睡着，辛氏披上衣，裸下体，开门径奔道士房去。道士正在作法催符。婢急跟出，呼曰：“此道士房，不可去！”亦不应。道士语婢曰：“你外去。”以手扯辛氏。婢近前批主母两颊，亦不管；又在面上打两掌，曰：“你未穿衣。”辛氏方醒，曰：“我是梦中来，何故真身在此？喜得你唤醒也。”手携婢曰：“快和我进去，好羞人也！”入房蹴夫醒，详言其情及得婢唤醒之事。夫曰：“哪有此理？你素恶他，故装此情捏之，岂有心既欲去，又肯叫婢挽之？这假话我不信。”次日，不得已述于夫兄言之。兄命弟逐去道士，亦不听；乃往县告之。县提去打二十，又会寄棒，打亦不痛，乃以收监。道士明是空身入监，随手取出都是银。以银贿禁子，令买酒肉入监食。禁子更加奉承，思求其方。后又解府解道，各官都加责，以无甚证据，不肯置之死。后竟托分上，放出逃去，不知所往。尧鲁一家长幼，后相继疾故，盖受其术所蛊也。唯辛氏贞正，寿考无恙，总理家政，以抚幼孙之长，至九十餘岁而卒。

There was a rich man named Yao Lu who believed in him. He invited Bing into his home, bowed to him morning and night, honored him with every courtesy, and begged to learn his art. The Daoist accepted the bows without a qualm but would not transmit the art. Every day he sat back and enjoyed the worship, drank himself drunk and slept, woke and wandered about, and took the family's reverence entirely for granted. But he had some power: whoever bowed to him would pour out their heart in devotion, drink with him at the same table, and sing the praises of his Way. Yao Lu's whole household, old and young, maids and menservants, all revered him. Only Lu's wife, Mistress Xin, never believed from first to last, and she urged her husband time and again to cut off this wicked man. Later the Daoist Bing learned of this. He gave two qian of silver to a young servant of the house and said, "When your mistress combs her hair, take one of the hairs that comes out in the comb and bring it to me." The boy brought him one that morning. Once the Daoist had the hair, he worked his spell at once. By mid-morning, Mistress Xin's heart was set on lying with the Daoist. She said to her maid, "My heart feels strange today." By noon it was worse, and she said again, "My heart feels very strange today." By mid-afternoon she could no longer hold herself back and said openly to her waiting-maid, "I used to hate that Daoist Bing bitterly; why do I adore him today? Look at my face. How does it look?" The maid said, "You look as if you want to sleep." After the evening meal, Xin's longing to sleep with the Daoist grew desperate; her only vexation was that the whole family was around. She forced the urge down and privately told the maid, "Tonight you must stay close to me. If I go into the Daoist's room, you must slap me twice across the face, hard. Whatever you do, don't forget." After they had gone to bed and her husband was asleep, Xin threw on an upper garment, left her lower body bare, opened the door, and ran straight for the Daoist's room. The Daoist was just then working his spell, urging on the talisman. The maid hurried after her, crying, "That is the Daoist's room; you must not go!" There was no answer. The Daoist told the maid, "Get outside," and pulled Xin in by the hand. The maid stepped up and slapped her mistress on both cheeks; Xin paid no heed. She struck her face twice more and said, "You have no clothes on." Only then did Xin wake. "I came here in a dream; why is my real body here? Thank heaven you woke me." She took the maid's hand and said, "Quick, come back in with me. How shameful!" In her room she kicked her husband awake and told him the whole story, including how the maid had woken her. Her husband said, "How could such a thing be? You have always hated him; you staged this scene to frame him. If your heart really wanted to go, why would you have told the maid to pull you back? I don't believe this lie." The next day, having no other choice, she told the whole thing to her husband's elder brother. The elder brother ordered his younger brother to drive the Daoist out; Yao Lu would not listen. So the brother went to the county and lodged a charge. The county had the Daoist brought in and given twenty strokes, then put him to the rod, but the beating did not hurt him, so they threw him in jail. The Daoist had plainly entered the jail with nothing on him, yet whatever he drew from his hand was silver. He bribed the jailers with silver and had them buy wine and meat to bring into the jail. The jailers grew still more fawning, hoping to get his formula out of him. Later he was sent up to the prefecture and then to the circuit; at each level the officials had him beaten, but as there was no solid evidence, they would not put him to death. In the end he called in favors, was released, and fled, no one knew where. Yao Lu's whole family, old and young, afterward fell ill and died one after another; they had been poisoned by his sorcery. Only Mistress Xin, chaste and upright, lived on unharmed to a great age. She ran the household, raised the young grandsons to manhood, and died past ninety.

按：妖术之暗中，如妖狐之投媚，必心邪而后能惑。苟心正者，虽入群妖之中，妖不能害。故傅奕不信死人之咒，而胡僧自死；仲淹下信杀子之鬼，而鬼自不来。辛氏心正，虽妖人灵法，能深疑于心，蚤嘱于婢，终不受其邪淫之毒。然则法虽巧，终不及人心之正也。后遇妖人者，其牢把心而勿睬之，彼邪亦安施哉？

Note: Sorcery strikes from the dark the way a fox-spirit offers its charms: the heart must be crooked first, and only then can it be beguiled. If the heart is upright, walk into a crowd of demons and the demons cannot harm you. So it was that Fu Yi refused to believe a curse could kill him, and the foreign monk died instead; Zhongyan refused to believe a ghost could kill his son, and the ghost never came. Mistress Xin's heart was upright. Though the sorcerer's potent art could cloud her mind, she had the wit to warn her maid beforehand, and in the end she escaped the poison of his wicked lust. So however clever the art, it is no match for an upright human heart. If you meet a sorcerer, hold your heart fast and pay him no mind; what can his evil do then?

炼丹难脱投毒药

When the Elixir Trick Can't Cheat Him: Poison in the Food

古潭一后生丁宇弘，机关伶俐，识尽世间情伪，人不能欺。偶遇一方士，自称能炼丹。宇弘早知其伪也，欲乘此以骗方士，故诈为不知之状而琐琐问之。方士曰：“丹是仙术，古来传于善人，专以济救贫穷者。先须采药炼成丹头，后用银一钱，与丹头同煎，可得三钱；一两可得三两。”宇弘曰：“更多可炼否？”方士曰：“只要有丹头，虽一百一千皆可炼。”宇弘先用银一钱与炼。方士加丹头三分，即煎出银三钱。宇弘喜，更以一两与煎，又得银三两。宇弘益喜，请方士到家，殷勤相待。及银已费尽，又求再炼添用，陆续炼出银三十餘两。惟以好言承奉之，愿学其术，终不多出银与炼，反将方士丹头之本骗来矣。

In Gutan there was a young man named Ding Yuhong, sharp and quick-witted, who knew all the falseness of the world; no one could deceive him. He chanced to meet an alchemist who claimed he could refine elixirs. Yuhong knew at once that it was a fraud, and decided to use the occasion to swindle the alchemist instead. So he feigned ignorance and plied the man with fussy questions. The alchemist said, "The elixir is an immortal art, handed down since ancient times to good men, meant specially to relieve the poor. First you must gather ingredients and refine them into elixir seed. Then take one qian of silver, melt it together with the seed, and you get three qian; one tael yields three taels." Yuhong asked, "Can you refine more than that?" The alchemist said, "As long as there is elixir seed, even a hundred taels or a thousand can be refined." Yuhong first gave him one qian of silver to refine. The alchemist added three fen of elixir seed, and out came three qian of silver. Yuhong was delighted, gave him a tael to melt, and again got three taels of silver. More delighted still, he invited the alchemist home and waited on him attentively. Whenever the silver was spent, he asked to refine more for spending money, and batch after batch they melted out thirty-odd taels of silver. He simply plied the man with fine words and said he wished to learn the art, but he never put up much silver of his own for the refining, so in the end it was the alchemist's own elixir-seed capital that got swindled away.

方士思家中不奈他何，故说：“吾丹头已用尽。可多带银本，出外采药，再在外大炼。”宇弘明知其引外行骗，只自思“我用心提防，彼何以骗？”更欲尽骗其身心丹头之银，乃带银五十两，与俱出外，不肯取出费用。方士叫其取银买物，宇弘曰：“丹以换银，今已成之银，何必轻用？可取丹来炼银作路费，我银留买药。”方士尽将己丹头三两，宇弘用银十两，共炼成三十两，彼此各分一半。又远行两日，寝食严防。方士无计可脱，乃背地买砒霜在身，晚又买一鲜鱼入店。宇弘往煮熟，装作两碗，方士往捧一碗在席，放毒于内。又再捧一碗，故打忿嚏，将口馋溅入鱼上。方士曰：“这碗亵渎了，我吃。”及至半夜，宇弘腹疼。延至明晓，方士往医家求止疼药，煎服愈甚。至午，宇弘发散唇裂，腹痛难当，心疑是方士投毒，哀求之曰：“吾止有银五十五两，你能救我命，尽半与你。”时弘已不能起床矣。方士取其银置己包袱内，近床以药与之曰：“吾游方人，将攒他人银。你好奸狡，反骗去我银五十两。今止多得你五两，吾自行善心，以此药与你，凭你命当生死何如。”遂负行李逃去。宇弘急命店主以药煎。有认得者曰：“此解砒霜药也。”连服几次，疼稍止。再求近店人医之，三日始得痊愈，银已全被方士夺去矣，只沿路乞食而归。

The alchemist saw that in the man's house he could do nothing with him, so he said, "My elixir seed is used up. You should bring more silver capital; we will go out to gather ingredients and do a great refining on the road." Yuhong knew perfectly well the man was luring him out to swindle him, but he thought to himself, "If I keep my guard up, how can he swindle me?" What was more, he wanted to cheat the man out of every bit of elixir seed he still carried. So he took fifty taels of silver and set out with him, refusing to produce it for expenses. When the alchemist told him to take out silver to buy things, Yuhong said, "The elixir is for turning into silver. Now that we have finished silver, why spend it lightly? Bring out seed and refine silver for travel expenses; my silver stays for buying ingredients." The alchemist put in all three taels of elixir seed he had left, and Yuhong ten taels of silver; together they refined it into thirty taels, and each took half. They traveled two more days, and Yuhong kept strict guard even over sleep and meals. The alchemist had no scheme by which to get at him, so he secretly bought arsenic and kept it on his person. That evening he also bought a fresh fish and brought it into the inn. Yuhong went and boiled the fish and portioned it into two bowls.
