The alchemist carried one bowl to the table and put the poison in it. Then, carrying the other bowl, he deliberately let loose a violent sneeze and spattered saliva onto the fish. The alchemist said, "This bowl is defiled; I will eat it." By midnight Yuhong's belly began to hurt. The pain dragged on until dawn. The alchemist went to a doctor for a pain-relieving medicine, but once it was decocted and swallowed, the pain grew worse. By noon Yuhong's hair was coming loose and his lips were split; the belly pain was unendurable. Suspecting that the alchemist had poisoned him, he begged him: "I have only fifty-five taels of silver. If you can save my life, I will give you half of everything." By then Yuhong could no longer get out of bed. The alchemist took his silver and put it in his own bundle, then came to the bedside, gave him a medicine, and said, "I am a man of the road; I make my living gathering other people's silver. You are a cunning one; you swindled fifty taels out of me. Now I have gained only five taels extra from you. Out of the goodness of my heart I will give you this medicine; whether you live or die is up to your fate." Then he shouldered his baggage and fled. Yuhong hurriedly had the innkeeper decoct the medicine. Someone who recognized it said, "This is an antidote for arsenic." After several doses in a row, the pain gradually eased. He then found a doctor near the inn to treat him, and after three days he finally recovered. The silver had all been taken by the alchemist, and he could only beg for food along the road as he made his way home.

按：知防炼丹，莫如宇弘。虽百计不能骗，反骗方士银本几尽，可谓巧极矣。然终被其投毒，银尽还讫，又多去五两，且几乎丧命。幸而得生，沿路乞食，亦劳且辱矣。方士炼丹，其可信哉？

Note: No one knew better than Yuhong how to guard against elixir tricks. A hundred schemes could not swindle him; on the contrary, he swindled the alchemist out of nearly all his capital. You could call him clever in the extreme. Yet in the end the man poisoned him: all the silver went back, five taels more besides, and he nearly lost his life. Lucky to survive, he had to beg his way home along the road, toil and humiliation enough. Can an alchemist's elixir refining be believed?

第二十三類 法術騙

Category 23 · Sorcery Swindles

和尚詐照形謀反

A Monk Fakes Reflections to Foment Rebellion

僧術中有以法咒水密咒某人，心欲何事，後令人自取照之，各隨其心之所欲，自現其形。有米春元者，富過百萬，田連兩府，年逾五十，不思會試，唯安享豪華以為樂。妖僧聞其富，欲騙其厚利也，挾咒水之術，往叩其門，自言能望氣，每見此宅紫氣上沖，有鸞鳳之彩。此百代王侯之兆，當有立翊運之功、分河山之帶礪者。米春元未信。僧曰：「吾傳有秘術，以符咒水能知此生榮枯結果。人但齋戒三日，虔心來照，則今生是何成就，自現於水中。」米乃留此僧，令家下人各齋戒。

Among the arts of monks there is one that works by chanting spells over water. The monk secretly casts the spell on a man, and whatever that man longs for in his heart, when he is then told to look into the water himself, his own image appears in it accordingly. There was a man named Mi Chunyuan, worth over a million, whose fields stretched across two prefectures. He was past fifty and no longer thought of sitting the metropolitan examination; his only pleasure was living in ease and luxury. A sorcerer-monk heard of his wealth and schemed to cheat him out of a rich profit. Armed with the water-charming art, he went and knocked at Mi's gate, claiming that he could read the aura of a place: every time he passed this residence, he said, he saw purple vapors rising into the sky, shot through with the colors of the phoenix and the luan. This was an omen of kings and marquises for a hundred generations; someone here was destined to perform great service in upholding the dynasty's fortune and to receive a fief under an everlasting oath of enfeoffment. Mi Chunyuan did not believe him. The monk said, 'I hold a secret transmitted art: with talismans and enchanted water one can learn the rise and fall of this life and how it will end. A man need only fast for three days and come to look with a devout heart, and whatever he is meant to achieve in this life will appear of itself in the water.' Mi then took the monk in and ordered everyone in the household to fast.

至第三日，注大缸水於庭。僧密語咒水，令諸人自照。米照見戴丫天冠，穿蟒袍。幼子照之，亦同。長次二子，只紗帽圓領而已。米正室照，亦妃冠鳳袍。兩長婦照，唯珠冠翟服。米大異之，僅秘於心。

On the third day they filled a great vat with water in the courtyard. The monk muttered his spells over the water in secret, then had each person look in turn. Mi looked and saw himself wearing a pronged heavenly crown and a python robe. His youngest son looked and saw the same. The two elder sons saw only gauze caps and round-collared robes. Mi's principal wife looked and saw a consort's crown and a phoenix robe. The two elder daughters-in-law looked and saw only pearl crowns and pheasant-plumed gowns. Mi was astonished, but kept it locked in his heart.

後與流寓枝鄉官宴會，談及時事，枝曰：「今併後匹敵，金注支庶。禍之萌，必始宮闈。異日不為文皇之蹀血，或為沂王府之反召。此魯嫠所深卹者。」米曰：「往者逆未萌而折，宸豪已發而摧。國家如天之福，風雨何搖於牖戶也？」枝曰：「不然。文靜以監豎倡唐，姚衍以胖僧興國。若輔之得人，成敗安可料也？」米曰：「縱中土有故，水國偏在海隅，必無憂亂離也。」枝曰：「亦難保。讖云『某地出天子，江南作戰場』，正可慮也。」米曰：「使宸豪復興於今，成敗當何如？」枝曰：「今承平馳兵，更甚於昔。向令宸豪不久淹南康，某都不詐應反戈，安至以銅鐘灰也？」米聞言心喜。

Later, at a banquet with a certain Zhi, a former official who had taken up residence there, the talk turned to current affairs. Zhi said, 'As things stand, the rivals are evenly matched, and the stakes are loaded on the cadet line. The first sprout of calamity will surely appear inside the palace. One day there will be either a bloodbath like Emperor Wen's seizure of the throne, or a call to revolt from a princely house like the Prince of Yi's. That is what the widow of Lu grieved over, though affairs of state were none of her business.' Mi said, 'In the past, rebellion was broken before it sprouted, and Chenhao was crushed as soon as he rose. The state enjoys the fortune of Heaven itself—how can wind and rain shake its doors and windows?' Zhi said, 'Not so. Liu Wenjing was a petty palace overseer when he set the Tang in motion, and Yao Yan was a fat monk when he raised a dynasty to power. If a cause finds the right men to help it, who can foretell success or failure?' Mi said, 'Even if the central lands fall into turmoil, this watery region of ours lies off in a corner of the sea; surely we need not fear chaos and exile here.' Zhi said, 'That too is hard to guarantee. A prophecy says, "A Son of Heaven shall rise from a certain place, and Jiangnan shall become a battlefield." That is exactly what we have to fear.' Mi said, 'If Chenhao were to rise again today, how would it end?' Zhi said, 'The realm has been at peace so long that the troops have gone slack—worse than in those days. If Chenhao had not lingered so long at Nankang, and if a certain garrison had not feigned allegiance and then turned its spears against him, how would his grand enterprise have gone up in smoke?' Mi heard this and was secretly delighted.

又有一僧，能降神附童者，言往來禍福，如聲應響。米請降之密禱，以欲圖不軌事。神降曰：「金鍾興，玉氣旺，清福扶王帝業強。洪流掃蕩人安泰，裂土移宮鎮遠方。」米猶未決休咎，再求明報。降童喝曰：「此何事而敢絮叨也！」米不敢問，而未解神意。既而漁人於深淵得巨鐘，金色燁然。米以為瑞也，召枝某及二僧決謀逆，欲俟五月某日五更早，大小官俱出城送萬壽表，乃閉四門，伏兵城外，悉殲之。

There was another monk who could call down a spirit to possess a boy, and it spoke of coming fortunes and misfortunes like an echo answering a sound. Mi asked him to call the spirit down, and prayed in secret about his plan to commit treason. The spirit descended and said: 'The golden bell rises, the jade aura waxes; pure fortune upholds the king, the imperial enterprise grows strong. A mighty flood sweeps the land clean and the people rest in peace; carve up the soil, move the palace, and hold the far frontier.' Mi still could not tell whether this boded good or ill, and begged for a plainer answer. The possessed boy shouted, 'What manner of thing is this, that you dare babble on about it!' Mi dared not ask again, and still did not grasp the spirit's meaning. Soon afterward a fisherman hauled a huge bell out of a deep pool, gleaming gold. Mi took it as an auspicious sign. He summoned Zhi and the two monks to settle their plans for revolt: they would wait for a certain day in the fifth month, at dawn in the fifth watch, when officials high and low would all leave the city to send off the emperor's birthday memorial; then they would shut the four gates, station troops in ambush outside the walls, and slaughter them to a man.

至四更，兵卒供執事者早起，見城內伏兵處燈火異常，急報軍官調兵捕之。城中擾亂，又遣兵守城。見江中船無數，皆早炊飯。城上兵疑是助亂者，大呼曰：「某人謀逆，被捕獲斬首矣！」外伏者見內無號砲，城上有備，又聞呼喊聲，送表官皆不出城，知事必敗露，河邊數十號船，乘微明時各各逃散。後官以亂者作造謀反劫庫問，捕獲數十餘人，皆斬首。而首逆者，反以不知情為辭，只擬流三千里而死於道。

At the fourth watch, the soldiers on duty rose early and noticed unusual lights and fires at the places inside the city where men lay in ambush. They rushed a report to the officers, who called out troops to make arrests. The city fell into commotion, and more soldiers were sent to man the walls. From the walls they saw countless boats on the river, all cooking an early meal. Suspecting they had come to aid the revolt, the soldiers on the wall shouted, 'So-and-so plotted treason—he has been caught and beheaded!' The men in ambush outside saw no signal cannon from within, saw that the walls were manned, and heard the shouting; the officials escorting the memorial never came out of the city. Knowing the plot must have been exposed, the several dozen boats along the river scattered and fled in the first faint light. Later the authorities treated the rioters as having plotted rebellion and an attempt to rob the treasury; several dozen men were captured, and all were beheaded. But the ringleader pleaded that he had known nothing of it; he was sentenced only to exile three thousand li, and died on the road.

按：米春元年老巨富，已無心向功名，更何心圖王侯？止以咒水妖僧啟其端，降神妖僧決其志，又以枝某失職，怏怏釀成大逆。二僧已就誅，而枝某幸脫於網。天何緩討凶人哉？猶幸聖朝清明，小丑旋殄，固太平之洪福，亦此地民風素良善忠順，不當受此叛逆者之荼毒也。然信僧惑邪之禍亦酷矣。後人其深鑑之，其深戒之。

Note: Mi Chunyuan was old and enormously rich, with no thought left for rank and fame—still less any ambition to make himself a king or a marquis. It was only the water-charming sorcerer-monk who opened the door, the spirit-calling sorcerer-monk who steeled his resolve, and Zhi, out of office and nursing his resentment, who brewed it into high treason. The two monks were executed, but Zhi had the luck to slip through the net. Why is Heaven so slow to punish the wicked? Fortunately the reign is enlightened, and the petty traitors were quickly wiped out. That was the great good fortune of a peaceful age—and it was also because the people of this region have always been decent, loyal, and law-abiding, and did not deserve to be poisoned by such rebels. Yet the ruin that comes from trusting monks and being seduced by heresy is cruel enough. Let those who come after take this deeply as a mirror, and deeply as a warning.

妖術託夢劫其家

Sorcerers Send a Dream, Then Plunder the House

老狐晝伏岩洞，夜出尋食草木之實。有偶於草木中吸得天地氤氳之情者，便有靈變，能幻化為美婦，以迷誘人，採人之陽精，以益其靈通。法師捕得而烹之。和尚如求得狐心，焙而乾之，熏以好香，於深山中構一草廬，以狐心奉祀於中，日誦諸般懺文經卷超度之，夜則群妖眾怪嗥者、呼者、悲者、泣者、叫者、嘯者、能為人言或蠻語者，千怪萬狀，於草廬外哀弔，極其淒涼。要極大膽之人，方敢中處。弔過七日，亦漸漸稀少。晝夜常誦經作法，備果食供奉。積至四十九日，然後焚了草廬，把狐心領回，香火祀之。如明日欲往見某人，先夜以錦囊盛狐心，置於心上，夜必夢婦人領之，先見其人：次日往拜其人，已夢中相會。後有所干求，人必以為異而多從之。此僧家騙化之一術也。

Old foxes hide in rocky caves by day and come out at night to feed on the fruits of grasses and trees. If one happens to breathe in the generative essence of heaven and earth among the plants, it gains spiritual powers: it can transform into a beautiful woman to bewitch and seduce men, draining their yang essence to increase its own magic. When a ritual master catches such a fox and cooks it, a monk who manages to obtain the fox's heart roasts it dry, fumes it with fine incense, builds a thatched hut deep in the mountains, and enshrines the heart inside. By day he chants every kind of penitential text and sutra to deliver its soul; by night, hordes of demons and monsters—howling, calling, moaning, weeping, shrieking, whistling, some speaking human words, some barbarian tongues, in a thousand uncanny shapes—come to mourn outside the hut, a scene of utter desolation. It takes a man of extraordinary nerve to dare stay inside. After seven days of mourning, the visitors gradually thin out. Day and night he keeps chanting sutras and performing rites, laying out fruit and food as offerings. When forty-nine days have passed, he burns the hut down, takes the fox heart home, and keeps it on an altar with incense. If he means to call on someone the next day, the night before he puts the fox heart in a brocade pouch and lays it over his own heart. That night he is sure to dream of a woman who leads him to meet the man. The next day, when he goes to pay his respects, the man has already met him in a dream. Afterward, whatever he asks for, people find it uncanny and usually grant it. This is one of the tricks monks use to cheat people in the name of collecting alms.

有富家羊老，生二子娶二媳矣。蓄積盈餘，極是慳吝，分文不肯施捨。忽夜夢兩高僧來化緣，次日果有兩僧到，容貌儼如夢中所會者，稱言：「你取財太急，人多怨氣。吾與你有夙緣，特來為你懺悔。」羊老信之，問：「懺悔當如何？」僧曰：「你合家當齋戒三日，再買果餅麵食及三牲豬羊肉，半葷半素，吾為你作法請將，誦經供佛，將生前罪過解釋，再祈後增福祿，便家門請吉，死後免墮矣。」羊老依言，齋戒買辦。

There was a rich man called Old Yang, who had two sons and had married in two daughters-in-law. His savings were more than ample, but he was a terrible miser and would not part with a single cash in charity. One night he suddenly dreamed that two eminent monks came begging alms. The next day two monks did in fact arrive, looking exactly like the ones he had met in the dream. They said, 'You have been too grasping in gathering wealth, and much resentment has built up against you. We share a bond with you from a former life, and have come especially to perform repentance on your behalf.' Old Yang believed them and asked, 'How should the repentance be done?' The monks said, 'Your whole household must fast for three days. Then buy fruit, cakes, and pastries, and the three sacrificial meats—pork and mutton—half meat and half vegetarian. We will perform rites for you, call down the spirit generals, chant sutras, and make offerings to the Buddha, absolving the sins of your lifetime. Then we will pray for increased fortune and emolument hereafter, so that your household enjoys good luck and after death you escape falling into perdition.' Old Yang did as they said, fasting and buying the provisions.

至第三日，又有兩僧到，又留相助誦經。至晚，來一僧念咒燒符，降遣羊老自跳自喊，取利劍在手，指其妻、子曰：「此鬼也。」悉手刃之。又追殺二媳。媳求僧解勸。僧指羊老喝曰：「坐！」羊老遂提劍咬牙，昏昏而坐，不醒人事。四僧入，輪姦二婦訖，以索縛之。搜其家財幣，捆作四擔，夤夜逃去。

On the third day two more monks arrived, and they too were kept on to help chant the sutras. That evening one monk chanted spells and burned talismans, and set Old Yang jumping and shouting under a spell. Yang snatched up a sharp sword, pointed at his wife and sons, and cried, 'These are demons!'—and cut them all down with his own hand. Then he chased after the two daughters-in-law to kill them. The women begged the monks to restrain him. A monk pointed at Old Yang and barked, 'Sit!' Sword in hand and teeth clenched, Yang sank into a dazed stupor, dead to the world. The four monks went in, raped the two women one after another, and bound them with ropes. They ransacked the house for money and valuables, tied the loot into four carrying loads, and fled in the dead of night.

次日，有人入其家者，見羊老被髮伏劍，睜眼語，急出呼眾入看。親眾群擁而入，羊老只說要殺鬼。眾向前奪去其劍，呼其名曰：「你何故如此？」羊老漸漸復蘇。人又問之，才知應曰：「吾夢見鬼多，正在此殺鬼，得你們叫我醒也。」及入後室，妻與子皆被殺。羊老大哭曰：「此我記得，殺三鬼在此，又趕殺二鬼婆，被僧攔開。」及入房，二婦皆捆在床，乃呼鄰婦來解之。各稱被僧所姦，金銀財帛皆收拾去矣。一家痛恨無窮，一邊收殮三尸，一邊遣人四路趕僧，皆趕上兩日路，並不見踪而還。

The next day, someone came into the house and found Old Yang with his hair hanging loose, crouched over the sword, eyes open and muttering. He rushed out and called a crowd in to look. Relatives and neighbors poured in. Old Yang kept saying he had to kill demons. They stepped forward, wrested the sword away, and called him by name: 'What has happened to you?' Little by little, Old Yang came around. When they questioned him again, he could finally answer: 'I dreamed the place was full of demons, and I was right here killing them—until you called me awake.' When they went into the back rooms, his wife and sons had all been killed. Old Yang wailed, 'That much I remember: I killed three demons here, then chased two demon-hags to kill them too, but the monks held me off.' When they went into the inner room, both women were tied up on the beds. They called in neighbor women to free them. Each said she had been raped by the monks, and that all the gold, silver, and valuables had been gathered up and carried off. The household's grief and hatred knew no end. While they laid out the three corpses for burial, they also sent men chasing the monks on every road; the pursuers went a full two days' journey, saw no trace of them, and came back.

按：羊老素慳吝，則為富不仁之事有矣。乃僧悚以怨氣，便信其說，而留以作福懺悔，則心先自疚故也。僧欲行術劫財，而先形於夢，此亦得狐心引夢之術而用之。彼夢謂高僧而反為劫僧，不信晝所為而信夜所夢，亦惑矣；不行善於平昔，而求懺悔於修齋，亦愚矣。今人多殘忍不仁，貪暴不義，而欲飯僧供佛，追修懺悔，何異羊老之覆哉！甚矣！惡不可為，而僧不可信也。鑑此當為之凜凜。

Note: Old Yang was a lifelong miser, so he must have done his share of a rich man's heartless deeds. When the monks frightened him with talk of resentment, he believed them at once and kept them on to work blessings and repentance—because his conscience was already guilty. The monks meant to use magic to rob him, and first showed themselves in a dream; this was the fox-heart dream-sending art, put to use. He dreamed they were eminent monks, and they turned out to be robber monks. To disbelieve what is done by daylight but believe what is dreamed at night is delusion; to do no good in ordinary times but seek repentance through a fast is folly. Nowadays many people are cruel and heartless, greedy and unjust, yet hope to feed monks and make offerings to the Buddha, buying repentance after the fact. How are they any different from Old Yang in his ruin? It is beyond measure: evil must not be done, and monks are not to be trusted. Let this example make you shudder.

摩臉賊拐帶幼童

The Face-Stroking Thief Kidnaps Young Boys

往年，京城中有幼童出外，嘗被人拐帶而去，尋之又無踪。後累累有之。人多見一僧，摩幼童之臉，則幼童隨之而行。既而尋，已失之。故京城盛傳謂之「摩臉賊」。時在京僧釋人多，未察其孰是也。

Some years ago, young boys in the capital who went out were sometimes kidnapped and carried off, and searches found no trace of them. Later it happened again and again. Many people had seen a monk stroke a boy's face, whereupon the boy would follow him off; when they went looking soon after, the boy was already gone. So the capital buzzed with talk of a 'face-stroking thief.' At that time there were a great many monks in the capital, and no one could tell which one it was.

忽宓富人，止生一子，出外不返，四下跟尋甚急，各處出賞帖曰：「有收留得者，賞銀二十兩；報信者賞銀一十兩。」四出掛帖出賞，終莫得下落。

Then a rich man named Mi, who had only one son, had the boy go out and never come back. The family searched frantically in every direction and posted reward notices all around: 'Twenty taels of silver to anyone sheltering him; ten taels for information.' They put up notices and offered rewards everywhere, but never learned his whereabouts.

住宓家小屋人班八，以淘街為生。一日，懶去掏街，往城外晦真庵閒遊，轉入後室，四旁周覽。忽破水障中一小士露頭來。班八認是宓家人，忙呼之曰：「家中四處尋你，何故在此？」宓子曰：「僧閉禁我在此，你快來救我！」班八看房門已鎖，恐一人難帶此子出，謂之曰：「你小心暫在此，我報你令尊知，即來取你矣。」飛跑而歸，報宓老曰：「令郎受禁在晦真庵中，速去救之。」宓老即招五十餘人，前後到庵。班八引至庵後房中，打開門，認出宓子，又搜出十數童輩，即令眾人捆住僧小山並同庵三人，都縛來狀送到官。

A man named Ban Ba, who lived in a small house on the Mi property, made his living cleaning the streets. One day, too lazy to go clean the streets, he wandered out to Huizhen Hermitage outside the city for a stroll, turned into the rear quarters, and poked around on every side. Suddenly a small boy's head showed through a broken slatted screen. Ban Ba recognized him as the Mi family's son and called out at once, 'Your family is searching everywhere for you—what are you doing here?' The Mi boy said, 'The monk has me locked up in here. Come rescue me, quick!' Ban Ba saw that the door was locked. Afraid he could not get the boy out alone, he told him, 'Stay put and keep quiet for now. I will report to your father and come straight back for you.' He ran home at full speed and told Old Mi, 'Your son is being held in Huizhen Hermitage. Go rescue him at once.' Old Mi immediately rounded up fifty-odd men, who converged on the hermitage from front and back. Ban Ba led them to the back room, broke the door open, and picked out the Mi boy; a search turned up a dozen more boys. He had the men bind the monk Xiaoshan and the three others of the hermitage, and they were all trussed up and delivered to the authorities with a formal complaint.

官先審問眾童曰：「汝等如何被引入庵？」眾童曰：「和尚以手摩我眼睛，便見兩邊背後都是猛虎毒蛇，將來咬人傷人，唯面前一條路清淨好行，我輩只向前走，便到此庵，被和尚幽閉住。」又問曰：「和尚留汝等在庵幹何事？」眾童曰：「可恨這禿子，不拘日夜，將我等做苦春，極是疼痛。若不從，便將大杖撻打。眾人怕他，只得從他所為。」又問曰：「先拐來的後必長大，都放在何處去？」眾童曰：「有病者有長大者，和尚說放他回去，未知後都回家否。」官再審僧小山曰：「你拐來眾童後，病的長的都放哪裡去？」僧不敢應。再問同庵三人，都云：「毒死埋訖。」官聞言大怒，將小山打四十，同庵者各打二十，曰：「此罪不容於死。」令鎖出衙門外，許失童之家君聚手毆，打得身無完膚，有割其陽塞於僧口者，半日而死。人莫不恨其淫而快其死。後將其庵焚之，拐帶之禍遂息。

The magistrate first questioned the boys: 'How were you lured into the hermitage?' The boys said, 'The monk rubbed his hand over our eyes, and then we saw fierce tigers and venomous snakes on both sides and behind us, coming to bite and maul us. Only the road straight ahead was clear and safe to walk. We just kept walking forward, and ended up at this hermitage, where the monk shut us up.' He asked, 'What did the monk keep you in the hermitage for?' The boys said, 'That baldy is hateful. Day and night he used our bodies for his pleasure, and it hurt terribly. If we refused, he beat us with a heavy staff. We were all afraid of him and had no choice but to submit.' He asked further, 'The boys kidnapped earlier must have grown up by now. Where were they sent?' The boys said, 'The ones who fell sick or grew up—the monk said he let them go home. We do not know whether they ever made it home.' The magistrate then interrogated the monk Xiaoshan: 'After you kidnapped these boys, where did you send the sick ones and the grown ones?' The monk did not dare answer. He pressed the three others of the hermitage, and all said, 'They were poisoned and buried.' The magistrate flew into a rage at these words. He had Xiaoshan given forty strokes and the other three twenty each, declaring, 'Even death is too good for this crime.' He ordered them chained outside the yamen gate and allowed the fathers of the missing boys to gather and beat them. They beat them until not an inch of skin was left whole; one man cut off the monk's genitals and stuffed them into his mouth. He was dead within half a day. There was no one who did not loathe his depravity and relish his death. Afterward the hermitage was burned down, and the scourge of kidnappings came to an end.

按：好男風者，禽瀆之行。此僧必有春意之方，非拐諸幼童，無以快其欲。又習得妖法，摩其眼睛則昏花見怪，故可誘引童男。其罪浮於天矣。積惡貫盈，眾戮其身，言之羞口舌，書之污簡牘，人誰不切齒之！世有負男子之軀者，其可襲此僧之惡行哉！

Note: A taste for boys is the conduct of beasts. This monk must have had some aphrodisiac formula; without kidnapping young boys he had no way to glut his lust. He had also learned a sorcerer's art: a rub over their eyes made their sight blur and showed them terrors, so he could lure the boys away. His crimes rose higher than the sky. When his measure of evil was full, the crowd did the killing. To speak of it shames the tongue; to write of it soils the page. Who would not gnash his teeth at it? Any man who bears a man's body—how could he follow in this monk's evil ways?

第二十四類 引嫖騙

Category 24 · Luring Men into the Brothels

父尋子而自落嫖

A Father Goes Looking for His Son and Falls into Whoring Himself

富人左東溪，止生一子少山，常帶千金財本，往南京買賣。既而入院毛月華，一年不歸。東溪問於人，知子以嫖故，因貪歡忘返，累以信促之歸。初猶回音，推託以帳未取完，後信往亦不答。東溪聞其財本已費過半矣，心中甚怒。欲自往尋之，又思空行費盤纏，乃帶三百金貨物，僱僕施來祿同往京尋子。

The rich man Zuo Dongxi had only one son, Shaoshan, who would regularly take a thousand taels of trading capital to Nanjing to do business. Then the boy moved in with a courtesan named Mao Yuehua and stayed away a whole year. Dongxi asked around and learned that his son had taken to the brothels and grown so greedy for pleasure that he forgot to come back, and he sent letter after letter pressing him to return home. At first Shaoshan still wrote back, putting him off with talk of accounts not yet collected; later he stopped answering at all. When Dongxi heard that more than half the capital had already been spent, he was furious. He wanted to go after the boy himself, but reckoned that making the trip empty-handed would waste the travel money, so he took three hundred taels' worth of goods, hired a servant named Shi Lailu, and went with him to Nanjing to find his son.

人貨到京，早有人報知少山云：「爾父帶貨來賣，兼欲尋汝。」少山聞言甚悶，急呼其媽毛惜卿謀之曰：「家父特來催我歸，爾計能陷他亦嫖，則我在此可久；不然，今須與你別矣。」惜卿曰：「你但深藏其間，忽與相見，我自有理會。」即遣人邀前院荀榮媽來，託他巧為牢籠。榮媽許諾而去。

When the man and his goods reached Nanjing, someone had already reported to Shaoshan: 'Your father has brought goods to sell, and he means to find you as well.' Shaoshan was dismayed at the news. He quickly called in his madam, Mao Xiqing, to scheme with her: 'My father has come expressly to press me home. If you have a plan that can trap him into whoring too, then I can stay here for good; otherwise I must part from you now.' Xiqing said, 'Just hide yourself deep inside and don't let him see you. I will take care of the rest.' She sent at once for Mama Xun Rong from the house in front and asked her to lay a clever snare for him. Mama Rong promised and left.

東溪問在京客夥，知子在毛惜卿家嫖其女月華，徑尋惜卿家來，欲呼子歸。惜卿出而款待甚恭。東溪曰：「小頑少山在你家，我到京十餘日矣，可叫他出來見我。」惜卿悚敬曰：「相公即少山令尊乎？妾幸披雲睹日也。令郎前在寒舍兩三個月，今月餘前送別久矣。」即喚女月華出見，指曰：「此爾翁也。」命下拜。東溪不禮之。又命設席。東溪曰：「吾為不肖子而來，豈索汝酒食乎？速叫兒與我歸，亦不消你假意相留。」月華曰：「果是前月已去，云欲收帳回家。若果在此，何敢相瞞？」東溪不信，定要究子下落。惜卿曰：「茅舍隻數間，任相公遍搜之。豈能藏得？」月華領東溪入內，四下覓之，無踪。東溪大怒曰：「牙人說在此，如何藏開，說這鬼話？若吾兒不見，是你家謀死，必當官告你，著你尋覓！」月華驚曰：「從來院中那有謀人者？相公勿輕易怪人。」東溪詬罵而出。

From fellow merchants staying in Nanjing, Dongxi learned that his son was whoring at Mao Xiqing's house with her girl Yuehua, and he went straight to Xiqing's place to call his son home. Xiqing came out and received him with every show of respect. Dongxi said, 'My wretched boy Shaoshan is in your house. I have been in Nanjing more than ten days. Call him out to see me.' Xiqing put on a show of awed reverence: 'Sir, are you Shaoshan's own father? What luck for me—the clouds part and I see the sun. Your son did stay at my humble house for two or three months, but we saw him off more than a month ago.' She called her daughter Yuehua out to greet him, pointed at Dongxi, and said, 'This is your father-in-law,' and told her to kneel and bow. Dongxi refused the courtesy. Xiqing ordered a feast laid out. Dongxi said, 'I came for an unfilial son—do you think I am after your wine and food? Call my son out at once to go home with me, and spare me your false show of hospitality.' Yuehua said, 'He truly left last month, saying he was going to collect his accounts and head home. If he were really here, how would we dare hide it from you?' Dongxi did not believe her and insisted on learning his son's whereabouts. Xiqing said, 'My poor place has only a few rooms—search it through as you please. How could we hide anyone?' Yuehua led Dongxi inside; he searched every corner and found no trace. Dongxi flew into a rage: 'The go-between said he was here. How dare you stash him away and spin me these ghost tales? If my son cannot be found, then your house has murdered him, and I will lodge a charge with the authorities and make you produce him!' Yuehua said in alarm, 'When has anyone in the quarter ever murdered a man? Sir, do not be so quick to accuse people.' Dongxi stormed out cursing.

行過院前，窗內一女將盆水傾出，淋東溪一身，冠服盡濕。時怒未散，問「此是誰人家？」僕來祿曰：「此一行都是樂戶人家。」東溪即入其門指罵。荀榮媽出，驚惶問故，知是女荀慶雲誤傾水淋著，即喚出，棒打無數。慶雲哀求勸救，東溪亦不睬。榮媽曰：「你好將新服換與相公，向前叩頭求赦，留在此陪人禮，免後日生禍。」

As he passed the front of the next house, a girl inside flung out a basin of water that drenched Dongxi from head to foot, soaking his hat and robes. His anger had not yet cooled. 'Whose house is this?' he asked. His servant Lailu said, 'This whole row is licensed pleasure houses.' Dongxi marched straight in the gate, pointing and cursing. Mama Xun Rong came out in alarm and asked what had happened; learning that her girl Xun Qingyun had carelessly thrown the water on him, she called the girl out at once and beat her with a stick again and again. Qingyun begged for mercy and pleaded for someone to rescue her, but Dongxi paid no heed. Mama Rong said, 'You had better bring a fresh set of clothes for the master to change into, then go to him, kowtow, and beg his pardon—and keep him here to make proper amends, so this does not bring trouble on us later.'

慶雲叩頭訖，引入內房，取一套新衣與更，跪曰：「我等人家，最怕得罪於人。萬望海度涵容，恕妾罪過。」東溪曰：「我原不怪你，只衣濕難行。我今換去，明日即送還矣。」拂衣便起。慶雲挽曰：「更有杯酒陪禮。若便去，媽又怪責我矣。」東溪曰：「何消酒？」時筵已排列，慶雲曲意陪奉，東溪亦放懷樂飲。至晚欲去，慶雲懇留曰：「今半載空房，若不宿而去，真對面不相逢也。你宿則媽歡喜，謂我善留客。此豈費房錢乎？」又飲到二更而睡。東溪思房錢終是還之，且假意不動，以試何如。慶雲偎抱撫摩之曰：「君作柳下惠，坐懷不亂耶？是入寶山空手回也。且暮夜無知，誰知爾貞節男者？」東溪笑而從之。

When Qingyun had finished kowtowing, she led him into an inner room, brought a new set of clothes for him to change into, and knelt, saying, 'People in houses like ours live in dread of giving offense. I beg you to be generous and forgive my fault.' Dongxi said, 'I never blamed you to begin with—wet clothes are just hard to walk in. I will change now and send these back tomorrow.' He shook out his sleeves and made to rise. Qingyun held him back: 'There is still a cup of wine to make amends. If you leave right away, Mama will scold me again.' Dongxi said, 'What need is there for wine?' But by then the feast was already laid out. Qingyun waited on him with every art of pleasing, and Dongxi let himself go and drank merrily. Toward evening he wanted to leave. Qingyun begged him earnestly to stay: 'This room has stood empty for half a year. If you go without staying the night, it is as though we met face to face and never met at all. If you stay, Mama will be pleased and say I know how to keep a guest. And it will not even cost you room money.' They drank on until the second watch, then went to bed. Dongxi reckoned he would pay for the room in the end anyway, so for the moment he feigned indifference and lay still, to see what would happen. Qingyun nestled into his arms and stroked him, saying, 'Are you playing Liuxia Hui, sitting with a woman in your lap and staying unmoved? That is walking into a mountain of treasure and coming back empty-handed. Besides, in the dark of night no one is watching—who would ever know what a chaste man you were?' Dongxi smiled and gave in.

次日近午方起。才梳洗罷，酒席已備，慢慢勸飲，彈唱以奉之。靠晚，又欲歸。慶雲留曰：「肯宿媽媽甚喜。若一宵而別，真是萍水之逢，落花有意，流水無心也。妾縱奉侍不周，君何不做甘雨，濟我半載旱人？」東溪又為留一夜。第三日，堅要歸，求還舊服。慶雲曰：「已遣人送往貴寓矣。」東溪曰：「承賜身上服，明日送還。」慶雲曰：「只恐不中服，何不收作表記？」又取出一箱玩物，欲擇一件相贈。東溪見箱中皆珠玉寶玩，僅取一牙扇墜。慶雲曰：「此不敢奉，此銀的敬奉。」東溪曰：「只領你意耳，何必送銀物？」慶雲曰：「此牙的是禮部公子所贈，旁刻有號。凡孤老所賜，唯銀得用。若簪甸諸玩物，須存留之。後日有會，問及即在，方表不忘之意，故不敢轉贈於人。此銀扇墜，乃預打造，以回答人者，旁鑄有妾名，故願相贈也。」東溪受之而歸。

The next day he did not rise till nearly noon. He had barely finished washing and combing when the wine feast was ready; they plied him slowly with drink and sang and played for him. Toward evening he again wanted to go back. Qingyun kept him: 'If you are willing to stay, Mama will be so pleased. To part after a single night would be a duckweed meeting on the water—the fallen flowers have feelings, but the flowing stream has no heart. Even if my service has been wanting, why will you not be a sweet rain and relieve a woman who has suffered a half-year drought?' So Dongxi stayed another night. On the third day he insisted on going back and asked for his old clothes. Qingyun said, 'I have already sent someone to deliver them to your lodgings.' Dongxi said, 'These clothes you gave me, I will return tomorrow.' Qingyun said, 'I only fear they do not suit you. Why not keep them as a keepsake?' She also brought out a box of trinkets, meaning to choose one as a gift for him. Dongxi saw that the box was full of pearls, jades, and precious curios, and took only an ivory fan pendant. Qingyun said, 'That one I dare not give you—but this silver one I offer with respect.' Dongxi said, 'I am grateful for the thought alone; why give me something of silver?' Qingyun said, 'The ivory one was a gift from the son of a Ministry of Rites official, with his mark carved on the side. Of all the gifts our patrons give us, only silver may be spent. Hairpins, inlay, and such trinkets must be kept. If the giver visits later and asks after it, it has to be here to show we have not forgotten him—so I dare not pass it on to anyone else. This silver fan pendant was made in advance for giving in return, with my name cast on the side, so I would like you to have it.' Dongxi accepted it and went back.

明日，謂來祿曰：「看妓家極難做，只誤傾一盆水，費盡小心承奉人，唯恐不當人意，我豈可過吃她物？我宿兩晚，又吃四席酒，以銀四兩與之；受一銀扇墜，以金銀玉三枝簪答之，並這身衣服，你送去還他，我不再去。」原來前兩夜來祿亦得婢桂英伴宿，兩人情意綢繆，更相捨不得。臨行囑咐曰：「主人若再來嫖，又得再會。」故來祿只願得主肯嫖，力勸曰：「前日空手去，也這般相敬；今日有銀有簪送她，她不留宿，豈不留酒乎？再吃她何妨？」東溪信之，再與僕往，以銀與簪送之。

The next day he said to Lailu, 'It strikes me how hard a courtesan's house has to work. Over one carelessly spilled basin of water, they wore themselves out waiting on me, afraid only of displeasing me. How could I go on consuming her substance? I stayed two nights and ate four feasts of wine—give her four taels of silver. I accepted a silver fan pendant—answer it with three hairpins, of gold, silver, and jade. Take these clothes I am wearing back too. You deliver them; I am not going again.' As it happened, on the two previous nights Lailu had been given the maid Guiying to share his bed, and the two had grown tenderly attached and could hardly bear to part. As he left she had charged him, 'If your master comes whoring again, we can meet again.' So Lailu's one wish was for his master to keep whoring, and he urged him hard: 'The other day you went empty-handed, and they still treated you with such respect. Today you have silver and hairpins to give her—even if she does not keep you the night, will she not keep you for wine? What harm in one more meal at her place?' Dongxi believed him and went again with the servant to present the silver and the hairpins.

慶雲得了，喜色滿面，持入誇與媽曰：「左相公送我銀四兩、簪三根。非妾趨奉得歡喜，豈送許多禮乎？」荀媽亦大喜，出叩謝曰：「本不當受厚禮，既蒙賜，還在寒舍消耍幾日。」東溪假辭要回，慶雲挽入內房，酒席已備。東溪曰：「又煩宴我，後何以報？」慶雲曰：「前日止是陪禮，今日所賜銀，已准後帳。」東溪曰：「前銀還前，我若嫖，須從今日算起。」由是日夜流連，忘其時月。來祿亦得再與桂英會，二人喜不自勝，侍奉加殷勤，使喚加聽命。主僕皆樂而忘歸矣。

Qingyun took the gifts, her face alight with joy, and carried them in to boast to Mama: 'Master Zuo sent me four taels of silver and three hairpins. If I had not pleased him with my attentions, would he send so many gifts?' Mama Xun was delighted and came out to kowtow her thanks: 'We really should not accept such generous gifts, but since you honor us with them, do stay and amuse yourself at our humble house a few more days.' Dongxi made a show of insisting on going back; Qingyun drew him into the inner room, where the feast was already laid. Dongxi said, 'Troubling you to feast me again—how shall I repay it later?' Qingyun said, 'Last time was only an apology. The silver you gave today already counts toward the bill to come.' Dongxi said, 'That silver settles what went before. If I am to whore, the reckoning starts from today.' From then on he lingered day and night and lost track of the months. Lailu, too, was reunited with Guiying; the two were beside themselves with joy, waited on their betters with redoubled zeal, and jumped to obey every order. Master and servant alike were having too good a time to think of going home.

東溪時或謂僕曰：「當要知止，銀費去多矣。」來祿便誘曰：「人有金帛，正要追歡買笑。相公掌如許大家，才得此幾月快心，縱此銀用盡，家中何患無吃著？不及趁此未老時行樂，人生寧有百年，何必作守銀虜也？」東溪心本迷戀，又累被來祿勸誘，並不知回頭。不覺半年餘，三百金幾盡。桂英時向來祿索衣服簪珥，來祿轉於主，主曰：「亦未知我用多少，須與荀媽算之，然後留盤纏回去。」及算過，已用過三百餘兩，盡貨物還之，尚未夠，盤纏全無辦。來祿曰：「小主本多，可去借些。」東溪曰：「不好開口。你去婉轉言之。」少山知父本嫖盡，撫掌大笑，令月華設席請父。及慶雲來餞行，然後東溪與子默默同歸。只謂緣遇使然，不知為計所陷。

From time to time Dongxi would say to his servant, 'A man should know when to stop. I have spent a great deal of silver.' Lailu would egg him on: 'When a man has gold and silk, that is exactly when he should chase pleasure and buy smiles. You run such a great household, master, and only now have you had a few months of real enjoyment. Even if this silver runs out, what worry is there of food or clothing at home? Why not take your pleasure while you are not yet old? Does a life last a hundred years? Why be a slave standing guard over silver?' Dongxi's heart was already besotted, and with Lailu urging him on again and again, he never thought of turning back. Before he knew it, more than half a year had passed and the three hundred taels were nearly gone. Guiying kept asking Lailu for clothes and hairpins, and Lailu passed the requests on to his master. The master said, 'I do not even know how much I have spent. I must settle accounts with Mama Xun, and then keep back travel money for the trip home.' When the reckoning was done, he had spent more than three hundred taels; even handing over all his goods did not cover it, and there was no travel money to be had at all. Lailu said, 'The young master has plenty of capital. You could borrow some from him.' Dongxi said, 'I cannot bring myself to ask. You go and put it to him tactfully.' Shaoshan, learning that his father's capital had been whored away to the last, clapped his hands and roared with laughter, and had Yuehua lay a feast for his father. Qingyun came too, to see him off. Then Dongxi and his son made the journey home together in silence. He put it all down to fate, and never knew he had been caught in a scheme.

按：尤物移人，麗色傾城，自昔慨之。安有入蠖中而皓然不滓者？東溪非為而來，直欲尋子而歸。其深知妓之迷人與嫖之破家深矣，乃入其中而掘泥揚波，更甚於子。不邇聲色、不溺情慾者既幾人哉！孔（子）曰：「吾未見好德如好色者也。」則賢之易色者信難矣。故院中語曰：「不怕深來不乖，只怕深乖不來。」則唯勿蹈其地者，可超然樊籠外矣。不然，未有不受其羈迷者。

Note: That a rare beauty can bewitch a man and a lovely face topple a city has been lamented since ancient times. Who ever stepped into the mire and came out white and unstained? Dongxi did not go there for pleasure; he only meant to find his son and bring him home. He knew full well how courtesans bewitch men and how whoring ruins a family—yet once inside, he churned the mud and rode the waves even deeper than his son. How few are the men who keep their distance from song and beauty and do not drown in desire! Confucius said, 'I have never seen a man who loves virtue the way he loves beauty.' One who is worthy enough to turn his love from beauty is truly rare. Hence the saying in the quarter: 'They never fear that a steady customer will not be tamed; they only fear that the truly wary man will not come.' Only the man who never sets foot on that ground can stand free beyond the cage. Otherwise none escapes its bonds and its spell.

Original Chinese by 張應俞 (Zhang Yingyu), published around 1617 as 《江湖歷覽杜騙新書》. The Chinese text is public domain. English is an original meaning-first study rendering, not the 2017 Columbia University Press selection.
